Thus Spoke Zarathustra

A Book for Everyone and No One — Also sprach Zarathustra, retold from the German

by Friedrich Nietzsche

1883–1885 2026

This is an AI modernization of Thus Spoke Zarathustra into contemporary English. The original is available from Project Gutenberg. Also available as an epub.

Editor's Introduction

Friedrich Nietzsche (1844–1900) had been a professor of classical philology at Basel at twenty-four and had retired with bad health at thirty-four. By the early 1880s he was living on a small pension, moving between boarding houses in Switzerland and Italy, half-blind and often ill, and writing books that hardly sold. In August 1881, walking by a lake near Sils-Maria in the Swiss Alps, he had the idea he called the eternal recurrence: the thought that everything that happens will happen again, exactly the same, forever. Thus Spoke Zarathustra grew out of it. He wrote the first part in about ten days in early 1883 and published the book in four parts between 1883 and 1885. The fourth part was printed at his own expense in a few dozen copies, most of which he kept.

Zarathustra is the Persian prophet the Greeks called Zoroaster. Nietzsche chose him because, as he later explained, Zarathustra was the first to make the struggle between good and evil the engine of the world, and so he should be the first to see past it. The book is not an argument laid out in steps. It is a story told in short speeches, songs and parables: a prophet comes down from his mountain after ten years alone, tells a crowd that God is dead and that man is something that must be overcome, is laughed at, gathers a few disciples, sends them away, and climbs back up, several times, as he works toward the hardest of his ideas.

The style imitates Luther's German Bible on purpose: "Thus spoke Zarathustra" closes most of the speeches the way a refrain closes a psalm, and there are parables, beatitudes, a last supper and a donkey. The parody is the point, and much of the book is funny. Some of its terms need a word. The overman is Nietzsche's Übermensch, the higher type he says humanity should be a bridge toward; the word is kept as "overman" because the book plays it against "going under" from its first page. The will to power, the last man and the eternal recurrence are his own phrases, and each is explained by the book itself.

Nietzsche collapsed in Turin in January 1889 and spent his last eleven years unable to work. His sister, Elisabeth Förster-Nietzsche, controlled his papers and shaped his reputation, later presenting him as a prophet of German nationalism; he had broken with her husband over his antisemitism. This edition is translated from Nietzsche's German, with Thomas Common's 1909 English translation consulted for sense only. Common wrote in the language of the King James Bible; the German is plainer than that, and so is this.

Part One

Zarathustra’s Prologue

I

When Zarathustra was thirty years old, he left his home and the lake of his home and went up into the mountains. There he enjoyed his spirit and his solitude, and for ten years he did not grow tired of it. But at last his heart changed — and one morning he rose with the dawn, stepped out before the sun, and spoke to it like this:

“You great star! What would your happiness be, if you did not have those you shine for?

For ten years you have come up here to my cave: you would have grown sick of your light and of this road, if not for me, my eagle, and my serpent.

But every morning we waited for you, took your overflow from you, and blessed you for it.

Look! I am weary of my wisdom, like a bee that has gathered too much honey. I need hands that reach out for it.

I want to give it away and hand it out, until the wise among men are glad once again of their folly, and the poor once again of their riches.

For that I must go down into the depths, as you do in the evening when you sink behind the sea and bring your light even to the underworld, you overrich star!

Like you, I must go under, as men call it, the men to whom I want to go down.

So bless me, then, you calm eye, which can look without envy even on a happiness that is all too great!

Bless the cup that wants to overflow, so that the water may flow from it golden and carry the reflection of your delight everywhere!

Look! This cup wants to be empty again, and Zarathustra wants to be a man again.”

— Thus began Zarathustra’s going under.

II

Zarathustra came down the mountain alone, and no one met him. But when he came into the forest, there suddenly stood before him an old man, who had left his holy hut to look for roots in the woods. And the old man spoke to Zarathustra like this:

This wanderer is no stranger to me: many years ago he passed by here. Zarathustra was his name; but he has changed. Back then you carried your ashes up the mountain: will you carry your fire down into the valleys today? Are you not afraid of the punishment for arsonists?

Yes, I recognize Zarathustra. His eye is clear, and no disgust lurks around his mouth. Does he not walk like a dancer?

Zarathustra has changed, Zarathustra has become a child, Zarathustra is awake: what do you want now among the sleepers?

You lived in your solitude as in the sea, and the sea carried you. Woe, do you want to climb out onto the land? Woe, do you want to drag your own body around again?

Zarathustra answered: “I love mankind.”

Why, said the saint, did I go into the forest and the wilderness at all? Was it not because I loved mankind all too much?

Now I love God: mankind I do not love. Man is too imperfect a thing for me. Love of man would kill me.

Zarathustra answered: “Who said anything about love! I am bringing mankind a gift.”

Give them nothing, said the saint. Better to take something off their shoulders and carry it with them — that will do them the most good, as long as it does you good!

And if you want to give to them, give them no more than alms, and make them beg for it, too!

“No,” answered Zarathustra, “I give no alms. I am not poor enough for that.”

The saint laughed at Zarathustra and said: Then see to it that they accept your treasures! They are suspicious of hermits and do not believe that we come to give gifts.

Our footsteps sound too lonely to them as they echo through the streets. And just as when they lie in bed at night and hear a man walking about, long before the sun is up, they probably ask themselves: where is that thief going?

Do not go to men, and stay in the forest! Go to the animals instead! Why will you not be as I am — a bear among bears, a bird among birds?

“And what does the saint do in the forest?” asked Zarathustra.

The saint answered: I make songs and sing them, and when I make songs, I laugh, I weep, and I growl: that is how I praise God.

With singing, weeping, laughing, and growling I praise the God who is my god. But what do you bring us as a gift?

When Zarathustra had heard these words, he saluted the saint and said: “What could I have to give you! But let me go quickly, before I take something from you!” — And so they parted from each other, the old man and the man, laughing, just as two boys laugh.

But when Zarathustra was alone, he spoke to his heart: “Could it be possible! This old saint in his forest has not yet heard that God is dead!” —

III

When Zarathustra came into the nearest town, which lies at the edge of the forest, he found a great crowd of people gathered in the marketplace: for it had been announced that there would be a tightrope walker to see. And Zarathustra spoke to the people like this:

I teach you the overman. Man is something that must be overcome. What have you done to overcome him?

All beings so far have created something beyond themselves: and you want to be the ebb of this great flood, and would rather go back to the beasts than overcome man?

What is the ape to man? A laughingstock, or a painful shame. And that is exactly what man shall be to the overman: a laughingstock, or a painful shame.

You have made your way from worm to man, and much in you is still worm. Once you were apes, and even now man is more of an ape than any ape.

But even the wisest among you is only a discord, a hybrid of plant and ghost. But do I tell you to become ghosts or plants?

Look, I teach you the overman!

The overman is the meaning of the earth. Let your will say: the overman shall be the meaning of the earth!

I implore you, my brothers, remain faithful to the earth, and do not believe those who speak to you of hopes beyond the earth! They are poisoners, whether they know it or not.

They are despisers of life, dying off and poisoned themselves, and the earth is weary of them: so let them go!

Once, blasphemy against God was the greatest blasphemy; but God died, and these blasphemers died with him. To blaspheme against the earth is now the most terrible thing — and to honor the bowels of the unfathomable more highly than the meaning of the earth!

Once the soul looked on the body with contempt: and in those days that contempt was the highest thing — the soul wanted the body thin, hideous, starved. In that way it thought it could slip away from the body and from the earth.

Oh, that soul was itself still thin, hideous, and starved: and cruelty was that soul’s lust!

But you too, my brothers, tell me: what does your body say about your soul? Is your soul not poverty and filth and a wretched contentment?

Truly, man is a polluted river. One must be a sea to take in a polluted river without becoming unclean.

Look, I teach you the overman: he is this sea; in him your great contempt can go under.

What is the greatest thing you can experience? It is the hour of the great contempt. The hour in which even your happiness turns to disgust for you, and your reason and your virtue as well.

The hour when you say: “What does my happiness matter! It is poverty and filth and a wretched contentment. But my happiness ought to justify existence itself!”

The hour when you say: “What does my reason matter! Does it crave knowledge as the lion craves its food? It is poverty and filth and a wretched contentment!”

The hour when you say: “What does my virtue matter! It has never yet made me rage. How tired I am of my good and my evil! All of that is poverty and filth and a wretched contentment!”

The hour when you say: “What does my justice matter! I do not see that I am fire and fuel. But the just man is fire and fuel!”

The hour when you say: “What does my pity matter! Is pity not the cross to which he is nailed who loves mankind? But my pity is no crucifixion.”

Have you ever spoken like this? Have you ever cried out like this? Ah, if only I had heard you cry out like this!

Not your sin — your easy satisfaction cries out to heaven; your stinginess even in your sinning cries out to heaven!

Where, then, is the lightning that would lick you with its tongue? Where is the madness you ought to be inoculated with?

Look, I teach you the overman: he is this lightning, he is this madness! —

When Zarathustra had spoken like this, someone in the crowd shouted: “We’ve heard enough about the tightrope walker; now let’s see him too!” And all the people laughed at Zarathustra. But the tightrope walker, who thought the words were meant for him, set to work.

IV

But Zarathustra looked at the people and wondered. Then he spoke like this:

Man is a rope, tied between beast and overman — a rope over an abyss.

A dangerous going-over, a dangerous on-the-way, a dangerous looking-back, a dangerous shuddering and standing-still.

What is great in man is that he is a bridge and not a goal: what can be loved in man is that he is a going over and a going under.

I love those who do not know how to live except by going under, for they are the ones who go over.

I love the great despisers, because they are the great reverers, and arrows of longing for the other shore.

I love those who do not first look behind the stars for a reason to go under and to be a sacrifice, but who sacrifice themselves to the earth, so that the earth may one day belong to the overmen.

I love the one who lives in order to know, and who wants to know so that one day the overman may live. And so he wants his going under.

I love the one who works and invents so that he may build a house for the overman and make ready earth, animal, and plant for him: for that is how he wants his going under.

I love the one who loves his virtue: for virtue is the will to go under, and an arrow of longing.

I love the one who does not hold back a single drop of spirit for himself, but wants to be wholly the spirit of his virtue: so he strides over the bridge as spirit.

I love the one who makes his virtue his bent and his fate: so for the sake of his virtue he wants to live on, and to live no longer.

I love the one who does not want too many virtues. One virtue is more virtue than two, because it is more of a knot for fate to hang on.

I love the one whose soul squanders itself, who wants no thanks and gives nothing back: for he always gives and does not want to keep himself.

I love the one who is ashamed when the dice fall in his favor, and who then asks: am I a cheat, then? — for he wants to perish.

I love the one who throws golden words out ahead of his deeds and always does still more than he promises: for he wants his going under.

I love the one who justifies the people of the future and redeems the people of the past: for he wants to perish of the people of the present.

I love the one who chastises his god because he loves his god: for he must perish of his god’s wrath.

I love the one whose soul is deep even in its wounds, and who can perish of a small experience: so he gladly goes over the bridge.

I love the one whose soul is so overfull that he forgets himself, and all things are in him: so all things become his going under.

I love the one who is free in spirit and free in heart: so his head is only the bowels of his heart, but his heart drives him to his going under.

I love all those who are heavy drops, falling one by one from the dark cloud that hangs over mankind: they announce that the lightning is coming, and they perish as its heralds.

Look, I am a herald of the lightning and a heavy drop from the cloud: but this lightning is called overman. —

V

When Zarathustra had spoken these words, he looked at the people again and fell silent. “There they stand,” he said to his heart, “there they laugh: they do not understand me; I am not the mouth for these ears.

Must one first smash their ears, so that they learn to hear with their eyes? Must one rattle like kettledrums and preachers of repentance? Or do they believe only a man who stammers?

They have something they are proud of. What is it they call the thing that makes them proud? They call it culture; it sets them apart from the goatherds.

That is why they do not like to hear the word ‘contempt’ spoken of themselves. So I will speak to their pride.

So I will speak to them of the most contemptible thing of all: and that is the last man.”

And Zarathustra spoke to the people like this:

It is time for man to set himself his goal. It is time for man to plant the seed of his highest hope.

His soil is still rich enough for it. But one day this soil will be poor and tame, and no tall tree will be able to grow from it anymore.

Woe! The time is coming when man will no longer shoot the arrow of his longing out beyond man, and the string of his bow will have forgotten how to whir!

I tell you: you must still have chaos in you to give birth to a dancing star. I tell you: you still have chaos in you.

Woe! The time is coming when man will give birth to no more stars. Woe! The time of the most contemptible man is coming, the man who can no longer hold himself in contempt.

Look! I show you the last man.

“What is love? What is creation? What is longing? What is a star?” asks the last man, and blinks.

The earth has grown small by then, and on it hops the last man, who makes everything small. His kind is as impossible to wipe out as the earth flea; the last man lives longest.

“We have invented happiness,” say the last men, and they blink.

They have left the places where it was hard to live: for they need warmth. They still love their neighbor and rub up against him: for they need warmth.

Getting sick and being suspicious count as sins to them: they go about carefully. What a fool, whoever still stumbles over stones or people!

A little poison now and then: that makes for pleasant dreams. And a lot of poison at the end, for a pleasant death.

They still work, for work is entertainment. But they take care that the entertainment does not tire them out.

No one becomes poor or rich anymore: both are too much bother. Who still wants to rule? Who still wants to obey? Both are too much bother.

No shepherd and one herd! Everyone wants the same thing, everyone is the same: whoever feels differently goes into the madhouse of his own accord.

“In the old days the whole world was mad,” say the most refined of them, and they blink.

They are clever and know everything that has ever happened: so there is no end to their mockery. They still quarrel, but they soon make up — otherwise it upsets the stomach.

They have their little pleasure for the day and their little pleasure for the night: but they have great respect for health.

“We have invented happiness,” say the last men, and they blink —

And here ended the first speech of Zarathustra, which is also called “the Prologue”: for at this point the shouting and glee of the crowd interrupted him. “Give us this last man, oh Zarathustra,” they cried, “make us into these last men! Then we will make you a present of the overman!” And all the people cheered and smacked their lips. But Zarathustra grew sad and said to his heart:

They do not understand me: I am not the mouth for these ears.

I must have lived too long in the mountains; I listened too much to brooks and trees: now I talk to them as if they were goatherds.

My soul is unmoved and bright as the mountains in the morning. But they think I am cold, a mocker who makes terrible jokes.

And now they look at me and laugh: and as they laugh, they hate me too. There is ice in their laughter.

VI

But then something happened that struck every mouth dumb and fixed every eye in a stare. For in the meantime the tightrope walker had begun his performance: he had stepped out of a little door and was walking along the rope, which was stretched between two towers so that it hung over the marketplace and the people. Just as he reached the middle of his way, the little door opened once more, and a fellow in motley, dressed like a jester, sprang out and went after the first man with quick steps. “Forward, lame-foot!” cried his terrible voice. “Forward, lazybones, sneak, pasty-face! Move, or I’ll tickle you with my heel! What are you doing here between towers? The tower is where you belong — they ought to lock you up in it! You’re blocking the open road for a better man than you!” — And with every word he came closer and closer to him: but when he was only a single step behind him, the terrible thing happened that struck every mouth dumb and fixed every eye in a stare: — he let out a shriek like a devil and leaped over the man who stood in his way. But the other, when he saw his rival win like this, lost his head and the rope; he threw away his pole and shot down into the depths faster than it, a whirlwind of arms and legs. The marketplace and the people were like the sea when a storm drives into it: everyone fled apart and over one another, and most of all where the body was bound to come crashing down.

But Zarathustra stood still, and the body fell right beside him, badly smashed and broken, but not yet dead. After a while consciousness returned to the shattered man, and he saw Zarathustra kneeling beside him. “What are you doing there?” he said at last. “I knew long ago that the devil would trip me up. Now he is dragging me off to hell: will you try to stop him?”

“On my honor, friend,” answered Zarathustra, “none of the things you speak of exist: there is no devil and no hell. Your soul will be dead even sooner than your body: so fear nothing anymore!”

The man looked up at him with distrust. “If you are telling the truth,” he said then, “then I lose nothing when I lose my life. I am not much more than an animal that has been taught to dance with blows and scraps of food.”

“Not so,” said Zarathustra. “You made danger your calling, and there is nothing in that to despise. Now you perish by your calling: for that I will bury you with my own hands.”

When Zarathustra had said this, the dying man answered no more; but he moved his hand, as if he were reaching for Zarathustra’s hand to thank him. —

VII

Meanwhile evening came, and the marketplace hid itself in darkness: then the people drifted away, for even curiosity and terror grow tired. But Zarathustra sat on the ground beside the dead man and was lost in thought: so he forgot the time. But at last night came, and a cold wind blew over the lonely man. Then Zarathustra rose and said to his heart:

Truly, a fine catch Zarathustra made today! He caught no man, but he did catch a corpse.

Human existence is uncanny, and still without meaning: a jester can become its fate.

I want to teach people the meaning of their being: which is the overman, the lightning out of the dark cloud that is man.

But I am still far from them, and my sense does not speak to their senses. To people I am still something halfway between a fool and a corpse.

Dark is the night, dark are the ways of Zarathustra. Come, you cold and stiff companion! I will carry you to where I will bury you with my own hands.

VIII

When Zarathustra had said this to his heart, he loaded the corpse onto his back and set off on his way. And he had not yet gone a hundred steps when a man crept up to him and whispered in his ear — and look! The one who spoke was the jester from the tower. “Get away from this town, oh Zarathustra,” he said; “too many people here hate you. The good and the just hate you, and they call you their enemy and despiser; the believers of the true faith hate you, and they call you a danger to the crowd. It was your good luck that they laughed at you: and truly, you talked like a jester. It was your good luck that you made friends with the dead dog; by lowering yourself like that you saved yourself, for today. But go away from this town — or tomorrow I will leap over you, a living man over a dead one.” And when he had said this, the man disappeared; but Zarathustra walked on through the dark streets.

At the town gate the gravediggers met him: they held their torch up to his face, recognized Zarathustra, and mocked him a great deal. “Zarathustra is carrying off the dead dog: good for Zarathustra, he’s turned gravedigger! For our hands are too clean for this roast. Does Zarathustra mean to steal the devil’s mouthful? Well, go ahead! And enjoy your meal! If only the devil isn’t a better thief than Zarathustra! — he’ll steal them both, he’ll gobble them both up!” And they laughed together and put their heads together.

Zarathustra said not a word to this and went on his way. When he had walked for two hours, past forests and swamps, he had heard too much of the hungry howling of the wolves, and he grew hungry himself. So he stopped at a lonely house in which a light was burning.

Hunger falls on me like a robber, said Zarathustra. In forests and swamps my hunger falls on me, and in the deep of the night.

My hunger has strange moods. Often it comes to me only after a meal, and today it did not come all day: where has it been?

And with that Zarathustra knocked at the door of the house. An old man appeared; he carried the light and asked: “Who comes to me and to my bad sleep?”

“A living man and a dead one,” said Zarathustra. “Give me something to eat and drink; I forgot to during the day. He who feeds the hungry refreshes his own soul: so says wisdom.”

The old man went away, but came back at once and offered Zarathustra bread and wine. “This is a bad place for the hungry,” he said; “that is why I live here. Animals and people come to me, the hermit. But tell your companion to eat and drink too; he is more tired than you are.” Zarathustra answered: “My companion is dead; I doubt I can talk him into it.” “That’s no concern of mine,” said the old man grumpily; “whoever knocks at my house must take what I offer him. Eat, and fare well!” —

Then Zarathustra walked for another two hours and trusted to the path and to the light of the stars: for he was used to walking at night and loved to look everything that sleeps in the face. But when the morning grayed, Zarathustra found himself in a deep forest, and no path showed itself to him anymore. Then he laid the dead man in a hollow tree above where his head would lie — for he wanted to protect him from the wolves — and laid himself down on the ground and the moss. And at once he fell asleep, tired in body but with an unmoved soul.

IX

Zarathustra slept a long time, and not only the dawn passed over his face but the morning as well. But at last his eye opened: in wonder Zarathustra looked into the forest and the stillness, in wonder he looked into himself. Then he rose quickly, like a seafarer who all at once sees land, and shouted for joy: for he saw a new truth. And he spoke to his heart like this:

A light has dawned on me: I need companions, and living ones — not dead companions and corpses that I carry with me wherever I want.

Rather, I need living companions who follow me because they want to follow themselves — and to go where I want.

A light has dawned on me: let Zarathustra speak not to the people, but to companions! Zarathustra shall not become the shepherd and the dog of a herd!

To lure many away from the herd — that is what I came for. The people and the herd shall be angry with me: Zarathustra wants the shepherds to call him a robber.

Shepherds, I say, but they call themselves the good and the just. Shepherds, I say: but they call themselves the believers of the true faith.

Look at the good and the just! Whom do they hate most? The one who breaks their tablets of values, the breaker, the lawbreaker: — but he is the creator.

Look at the believers of every faith! Whom do they hate most? The one who breaks their tablets of values, the breaker, the lawbreaker: — but he is the creator.

The creator seeks companions, not corpses, and not herds and believers either. The creator seeks fellow creators, those who write new values on new tablets.

The creator seeks companions, and fellow harvesters: for everything around him stands ripe for the harvest. But he lacks the hundred sickles: so he tears off the ears of grain by hand and is annoyed.

The creator seeks companions, and those who know how to whet their sickles. They will be called destroyers and despisers of good and evil. But they are the harvesters and the celebrators.

Zarathustra seeks fellow creators; Zarathustra seeks fellow harvesters and fellow celebrators: what has he to do with herds and shepherds and corpses!

And you, my first companion, farewell! I buried you well in your hollow tree; I hid you well from the wolves.

But I am leaving you; the time is up. Between dawn and dawn a new truth came to me.

I am not to be a shepherd, nor a gravedigger. I will not even speak with the people again; for the last time I have spoken to a dead man.

I will join the creators, the harvesters, the celebrators: I will show them the rainbow and all the stairways of the overman.

I will sing my song to those who live alone and to those who live alone together; and whoever still has ears for the unheard-of, I will make his heart heavy with my happiness.

I am bound for my goal, I go my own way; over the hesitant and the dawdlers I will leap. So let my going be their going under!

X

Zarathustra had said this to his heart when the sun stood at noon: then he looked up questioningly — for above him he heard the sharp cry of a bird. And look! An eagle was sweeping through the air in wide circles, and a serpent hung from it, not like prey but like a friend: for she kept herself coiled around his neck.

“They are my animals!” said Zarathustra, and his heart was glad.

“The proudest animal under the sun and the shrewdest animal under the sun — they have gone out scouting.

They want to find out whether Zarathustra is still alive. Truly, am I still alive?

I found it more dangerous among men than among animals; Zarathustra walks dangerous paths. May my animals lead me!”

When Zarathustra had said this, he remembered the words of the saint in the forest, sighed, and spoke to his heart like this:

If only I were shrewder! If only I were shrewd to the very core, like my serpent!

But I am asking for the impossible: so I ask my pride instead to go always with my shrewdness!

And if one day my shrewdness leaves me — ah, it loves to fly away! — then may my pride still fly with my folly!

— Thus began Zarathustra’s going under.

Zarathustra’s Discourses

On the Three Metamorphoses

Three metamorphoses of the spirit I name for you: how the spirit becomes a camel, the camel a lion, and the lion at last a child.

There are many heavy things for the spirit, for the strong, load-bearing spirit in which reverence dwells: its strength longs for the heavy and the heaviest.

What is heavy? asks the load-bearing spirit; and so it kneels down, like the camel, and wants to be well loaded.

What is the heaviest thing, you heroes? asks the load-bearing spirit, so that I may take it upon myself and rejoice in my strength.

Is it not this: to humble yourself, in order to wound your pride? To let your folly shine out, in order to mock your wisdom?

Or is it this: to part from our cause just when it celebrates its victory? To climb high mountains in order to tempt the tempter?

Or is it this: to feed on the acorns and grass of knowledge, and for the sake of truth to suffer hunger in the soul?

Or is it this: to be sick, and send the comforters home, and make friends with the deaf, who never hear what you want?

Or is it this: to step into dirty water, if it is the water of truth, and not to push away cold frogs and hot toads?

Or is it this: to love those who despise us, and to hold out a hand to the ghost when it wants to frighten us?

All these heaviest things the load-bearing spirit takes upon itself: like the camel that hurries into the desert with its load, so it hurries into its desert.

But in the loneliest desert the second metamorphosis takes place: here the spirit becomes a lion; it wants to seize freedom as its prey and be master in its own desert.

Here it seeks out its last master: it wants to become his enemy and the enemy of its last god; it wants to wrestle for victory with the great dragon.

What is the great dragon that the spirit no longer wants to call master and god? “You-shall” is the name of the great dragon. But the spirit of the lion says “I will.”

“You-shall” lies in its path, glittering with gold, a scaly beast, and on every scale “You-shall!” shines in gold.

Values a thousand years old shine on these scales, and so speaks the mightiest of all dragons: “All the value of things — it shines on me.”

“All value has already been created, and all created value — that is me. Truly, there shall be no more ‘I will’!” So speaks the dragon.

My brothers, why is the lion needed in the spirit? Why is the beast of burden not enough, the one that renounces and is reverent?

To create new values — even the lion cannot yet do that: but to create freedom for itself for new creating — that the lion’s might can do.

To create freedom for itself, and a holy No even to duty: for that, my brothers, the lion is needed.

To take for itself the right to new values — that is the most terrible taking for a load-bearing and reverent spirit. Truly, to such a spirit it is robbery, and the business of a beast of prey.

Once it loved the “You-shall” as its holiest thing: now it must find delusion and caprice even in the holiest, so that it may steal its freedom away from its love: it takes a lion for this robbery.

But tell me, my brothers, what can the child still do that even the lion could not? Why must the preying lion still become a child?

The child is innocence and forgetting, a new beginning, a game, a wheel rolling out of itself, a first movement, a holy Yes.

Yes, for the game of creating, my brothers, a holy Yes is needed: the spirit now wills its own will; the one who was lost to the world now wins his own world.

Three metamorphoses of the spirit I have named for you: how the spirit became a camel, the camel a lion, and the lion at last a child. —

Thus spoke Zarathustra. And at that time he was staying in the town that is called: the Motley Cow.

On the Professors of Virtue

Zarathustra heard people praise a wise man who was said to speak well about sleep and about virtue: he was greatly honored and rewarded for it, and all the young men sat before his professor’s chair. Zarathustra went to him, and he sat before his chair with all the young men. And the wise man spoke like this:

Honor sleep, and be bashful before it! That is the first thing! And stay out of the way of all who sleep badly and lie awake at night!

Even the thief is bashful before sleep: he always steals softly through the night. But the night watchman is shameless; shamelessly he carries his horn.

Sleeping is no small art: you have to stay awake all day for it.

Ten times a day you must overcome yourself: that makes for a good tiredness, and it is poppy for the soul.

Ten times you must reconcile yourself with yourself again; for overcoming is bitterness, and the unreconciled sleep badly.

Ten truths you must find in a day: otherwise you will still be searching for truth at night, and your soul will have stayed hungry.

Ten times a day you must laugh and be cheerful: otherwise your stomach, that father of gloom, will disturb you in the night.

Few people know it: but you must have all the virtues in order to sleep well. Shall I bear false witness? Shall I commit adultery?

Shall I covet my neighbor’s maidservant? None of that would go well with good sleep.

And even when you have all the virtues, there is still one more thing you must know how to do: send the virtues themselves to sleep at the right time.

So that they do not quarrel with one another, the nice little women! And over you, you unhappy wretch!

Peace with God and with your neighbor: that is what good sleep wants. And peace even with your neighbor’s devil! Otherwise he will haunt you at night.

Honor the authorities and obey them, even the crooked authorities! That is what good sleep wants. How can I help it if power likes to walk on crooked legs?

I will always call him the best shepherd who leads his sheep to the greenest pasture: that is what goes well with good sleep.

I do not want many honors, or great treasures: that inflames the spleen. But it is hard to sleep well without a good name and a little treasure.

A little company is more welcome to me than bad company: but it must come and go at the right time. That is what goes well with good sleep.

I am also very fond of the poor in spirit: they promote sleep. Blessed are they, especially if you always tell them they are right.

That is how the day passes for the virtuous man. And when night comes, I take good care not to summon sleep! Sleep, who is the master of the virtues, does not want to be summoned!

Instead I think over what I did and thought during the day. Chewing it over, patient as a cow, I ask myself: what, then, were your ten overcomings?

And what were the ten reconciliations, and the ten truths, and the ten laughs with which my heart treated itself?

As I weigh such things, rocked by forty thoughts, sleep suddenly overtakes me, the unsummoned one, the master of the virtues.

Sleep taps on my eyes: and they grow heavy. Sleep touches my mouth: and it stays open.

Truly, on soft soles he comes to me, the dearest of thieves, and steals my thoughts from me: I stand there as stupid as this chair.

But I do not stand there for long: I am already lying down. —

When Zarathustra heard the wise man speak like this, he laughed in his heart: for a light had dawned on him. And he spoke to his heart like this:

This wise man with his forty thoughts is a fool to me: but I do believe he knows all about sleeping.

Happy already is anyone who lives near this wise man! Sleep like that is catching; it is catching even through a thick wall.

There is a magic even in his professor’s chair. And it was not for nothing that the young men sat before this preacher of virtue.

His wisdom says: stay awake in order to sleep well. And truly, if life had no meaning and I had to choose nonsense, this would be the nonsense most worth choosing for me too.

Now I understand clearly what people once looked for above all when they looked for teachers of virtue. Good sleep is what they looked for, and poppy-flowered virtues to go with it!

To all these much-praised wise men of the lecture chairs, wisdom was sleep without dreams: they knew no better meaning of life.

Even today there may well be a few like this preacher of virtue, and not always so honest: but their time is up. And they will not stand for much longer: they are already lying down.

Blessed are the sleepy ones: for they shall soon nod off. —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Believers in Worlds Behind

Once Zarathustra too cast his delusion beyond man, like all the believers in worlds behind. Back then the world seemed to me the work of a suffering and tormented god.

Back then the world seemed to me a dream, and the poem of a god; colored smoke before the eyes of a divinely dissatisfied one.

Good and evil and pleasure and pain and I and you — colored smoke, it seemed to me, before creative eyes. The creator wanted to look away from himself — so he created the world.

It is a drunken pleasure for the sufferer to look away from his suffering and to lose himself. Drunken pleasure and losing-oneself: that is what the world once seemed to me.

This world, the eternally imperfect one, the image of an eternal contradiction, and an imperfect image — a drunken pleasure for its imperfect creator: — that is what the world once seemed to me.

So I too once cast my delusion beyond man, like all the believers in worlds behind. Beyond man in truth?

Ah, brothers, this god whom I created was man’s work and man’s madness, like all gods!

He was man, and only a poor piece of man and of the “I”: out of my own ashes and embers this ghost came to me, and truly! It did not come to me from beyond!

What happened, my brothers? I overcame myself, the sufferer; I carried my own ashes up the mountain; I invented a brighter flame for myself. And look! Then the ghost fled from me!

It would be suffering for me now, and torment for the one who has recovered, to believe in such ghosts: it would be suffering for me now, and humiliation. This is how I speak to the believers in worlds behind.

It was suffering and incapacity — that is what created all worlds behind; and that brief madness of happiness that only the one who suffers most experiences.

Weariness, which wants to reach the ultimate in one leap, in one death-leap; a poor, ignorant weariness that no longer even wants to want: that is what created all gods and worlds behind.

Believe me, my brothers! It was the body that despaired of the body — it groped at the ultimate walls with the fingers of a deluded spirit.

Believe me, my brothers! It was the body that despaired of the earth — it heard the belly of being speaking to it.

And then it wanted to push its head through the ultimate walls, and not only its head — over into “that other world.”

But “that other world” is well hidden from man, that dehumanized, inhuman world which is a heavenly nothing; and the belly of being does not speak to man at all, except as man.

Truly, all being is hard to prove and hard to get to speak. Tell me, brothers, is not the strangest of all things still the best proved?

Yes, this “I,” and the contradiction and confusion of the “I,” still speak most honestly of its being — this creating, willing, valuing “I,” which is the measure and value of things.

And this most honest being, the “I” — it speaks of the body, and it still wants the body, even when it makes poems and raves and flutters about on broken wings.

It learns to speak more and more honestly, the “I”: and the more it learns, the more words and honors it finds for the body and the earth.

My “I” taught me a new pride, and I teach it to mankind: no longer to bury your head in the sand of heavenly things, but to carry it freely, an earthly head, which creates the meaning of the earth!

I teach mankind a new will: to want this way that man has walked blindly, and to call it good, and no longer to slink off to the side of it, like the sick and the dying!

It was the sick and the dying who despised the body and the earth and invented the heavenly realm and the redeeming drops of blood: but even these sweet and gloomy poisons they took from the body and the earth!

They wanted to escape their misery, and the stars were too far away for them. So they sighed: “Oh, if only there were heavenly paths by which to sneak into another being and another happiness!” — so they invented their sneaking ways for themselves, and their bloody little potions!

Now they imagined themselves carried away from their body and this earth, these ingrates. Yet to whom did they owe the spasm and the bliss of their rapture? To their body and this earth.

Zarathustra is gentle with the sick. Truly, he is not angry at their kinds of comfort and ingratitude. May they become convalescents and overcomers, and create a higher body for themselves!

Nor is Zarathustra angry with the convalescent when he looks tenderly back at his delusion and creeps around the grave of his god at midnight: but to me even his tears are still sickness and a sick body.

There have always been many sickly people among those who make poems and crave God; they furiously hate the seeker of knowledge and that youngest of the virtues, which is called: honesty.

They always look back toward dark ages: then, of course, delusion and faith were a different thing; the raving of reason was likeness to God, and doubt was sin.

I know these godlike ones all too well: they want people to believe in them, and doubt to be sin. All too well I also know what they themselves believe in most.

Truly not in worlds behind and redeeming drops of blood: rather, they too believe most in the body, and their own body is their thing in itself.

But to them it is a sickly thing: and they would gladly jump out of their skin. That is why they listen to the preachers of death, and themselves preach worlds behind.

Listen instead, my brothers, to the voice of the healthy body: it is a more honest and purer voice.

More honestly and more purely speaks the healthy body, the perfect body, built square and true: and it speaks of the meaning of the earth.

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Despisers of the Body

I want to say my word to the despisers of the body. I do not want them to learn differently and teach differently, but only to say farewell to their own bodies — and so fall silent.

“I am body and soul” — so the child speaks. And why should one not speak like the children?

But the awakened one, the one who knows, says: I am body entirely, and nothing besides; and soul is only a word for something about the body.

The body is a great reason, a multitude with one sense, a war and a peace, a herd and a shepherd.

Your little reason too, my brother, which you call “spirit,” is a tool of your body, a little tool and toy of your great reason.

“I,” you say, and you are proud of this word. But the greater thing, which you do not want to believe in, is your body and its great reason: it does not say “I,” but does “I.”

What the sense feels, what the spirit knows, never has its end in itself. But sense and spirit would like to persuade you that they are the end of all things: that is how vain they are.

Sense and spirit are tools and toys: behind them still lies the Self. The Self seeks with the eyes of the senses too; it listens with the ears of the spirit too.

The Self is always listening and seeking: it compares, compels, conquers, destroys. It rules, and is the ruler of the “I” as well.

Behind your thoughts and feelings, my brother, stands a mighty commander, an unknown sage — whose name is Self. He lives in your body; he is your body.

There is more reason in your body than in your best wisdom. And who knows, then, why your body needs precisely your best wisdom?

Your Self laughs at your “I” and its proud leaps. “What are these leaps and flights of thought to me?” it says to itself. “A detour to my purpose. I am the leading strings of the ‘I’ and the prompter of its ideas.”

The Self says to the “I”: “Feel pain here!” And then it suffers, and thinks about how it might suffer no more — and that is exactly why it is meant to think.

The Self says to the “I”: “Feel pleasure here!” Then it is glad, and thinks about how it might often be glad again — and that is exactly why it is meant to think.

I want to say a word to the despisers of the body. It is their esteeming that makes them despise. What is it that created esteeming and despising and value and will?

The creating Self created esteeming and despising for itself; it created pleasure and pain for itself. The creating body created the spirit for itself, as a hand of its will.

Even in your folly and contempt, you despisers of the body, you serve your Self. I tell you: your Self itself wants to die, and is turning away from life.

It can no longer do what it would most like to do: — to create beyond itself. That is what it would most like to do; that is its whole fervor.

But now it has grown too late for that: — so your Self wants to go under, you despisers of the body.

Your Self wants to go under, and that is why you became despisers of the body! For you can no longer create beyond yourselves.

And that is why you are now angry with life and the earth. An unconscious envy lies in the sidelong glance of your contempt.

I do not go your way, you despisers of the body! You are no bridges to the overman for me! —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On Joys and Passions

My brother, if you have a virtue, and it is your own virtue, then you have it in common with no one.

Of course, you want to call it by name and caress it; you want to tug its ear and have some fun with it.

And look! Now you have its name in common with the people, and you have become people and herd with your virtue!

You would do better to say: “Unspeakable and nameless is what makes the torment and the sweetness of my soul, and is the hunger of my very bowels as well.”

Let your virtue be too high for the familiarity of names: and if you must speak of it, do not be ashamed to stammer about it.

So speak and stammer: “This is my good, this I love, this pleases me wholly, this alone is how I want the good.

I do not want it as a god’s law; I do not want it as a human statute or a human necessity: let it be no signpost for me to over-earths and paradises.

It is an earthly virtue that I love: there is little prudence in it, and least of all the reason of everyone.

But this bird built its nest with me: that is why I love it and hold it to my heart — now let it sit with me on its golden eggs.”

That is how you should stammer and praise your virtue.

Once you had passions and called them evil. But now you have only your virtues: they grew out of your passions.

You laid your highest goal on the heart of these passions: then they became your virtues and your passions of joy.

And whether you came from the race of the hot-tempered, or of the lustful, or of the fanatics of faith, or of the vengeful:

in the end all your passions became virtues, and all your devils angels.

Once you had wild dogs in your cellar: but in the end they turned into birds and lovely singers.

Out of your poisons you brewed your balm; you milked your cow, Gloom — now you drink the sweet milk of her udder.

And from now on nothing evil grows out of you anymore, except the evil that grows out of the struggle among your virtues.

My brother, if you are lucky, you have one virtue and no more: then you go over the bridge more easily.

It is a distinction to have many virtues, but a hard lot; and many a man has gone into the desert and killed himself because he was tired of being the battle and the battlefield of virtues.

My brother, are war and battle evil? But this evil is necessary; necessary are the envy and the mistrust and the slander among your virtues.

Look how each of your virtues craves the highest: it wants your whole spirit, so that it may be its herald; it wants your whole strength in wrath, hatred, and love.

Each virtue is jealous of the others, and jealousy is a terrible thing. Even virtues can perish of jealousy.

Whoever is ringed by the flame of jealousy will at last, like the scorpion, turn the poisoned sting against himself.

Ah, my brother, have you never yet seen a virtue slander and stab itself?

Man is something that must be overcome: and that is why you should love your virtues — for you will perish of them. —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Pale Criminal

You do not want to kill, you judges and sacrificers, until the animal has nodded? Look, the pale criminal has nodded: out of his eye speaks the great contempt.

“My ‘I’ is something that shall be overcome: my ‘I’ is to me the great contempt for man”: that is what speaks out of this eye.

That he judged himself was his highest moment: do not let the exalted one sink back into his lowliness!

There is no redemption for one who suffers so from himself, unless it is a quick death.

Your killing, you judges, should be an act of pity and not of revenge. And as you kill, see to it that you yourselves justify life!

It is not enough that you be reconciled with the one you kill. Let your sadness be love for the overman: that is how you justify your still-being-alive!

“Enemy” you shall say, but not “villain”; “sick man” you shall say, but not “scoundrel”; “fool” you shall say, but not “sinner.”

And you, red judge, if you were to say out loud everything you have already done in your thoughts, then everyone would cry: “Away with this filth and this poisonous worm!”

But the thought is one thing, the deed is another, and the image of the deed another again. The wheel of cause does not roll between them.

An image made this pale man pale. He was equal to his deed when he did it: but he could not bear its image once it was done.

Now he always saw himself as the doer of one deed. Madness, I call this: the exception turned itself, for him, into the essence.

A stroke of chalk spellbinds a hen; the stroke he struck spellbound his poor reason — madness after the deed, I call this.

Listen, you judges! There is another madness besides: and it comes before the deed. Ah, you did not crawl deep enough into this soul for me!

So says the red judge: “Why did this criminal murder? He wanted to rob.” But I tell you: his soul wanted blood, not loot: he thirsted for the bliss of the knife!

But his poor reason did not understand this madness, and talked him round. “What does blood matter!” it said. “Do you not want at least to get some loot out of it? To take some revenge?”

And he listened to his poor reason: its words lay on him like lead — so he robbed when he murdered. He did not want to be ashamed of his madness.

And now the lead of his guilt lies on him again, and again his poor reason is so stiff, so paralyzed, so heavy.

If only he could shake his head, his burden would roll off: but who will shake this head?

What is this man? A heap of diseases that reach out into the world through the spirit: there they want to seize their prey.

What is this man? A tangle of wild serpents that seldom have peace with one another — so they go off on their own and seek prey in the world.

Look at this poor body! What it suffered and craved, this poor soul interpreted for itself — it interpreted it as murderous lust and greed for the bliss of the knife.

Whoever falls sick now is overcome by the evil that is evil now: he wants to cause pain with whatever causes him pain. But there were other times, and another evil and good.

Once, doubt was evil, and the will to a self. Back then the sick man became a heretic and a witch: as heretic and witch he suffered and wanted to cause suffering.

But this will not go into your ears: it harms your good people, you tell me. But what do I care about your good people!

Much about your good people disgusts me, and truly not their evil. How I wish they had a madness to perish of, like this pale criminal!

Truly, I wish their madness were called truth, or loyalty, or justice: but they have their virtue in order to live long, and in a wretched contentment.

I am a railing beside the torrent: let whoever can grasp me, grasp me! But I am not your crutch. —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On Reading and Writing

Of all that is written, I love only what someone writes with his own blood. Write with blood, and you will discover that blood is spirit.

It is not easy to understand the blood of another: I hate the idlers who read.

Whoever knows the reader does nothing more for the reader. Another century of readers — and spirit itself will stink.

That everyone may learn to read ruins, in the long run, not only writing but thinking too.

Once spirit was God, then it became man, and now it is even becoming mob.

Whoever writes in blood and in proverbs does not want to be read, but to be learned by heart.

In the mountains the shortest way is from peak to peak: but for that you must have long legs. Proverbs should be peaks: and those they are spoken to should be great and tall.

The air thin and pure, danger near, and the spirit full of a joyful malice: these things go well together.

I want goblins around me, for I am brave. Courage that scares away ghosts makes goblins for itself — courage wants to laugh.

I no longer feel what you feel: this cloud that I see beneath me, this blackness and heaviness that I laugh at — that is exactly your thundercloud.

You look upward when you long to be lifted up. And I look down, because I am lifted up.

Which of you can laugh and be lifted up at the same time?

Whoever climbs the highest mountains laughs at all tragic plays and all tragic solemnity.

Brave, carefree, mocking, violent — that is how wisdom wants us: she is a woman, and she only ever loves a warrior.

You tell me: “Life is hard to bear.” But then why do you have your pride in the morning and your resignation in the evening?

Life is hard to bear: but do not act so delicate with me! We are all of us fine, sturdy pack donkeys, he-donkeys and she-donkeys alike.

What do we have in common with the rosebud, which trembles because a drop of dew lies on its body?

It is true: we love life, not because we are used to living, but because we are used to loving.

There is always some madness in love. But there is always some reason in madness, too.

And to me as well, who am fond of life, it seems that butterflies and soap bubbles, and whatever is like them among human beings, know the most about happiness.

To see these light, foolish, dainty, nimble little souls flutter about — that moves Zarathustra to tears and to songs.

I would believe only in a god who could dance.

And when I saw my devil, I found him serious, thorough, deep, solemn: he was the spirit of gravity — through him all things fall.

One kills not with anger but with laughter. Come, let us kill the spirit of gravity!

I have learned to walk: since then I let myself run. I have learned to fly: since then I do not need to be pushed before I can move from the spot.

Now I am light, now I fly, now I see myself beneath myself, now a god dances through me.

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Tree on the Mountainside

Zarathustra’s eye had seen that a certain young man was avoiding him. And as he walked alone one evening through the mountains that surround the town called “the Motley Cow”: look, as he walked he came upon this young man, sitting leaning against a tree and gazing with tired eyes into the valley. Zarathustra took hold of the tree the young man was sitting by, and spoke like this:

If I wanted to shake this tree here with my hands, I would not be able to.

But the wind, which we do not see, torments it and bends it wherever it likes. We are bent and tormented worst of all by invisible hands.

At that the young man stood up in dismay and said: “I hear Zarathustra, and I was just now thinking of him.” Zarathustra replied:

“Why does that frighten you? — But it is with man as it is with the tree.

The more he wants to rise up into the height and the light, the more strongly his roots strive earthward, downward, into the dark, the deep — into evil.”

“Yes, into evil!” cried the young man. “How is it possible that you have discovered my soul?”

Zarathustra smiled and said: “Many a soul will never be discovered, unless it is first invented.” “Yes, into evil!” cried the young man once more.

“You spoke the truth, Zarathustra. I no longer trust myself since I have wanted to rise into the heights, and no one trusts me anymore — how does that happen?

I change too fast: my today refutes my yesterday. When I climb, I often skip the steps — and no step forgives me for that.

When I am up there, I always find myself alone. No one speaks to me; the frost of solitude makes me shiver. What do I even want in the heights?

My contempt and my longing grow together; the higher I climb, the more I despise the one who climbs. What does he even want in the heights?

How ashamed I am of my climbing and stumbling! How I mock my own violent panting! How I hate the one who flies! How tired I am in the heights!”

Here the young man fell silent. And Zarathustra looked at the tree beside which they stood, and spoke like this:

This tree stands alone here on the mountainside; it has grown high up, far above man and beast.

And if it wanted to speak, it would have no one who could understand it: so high has it grown.

Now it waits and waits — what is it waiting for? It lives too close to the seat of the clouds: perhaps it is waiting for the first lightning?

When Zarathustra had said this, the young man cried out with violent gestures: “Yes, Zarathustra, you speak the truth. It was my going under I longed for when I wanted to rise into the heights, and you are the lightning I was waiting for! Look, what am I anymore, since you appeared among us? It is envy of you that has destroyed me!” — So spoke the young man, and wept bitterly. But Zarathustra put his arm around him and led him away with him.

And when they had walked together for a while, Zarathustra began to speak like this:

It tears my heart. Better than your words can say it, your eye tells me all your danger.

You are not free yet; you are still searching for freedom. Your searching has kept you up all night and made you too wakeful.

You want to go up into the free heights; your soul thirsts for the stars. But your wicked instincts thirst for freedom too.

Your wild dogs want to be free; they bark for joy in their cellar when your spirit sets out to unlock every prison.

To me you are still a prisoner who dreams up freedom for himself: ah, the soul of such prisoners grows shrewd, but also cunning and bad.

The one who has freed his spirit still has to purify himself. Much prison and mold is still left in him: his eye still has to become pure.

Yes, I know your danger. But by my love and my hope I implore you: do not throw away your love and your hope!

You still feel noble, and the others still feel that you are noble too, the ones who resent you and send you evil looks. Know that a noble man stands in everyone’s way.

A noble man stands in the way of the good as well: and even when they call him a good man, they mean by it to push him aside.

The noble man wants to create what is new, and a new virtue. The good man wants what is old, and wants the old to be preserved.

But the danger for the noble man is not that he will become a good man, but that he will become insolent, a scoffer, a destroyer.

Ah, I have known noble men who lost their highest hope. And then they slandered all high hopes.

Then they lived insolently in brief pleasures, and hardly cast a goal beyond the day.

“Spirit is also sensual pleasure” — that is what they said. Then the wings of their spirit broke: now it crawls about and dirties whatever it gnaws.

Once they thought of becoming heroes: pleasure-seekers is what they are now. To them the hero is a grief and a horror.

But by my love and my hope I implore you: do not throw away the hero in your soul! Hold your highest hope holy! —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Preachers of Death

There are preachers of death: and the earth is full of those to whom turning away from life must be preached.

The earth is full of the superfluous; life is spoiled by the many-too-many. May they be lured out of this life with “eternal life”!

“The yellow ones”: that is what the preachers of death are called, or “the black ones.” But I want to show them to you in other colors too.

There are the terrible ones, who carry the beast of prey around inside themselves and have no choice but lusts or tearing their own flesh. And even their lusts are a tearing of their own flesh.

They have not even become human beings yet, these terrible ones: let them preach turning away from life, and depart this life themselves!

There are the consumptives of the soul: they are hardly born before they begin to die, and they long for doctrines of weariness and renunciation.

They would gladly be dead, and we ought to approve of their will! Let us beware of waking these dead and of damaging these living coffins!

They meet a sick man or an old man or a corpse; and at once they say: “Life is refuted!”

But only they are refuted, they and their eye, which sees only the one face of existence.

Wrapped in thick melancholy, and eager for the little accidents that bring death: that is how they wait, clenching their teeth.

Or else: they reach for candy and mock their own childishness as they do it: they cling to the straw of their life and mock themselves for still clinging to a straw.

Their wisdom goes: “A fool, whoever stays alive — but such fools are we! And that is exactly the most foolish thing about life!” —

“Life is only suffering” — so say others, and they do not lie: then see to it that you stop! Then see to it that the life that is only suffering stops!

And let the teaching of your virtue be this: “You shall kill yourself! You shall steal yourself away!” —

“Lust is sin” — so say some of those who preach death — “let us step aside and father no children!”

“Giving birth is hard” — say others — “why give birth anymore? One only gives birth to the unhappy!” And they too are preachers of death.

“Pity is needed” — so say the third kind. “Take what I have! Take what I am! The less life will bind me!”

If they were pitying to the core, they would spoil life for their neighbors. To be wicked — that would be their true goodness.

But they want to get free of life: what do they care that they bind others even tighter with their chains and their gifts! —

And you too, for whom life is frantic work and unrest: are you not very tired of life? Are you not very ripe for the sermon of death?

All of you who love frantic work and what is fast, new, and strange — you can hardly bear yourselves; your hard work is flight, and the will to forget yourselves.

If you believed more in life, you would throw yourselves away less on the moment. But you do not have enough in you for waiting — not even for laziness!

Everywhere the voice of those who preach death rings out: and the earth is full of those to whom death must be preached.

Or “eternal life”: it is all the same to me — as long as they depart quickly!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On War and Warriors

We do not want to be spared by our best enemies, nor by those we love to the core. So let me tell you the truth!

My brothers in war! I love you to the core; I am and have always been one of your kind. And I am also your best enemy. So let me tell you the truth!

I know about the hatred and envy in your hearts. You are not great enough not to know hatred and envy. Then be great enough not to be ashamed of them!

And if you cannot be saints of knowledge, then at least be its warriors for me. They are the companions and forerunners of such holiness.

I see many soldiers: if only I saw many warriors! “Uni-form,” they call what they wear: may what they hide beneath it not be uni-form!

You should be the kind whose eye is always searching for an enemy — for your enemy. And with some of you there is hatred at first sight.

You should seek your enemy, you should wage your war, and wage it for your thoughts! And if your thought is defeated, your honesty should still cry out in triumph over that!

You should love peace as a means to new wars. And the short peace more than the long.

I do not counsel you to work, but to fight. I do not counsel you to peace, but to victory. Let your work be a fight, let your peace be a victory!

One can be silent and sit still only when one has bow and arrow: otherwise one chatters and squabbles. Let your peace be a victory!

You say it is the good cause that hallows even war? I tell you: it is the good war that hallows every cause.

War and courage have done more great things than love of the neighbor. Not your pity but your bravery has so far saved the unfortunate.

What is good? you ask. To be brave is good. Let the little girls say: “Being good is whatever is pretty and touching at the same time.”

They call you heartless: but your heart is genuine, and I love the shyness of your warmth. You are ashamed of your flood tide, and others are ashamed of their ebb.

You are ugly? Well then, my brothers! Then wrap the sublime around you, the mantle of the ugly!

And when your soul grows great, it grows arrogant, and in your sublimity there is malice. I know you.

In malice the arrogant man meets the weakling. But they misunderstand each other. I know you.

You may have only enemies who are to be hated, not enemies to be despised. You must be proud of your enemy: then your enemy’s successes are your successes too.

Rebellion — that is the nobility of the slave. Let your nobility be obedience! Let your very commanding be an obeying!

To a good warrior “you shall” sounds more pleasant than “I will.” And everything you hold dear, you should first let yourselves be commanded to.

Let your love of life be love of your highest hope: and let your highest hope be the highest thought of life!

But your highest thought you should let me command you — and it goes: man is something that must be overcome.

So live your life of obedience and of war! What does long life matter! What warrior wants to be spared!

I do not spare you, I love you to the core, my brothers in war! —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the New Idol

Somewhere there are still peoples and herds, but not where we are, my brothers: here there are states.

State? What is that? Well then! Now open your ears to me, for now I will tell you my word about the death of peoples.

State is the name of the coldest of all cold monsters. Coldly it lies, too; and this lie crawls out of its mouth: “I, the state, am the people.”

It is a lie! It was creators who created the peoples and hung a faith and a love over them: that is how they served life.

It is destroyers who set traps for the many and call them the state: they hang a sword and a hundred cravings over them.

Where there is still a people, it does not understand the state, and hates it as the evil eye and as a sin against customs and laws.

This sign I give you: every people speaks its own tongue of good and evil, which its neighbor does not understand. It invented its own language for itself in its customs and laws.

But the state lies in every tongue of good and evil; and whatever it says, it lies — and whatever it has, it has stolen.

Everything about it is false; it bites with stolen teeth, this biter. Even its bowels are false.

A confusion of tongues of good and evil: this sign I give you as the sign of the state. Truly, this sign points to the will to death! Truly, it beckons to the preachers of death!

Far too many are born: the state was invented for the superfluous!

Just look how it lures them to itself, the many-too-many! How it gulps them down, and chews them, and chews them again like cud!

“On earth there is nothing greater than I: I am the ordering finger of God” — so roars the monster. And it is not only the long-eared and the short-sighted who sink to their knees!

Ah, even in you, you great souls, it whispers its gloomy lies! Ah, it finds out the rich hearts that like to squander themselves!

Yes, it finds you out too, you conquerors of the old God! You grew weary in the struggle, and now your weariness serves even the new idol!

Heroes and honorable men it would like to set up around itself, the new idol! It loves to bask in the sunshine of good consciences — the cold monster!

It will give you everything, if you worship it, the new idol: that is how it buys the luster of your virtue and the look in your proud eyes.

It wants to use you as bait for the many-too-many! Yes, a hellish trick has been invented here, a horse of death, jingling in the finery of divine honors!

Yes, a dying for the many has been invented here, which praises itself as life: truly, a service dear to the hearts of all preachers of death!

State I call it, where all are poison-drinkers, the good and the bad: state, where all lose themselves, the good and the bad: state, where the slow suicide of all — is called “life.”

Just look at these superfluous ones! They steal the works of the inventors and the treasures of the wise: culture is what they call their theft — and everything turns to sickness and misery for them!

Just look at these superfluous ones! They are always sick; they vomit their bile and call it a newspaper. They devour one another and cannot even digest themselves.

Just look at these superfluous ones! They acquire riches and become poorer by them. They want power, and first of all the crowbar of power, a lot of money — these men of no means!

Watch them climb, these nimble apes! They climb over one another and so drag each other down into the mud and the depths.

They all want to get to the throne: that is their madness — as if happiness sat on the throne! Often it is mud that sits on the throne — and often the throne sits on mud, too.

Madmen they all are to me, and climbing apes, and overheated. Their idol smells foul to me, the cold monster: they all smell foul to me together, these idol-worshipers.

My brothers, do you want to suffocate in the fumes of their mouths and their cravings? Better to smash the windows and leap out into the open air!

Get out of the way of the bad smell! Go away from the idol-worship of the superfluous!

Get out of the way of the bad smell! Go away from the smoke of these human sacrifices!

The earth still stands open to great souls, even now. Many places are still empty for those who live alone and those who live alone together, places where the scent of quiet seas drifts around.

A free life still stands open to great souls. Truly, whoever possesses little is all the less possessed: praised be a little poverty!

There, where the state ends, there the human being who is not superfluous first begins: there begins the song of the necessary, the one and only, irreplaceable tune.

There, where the state ends — look over there, my brothers! Do you not see it, the rainbow and the bridges of the overman? —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Flies of the Marketplace

Flee, my friend, into your solitude! I see you deafened by the noise of the great men and stung all over by the stings of the little ones.

Forest and rock know how to keep a dignified silence with you. Be like the tree again that you love, the broad-branched one: silent and listening, it hangs over the sea.

Where solitude ends, the marketplace begins; and where the marketplace begins, there begins the noise of the great actors too, and the buzzing of the poisonous flies.

In the world even the best things are still worth nothing without someone to stage them first: the people call these stagers great men.

The people understand little of what is great, that is: what creates. But they have a nose for all the stagers and actors of great things.

Around the inventors of new values the world turns: — it turns invisibly. But around the actors turn the people and fame: that is the way of the world.

The actor has spirit, but little conscience of the spirit. He always believes in whatever makes others believe most strongly — makes them believe in him!

Tomorrow he has a new faith, and the day after tomorrow a newer one. He has quick senses, like the people, and a changeable nose for the weather.

To bowl people over — that, to him, means to prove. To drive them wild — that, to him, means to convince. And blood counts with him as the best of all reasons.

A truth that slips only into fine ears he calls a lie and a nothing. Truly, he believes only in gods who make a great noise in the world!

The marketplace is full of solemn jesters — and the people boast of their great men! To them these are the masters of the hour.

But the hour presses them: so they press you. And they want a Yes or a No from you too. Woe, would you set your chair between For and Against?

Do not be jealous of these absolute and pushy ones, you lover of truth! Truth has never yet hung on the arm of an absolute man.

Because of these sudden ones, go back into your safety: only in the marketplace is one ambushed with Yes? or No?

All deep wells experience slowly: they must wait a long time before they know what has fallen into their depths.

Everything great happens far from the marketplace and from fame: the inventors of new values have always lived far from the marketplace and from fame.

Flee, my friend, into your solitude: I see you stung all over by poisonous flies. Flee to where a rough, strong wind blows!

Flee into your solitude! You have lived too close to the small and the wretched. Flee from their invisible revenge! Toward you they are nothing but revenge.

Do not raise your arm against them anymore! They are countless, and it is not your lot to be a fly swatter.

Countless are these small and wretched ones; and raindrops and weeds have already been the downfall of many a proud building.

You are not a stone, but already you have been hollowed out by many drops. Many drops will yet make you crack and burst apart.

I see you worn out by poisonous flies, I see you scratched bloody in a hundred places; and your pride will not even get angry.

They want blood from you in all innocence; their bloodless souls crave blood — and so they sting, in all innocence.

But you deep one, you suffer too deeply even from small wounds; and before you have even healed, the same poisonous worm has crawled over your hand.

You are too proud, to my mind, to kill these sweet-toothed nibblers. But take care that it does not become your doom to bear all their poisonous injustice!

They buzz around you with their praise, too: their praise is pushiness. They want to be near your skin and your blood.

They flatter you as one flatters a god or a devil; they whimper before you as before a god or a devil. What does it matter! They are flatterers and whimperers, and nothing more.

Often, too, they show themselves to you as lovable. But that has always been the shrewdness of cowards. Yes, cowards are shrewd!

They think a great deal about you with their narrow souls — you are always questionable to them! Everything that is much questioned becomes questionable.

They punish you for all your virtues. From the bottom of their hearts they forgive you only — your mistakes.

Because you are gentle and just in mind, you say: “They are not to blame for their small existence.” But their narrow souls think: “All great existence is to blame.”

Even when you are gentle with them, they still feel despised by you; and they repay your good turns with hidden ill turns.

Your wordless pride always goes against their taste; they rejoice if for once you are modest enough to be vain.

Whatever we recognize in a person, we also set alight in him. So beware of the small ones!

Before you they feel small, and their lowness smolders and glows against you in invisible revenge.

Did you not notice how often they fell silent when you came up to them, and how their strength went out of them like smoke from a dying fire?

Yes, my friend, you are the bad conscience of your neighbors: for they are unworthy of you. So they hate you and would like to suck your blood.

Your neighbors will always be poisonous flies; what is great in you — that itself must make them more poisonous and ever more fly-like.

Flee, my friend, into your solitude, and to where a rough, strong wind blows. It is not your lot to be a fly swatter. —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On Chastity

I love the forest. It is bad to live in the cities: there are too many people there who are in heat.

Is it not better to fall into the hands of a murderer than into the dreams of a woman in heat?

And just look at these men: their eye says it — they know nothing better on earth than to lie with a woman.

There is mud at the bottom of their souls; and woe, if their mud has spirit in it as well!

If only you were at least perfect as animals! But innocence belongs to the animal.

Do I advise you to kill your senses? I advise the innocence of the senses.

Do I advise chastity? With some, chastity is a virtue, but with many it is almost a vice.

These people abstain, no doubt: but the bitch Sensuality looks out with envy from everything they do.

Even up into the heights of their virtue and right into the cold spirit, this beast follows them, and its discontent with it.

And how nicely the bitch Sensuality knows how to beg for a piece of spirit when she is refused a piece of meat!

You love tragedies and everything that breaks the heart? But I am suspicious of your bitch.

Your eyes are too cruel for my liking, and you look lustfully at those who suffer. Has your lust not merely put on a disguise and called itself pity?

And I give you this parable too: not a few who wanted to drive out their devil went into the swine themselves.

Whoever finds chastity hard should be advised against it, so that it does not become the road to hell — that is, to the mud and heat of the soul.

Do I speak of dirty things? To me that is not the worst.

It is not when the truth is dirty, but when it is shallow, that the seeker of knowledge is reluctant to step into its water.

Truly, there are people who are chaste to the core: they are gentler of heart, they laugh more gladly and more richly than you.

They laugh at chastity too, and ask: “What is chastity!

Is chastity not folly? But this folly came to us, and not we to it.

We offered this guest a roof and a heart: now he lives with us — let him stay as long as he likes!”

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Friend

“There is always one too many around me” — so thinks the hermit. “Always one times one — in the long run that makes two!”

I and Me are always too deep in conversation: how could it be endured if there were no friend?

For the hermit the friend is always the third: the third is the cork that keeps the conversation of the two from sinking into the depths.
