Ah, there are too many depths for all hermits. That is why they long so for a friend and for his height.

Our faith in others betrays what we would like to have faith in, in ourselves. Our longing for a friend is our betrayer.

And often with love one only wants to leap over envy. And often one attacks and makes oneself an enemy, to hide that one can be attacked.

“At least be my enemy!” — so speaks true reverence, which does not dare to ask for friendship.

If you want to have a friend, you must also be willing to wage war for him: and to wage war, you must be able to be an enemy.

You should honor even the enemy in your friend. Can you step right up close to your friend without stepping over to his side?

In your friend you should have your best enemy. You should be closest to him with your heart when you resist him.

You want to wear no clothes in front of your friend? It is supposed to honor your friend that you give yourself to him just as you are? But he wishes you to the devil for it!

Whoever hides nothing of himself gives offense: that is how much reason you have to fear nakedness! Yes, if you were gods, then you might be ashamed of your clothes!

You cannot dress yourself up beautifully enough for your friend: for you are to be an arrow for him, and a longing for the overman.

Have you ever watched your friend asleep — to find out what he looks like? What else is the face of your friend? It is your own face, in a rough and imperfect mirror.

Have you ever watched your friend asleep? Were you not frightened that your friend looks like that? Oh, my friend, man is something that must be overcome.

In guessing and in keeping silent the friend should be a master: you must not want to see everything. Your dream should tell you what your friend does when he is awake.

Let your pity be a guessing: so that you first know whether your friend wants pity. Perhaps what he loves in you is the unbroken eye and the gaze of eternity.

Let your pity for your friend hide under a hard shell; you should break a tooth on it. That way it will have its delicacy and its sweetness.

Are you pure air and solitude and bread and medicine to your friend? Many a man cannot loosen his own chains, and yet he is his friend’s redeemer.

Are you a slave? Then you cannot be a friend. Are you a tyrant? Then you cannot have friends.

For all too long a slave and a tyrant were hidden in woman. That is why woman is not yet capable of friendship: she knows only love.

In woman’s love there is injustice and blindness toward everything she does not love. And even in woman’s knowing love there is still always ambush and lightning and night beside the light.

Woman is not yet capable of friendship: women are still cats, and birds. Or, at best, cows.

Woman is not yet capable of friendship. But tell me, you men, which of you is capable of friendship?

Oh, your poverty, you men, and the stinginess of your souls! As much as you give your friend, I will give even to my enemy, and will not have grown any poorer by it.

There is comradeship: may there be friendship!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Thousand and One Goals

Zarathustra saw many lands and many peoples: and so he discovered the good and evil of many peoples. Zarathustra found no greater power on earth than good and evil.

No people could live without first esteeming; but if it wants to preserve itself, it must not esteem as its neighbor esteems.

Much that one people called good, another called scorn and disgrace: so I found it. Much I found called evil here, and there decked out in purple honors.

Never did one neighbor understand another: his soul always marveled at his neighbor’s madness and wickedness.

A tablet of goods hangs over every people. Look, it is the tablet of its overcomings; look, it is the voice of its will to power.

Whatever it finds hard, it counts as praiseworthy; whatever is indispensable and hard is called good; and whatever frees it even from the direst need, the rare thing, the hardest of all — that it praises as holy.

Whatever makes it rule and conquer and shine, to the horror and envy of its neighbor: that it counts as the high, the first, the measure, the meaning of all things.

Truly, my brother, once you have recognized a people’s need and land and sky and neighbor, you can surely guess the law of its overcomings, and why it climbs this ladder toward its hope.

“You shall always be the first and stand out above the others: your jealous soul shall love no one, except the friend” — this made the soul of a Greek tremble: and so he walked his path of greatness.

“To speak the truth and to be good with bow and arrow” — this seemed at once dear and hard to that people from whom my name comes — the name that is at once dear and hard to me.

“To honor father and mother and to do their will down to the root of the soul”: this tablet of overcoming another people hung up over itself, and became mighty and eternal by it.

“To practice loyalty, and for loyalty’s sake to stake honor and blood even on wicked and dangerous causes”: by teaching itself this, another people mastered itself, and by mastering itself this way it became pregnant and heavy with great hopes.

Truly, human beings gave themselves all their good and evil. Truly, they did not take it, they did not find it, it did not fall to them as a voice from heaven.

It was man who first put values into things, to preserve himself — he was the first to create a meaning for things, a human meaning! That is why he calls himself “man”, which means: the esteemer.

Esteeming is creating: hear it, you creators! Esteeming itself is the treasure and the jewel of all esteemed things.

Only through esteeming is there value: and without esteeming, the nut of existence would be hollow. Hear it, you creators!

A change of values — that is a change of the creators. Whoever must be a creator always destroys.

At first the creators were peoples, and only later individuals; truly, the individual himself is still the youngest creation.

Peoples once hung a tablet of the good over themselves. Love that wants to rule and love that wants to obey created such tablets together.

The pleasure in the herd is older than the pleasure in the “I”: and as long as the good conscience is called herd, only the bad conscience says: I.

Truly, the cunning “I”, the loveless one, that wants its own advantage in the advantage of the many: that is not the origin of the herd, but its going under.

It was always lovers and creators who created good and evil. The fire of love glows in the names of all the virtues, and the fire of wrath.

Zarathustra saw many lands and many peoples: Zarathustra found no greater power on earth than the works of the lovers: “good” and “evil” is their name.

Truly, the power of this praising and blaming is a monster. Tell me, brothers, who will subdue it for me? Tell me, who will throw the fetter over the thousand necks of this beast?

There have been a thousand goals so far, for there have been a thousand peoples. Only the fetter for the thousand necks is still missing, the one goal is missing. Humanity still has no goal.

But tell me, my brothers: if humanity still lacks a goal, is it not also still lacking — humanity itself? —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On Love of the Neighbor

You crowd around your neighbor and have fine words for it. But I tell you: your love of the neighbor is your bad love of yourselves.

You flee to your neighbor to get away from yourselves, and you would like to make a virtue of it: but I see through your “selflessness”.

The “you” is older than the “I”; the “you” has been declared holy, but not yet the “I”: so man crowds toward his neighbor.

Do I advise love of the neighbor? I would sooner advise flight from the neighbor and love of the farthest!

Higher than love of the neighbor is love of the farthest and of the future; higher still than love of human beings is love of things and of ghosts.

This ghost that runs on ahead of you, my brother, is more beautiful than you; why do you not give it your flesh and your bones? But you are afraid, and you run to your neighbor.

You cannot stand yourselves, and you do not love yourselves enough: so now you want to seduce your neighbor into loving you, and gild yourselves with his mistake.

I wish you could not stand all sorts of neighbors and the people next door to them; then you would have to create your friend and his overflowing heart out of yourselves.

You invite a witness when you want to speak well of yourselves; and when you have seduced him into thinking well of you, you think well of yourselves.

It is not only the one who speaks against what he knows who lies, but even more the one who speaks against what he does not know. And that is how you speak of yourselves when you go into company, and you deceive your neighbor with yourselves.

This is what the fool says: “Mixing with people ruins the character, especially if you have none.”

The one goes to his neighbor because he is looking for himself, and the other because he would like to lose himself. Your bad love of yourselves makes your solitude a prison.

It is the more distant ones who pay for your love of the neighbor; and as soon as there are five of you together, a sixth must always die.

I do not love your festivals either: I found too many actors there, and even the spectators often behaved like actors.

I do not teach you the neighbor, but the friend. Let the friend be the festival of the earth to you, and a foretaste of the overman.

I teach you the friend and his overfull heart. But you must know how to be a sponge, if you want to be loved by overfull hearts.

I teach you the friend in whom the world stands complete, a bowl of the good — the creating friend, who always has a complete world to give away.

And as the world rolled apart for him, so it rolls together again for him in rings, as the becoming of good through evil, as the becoming of purposes out of chance.

Let the future and the farthest be the cause of your today: in your friend you shall love the overman as your cause.

My brothers, I do not advise love of the neighbor: I advise love of the farthest.

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Way of the Creator

Do you want to go into isolation, my brother? Do you want to seek the way to yourself? Wait a little longer, and listen to me.

“Whoever seeks easily gets lost himself. All isolation is guilt”: so says the herd. And you belonged to the herd for a long time.

The voice of the herd will still ring in you too. And when you say, “I no longer share one conscience with you,” it will be a lament and a pain.

Look, this pain itself was born of that one conscience: and the last glimmer of that conscience still glows on your gloom.

But you want to go the way of your gloom, which is the way to yourself? Then show me your right and your strength for it!

Are you a new strength and a new right? A first movement? A wheel rolling out of itself? Can you also compel stars to revolve around you?

Ah, there is so much lusting after the heights! There are so many convulsions of the ambitious! Show me that you are not one of the lustful and the ambitious!

Ah, there are so many great thoughts that do no more than a bellows: they puff things up and make them emptier.

You call yourself free? I want to hear your ruling thought, and not that you have escaped from a yoke.

Are you one who was allowed to escape a yoke? There is many a man who threw away his last value when he threw away his servitude.

Free from what? What does Zarathustra care about that! But your eye should tell me clearly: free for what?

Can you give yourself your own evil and your own good, and hang your will over yourself like a law? Can you be your own judge, and the avenger of your law?

It is a terrible thing to be alone with the judge and avenger of your own law. That is how a star is flung out into empty space and into the icy breath of being alone.

Today you still suffer from the many, you single one: today you still have all your courage, and your hopes.

But one day solitude will make you weary, one day your pride will bow and your courage gnash its teeth. One day you will cry out: “I am alone!”

One day you will no longer see your heights, and see your lowness all too near; your very sublimity will frighten you like a ghost. One day you will cry out: “Everything is false!”

There are feelings that want to kill the solitary one; if they do not succeed, well, then they themselves must die! But are you capable of that — of being a murderer?

Do you know yet, my brother, the word “contempt”? And the torment of your justice, to be just to those who despise you?

You force many to change their minds about you; they hold that hard against you. You came near them and yet went on past: that they will never forgive you.

You go up beyond them: but the higher you climb, the smaller you look to the eye of envy. But the one who flies is hated most of all.

“How could you be just to me!” — you must say — “I choose your injustice as the share allotted to me.”

They throw injustice and dirt at the solitary one: but, my brother, if you want to be a star, you must shine on them no less for that!

And beware of the good and the just! They like to crucify those who invent their own virtue for themselves — they hate the solitary one.

Beware of holy simplicity too! Everything that is not simple is unholy to it; it also likes to play with fire — the fire of the stake.

And beware too of the fits of your love! The solitary one is too quick to hold out his hand to whoever he meets.

There are people to whom you must not give your hand, but only your paw: and I want your paw to have claws as well.

But the worst enemy you can meet will always be yourself; you yourself lie in wait for yourself in caves and forests.

Solitary one, you are going the way to yourself! And your way leads past yourself, and past your seven devils!

You will be a heretic to yourself, and a witch and a soothsayer and a fool and a doubter and an unholy one and a villain.

You must be willing to burn yourself up in your own flame: how could you become new, if you had not first become ashes!

Solitary one, you are going the way of the creator: you want to create a god for yourself out of your seven devils!

Solitary one, you are going the way of the lover: you love yourself, and that is why you despise yourself, as only lovers despise.

The lover wants to create, because he despises! What does he know of love who did not have to despise precisely what he loved!

Go into your isolation with your love and with your creating, my brother; and only late will justice come limping after you.

Go into your isolation with my tears, my brother. I love the one who wants to create beyond himself, and so perishes. —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On Little Old and Young Women

“Why do you creep so timidly through the twilight, Zarathustra? And what are you hiding so carefully under your cloak?

Is it a treasure someone gave you? Or a child that was born to you? Or are you now walking the ways of thieves yourself, you friend of the wicked?” —

Truly, my brother! said Zarathustra, it is a treasure that was given to me: it is a little truth that I am carrying.

But it is as unruly as a young child; and if I do not hold its mouth shut, it screams far too loudly.

As I was going my way alone today, at the hour when the sun sinks, a little old woman met me and spoke like this to my soul:

“Zarathustra has said many things to us women too, but he has never spoken to us about woman.”

And I answered her: “About woman, one should speak only to men.”

“Speak to me too about woman,” she said; “I am old enough to forget it again at once.”

And I obliged the little old woman and spoke to her like this:

Everything about woman is a riddle, and everything about woman has one solution: it is called pregnancy.

For woman, man is a means: the end is always the child. But what is woman for man?

The real man wants two things: danger and play. That is why he wants woman, as the most dangerous plaything.

Man should be trained for war, and woman for the recreation of the warrior: everything else is folly.

Fruits that are all too sweet — the warrior does not like them. That is why he likes woman; even the sweetest woman is still bitter.

Woman understands children better than man does, but man is more childlike than woman.

In the real man a child is hidden: it wants to play. Come, you women, discover the child in man for me!

Let woman be a plaything, pure and fine, like a precious stone, lit by the virtues of a world that does not yet exist.

Let the ray of a star shine in your love! Let your hope be called: “May I give birth to the overman!”

Let there be courage in your love! With your love you shall go after the one who makes you afraid!

Let your honor be in your love! Otherwise woman understands little about honor. But let this be your honor: always to love more than you are loved, and never to be second.

Let man fear woman when she loves: then she makes every sacrifice, and every other thing counts as worthless to her.

Let man fear woman when she hates: for at the bottom of his soul man is only evil, but woman there is bad.

Whom does woman hate most? — Thus spoke the iron to the magnet: “I hate you most, because you attract me, but are not strong enough to pull me to you.”

Man’s happiness is called: I will. Woman’s happiness is called: he will.

“Look, just now the world has become perfect!” — so thinks every woman when she obeys out of whole love.

And woman must obey, and find a depth for her surface. Woman’s nature is surface, a restless, stormy skin over shallow water.

But man’s nature is deep; his current roars in underground caves: woman senses his power, but does not grasp it. —

Then the little old woman answered me: “Zarathustra has said many nice things, especially for those who are young enough for them.

It is strange: Zarathustra knows little about women, and yet he is right about them! Does this happen because with woman nothing is impossible?

And now take a little truth by way of thanks! After all, I am old enough for it!

Wrap it up and hold its mouth shut: otherwise it will scream far too loudly, this little truth.”

“Woman, give me your little truth!” I said. And the little old woman spoke like this:

“You are going to women? Do not forget the whip!” —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Adder’s Bite

One day Zarathustra had fallen asleep under a fig tree, because it was hot, and he had laid his arms over his face. Then an adder came and bit him in the neck, so that Zarathustra cried out in pain. When he had taken his arm from his face, he looked at the serpent: and it recognized the eyes of Zarathustra, writhed awkwardly, and tried to get away. “Not so,” said Zarathustra; “you have not yet accepted my thanks! You woke me in time; my way is still long.” “Your way is short now,” said the adder sadly; “my poison kills.” Zarathustra smiled. “When did a dragon ever die of a serpent’s poison?” he said. “But take your poison back! You are not rich enough to give it to me as a gift.” Then the adder fell on his neck once more and licked his wound.

When Zarathustra told this story to his disciples one day, they asked: “And what, oh Zarathustra, is the moral of your story?” Zarathustra answered them like this:

The good and the just call me the destroyer of morals: my story is immoral. —

But if you have an enemy, do not repay him evil with good: for that would put him to shame. Instead, prove that he has done you some good.

And rather be angry than put anyone to shame! And when you are cursed, I do not like it when you want to bless in return. Better to join in the cursing a little!

And if a great wrong has been done to you, then quickly do five little wrongs on top of it! It is a horrible sight, the one who is weighed down by the wrong alone.

Did you know this already? A wrong shared is half a right. And the one who can bear the wrong should take it on himself!

A little revenge is more human than no revenge at all. And if the punishment is not also a right and an honor for the one who transgresses, then I do not like your punishing either.

It is nobler to put yourself in the wrong than to insist on being right, especially when you are right. Only, you must be rich enough for it.

I do not like your cold justice; and out of the eye of your judges there always looks at me the executioner and his cold steel.

Tell me, where is the justice to be found that is love with seeing eyes?

Then invent for me the love that bears not only all punishment but also all guilt!

Then invent for me the justice that acquits everyone, except the one who judges!

Do you want to hear this too? In the one who wants to be just down to the ground, even the lie becomes a kindness to mankind.

But how could I want to be just down to the ground! How can I give each his own! Let this be enough for me: I give each my own.

Finally, my brothers, beware of doing wrong to any hermit! How could a hermit forget! How could he repay!

A hermit is like a deep well. It is easy to throw a stone into it; but once it has sunk to the bottom, tell me, who will bring it out again?

Beware of insulting the hermit! But if you have done it, well then, kill him too!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On Child and Marriage

I have a question for you alone, my brother: like a sounding lead I cast this question into your soul, so that I may know how deep it is.

You are young and you wish for a child and marriage. But I ask you: are you a man who is allowed to wish for a child?

Are you the victorious one, the self-conqueror, the commander of your senses, the master of your virtues? That is what I ask you.

Or is it the animal and need that speak out of your wish? Or loneliness? Or discord with yourself?

I want your victory and your freedom to long for a child. You shall build living monuments to your victory and your liberation.

You shall build beyond yourself. But first you must be built yourself, square and true in body and soul.

You shall not only plant yourself onward, but upward! May the garden of marriage help you to do it!

You shall create a higher body, a first motion, a wheel rolling out of itself — you shall create a creator.

Marriage: that is what I call the will of two to create the one that is more than those who created it. Reverence for each other, as for those who will such a will — that is what I call marriage.

Let this be the meaning and the truth of your marriage. But what the many-too-many call marriage, these superfluous ones — ah, what shall I call that?

Ah, this poverty of the soul for two! Ah, this filth of the soul for two! Ah, this wretched contentment for two!

Marriage, they call all this; and they say their marriages are made in heaven.

Well, I do not like it, this heaven of the superfluous! No, I do not like them, these animals tangled together in heaven’s net!

And let the god keep far from me, too, who comes limping up to bless what he did not join together!

Do not laugh at such marriages! What child would not have reason to weep over its parents?

This man seemed worthy to me, and ripe for the meaning of the earth: but when I saw his wife, the earth seemed to me a madhouse, a home for the senseless.

Yes, I wish the earth would shake in convulsions when a saint and a goose mate with each other.

This one set out like a hero in search of truths, and in the end he carried off as his prize a little dressed-up lie. His marriage, he calls it.

That one was standoffish with people and choosy in his choosing. But all at once he spoiled his company once and for all: his marriage, he calls it.

That one looked for a maid with the virtues of an angel. But all at once he became the maid of a woman, and now he would need to become an angel on top of it.

I have found all buyers careful, and all of them have cunning eyes. But even the most cunning of them still buys his wife in a sack, sight unseen.

Many short follies — that is what you call love. And your marriage puts an end to many short follies, as one long stupidity.

Your love for woman, and woman’s love for man: ah, if only it were pity for suffering and veiled gods! But mostly two animals sniff each other out.

But even your best love is only an ecstatic parable and a painful fire. It is a torch that should light your way to higher paths.

One day you shall love beyond yourselves! So first learn to love! And that is why you had to drink the bitter cup of your love.

There is bitterness in the cup of even the best love: that is how it stirs longing for the overman, that is how it stirs thirst in you, the creator!

Thirst in the creator, arrow and longing for the overman: tell me, my brother, is this your will to marriage?

Holy, I call such a will and such a marriage. —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On Free Death

Many die too late, and some die too early. The teaching still sounds strange: “Die at the right time!”

Die at the right time: that is what Zarathustra teaches.

Of course, if someone never lives at the right time, how could he ever die at the right time? If only he had never been born! — That is my advice to the superfluous.

But even the superfluous still make a great show of their dying, and even the hollowest nut still wants to be cracked.

Everyone takes dying seriously: but death is not yet a festival. People have not yet learned how to consecrate the most beautiful festivals.

I show you the death that fulfills, which becomes a spur and a pledge to the living.

The one who fulfills his life dies his death victorious, surrounded by people who hope and people who pledge.

That is how one should learn to die; and there should be no festival at which such a dying man does not consecrate the oaths of the living!

To die like that is best; but the second best is to die in battle and squander a great soul.

But hateful alike to the fighter and to the victor is your grinning death, which creeps up like a thief — and yet comes as master.

I praise to you my death, the free death, which comes to me because I will it.

And when will I will it? — Whoever has a goal and an heir wants death at the right time for the goal and the heir.

And out of reverence for the goal and the heir he will hang no more withered wreaths in the sanctuary of life.

Truly, I do not want to be like the rope-makers: they draw out their thread longer and longer, and all the while they themselves keep walking backward.

Many a man also grows too old for his truths and his victories; a toothless mouth no longer has the right to every truth.

And everyone who wants fame must take leave of honor in good time, and practice the difficult art of knowing, at the right time, how to — go.

You must stop letting yourself be eaten when you taste best: that is something known by those who want to be loved for a long time.

There are sour apples, of course, whose lot is to wait until the last day of autumn: and they become ripe, yellow, and wrinkled all at once.

In some the heart grows old first, and in others the spirit. And some are gray-haired in their youth: but young late stays young long.

For many a man, life turns out badly: a poison worm eats its way into his heart. Then let him see to it that his dying turns out all the better.

Many a man never grows sweet; he is already rotting in summer. It is cowardice that holds him fast to his branch.

Far too many live, and far too long they hang on their branches. If only a storm would come and shake all this rotten and worm-eaten stuff from the tree!

If only preachers of quick death would come! Those would be the right storms for me, the right shakers of the trees of life! But I hear only slow death preached, and patience with everything “earthly”.

Ah, you preach patience with the earthly? It is this earthly that has too much patience with you, you blasphemers!

Truly, that Hebrew died too early whom the preachers of slow death honor: and it has been the doom of many since then that he died too early.

He knew only tears as yet, and the melancholy of the Hebrew, together with the hatred of the good and the just — the Hebrew Jesus: and then the longing for death came over him.

If only he had stayed in the wilderness, far from the good and the just! Perhaps he would have learned to live and learned to love the earth — and to laugh as well!

Believe me, my brothers! He died too early; he himself would have taken back his teaching if he had lived to my age! He was noble enough to take it back!

But he was still unripe. Unripe is the young man’s love, and unripe, too, is his hatred of man and earth. His heart and the wings of his spirit are still tethered and heavy.

But in the grown man there is more of the child than in the youth, and less melancholy: he understands death and life better.

Free for death and free in death, a holy No-sayer when it is no longer time for Yes: that is how he understands death and life.

That your dying may be no blasphemy against man and earth, my friends: that is what I ask of the honey of your soul.

In your dying your spirit and your virtue should still glow, like a sunset glow around the earth: or else your dying has turned out badly.

That is how I want to die myself, so that you, my friends, will love the earth more for my sake; and I want to become earth again, so that I may have rest in her who bore me.

Truly, Zarathustra had a goal, he threw his ball: now you, my friends, are the heirs of my goal, to you I throw the golden ball.

More than anything I love to see you, my friends, throwing the golden ball! And so I linger a little while longer on the earth: forgive me the lingering!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Gift-Giving Virtue

I

When Zarathustra had taken leave of the town his heart had grown fond of, whose name is “the Motley Cow” — many who called themselves his disciples followed him and kept him company on his way. So they came to a crossroads: there Zarathustra told them that from now on he wanted to go alone; for he was a friend of going alone. But at parting his disciples handed him a staff, on whose golden handle a serpent coiled around the sun. Zarathustra was glad of the staff and leaned on it; then he spoke to his disciples like this:

Tell me, then: how did gold come to have the highest value? Because it is uncommon and useless and gleaming and gentle in its luster; it always gives itself away.

Only as an image of the highest virtue did gold come to have the highest value. The glance of the giver gleams like gold. The gleam of gold makes peace between moon and sun.

The highest virtue is uncommon and useless, it is gleaming and gentle in its luster: a gift-giving virtue is the highest virtue.

Truly, I can guess what you are, my disciples: like me, you strive for the gift-giving virtue. What would you have in common with cats and wolves?

This is your thirst, to become sacrifices and gifts yourselves: and that is why you have the thirst to heap up all riches in your soul.

Your soul strives insatiably for treasures and jewels, because your virtue is insatiable in its wanting to give away.

You force all things to come to you and into you, so that they may flow back out of your well as the gifts of your love.

Truly, such gift-giving love must become a robber of all values; but whole and holy, I call this selfishness.

There is another selfishness, an all-too-poor one, a hungry one, which always wants to steal: that selfishness of the sick, the sick selfishness.

With the eye of a thief it looks at everything that gleams; with the greed of hunger it sizes up the one who has plenty to eat; and it is always creeping around the table of the givers.

Sickness speaks out of such craving, and an invisible degeneration; the thieving greed of this selfishness speaks of an ailing body.

Tell me, my brothers: what do we count as bad, and the worst of all? Is it not degeneration? — And we always suspect degeneration where the gift-giving soul is missing.

Our way goes upward, from our kind over to an overkind. But a horror to us is the degenerate mind that says: “Everything for me.”

Our mind flies upward: so it is a parable of our body, a parable of an elevation. The names of the virtues are parables of such elevations.

So the body goes through history, a becoming and a fighting thing. And the spirit — what is it to the body? The herald of its fights and victories, their companion and their echo.

All the names of good and evil are parables: they do not speak out, they only hint. He is a fool who wants knowledge from them!

Pay attention, my brothers, to every hour when your spirit wants to speak in parables: that is where your virtue has its origin.

Then your body is exalted and risen; with its delight it enraptures the spirit, so that the spirit becomes a creator and a valuer and a lover and a benefactor of all things.

When your heart surges broad and full like the river, a blessing and a danger to those who live along its banks: that is where your virtue has its origin.

When you are lifted above praise and blame, and your will wants to command all things, as the will of one who loves: that is where your virtue has its origin.

When you despise what is comfortable and the soft bed, and cannot make your bed far enough away from the soft: that is where your virtue has its origin.

When you are the willers of a single will, and this turning point of all need is what you call your necessity: that is where your virtue has its origin.

Truly, it is a new good and evil! Truly, a new deep rushing, and the voice of a new spring!

It is power, this new virtue; it is a ruling thought, and around it a shrewd soul: a golden sun, and around it the serpent of knowledge.

II

Here Zarathustra fell silent for a while and looked lovingly at his disciples. Then he went on speaking like this: — and his voice had changed.

Remain faithful to the earth, my brothers, with the power of your virtue! Let your gift-giving love and your knowledge serve the meaning of the earth! So I beg and implore you.

Do not let it fly away from the earthly and beat its wings against eternal walls! Ah, there has always been so much virtue that flew away!

Lead the virtue that flew away back to the earth, as I do — yes, back to body and life: so that it may give the earth its meaning, a human meaning!

A hundred times so far both spirit and virtue have flown astray and grasped wrong. Ah, all this delusion and blundering still lives in our body now: there it has become body and will.

A hundred times so far both spirit and virtue have tried and strayed. Yes, man was an experiment. Ah, much ignorance and error has become body in us!

Not only the reason of millennia — their madness, too, breaks out in us. It is dangerous to be an heir.

We are still fighting step by step with the giant Chance, and over all of humanity until now nonsense has ruled — the meaningless.

Let your spirit and your virtue serve the meaning of the earth, my brothers: and let the value of all things be set anew by you! That is why you shall be fighters! That is why you shall be creators!

Through knowing, the body purifies itself; experimenting with knowledge, it elevates itself; to the seeker of knowledge all drives become holy; to the one who is elevated the soul becomes joyful.

Physician, heal yourself: in that way you will heal your patient too. Let this be his best help, that he sees with his own eyes the one who makes himself whole.

There are a thousand paths that have never yet been walked; a thousand healths and hidden islands of life. Man and man’s earth are still unexhausted and undiscovered.

Keep watch and listen, you solitary ones! From the future come winds with secret beating of wings; and good news goes out to fine ears.

You solitary ones of today, you who withdraw, you shall one day be a people: from you who have chosen yourselves a chosen people shall grow: — and from it the overman.

Truly, the earth shall yet become a place of healing! And already a new scent lies around it, a scent that brings salvation — and a new hope!

III

When Zarathustra had said these words, he fell silent, like one who has not said his last word; for a long time he weighed the staff doubtfully in his hand. At last he spoke like this: — and his voice had changed.

I go alone now, my disciples! You too go away now, and alone! This is how I want it.

Truly, I advise you: go away from me and guard yourselves against Zarathustra! And better still: be ashamed of him! Perhaps he deceived you.

The man of knowledge must be able not only to love his enemies but also to hate his friends.

One repays a teacher badly if one always remains only a pupil. And why will you not pluck at my wreath?

You revere me; but what if one day your reverence topples over? Take care that a statue does not strike you dead!

You say you believe in Zarathustra? But what does Zarathustra matter! You are my believers: but what do any believers matter!

You had not yet sought yourselves: then you found me. That is what all believers do; that is why all faith amounts to so little.

Now I bid you lose me and find yourselves; and only when you have all denied me will I return to you.

Truly, with other eyes, my brothers, I will then seek my lost ones; with another love I will then love you.

And one day you shall have become my friends, and children of one hope: then I will be with you for the third time, to celebrate the great noon with you.

And this is the great noon: when man stands at the middle of his course between beast and overman, and celebrates his way to the evening as his highest hope: for it is the way to a new morning.

Then the one who goes under will bless himself for being one who goes over; and the sun of his knowledge will stand at noon for him.

“Dead are all gods: now we want the overman to live.” — let this one day, at the great noon, be our last will! —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

Part Two

Epigraph to Part Two

“— and only when you have all denied me will I return to you. Truly, with other eyes, my brothers, I will then seek my lost ones; with another love I will then love you.”

Zarathustra, “On the Gift-Giving Virtue”

The Child with the Mirror

After this Zarathustra went back into the mountains and into the solitude of his cave, and withdrew from people, waiting like a sower who has scattered his seed. But his soul grew full of impatience and of longing for those he loved: for he still had much to give them. For this is the hardest thing of all: to close the open hand out of love, and to keep a sense of shame as a giver.

So months and years passed for the solitary one; but his wisdom grew, and it caused him pain with its fullness.

But one morning he woke even before the dawn, lay thinking for a long time on his bed, and at last spoke to his heart:

Why was I so frightened in my dream that I woke up? Did not a child come to me, carrying a mirror?

“Oh Zarathustra,” the child said to me, “look at yourself in the mirror!”

But when I looked into the mirror, I cried out, and my heart was shaken: for it was not myself I saw there, but a devil’s grimace and sneering laughter.

Truly, I understand the dream’s sign and warning all too well: my teaching is in danger; weeds want to be called wheat!

My enemies have grown powerful and have distorted the image of my teaching, so that those dearest to me must be ashamed of the gifts I gave them.

I have lost my friends; the hour has come for me to seek my lost ones! —

With these words Zarathustra sprang up, but not like a frightened man gasping for air — rather like a seer and a singer whom the spirit seizes. His eagle and his serpent looked at him in wonder: for a coming happiness lay on his face like the dawn.

What has happened to me, my animals? — said Zarathustra. Am I not transformed? Did bliss not come to me like a whirlwind?

My happiness is foolish, and it will say foolish things: it is still too young — so be patient with it!

I am wounded by my happiness: let all who suffer be my physicians!

I may go down to my friends again, and to my enemies too! Zarathustra may speak and give again, and do the dearest thing for those he loves!

My impatient love overflows in torrents, downward, toward the sunrise and the sunset. Out of silent mountains and thunderstorms of pain my soul rushes roaring into the valleys.

Too long I longed and gazed into the distance. Too long I belonged to solitude: so I have forgotten how to be silent.

I have become mouth through and through, and the roar of a brook from high cliffs: I want to hurl my speech down into the valleys.

And let my torrent of love plunge where there is no path! How could a torrent not find its way to the sea at last!

There is a lake in me, it is true, a hermit lake, sufficient to itself; but my torrent of love sweeps it along with it, down — to the sea!

I walk new ways, a new speech comes to me; like all creators, I have grown tired of the old tongues. My spirit no longer wants to walk on worn-out soles.

All speaking runs too slowly for me: — into your chariot I leap, storm! And even you I will whip with my malice!

Like a cry and a shout of joy I will sail over wide seas, until I find the Blessed Isles where my friends dwell: —

And my enemies among them! How I love everyone now to whom I may simply speak! My enemies too belong to my bliss.

And when I want to mount my wildest horse, it is always my spear that helps me up best: it is my foot’s ever-ready servant: —

The spear that I hurl at my enemies! How grateful I am to my enemies that at last I may hurl it!

The tension in my cloud was too great: between the laughter of the lightning I will throw showers of hail into the depths.

Mightily my chest will heave then, mightily it will blow its storm over the mountains: so it will find relief.

Truly, my happiness and my freedom come like a storm! But my enemies shall believe that the evil one is raging over their heads.

Yes, you too, my friends, will be frightened by my wild wisdom; and perhaps you will run away from it, along with my enemies.

Ah, if only I knew how to lure you back with shepherds’ flutes! Ah, if only my lioness Wisdom would learn to roar tenderly! And we have already learned much together!

My wild wisdom grew pregnant on lonely mountains; on rough stones she gave birth to her young, her youngest.

Now she runs foolishly through the hard desert, and seeks and seeks for soft grass — my old wild wisdom!

On the soft grass of your hearts, my friends! — on your love she would like to bed her dearest one!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Blessed Isles

The figs are falling from the trees, they are good and sweet; and as they fall, their red skins split. I am a north wind to ripe figs.

So, like figs, these teachings fall to you, my friends: now drink their juice and their sweet flesh! It is autumn all around, and clear sky, and afternoon.

Look, what fullness there is around us! And out of this overflow it is beautiful to gaze out at distant seas.

Once people said God when they gazed out at distant seas; but now I have taught you to say: overman.

God is a conjecture; but I want your conjecturing to reach no further than your creating will.

Could you create a god? — Then do not speak to me of any gods! But you could well create the overman.

Not you yourselves, perhaps, my brothers! But you could re-create yourselves into fathers and forefathers of the overman: and let this be your best creating! —

God is a conjecture: but I want your conjecturing to be bounded by what can be thought.

Could you think a god? — But let this be what the will to truth means to you: that everything be transformed into what is humanly thinkable, humanly visible, humanly feelable! You shall think your own senses through to the end!

And what you have called the world, that must first be created by you: your reason, your image, your will, your love shall itself become it! And truly, for your bliss, you seekers of knowledge!

And how would you bear life without this hope, you seekers of knowledge? You could not have been born into the incomprehensible, nor into the irrational.

But to open my heart to you completely, my friends: if there were gods, how could I bear not to be a god! Therefore there are no gods.

I drew the conclusion, it is true; but now it draws me. —

God is a conjecture: but who could drink all the torment of this conjecture without dying? Shall the creator be robbed of his faith, and the eagle of his soaring in eagle-distances?

God is a thought that makes everything straight crooked, and everything that stands spin. What? Time would be gone, and everything that passes away only a lie?

To think this is a whirling and a dizziness to human bones, and a vomiting to the stomach besides: truly, I call it the spinning sickness, to conjecture such a thing.

I call it evil and hostile to man: all this teaching of the One and the Full and the Unmoved and the Sated and the Imperishable!

Everything imperishable — that is only a parable! And the poets lie too much. —

But the best parables should speak of time and becoming: they should be a praise and a justification of everything that passes away!

Creating — that is the great redemption from suffering, and what makes life light. But for the creator to come to be, suffering itself is needed, and much transformation.

Yes, there must be much bitter dying in your life, you creators! So you are advocates and justifiers of everything that passes away.

For the creator to be himself the child that is newly born, he must also want to be the one who gives birth, and the pain of the one who gives birth.

Truly, I have gone my way through a hundred souls, and through a hundred cradles and birth pangs. I have taken many a farewell already; I know the heartbreaking last hours.

But so my creating will wills it, my fate. Or, to tell you more honestly: it is just such a fate that — my will wills.

Everything in me that feels suffers and is in prisons: but my willing always comes to me as my liberator and bringer of joy.

Willing liberates: that is the true teaching about will and freedom — so Zarathustra teaches it to you.

No-longer-willing and no-longer-esteeming and no-longer-creating! ah, may this great weariness always stay far from me!

In knowing, too, I feel only my will’s joy in begetting and becoming; and if there is innocence in my knowledge, this happens because the will to beget is in it.

This will lured me away from God and gods; what would there be to create, after all, if there were — gods!

But to man it drives me again and again, my ardent will to create; so it drives the hammer to the stone.

Ah, you human beings, an image sleeps for me in the stone, the image of my images! Ah, that it has to sleep in the hardest, ugliest stone!

Now my hammer rages cruelly against its prison. Pieces fly from the stone like dust: what do I care?

I want to finish it: for a shadow came to me — the stillest and lightest of all things once came to me!

The beauty of the overman came to me as a shadow. Ah, my brothers! What do they still matter to me — the gods! —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Pitying

My friends, a mocking word has reached your friend: “Just look at Zarathustra! Does he not walk among us as if among animals?”

But it is better said like this: “The seeker of knowledge walks among human beings as among animals.”

To the seeker of knowledge, however, man himself is called: the animal with red cheeks.

How did that happen to him? Is it not because he has had to be ashamed too often?

Oh my friends! So speaks the seeker of knowledge: shame, shame, shame — that is the history of man!

And that is why the noble man commands himself not to put others to shame: he commands himself to feel shame before everyone who suffers.

Truly, I do not like them, the merciful, who are blessed in their pity: they are too lacking in shame.

If I must be pitying, I still do not want to be called so; and if I am, then preferably from a distance.

I also like to cover my head and run away before I am recognized: and I bid you do the same, my friends!

May my fate always lead those who do not suffer across my path, like you, and those with whom I may share hope and meal and honey!

Truly, I have done this and that for those who suffer: but I always seemed to myself to be doing something better when I learned to enjoy myself better.

For as long as there have been human beings, man has enjoyed himself too little: that alone, my brothers, is our original sin!

And if we learn to enjoy ourselves better, that is the best way to unlearn hurting others and thinking up hurts.

That is why I wash the hand that helped the sufferer; that is why I wipe off my soul as well.

For I was ashamed to have seen the sufferer suffering, ashamed for the sake of his shame; and when I helped him, I trespassed harshly on his pride.

Great obligations do not make people grateful, but vengeful; and if the small kindness is not forgotten, it turns into a gnawing worm.

“Be reluctant to accept! Honor someone by accepting!” — that is my advice to those who have nothing to give away.

But I am a giver: I give gladly, as a friend to friends. But strangers and the poor may pick the fruit from my tree themselves: that puts them less to shame.

But beggars ought to be done away with altogether! Truly, it is annoying to give to them, and annoying not to give to them.

And the same goes for sinners and bad consciences! Believe me, my friends: the bites of conscience teach people to bite.

But the worst thing of all is small thoughts. Truly, better to have done evil than to have thought small!

Of course you say: “Our pleasure in small acts of malice spares us many a great evil deed.” But here one should not want to be sparing.

The evil deed is like a boil: it itches and scratches and breaks out — it speaks honestly.

“Look, I am sickness” — so speaks the evil deed; that is its honesty.

But the small thought is like a fungus: it creeps and crouches and wants to be nowhere — until the whole body is rotten and withered with little fungi.

But to the one who is possessed by a devil I whisper this word into his ear: “Better still, raise your devil up big! For you too there is still a way to greatness!” —

Ah, my brothers! We know a little too much about everyone! And many a person becomes transparent to us, but that is still far from meaning we can pass through him.

It is hard to live with human beings, because keeping silent is so hard.

And it is not to the one who repels us that we are most unjust, but to the one who does not concern us at all.

But if you have a friend who suffers, be a resting place for his suffering, but a hard bed, as it were, a camp bed: that is how you will serve him best.

And if a friend does you wrong, say: “I forgive you what you did to me; but that you did it to yourself — how could I forgive that!”

So speaks all great love: it overcomes even forgiveness and pity.

One should keep a firm hold on one’s heart; for if one lets it go, how soon the head goes running off too!

Ah, where in the world have greater follies happened than among the pitying? And what in the world has caused more suffering than the follies of the pitying?

Woe to all lovers who do not yet have a height that is above their pity!

Thus spoke the devil to me once: “Even God has his hell: it is his love of man.”

And recently I heard him say this word: “God is dead; God died of his pity for man.” —

So be warned against pity: from there a heavy cloud is still coming to mankind! Truly, I know how to read the signs of the weather!

But mark this word too: all great love is above all its pity: for it wants — to create what it loves!

“I offer myself to my love, and my neighbor as myself” — that is how all creators speak.

But all creators are hard. —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On Priests

And once Zarathustra gave his disciples a sign and spoke these words to them:

“Here are priests: and even though they are my enemies, pass by them quietly, with your swords asleep!

Among them too there are heroes; many of them have suffered too much —: so they want to make others suffer.

They are bad enemies: nothing is more vengeful than their humility. And whoever attacks them easily soils himself.

But my blood is related to theirs; and I want my blood honored even in theirs.” —

And when they had passed by, pain seized Zarathustra; and he had not wrestled long with his pain when he began to speak like this:

These priests grieve me. They also go against my taste; but that matters least to me, since I have been among human beings.

But I suffer and have suffered with them: to me they are prisoners and marked men. The one they call Redeemer has put them in chains: —

In chains of false values and delusion-words! Ah, if only someone would redeem them from their Redeemer!

They once thought they had landed on an island when the sea was tossing them about; but look, it was a sleeping monster!

False values and delusion-words: these are the worst monsters for mortals — long does doom sleep and wait in them.

But at last it comes and wakes and eats and swallows whatever built itself huts on it.

Oh, just look at these huts that these priests have built themselves! Churches, they call their sweet-smelling caves.

Oh, this falsified light, this swampy air! Here, where the soul, up toward its height — may not fly!

Instead, their faith commands: “Up the stairs on your knees, you sinners!”

Truly, I would rather see even the shameless than the twisted eyes of their shame and devotion!

Who created such caves and stairs of penance for themselves? Was it not those who wanted to hide and were ashamed before the clear sky?

And only when the clear sky looks in again through broken roofs, and down on grass and red poppies by broken walls — will I turn my heart again to the places of this god.

They called God whatever opposed them and hurt them: and truly, there was much of the hero in their worship!

And they knew no other way to love their god than by nailing man to the cross!

They meant to live as corpses, they dressed their corpses in black; even from their speeches I still smell the foul spice of burial vaults.

And whoever lives near them lives near black ponds, out of which the toad sings its song with sweet profundity.

They would have to sing me better songs for me to learn to believe in their Redeemer: his disciples would have to look more redeemed to me!

I would like to see them naked: for beauty alone should preach penance. But whom will this muffled-up gloom ever persuade!

Truly, their redeemers themselves did not come from freedom and freedom’s seventh heaven! Truly, they themselves never walked on the carpets of knowledge!

The spirit of these redeemers was made of gaps; but into every gap they had put their delusion, their gap-filler, which they called God.

Their spirit had drowned in their pity, and when they swelled and overswelled with pity, a great folly always floated to the top.

Eagerly and with shouting they drove their herd over their footbridge: as if there were only one footbridge to the future! Truly, these shepherds too still belonged among the sheep!

These shepherds had small spirits and roomy souls: but, my brothers, what small countries even the roomiest souls have been until now!

They wrote signs of blood on the way they walked, and their folly taught that truth is proved with blood.

But blood is the worst witness to truth; blood poisons even the purest teaching into delusion and hatred of the heart.

And if someone walks through fire for his teaching — what does that prove! It is more, truly, when one’s own teaching comes out of one’s own burning!

A sultry heart and a cold head: where these meet, the roaring wind arises, the “Redeemer.”

Truly, there have been greater ones and higher-born ones than those the people call redeemers, these sweeping, roaring winds!

And you must be redeemed, my brothers, by still greater ones than all the redeemers were, if you want to find the way to freedom!

There has never yet been an overman. I have seen both of them naked, the greatest man and the smallest man: —

They are still all too similar to each other. Truly, even the greatest I found — all too human!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Virtuous

One must speak with thunder and heavenly fireworks to slack and sleeping senses.

But the voice of beauty speaks softly: it steals only into the most awakened souls.

Softly my shield trembled and laughed for me today; that is the holy laughing and trembling of beauty.

It was at you, you virtuous ones, that my beauty laughed today. And its voice came to me like this: “They still want — to be paid!”

You still want to be paid, you virtuous ones! You want reward for virtue, and heaven for earth, and the eternal for your today?

And now you are angry with me because I teach that there is no rewarder and no paymaster? And truly, I do not even teach that virtue is its own reward.

Ah, this is my sorrow: reward and punishment have been lied into the ground of things — and now into the ground of your souls as well, you virtuous ones!

But like the snout of the boar my word shall tear open the ground of your souls; I want to be a plowshare to you.

All the secrets of your ground shall come to light; and when you lie rooted up and broken in the sun, your lie too will have been sorted out from your truth.

For this is your truth: you are too clean for the filth of the words: revenge, punishment, reward, retribution.

You love your virtue as a mother loves her child; but when did anyone hear of a mother who wanted to be paid for her love?

Your virtue is your dearest Self. The ring’s thirst is in you: every ring struggles and turns to reach itself again.

And every work of your virtue is like a star that dies out: its light is always still on its way and wandering — and when will it no longer be on its way?

So the light of your virtue is still on its way, even when the work is done. Let the work be forgotten and dead: its ray of light still lives and wanders.

That your virtue should be your Self and not something foreign, a skin, a cloak: that is the truth from the ground of your soul, you virtuous ones! —

But there are, to be sure, those for whom virtue means the cramp under a whip: and you have listened too much to their crying!

And there are others who call it virtue when their vices grow lazy; and when their hatred and their jealousy stretch their limbs for once, their “justice” wakes up and rubs its sleepy eyes.

And there are others who are dragged downward: their devils drag them. But the deeper they sink, the more fiercely their eye glows, and their craving for their god.

Ah, their crying too has reached your ears, you virtuous ones: “What I am not, that, that is God and virtue to me!”

And there are others who come along heavy and creaking, like wagons carrying stones downhill: they talk a great deal of dignity and virtue — they call their brake virtue!

And there are others who are like everyday clocks that have been wound up; they go tick-tock and want tick-tock to be called — virtue.

Truly, these give me my delight: wherever I find such clocks, I will wind them up with my mockery; and they shall purr for me while I do it!

And others are proud of their handful of justice, and for its sake commit outrages against all things: so that the world is drowned in their injustice.

Ah, how badly the word “virtue” dribbles out of their mouths! And when they say, “I am just,” it always sounds like, “I am just getting even!”

With their virtue they want to scratch out their enemies’ eyes; and they lift themselves up only to bring others down.

And then again there are those who sit in their swamp and speak out of the reeds like this: “Virtue — that means sitting still in the swamp.

We bite no one, and we get out of the way of anyone who wants to bite; and in all things we hold the opinion that is given to us.”

And then again there are those who love gestures and think: virtue is a kind of gesture.

Their knees are always worshiping, and their hands are hymns of praise to virtue, but their heart knows nothing of it.

And then again there are those who think it is virtue to say: “Virtue is necessary”; but at bottom they believe only that the police are necessary.

And many a man who cannot see what is high in people calls it virtue that he sees what is low in them all too closely: so he calls his evil eye virtue.

And some want to be edified and set upright, and call it virtue; and others want to be knocked over — and call that virtue too.

And in this way almost everyone believes he has a share in virtue; and at the very least, each one wants to be an expert on “good” and “evil.”

But Zarathustra did not come to say to all these liars and fools: “What do you know of virtue! What could you know of virtue!” —

But rather so that you, my friends, might grow weary of the old words you have learned from the fools and liars:

Grow weary of the words “reward,” “retribution,” “punishment,” “revenge in justice” —

Grow weary of saying: “What makes an action good is that it is selfless.”

Ah, my friends! That your Self be in the action as the mother is in the child: let that be your word for virtue!

Truly, I have taken from you a hundred words or so, and your virtue’s dearest toys; and now you are angry with me, the way children are angry.

They were playing by the sea — then the wave came and swept their toys away into the deep: now they cry.

But the same wave shall bring them new toys and pour out new colorful shells before them!

So they will be comforted; and like them, you too, my friends, shall have your comforts — and new colorful shells! —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Rabble

Life is a fountain of joy; but where the rabble drinks too, all wells are poisoned.

I am fond of everything clean; but I do not like to see the grinning mouths and the thirst of the unclean.

They cast their eye down into the well: now their repulsive smile gleams up at me out of the well.

They have poisoned the holy water with their lechery; and when they called their dirty dreams joy, they poisoned even the words.

The flame turns sullen when they lay their damp hearts on the fire; the spirit itself bubbles and smokes wherever the rabble steps up to the fire.

In their hands the fruit turns sickly sweet and overripe: their gaze makes the fruit tree windfallen and withered at the crown.

And many a man who turned away from life turned away only from the rabble: he did not want to share well and flame and fruit with the rabble.

And many a man who went into the desert and suffered thirst among beasts of prey only did not want to sit around the cistern with filthy camel drivers.

And many a man who came along like a destroyer, and like a hailstorm to every field of grain, only wanted to set his foot in the rabble’s jaws and so stop up its throat.

And the mouthful that choked me most was not knowing that life itself needs enmity and dying and crosses of torture: —

But rather, I once asked, and almost suffocated on my question: what? Does life need the rabble too?

Are poisoned wells needed, and stinking fires, and soiled dreams, and maggots in the bread of life?

Not my hatred but my disgust gnawed hungrily at my life! Ah, I often grew weary of the spirit when I found even the rabble rich in spirit!

And I turned my back on the rulers when I saw what they now call ruling: haggling and bargaining for power — with the rabble!

I lived among peoples with a foreign tongue, with my ears shut: so that the tongue of their haggling and their bargaining for power would stay foreign to me.

And holding my nose, I walked in ill humor through all of yesterday and today: truly, all of yesterday and today stinks of the scribbling rabble!

Like a cripple who has gone deaf and blind and dumb: that is how I lived for a long time, so as not to live with the power-rabble and the scribble-rabble and the pleasure-rabble.

My spirit climbed stairs with effort, and with caution; alms of joy were its refreshment; life crept along on a stick for the blind man.

What was it, then, that happened to me? How did I redeem myself from disgust? Who made my eye young again? How did I fly up to the height where no rabble sits at the well anymore?

Did my disgust itself create wings for me, and powers that can divine where springs are hidden? Truly, I had to fly to the very highest to find the fountain of joy again!

Oh, I have found it, my brothers! Here in the highest the fountain of joy wells up for me! And there is a life the rabble does not drink from too!

You flow almost too violently for me, spring of joy! And often you empty the cup again by wanting to fill it!

And I must still learn to approach you more modestly: my heart still flows toward you all too violently: —

My heart, on which my summer burns, my short, hot, melancholy, overblissful summer: how my summer heart longs for your coolness!

Gone, the lingering gloom of my spring! Over, the malice of my snowflakes in June! I have become summer entirely, and summer noon!

A summer in the highest, with cold springs and blissful stillness: oh come, my friends, so that the stillness may become more blissful still! For this is our height and our home: we live here too high and too steep for all the unclean and their thirst. Just cast your pure eyes into the fountain of my joy, you friends! How could that make it cloudy? It shall laugh back at you with its purity.

On the tree called Future we build our nest; eagles shall bring food in their beaks to us solitary ones!

Truly, no food that the unclean would be allowed to share! They would think they were eating fire, and burn their mouths!

Truly, we keep no homes ready here for the unclean! Our happiness would be an ice cave to their bodies and to their spirits!

And like strong winds we want to live above them, neighbors to the eagles, neighbors to the snow, neighbors to the sun: that is how strong winds live.

And like a wind I will one day blow among them, and with my spirit take the breath from their spirit: that is what my future wills.

Truly, Zarathustra is a strong wind to all lowlands; and this advice he advises to his enemies and to everything that spits and spews: beware of spitting against the wind!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Tarantulas

Look, this is the tarantula’s den! Do you want to see her yourself? Here hangs her web: touch it, so that it trembles.

Here she comes, willingly: welcome, tarantula! Black on your back sits your triangle, your emblem; and I know too what sits in your soul.

Revenge sits in your soul: wherever you bite, black scabs grow; with revenge your poison makes the soul whirl!

So I speak to you in a parable, you who make souls whirl, you preachers of equality! To me you are tarantulas, secretly vengeful ones!

But I will drag your hiding places into the light soon enough: that is why I laugh my laughter of the heights in your faces.

That is why I tear at your web, so that your rage may lure you out of your den of lies, and your revenge may leap out from behind your word “justice.”

For that man may be redeemed from revenge: that is for me the bridge to the highest hope, and a rainbow after long storms.

But the tarantulas, of course, want it otherwise. “What we call justice is precisely this: that the world be filled with the storms of our revenge” — so they talk with one another.

“We will take revenge on all who are not equal to us, and heap abuse on them” — so the tarantula hearts vow to one another.

“And ‘will to equality’ — that itself shall from now on be the name for virtue; and against everything that has power we will raise our outcry!”

You preachers of equality, it is the tyrant-madness of impotence that cries out of you like this for “equality”: your most secret tyrant-lusts disguise themselves like this in virtue-words!

Embittered conceit, pent-up envy, perhaps your fathers’ conceit and envy: it bursts out of you as a flame and a madness of revenge.

What the father kept silent about comes out speaking in the son; and I have often found the son to be the father’s secret laid bare.

They look like the inspired: but it is not the heart that inspires them — it is revenge. And when they grow refined and cold, it is not the spirit but envy that makes them refined and cold.

Their jealousy leads them onto the paths of thinkers too; and this is the mark of their jealousy — they always go too far: so that in the end their weariness has to lie down and sleep on the snow.

Revenge rings out of every one of their complaints, there is a wish to hurt in every one of their praises; and being judges seems like bliss to them.

But this is my advice to you, my friends: mistrust all in whom the urge to punish is powerful!

They are people of a bad kind and a bad descent; the hangman and the bloodhound look out of their faces.

Mistrust all those who talk a great deal about their justice! Truly, it is not only honey that their souls lack.

And when they call themselves “the good and the just,” do not forget that nothing stands between them and the Pharisee except — power!

My friends, I do not want to be mixed up with others or mistaken for them.

There are those who preach my teaching about life: and at the same time they are preachers of equality, and tarantulas.

That they speak up for life, even though they sit in their den, these poison spiders, turned away from life: the reason is that they want to hurt with it.

They want to hurt those who have power now: for among them the preaching of death is still most at home.

If it were otherwise, the tarantulas would teach otherwise: and it was precisely they who were once the best slanderers of the world and burners of heretics.

I do not want to be mixed up with these preachers of equality or mistaken for them. For this is how justice speaks to me: “People are not equal.”

Nor shall they become so! What would my love of the overman be, if I spoke otherwise?

On a thousand bridges and footpaths they shall throng toward the future, and ever more war and inequality shall be set between them: that is how my great love makes me speak!

They shall become inventors of images and ghosts in their hostilities, and with their images and ghosts they shall yet fight the highest fight against one another!

Good and evil, and rich and poor, and high and low, and all the names of values: they shall be weapons, and clanging marks of this, that life must overcome itself again and again!

Life itself wants to build itself up into the heights with pillars and steps: it wants to gaze into far distances and out toward blissful beauties — that is why it needs height!

And because it needs height, it needs steps, and the contradiction between the steps and those who climb them! Life wants to climb, and in climbing to overcome itself.

And just look at this, my friends! Here, where the tarantula’s den is, the ruins of an old temple rise up — just look at them with enlightened eyes!

Truly, whoever once piled his thoughts up toward the sky in stone here knew the secret of all life as well as the wisest!

That there is struggle and inequality even in beauty, and war for power and more-than-power: he teaches us that here in the plainest parable.

How divinely vault and arch break against each other here, in a wrestling match: how they strive against each other with light and shadow, these divine strivers —

Let us be enemies like this too, my friends, sure and beautiful! Let us strive divinely against one another! —

Woe! Now the tarantula has bitten me myself, my old enemy! Divinely sure and beautiful, she bit me on the finger!

“There must be punishment and justice” — so she thinks: “he shall not sing songs here in honor of enmity for nothing!”

Yes, she has taken her revenge! And woe! now with revenge she will make my soul whirl too!

But so that I do not whirl, my friends, tie me fast here to this pillar! I would rather be a pillar saint than a whirlwind of vengefulness!

Truly, Zarathustra is no twister and no whirlwind; and if he is a dancer, he is never a tarantula dancer! —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Famous Wise Men

You have served the people and the people’s superstition, all you famous wise men! — and not the truth! And that is exactly why they paid you reverence.

And that is also why they put up with your unbelief: because it was a joke and a detour on the way to the people. That is how the master lets his slaves have their way, and even enjoys their high spirits.

But the one the people hate as the dogs hate a wolf: that is the free spirit, the enemy of fetters, the non-worshiper, the one who makes his home in the forests.

To hunt him out of his hiding place — the people have always called that a “sense of what is right”: against him they still set their sharpest-toothed dogs.

“For the truth is there: after all, the people are there! Woe, woe to those who seek!” — so it has rung out from the beginning.

You wanted to justify your people in their reverence: that you called the “will to truth,” you famous wise men!

And your heart always said to itself: “I came from the people: from there the voice of God came to me as well.”

Stiff-necked and shrewd, like the donkey, you have always been the people’s advocates.

And many a powerful man who wanted to fare well with the people harnessed in front of his horses — a little donkey, a famous wise man.

And now, you famous wise men, I wish you would at last throw off the lion’s skin entirely!

The skin of the beast of prey, the spotted, motley one, and the shaggy mane of the researcher, the seeker, the conqueror!

Ah, for me to learn to believe in your “truthfulness,” you would first have to break your reverent will.

Truthful — that is what I call the one who goes into godless deserts and has broken his reverent heart.

In the yellow sand, burned by the sun, he may well squint thirstily over at the islands rich in springs, where living things rest under dark trees.

But his thirst does not persuade him to become like these comfortable ones: for where there are oases, there are idols too.

Hungry, violent, solitary, godless: that is how the lion-will wants itself to be.

Free from the happiness of serfs, redeemed from gods and worship, fearless and fearsome, great and solitary: that is the will of the truthful.

The truthful, the free spirits, have always lived in the desert, as the desert’s masters; but in the cities live the well-fed, famous wise men — the draft animals.

For they always pull, as donkeys pull — the people’s cart!

Not that I am angry with them for that: but to me they remain servants and creatures in harness, even when their harness gleams with gold.

And often they were good servants, worthy of praise. For this is how virtue speaks: if you must be a servant, look for the one whom your service profits most!

“Your master’s spirit and virtue shall grow because you are his servant: so you yourself will grow along with his spirit and his virtue!”

And truly, you famous wise men, you servants of the people! You yourselves have grown along with the people’s spirit and virtue — and the people through you! I say this to your credit!

But to me you remain the people even in your virtues, the people with dull eyes — the people who do not know what spirit is!

Spirit is the life that itself cuts into life: by its own torment it increases its own knowledge — did you know that already?

And the spirit’s happiness is this: to be anointed and consecrated with tears as the sacrificial animal — did you know that already?

And the blindness of the blind man, and his seeking and groping, shall yet bear witness to the power of the sun he looked into — did you know that already?

And the seeker of knowledge shall learn to build with mountains! It is a small thing for the spirit to move mountains — did you know that already?

You know only the sparks of the spirit: but you do not see the anvil that it is, nor the cruelty of its hammer!

Truly, you do not know the spirit’s pride! But still less could you bear the spirit’s modesty, if it ever wanted to speak!

And never yet have you been allowed to throw your spirit into a pit of snow: you are not hot enough for that! And so you do not know the raptures of its coldness either.

But in everything you are too familiar with the spirit; and you have often turned wisdom into a poorhouse and a hospital for bad poets.

You are no eagles: so you have never experienced the happiness in the terror of the spirit either. And whoever is not a bird should not make his camp above abysses.

To me you are lukewarm: but all deep knowledge flows cold. Ice-cold are the innermost wells of the spirit: a refreshment to hot hands and to those who take things in hand.

There you stand, respectable and stiff and with straight backs, you famous wise men! — no strong wind and no strong will drives you.

Have you never seen a sail go over the sea, rounded and swelling and trembling before the fury of the wind?

Like the sail, trembling before the fury of the spirit, my wisdom goes over the sea — my wild wisdom!

But you servants of the people, you famous wise men — how could you go with me! —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

The Night Song

It is night: now all the leaping fountains speak more loudly. And my soul too is a leaping fountain.

It is night: only now do all the songs of lovers awake. And my soul too is the song of a lover.

Something unstilled, unstillable is in me; it wants to be heard. A craving for love is in me, and it speaks the very language of love.

I am light: ah, that I were night! But this is my solitude, that I am girded round with light.

Ah, that I were dark and of the night! How I would suck at the breasts of light!

And I would bless even you, you little twinkling stars and glowworms up there! — and be blessed in your gifts of light.

But I live in my own light; I drink back into myself the flames that break out of me.

I do not know the happiness of those who take; and I have often dreamed that stealing must be more blessed still than taking.

This is my poverty, that my hand never rests from giving; this is my envy, that I see waiting eyes and the lit-up nights of longing.

Oh, the misery of all who give! Oh, the eclipse of my sun! Oh, the craving to crave! Oh, the ravenous hunger in fullness!

They take from me: but do I still touch their soul? There is a gulf between giving and taking; and the smallest gulf is the last to be bridged.

A hunger grows out of my beauty: I would like to hurt those I shine for, I would like to rob those I have given gifts to: — that is how I hunger for malice.

Pulling back my hand when another hand already reaches out to it; hesitating like the waterfall that hesitates even as it plunges: — that is how I hunger for malice.

Such revenge my fullness devises; such spite wells up out of my solitude.

My happiness in giving died in giving; my virtue grew weary of itself in its overflow!

Whoever always gives is in danger of losing his shame; whoever always hands out has calluses on hand and heart from sheer handing out.

My eye no longer overflows at the shame of those who beg; my hand has grown too hard to feel the trembling of hands that have been filled.

Where have the tears of my eye gone, and the down of my heart? Oh, the solitude of all who give! Oh, the silence of all who shine!

Many suns circle in empty space: to everything that is dark they speak with their light — to me they are silent.

Oh, this is the hostility of light toward what shines: without mercy it travels its paths.

Unjust in its deepest heart toward what shines, cold toward suns — so travels every sun.
