Like a storm the suns fly along their paths: that is their traveling. They follow their own relentless will: that is their coldness.

Oh, it is only you, you dark ones, you creatures of the night, who make warmth out of what shines! Oh, only you drink milk and comfort from the udders of light!

Ah, there is ice around me, my hand is burned by what is icy! Ah, there is thirst in me that pines for your thirst!

It is night: ah, that I must be light! And thirst for what belongs to the night! And solitude!

It is night: now my longing breaks out of me like a spring — I long to speak.

It is night: now all the leaping fountains speak more loudly. And my soul too is a leaping fountain.

It is night: only now do all the songs of lovers awake. And my soul too is the song of a lover. —

Thus sang Zarathustra.

The Dance Song

One evening Zarathustra was walking through the forest with his disciples; and as he was looking for a well, look, he came upon a green meadow quietly ringed by trees and bushes, and on it girls were dancing together. As soon as the girls recognized Zarathustra, they stopped dancing; but Zarathustra stepped up to them with a friendly gesture and spoke these words:

“Do not stop dancing, you lovely girls! No spoilsport has come to you with an evil eye, no enemy of girls.

I am God’s advocate before the devil: and the devil is the spirit of gravity. How could I, you light ones, be an enemy of divine dances? Or of girls’ feet with pretty ankles?

It is true that I am a forest and a night of dark trees: but whoever is not afraid of my darkness will also find slopes of roses under my cypresses.

And he will probably find the little god too, the one girls love best: he lies beside the well, quiet, with his eyes closed.

Truly, he fell asleep on me in broad daylight, that waster of days! Did he chase too many butterflies, perhaps?

Do not be angry with me, you beautiful dancers, if I punish the little god a little! He will probably cry and weep — but he is laughable even when he weeps!

And with tears in his eyes he shall ask you for a dance; and I myself will sing a song to his dance:

A dancing song and a mocking song on the spirit of gravity, my supreme, almighty devil, of whom they say that he is ‘the lord of the world’.” —

And this is the song that Zarathustra sang while Cupid and the girls danced together.

Into your eye I looked not long ago, oh life! And I seemed to myself to be sinking into the unfathomable.

But you pulled me out with a golden fishing rod; you laughed mockingly when I called you unfathomable.

“That is how all fish talk,” you said; “what they cannot fathom is unfathomable.

But I am only changeable and wild, and in everything a woman, and not a virtuous one:

Even if to you men I am called ‘the deep one’, or ‘the faithful one’, ‘the eternal one’, ‘the mysterious one’. —

But you men always present us with your own virtues — ah, you virtuous ones!”

Thus she laughed, the unbelievable one; but I never believe her or her laughter when she speaks evil of herself.

And when I talked alone with my wild wisdom, she said to me angrily: “You will, you crave, you love: that is the only reason you praise life!”

I almost gave her an evil answer then and told the angry one the truth; and there is no more evil answer than to “tell the truth” to one’s wisdom.

For this is how things stand among the three of us. From the bottom of my heart I love only life — and truly, most of all when I hate her!

But that I am fond of wisdom, and often too fond: that is because she reminds me so very much of life!

She has life’s eye, life’s laugh, and even life’s little golden fishing rod: how can I help it that the two look so alike?

And once, when life asked me: Who is she, then, this wisdom? — I said eagerly: “Ah, yes! Wisdom!

One thirsts for her and is never satisfied, one looks through veils, one snatches through nets.

Is she beautiful? What do I know! But even the oldest carp are still caught with her as bait.

She is changeable and stubborn; I have often seen her bite her lip and comb her hair against the grain.

Perhaps she is evil and false, and in everything a female; but just when she speaks badly of herself, that is when she seduces most.”

When I said this to life, she laughed maliciously and closed her eyes. “Whom are you talking about?” she said. “About me, perhaps?

And even if you were right — is that something to say to my face like this! But now go on and speak of your wisdom too!”

Ah, and now you opened your eye again, oh beloved life! And again I seemed to myself to be sinking into the unfathomable. —

Thus sang Zarathustra. But when the dance was over and the girls had gone away, he grew sad.

“The sun has long since gone down,” he said at last; “the meadow is damp, a coolness is coming from the forests.

Something unknown is around me and looks on thoughtfully. What! You are still alive, Zarathustra?

Why? What for? By what? Where to? Where? How? Is it not folly to go on living? —

Ah, my friends, it is the evening that asks such things from within me. Forgive me my sadness!

Evening has come: forgive me that evening has come!”

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

The Grave Song

“There is the island of graves, the silent one; there too are the graves of my youth. There I will carry an evergreen wreath of life.”

Resolving this in my heart, I sailed over the sea. —

Oh you visions and apparitions of my youth! Oh, all you glances of love, you divine twinklings of an eye! How quickly you died on me! I remember you today as I remember my dead.

From you, my dearest dead, a sweet scent comes to me, a scent that loosens the heart and the tears. Truly, it shakes and loosens the heart of the one who sails alone.

I am still the richest and the most to be envied — I, the most solitary! For I had you after all, and you have me still: tell me, to whom did such rosy apples fall from the tree as fell to me?

I am still the heir of your love and the earth it was left to, blooming in your memory with motley, wild-growing virtues, oh you most beloved!

Ah, we were made to stay close to each other, you sweet strange wonders; and you did not come to me and my desire like timid birds — no, but as the trusting to the one who trusts!

Yes, made for faithfulness, like me, and for tender eternities: must I now name you after your faithlessness, you divine glances and twinklings: I have not yet learned any other name.

Truly, you died on me too quickly, you fugitives. Yet you did not flee from me, nor did I flee from you: we are innocent toward each other in our faithlessness.

To kill me, they strangled you, you songbirds of my hopes! Yes, at you, my dearest ones, malice always shot its arrows — to hit my heart!

And it hit! For you were always what was dearest to my heart, my possession and what possessed me: that is why you had to die young, and far too early!

At the most vulnerable thing I had they shot the arrow: that was you, whose skin is like down, and still more like the smile that dies at a glance!

But this word I will speak to my enemies: what is all the murder of men compared with what you did to me!

You did me a greater evil than any murder of men; you took from me what can never be brought back: — this is how I speak to you, my enemies!

For you murdered the visions of my youth and my dearest wonders! You took my playmates from me, the blessed spirits! To their memory I lay down this wreath and this curse.

This curse on you, my enemies! For you made what was eternal in me short, as a tone breaks off in a cold night! It came to me only as a gleam of divine eyes — as a twinkling of an eye!

Thus spoke my purity once, in a good hour: “All beings shall be divine to me.”

Then you fell upon me with filthy ghosts; ah, where has that good hour fled now!

“All days shall be holy to me” — so the wisdom of my youth once spoke: truly, the words of a joyful wisdom!

But then you enemies stole my nights from me and sold them to sleepless torment: ah, where has that joyful wisdom fled now?

Once I longed for lucky omens from the birds: then you led an owl-monster across my path, a repulsive one. Ah, where did my tender longing flee then?

Once I vowed to renounce all disgust: then you turned my near ones and my neighbors into boils. Ah, where did my noblest vow flee then?

Once I walked blessed paths like a blind man: then you threw filth on the blind man’s path: and now the old footpath of the blind disgusts him.

And when I did my hardest thing and celebrated the victory of my overcomings: then you made those who loved me cry out that I was hurting them most of all.

Truly, that was always your doing: you made my best honey bitter for me, and the labor of my best bees.

To my charity you always sent the most insolent beggars; around my pity you always crowded the incurably shameless. In this way you wounded my virtue in its faith.

And if I laid down my holiest thing as an offering, at once your “piety” set its fatter gifts beside it: so that my holiest thing was smothered in the smoke of your fat.

And once I wanted to dance as I had never danced before: I wanted to dance away over all the heavens. Then you talked my dearest singer over to your side.

And now he struck up a dreadful, muffled tune; ah, he droned it into my ears like a gloomy horn!

Murderous singer, tool of malice, most innocent one! I was already standing ready for the best dance: then with your tones you murdered my rapture!

Only in the dance do I know how to speak the parable of the highest things: — and now my highest parable remained unspoken in my limbs!

Unspoken and unredeemed my highest hope remained! And all the visions and consolations of my youth died on me!

How did I ever bear it? How did I get over and overcome such wounds? How did my soul rise again from these graves?

Yes, there is something in me that cannot be wounded, cannot be buried, something that bursts rocks apart: it is called my will. It strides silent and unchanged through the years.

It wants to walk its own way on my feet, my old will; hard-hearted is its temper, and it cannot be wounded.

I cannot be wounded except in my heel. You live there still and are the same as ever, most patient one! Still you broke out through every grave!

In you lives still what was unredeemed in my youth; and as life and youth you sit here, hoping, on yellow ruins of graves.

Yes, you are still the shatterer of all graves for me: Hail to you, my will! And only where there are graves are there resurrections. —

Thus sang Zarathustra. —

On Self-Overcoming

“Will to truth” — is that what you call it, you wisest ones, the thing that drives you and sets you burning?

A will to the thinkability of all that is: that is what I call your will!

You want first to make all that is thinkable: for you doubt, with a healthy mistrust, whether it is thinkable already.

But it shall bend and yield to you! So your will wants it. It shall become smooth and subject to the spirit, as the spirit’s mirror and reflection.

That is your whole will, you wisest ones, a will to power; and that holds even when you talk of good and evil and of valuations. You want to create the world before which you can kneel: that is your last hope and your intoxication.

The unwise, of course, the people — they are like the river on which a boat floats along: and in the boat sit the valuations, solemn and cloaked.

You set your will and your values on the river of becoming; what the people believe to be good and evil betrays to me an old will to power.

It was you, you wisest ones, who put such guests in this boat and gave them pomp and proud names — you and your ruling will!

Now the river carries your boat along: it must carry it. It matters little if the broken wave foams and angrily contradicts the keel!

It is not the river that is your danger and the end of your good and evil, you wisest ones: it is that will itself, the will to power — the unexhausted, begetting will of life.

But so that you may understand my word about good and evil, I will also tell you my word about life, and about the nature of all that lives.

I followed what lives; I walked the greatest and the smallest paths, so as to know its nature.

With a hundredfold mirror I caught its glance even when its mouth was shut, so that its eye might speak to me. And its eye spoke to me.

But wherever I found something living, there I also heard the talk of obedience. Everything living is something that obeys.

And this is the second thing: whoever cannot obey himself is commanded. That is the nature of what lives.

But this is the third thing I heard: that commanding is harder than obeying. And not only because the one who commands bears the burden of all who obey, and because this burden easily crushes him: —

An experiment and a risk appeared to me in all commanding; and whenever it commands, the living thing risks itself in doing so.

Yes, even when it commands itself: even then it must pay for its commanding. It must become judge and avenger and victim of its own law.

How does this happen! I asked myself. What persuades the living thing to obey and to command, and in commanding still to practice obedience?

Hear my word now, you wisest ones! Test it in earnest, whether I have crept into the very heart of life, and down to the roots of its heart!

Wherever I found something living, I found will to power; and even in the will of the one who serves I found the will to be master.

That the weaker should serve the stronger: to this its will persuades it, the will that wants to be master over what is weaker still. This one pleasure it will not do without.

And as the smaller gives itself up to the greater, so that it may have pleasure and power over the smallest: so even the greatest gives itself up, and for the sake of power stakes — its life.

That is the self-surrender of the greatest: that it is risk and danger, and a game of dice with death.

And where there are sacrifice and services and loving glances: there too is the will to be master. By secret paths the weaker steals into the castle, and right into the heart of the mightier — and there steals power.

And life itself told me this secret. Look, it said, I am that which must always overcome itself.

“Of course, you call it a will to procreation, or a drive toward a goal, toward the higher, the farther, the more manifold: but all of this is one thing and one secret.

I would rather go under than renounce this one thing; and truly, wherever there is going under and the falling of leaves, look, there life sacrifices itself — for power!

That I must be struggle and becoming and goal and the contradiction of goals: ah, whoever guesses my will surely guesses too on what crooked paths it must walk!

Whatever I create and however much I love it — soon I must be its opponent, and the opponent of my love: so my will wants it.

And you too, seeker of knowledge, are only a path and a footprint of my will: truly, my will to power walks even on the feet of your will to truth!

He certainly did not hit the truth who shot at it with the phrase ‘will to existence’: that will — does not exist!

For what does not exist cannot will; but what already exists, how could it still want to exist!

Only where there is life is there also will: not will to life, but — so I teach you — will to power!

The living esteem many things more highly than life itself; but out of the esteeming itself speaks — the will to power!” —

Thus life once taught me: and with this, you wisest ones, I will yet solve the riddle of your hearts.

Truly I tell you: good and evil that would last forever — there is no such thing! Out of itself it must overcome itself again and again.

With your values and your words of good and evil you exercise force, you esteemers of value: and this is your hidden love and the shining, trembling and overflowing of your soul.

But a stronger force grows out of your values, and a new overcoming: on it egg and eggshell break.

And whoever must be a creator in good and evil: truly, he must first be a destroyer and break values.

So the highest evil belongs to the highest goodness: but that is the creative goodness. —

Let us speak of it, you wisest ones, even if it is bad. To be silent is worse; all truths kept silent become poisonous.

And let everything that can break on our truths — break! There are many houses still to build!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Sublime Ones

Still is the floor of my sea: who would guess that it hides playful monsters!

Unshakable is my depth: but it glitters with swimming riddles and laughter.

I saw a sublime one today, a solemn one, a penitent of the spirit: oh, how my soul laughed at his ugliness!

His chest lifted up, and like those who are drawing in their breath: that is how he stood there, the sublime one, and silent:

Hung all over with ugly truths, the spoils of his hunt, and rich in torn clothes; many thorns hung on him too — but I saw no rose yet.

He had not yet learned laughter and beauty. Gloomy, this hunter came back from the forest of knowledge.

He came home from the fight with wild beasts: but out of his seriousness a wild beast still looks too — one not yet overcome!

He still stands there like a tiger about to spring; but I do not like these tensed souls, and my taste is unkind to all these withdrawn ones.

And you tell me, friends, that there is no arguing about taste and tasting? But all of life is an argument about taste and tasting!

Taste: that is the weight and the scales and the weigher, all at once; and woe to every living thing that wanted to live without arguing over weight and scales and weigher!

If he grew tired of his sublimity, this sublime one: only then would his beauty begin — and only then do I want to taste him and find him to my taste.

And only when he turns away from himself will he leap over his own shadow — and, truly! into his sun.

Far too long he sat in the shade, and the cheeks of the penitent of the spirit grew pale; he nearly starved on his expectations.

There is contempt in his eye still, and disgust hides around his mouth. He is resting now, it is true, but his rest has not yet lain down in the sun.

He should do as the bull does; and his happiness should smell of the earth, and not of contempt for the earth.

I would like to see him as a white bull, snorting and bellowing as he goes ahead of the plowshare: and his bellowing should still praise everything earthly!

His face is still dark; the shadow of the hand plays over it. The sense of his eye is still in shadow.

His deed itself is still the shadow over him: the hand darkens the one whose hand it is. He has not yet overcome his deed.

I do love the bull’s neck on him: but now I want to see the eye of the angel as well.

He must still unlearn his hero’s will, too: I want him to be one who is lifted, and not only one who is sublime: — the ether itself should lift him, the will-less one!

He conquered monsters, he solved riddles: but he should also absolve his monsters and riddles; he should still transform them into heavenly children.

His knowledge has not yet learned to smile and to be without jealousy; his flooding passion has not yet grown still in beauty.

Truly, his longing should fall silent and sink down not in satiety, but in beauty! Grace belongs to the generosity of the great-hearted.

His arm laid over his head: that is how the hero should rest, and that is how he should overcome even his resting.

But to the hero above all, the beautiful is the hardest thing of all. Beauty cannot be won by any violent will.

A little more, a little less: just this counts for much here, it counts for the most.

To stand with muscles slack and with the will unharnessed: that is the hardest thing for all of you, you sublime ones!

When power becomes gracious and comes down into the visible: beauty is what I call such a coming down.

And from no one do I want beauty as much as from you, you mighty one: let your goodness be your last overpowering of yourself.

I believe you capable of every evil: that is why I want the good from you.

Truly, I have often laughed at the weaklings who think themselves good because their paws are lame!

You shall strive after the virtue of the pillar: it grows ever more beautiful and more delicate, but inwardly harder and more load-bearing, the higher it rises.

Yes, you sublime one, one day you shall be beautiful too, and hold up the mirror to your own beauty.

Then your soul will shudder with divine desires; and there will be worship even in your vanity!

For this is the secret of the soul: only when the hero has abandoned it does there approach it, in a dream — the over-hero.

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Land of Culture

I flew too far into the future: a horror came over me.

And when I looked around me, look! time was my only contemporary.

Then I fled backward, homeward — and faster and faster: and so I came to you, you people of the present, and into the land of culture.

For the first time I brought an eye for you along, and good desire: truly, I came with longing in my heart.

But what happened to me? Frightened as I was — I had to laugh! Never had my eye seen anything so speckled with color!

I laughed and laughed, while my foot was still trembling, and my heart too: “Why, this is the home of all the paint pots!” — I said.

Painted with fifty splotches on your faces and limbs: that is how you sat there, to my amazement, you people of the present!

And with fifty mirrors around you, flattering your play of colors and repeating it after you!

Truly, you could wear no better mask, you people of the present, than your own face! Who could ever — recognize you!

Written all over with the signs of the past, and those signs painted over with new signs: that is how well you have hidden yourselves from all interpreters of signs!

And even if one is a searcher of the innermost parts: who still believes that you have any innermost parts! You seem to be baked out of paint and scraps of glued paper.

All ages and peoples look out motley from your veils; all customs and faiths speak motley from your gestures.

Whoever took away your veils and wraps and colors and gestures would have just enough left over to scare the birds.

Truly, I myself am the scared bird that once saw you naked and without color; and I flew away when the skeleton beckoned to me with love.

I would rather be a day laborer in the underworld, among the shades of what once was! — why, even the dwellers of the underworld are plumper and fuller than you!

This, yes, this is bitterness to my bowels: that I can stand you neither naked nor clothed, you people of the present!

Everything unhomely in the future, and whatever ever made strayed birds shudder, is truly homelier and cozier than your “reality.”

For this is how you talk: “We are wholly real, and without faith or superstition”: that is how you puff out your chests — ah, and without even having chests!

Yes, how could you be able to believe, you speckled ones! — you who are paintings of everything that was ever believed!

You are walking refutations of faith itself, and the breaking of every thought’s limbs. Unbelievable: that is what I call you, you real ones!

All the ages chatter against one another in your spirits; and the dreams and chatter of all the ages were more real still than your waking is!

You are barren: that is why you lack faith. But whoever had to create always had his truth-dreams and star-signs too — and believed in believing! —

You are half-open gates at which gravediggers wait. And this is your reality: “Everything deserves to perish.”

Ah, how you stand there before me, you barren ones, how lean in the ribs! And many of you have surely noticed it yourselves.

And he said: “Did some god perhaps steal something from me secretly while I slept? Truly, enough to form himself a little woman from it!

Amazing is the poverty of my ribs!” So many a person of the present has already spoken.

Yes, you make me laugh, you people of the present! And especially when you marvel at yourselves!

And woe to me if I could not laugh at your marveling, and had to drink down everything repulsive in your bowls!

But as it is, I will take you more lightly, since I have something heavy to carry; and what does it matter to me if beetles and winged worms settle on my bundle as well!

Truly, it shall not grow any heavier for me on that account! And it is not from you, you people of the present, that my great weariness shall come. — Ah, where shall I climb now with my longing! From every mountain I look out for fatherlands and motherlands.

But I have found a home nowhere: I am unsettled in every settlement and a departure at every gate.

Strangers to me, and a mockery, are the people of the present, to whom my heart drove me not long ago; and I am driven out of fatherlands and motherlands.

So now I love only my children’s land, the undiscovered one, in the farthest sea: I bid my sails search and search for it.

To my children I will make up for being the child of my fathers: and to all the future — for this present!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On Immaculate Perception

When the moon rose yesterday, I imagined it wanted to give birth to a sun: so broad and pregnant did it lie on the horizon.

But it lied to me with its pregnancy; and I would sooner believe in the man in the moon than in the woman.

Of course, there is not much of a man in him either, this shy night-prowler. Truly, he wanders over the rooftops with a bad conscience.

For he is lustful and jealous, the monk in the moon, lustful for the earth and for all the joys of lovers.

No, I do not like him, this tomcat on the rooftops! Repulsive to me are all who creep around half-closed windows!

Pious and silent he wanders along on carpets of stars: — but I do not like any soft-treading feet of men on which not even a spur jingles.

Every honest man’s step speaks up; but the cat steals away over the floor. Look, catlike and dishonest the moon comes along. —

This parable I give to you, you sentimental hypocrites, to you, the “pure perceivers”! I call you — the lustful!

You too love the earth and what is earthly: I have guessed you well! — but there is shame in your love, and a bad conscience — you are like the moon!

Your spirit has been talked into despising what is earthly, but your bowels have not: and they are the strongest thing in you!

And now your spirit is ashamed of doing what your bowels want, and to escape its own shame it goes by sneaking paths and lying paths.

“That would be the highest thing for me” — so your lying spirit talks to itself — “to look at life without desire, and not like a dog with its tongue hanging out:

To be happy in looking, with a will that has died, without the grip and greed of selfishness — cold and ash-gray all over the body, but with drunken moon-eyes!”

“That would be the dearest thing to me” — so the seduced one seduces himself — “to love the earth the way the moon loves it, and to touch its beauty with the eye alone.

And this I would call the immaculate perception of all things: that I want nothing from things, except to be allowed to lie before them like a mirror with a hundred eyes.” —

Oh, you sentimental hypocrites, you lustful ones! You lack innocence in your desire: and so now you slander desiring!

Truly, you do not love the earth as creators, as begetters, as those who delight in becoming!

Where is innocence? Where there is the will to beget. And whoever wants to create beyond himself has, for me, the purest will.

Where is beauty? Where I must will with all my will; where I want to love and go under, so that an image does not remain only an image.

Loving and going under: these have rhymed for eternities. The will to love: that means being willing to die as well. That is how I speak to you cowards!

But now your unmanned leering wants to be called “contemplation”! And whatever lets itself be fingered by cowardly eyes is to be christened “beautiful”! Oh, you contaminators of noble names!

But this shall be your curse, you immaculate ones, you pure perceivers, that you will never give birth: even though you lie broad and pregnant on the horizon!

Truly, you fill your mouths with noble words: and we are supposed to believe that your hearts are overflowing, you liars?

But my words are humble, despised, crooked words: I gladly pick up what falls under the table at your meals.

With them I can still — tell hypocrites the truth! Yes, my fish bones, shells, and prickly leaves shall — tickle the noses of hypocrites!

There is always bad air around you and your meals: for your lustful thoughts, your lies and secrets, are in the air!

Just dare first to believe yourselves — yourselves and your bowels! Whoever does not believe himself always lies.

You have hung a god’s mask in front of yourselves, you “pure ones”: into a god’s mask your loathsome ringed worm has crawled.

Truly, you deceive, you “contemplative ones”! Zarathustra too was once the fool of your godlike skins; he did not guess the serpent coils they were stuffed with.

Once I imagined I saw a god’s soul playing in your games, you pure perceivers! Once I imagined there was no better art than your arts!

The distance hid from me the serpent filth and the foul smell: and that a lizard’s cunning was creeping around here, lustfully.

But I came near you: then the day came to me — and now it is coming to you — the moon’s love affair has come to an end!

Just look! There he stands, caught out and pale — before the dawn!

For already she is coming, the glowing one — her love for the earth is coming! Innocence and the creator’s desire are all the love of the sun!

Just look how impatiently she comes over the sea! Do you not feel the thirst and the hot breath of her love?

She wants to suck at the sea and drink its depth up to her height: and the desire of the sea rises with a thousand breasts.

It wants to be kissed and sucked by the thirst of the sun; it wants to become air and height and a footpath of light, and light itself!

Truly, like the sun I love life and all deep seas.

And this is what knowledge means to me: everything deep shall rise — up to my height!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On Scholars

As I lay asleep, a sheep ate at the ivy wreath on my head — ate, and said as it ate: “Zarathustra is no longer a scholar.”

It said this and went off, stiff-legged and proud. A child told me about it.

I like to lie here, where the children play, by the broken wall, among thistles and red poppies.

To the children I am still a scholar, and to the thistles and red poppies too. They are innocent, even in their mischief.

But to the sheep I am one no longer: so my lot wills it — blessed be it!

For this is the truth: I have moved out of the house of the scholars, and slammed the door behind me as well.

Too long my soul sat hungry at their table; I am not trained, as they are, to knowing as if to nutcracking.

I love freedom and the air over fresh earth; I would rather sleep on ox hides than on their honors and respectabilities.

I am too hot and scorched by my own thoughts: often they nearly take my breath away. Then I have to go out into the open, away from all dusty rooms.

But they sit cool in the cool shade: in everything they want only to be spectators, and they take care not to sit where the sun burns on the steps.

Like those who stand in the street and gape at the people going by: so they too wait, and gape at thoughts that others have thought.

If you take hold of them with your hands, they send up dust all around like flour sacks, and without meaning to; but who would guess that their dust comes from grain, and from the yellow delight of the summer fields?

When they act wise, their little sayings and truths make me shiver: there is often a smell about their wisdom, as if it came from the swamp: and truly, I have even heard the frog croaking out of it!

They are skillful, they have clever fingers: what is my simplicity next to their multiplicity! Their fingers understand all threading and knotting and weaving: so they knit the stockings of the spirit!

They are good clockworks: only take care to wind them up correctly! Then they tell the hour without fail and make a modest noise about it.

They work like mills and stamping machines: just throw them your seed grain! — they know well how to grind grain small and make white dust out of it.

They keep a close eye on one another’s fingers and do not trust each other the best. Inventive in little cunnings, they wait for those whose knowledge walks on lame feet — like spiders they wait.

I always saw them prepare poison with care; and every time they put glass gloves on their fingers to do it.

They know how to play with loaded dice too; and I found them playing so eagerly that they sweated over it.

We are strangers to one another, and their virtues go against my taste even more than their falsehoods and their loaded dice.

And when I lived among them, I lived above them. They held that against me.

They want to hear nothing of anyone walking over their heads; and so they put wood and earth and filth between me and their heads.

So they muffled the sound of my steps: and until now I have been heard worst of all by the most learned.

All of mankind’s faults and weakness they laid between themselves and me: — a “false floor” is what they call that in their houses.

But in spite of it I walk with my thoughts over their heads; and even if I wanted to walk on my own faults, I would still be above them and their heads.

For men are not equal: so speaks justice. And what I will, they may not will!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On Poets

“Since I have come to know the body better,” Zarathustra said to one of his disciples, “spirit has been spirit to me only in a manner of speaking; and all that is ‘imperishable’ — that too is only a parable.”

“I heard you say that once before,” answered the disciple, “and that time you added: ‘But the poets lie too much.’ Why did you say that the poets lie too much?”

“Why?” said Zarathustra. “You ask why? I am not one of those who may be asked for their why.

Is my experience from yesterday, then? It is a long time since I experienced the reasons for my opinions.

Would I not have to be a barrel of memory if I wanted to keep my reasons with me as well?

It is already too much for me to keep my opinions themselves; and many a bird flies away.

And now and then I also find a creature that has strayed into my dovecote, one that is strange to me and trembles when I lay my hand on it.

But what was it Zarathustra once said to you? That the poets lie too much? — But Zarathustra too is a poet.

Do you believe, then, that he spoke the truth there? Why do you believe it?”

The disciple answered: “I believe in Zarathustra.” But Zarathustra shook his head and smiled.

Faith does not make me blessed, he said, least of all faith in me.

But suppose that someone said in all seriousness that the poets lie too much: he is right — we lie too much.

We also know too little and are bad learners: so we simply have to lie.

And which of us poets has not doctored his wine? Many a poisonous hodgepodge has been brewed in our cellars, many an indescribable thing has been done there.

And because we know little, we take a heartfelt liking to the poor in spirit, especially when they are young women!

And we are even greedy for the things that little old women tell each other in the evenings. That is what we ourselves call the Eternal-Feminine in us.

And as if there were a special secret way into knowledge that is blocked up for those who learn something: so we believe in the people and their “wisdom.”

But this is what all poets believe: that whoever lies in the grass or on lonely slopes and pricks up his ears learns something of the things that are between heaven and earth.

And when tender stirrings come over them, the poets always think that nature herself is in love with them:

And that she creeps up to their ear to whisper secrets into it, and loving flatteries: and they puff themselves up and boast of it before all mortals!

Ah, there are so many things between heaven and earth that only the poets have let themselves dream of!

And especially above heaven: for all gods are poets’ parables, poets’ pilferings!

Truly, we are always drawn upward — that is, to the realm of the clouds: on these we set our motley stuffed dolls and then call them gods and overmen: —

For they are just light enough for those chairs! — all these gods and overmen.

Ah, how tired I am of all the inadequate that insists on being an event! Ah, how tired I am of the poets!

When Zarathustra spoke like this, his disciple was angry with him, but kept silent. And Zarathustra kept silent too; and his eye had turned inward, as if it were gazing into far distances. At last he sighed and drew a breath.

I am of today and of the past, he said then; but there is something in me that is of tomorrow and the day after tomorrow and of someday.

I grew tired of the poets, the old and the new: to me they are all surface, and shallow seas.

They did not think far enough into the depth: that is why their feeling did not sink to the bottom.

A little lust and a little boredom: that has been the best of their thinking so far.

All their harp-jingle-jangle is to me the breath and flitting of ghosts; what have they ever known of the fervor of tones! —

Nor are they clean enough for me: they all muddy their waters so that they may seem deep.

And they like to present themselves as reconcilers by it: but to me they remain go-betweens and mixers, and half-and-halfs, and unclean! —

Ah, I did cast my net into their seas and wanted to catch good fish; but every time I pulled up the head of some old god.

So the sea gave the hungry man a stone. And they themselves may well come from the sea.

Certainly, one finds pearls in them: all the more do they themselves resemble hard shellfish. And instead of a soul I have often found salted slime in them.

They have learned from the sea its vanity as well: is the sea not the peacock of peacocks?

Even before the ugliest of all buffaloes it spreads out its tail, and it never tires of its lace fan of silver and silk.

The buffalo looks on defiantly, near to the sand in his soul, nearer still to the thicket, but nearest of all to the swamp.

What does he care for beauty and sea and the finery of peacocks! This parable I speak to the poets.

Truly, their spirit itself is the peacock of peacocks and a sea of vanity!

The poet’s spirit wants spectators: even if they should be buffaloes! —

But I grew tired of this spirit: and I see the time coming when it will grow tired of itself.

I have already seen the poets transformed, and their gaze turned against themselves.

I saw penitents of the spirit coming: they grew out of the poets.

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On Great Events

There is an island in the sea — not far from Zarathustra’s Blessed Isles — on which a fire-mountain is always smoking. The people say of it, and the little old women among the people say it most of all, that it has been set like a boulder before the gate of the underworld: but that through the fire-mountain itself runs the narrow path leading down to this gate of the underworld.

Now at the time when Zarathustra was staying on the Blessed Isles, it happened that a ship dropped anchor at the island where the smoking mountain stands; and its crew went ashore to shoot rabbits. Toward the hour of noon, however, when the captain and his men were together again, they suddenly saw a man coming toward them through the air, and a voice said clearly: “It is time! It is high time!” But when the figure was nearest to them — it flew past quickly, like a shadow, in the direction where the fire-mountain lay — they recognized, to their utter dismay, that it was Zarathustra; for all of them had seen him before, except the captain himself, and they loved him as the people love: with love and awe together in equal parts.

“Look at that!” said the old helmsman. “There goes Zarathustra to hell!” —

At about the same time as these sailors landed on the fire island, a rumor went around that Zarathustra had disappeared; and when his friends were asked, they told how he had gone aboard a ship by night, without saying where he meant to travel.

So there was unrest; but after three days the sailors’ story was added to this unrest — and now all the people said that the devil had taken Zarathustra. His disciples laughed at this talk, it is true; and one of them even said: “I would sooner believe that Zarathustra has taken the devil.” But at the bottom of their souls they were all full of worry and longing: so their joy was great when, on the fifth day, Zarathustra appeared among them.

And this is the story of Zarathustra’s conversation with the fire-dog.

The earth, he said, has a skin; and this skin has diseases. One of these diseases, for example, is called: “man.”

And another of these diseases is called “fire-dog”: about him people have told each other many lies, and let themselves be told many lies.

To get to the bottom of this secret I went over the sea: and I have seen the truth naked — truly! barefoot up to the neck.

Now I know what the fire-dog is all about; and likewise all the devils of eruption and overthrow, which not only little old women are afraid of.

Come out, fire-dog, out of your depths! I cried. And confess how deep those depths are! Where does it come from, this stuff you snort up?

You drink plentifully at the sea: your oversalted eloquence gives that away! In truth, for a dog of the depths, you take your nourishment too much from the surface!

At most I take you for the ventriloquist of the earth: and whenever I heard the devils of overthrow and eruption speak, I found them just like you: salty, lying, and shallow.

You know how to roar and how to darken things with ash! You are the best of bigmouths, and you have learned more than enough of the art of bringing mud to the boil.

Wherever you are, there must always be mud close by, and much that is spongy, cavernous, squeezed in: it wants to get out into freedom.

“Freedom” is what you all like best to roar: but I have unlearned my belief in “great events” as soon as there is much roaring and smoke around them.

And just believe me, friend Hell-Racket! The greatest events — they are not our loudest hours, but our stillest.

The world revolves not around the inventors of new noise, but around the inventors of new values; it revolves inaudibly.

And just admit it! Very little had ever happened when your noise and smoke cleared away. What does it matter that a city became a mummy, and a statue lies in the mud!

And I say this word as well to the overthrowers of statues. It is surely the greatest folly to throw salt into the sea and statues into the mud.

The statue lay in the mud of your contempt: but that is just its law, that out of contempt life and living beauty grow back in it!

Now it rises again with more godlike features, seductive in its suffering; and truly! it will yet thank you for overthrowing it, you overthrowers!

But this advice I give to kings and churches and to everything that is weak with age and weak in virtue — just let yourselves be overthrown! So that you may come back to life, and so that virtue may come back to you! —

So I spoke before the fire-dog: then he interrupted me sullenly and asked: “Church? What is that, then?”

Church? I answered. That is a kind of state, and the most deceitful kind. But be quiet, you hypocrite dog! You surely know your own kind best already!

Like you yourself, the state is a hypocrite dog; like you, it likes to speak with smoke and roaring — to make people believe, like you, that it speaks from the belly of things.

For the state wants, at all costs, to be the most important animal on earth; and people believe it, too. —

When I had said this, the fire-dog behaved as if out of his mind with envy. “What?” he screamed. “The most important animal on earth? And people believe it, too?” And so much steam and such horrible voices came out of his throat that I thought he would choke with rage and envy.

At last he grew quieter, and his panting eased off; but as soon as he was quiet, I said, laughing:

“You are angry, fire-dog: so I am right about you!

And so that I may stay right, hear now about another fire-dog: he really does speak from the heart of the earth.

His breath breathes gold and golden rain: that is what his heart wants. What are ash and smoke and hot slime to him any more!

Laughter flutters out of him like a many-colored cloud; he has no liking for your gurgling and spitting and the gripes of your bowels!

But the gold and the laughter — those he takes from the heart of the earth: for, just so you know, — the heart of the earth is made of gold.”

When the fire-dog heard this, he could no longer stand to listen to me. Ashamed, he drew in his tail, said “Woof! Woof!” in a small, meek voice, and crawled down into his cave. —

So Zarathustra told it. But his disciples hardly listened to him: so great was their eagerness to tell him about the sailors, the rabbits, and the flying man.

“What am I to make of this!” said Zarathustra. “Am I a ghost, then?

But it must have been my shadow. You have probably heard something already about the wanderer and his shadow?

But this much is certain: I must keep it on a shorter leash — or else it will ruin my reputation yet.”

And once again Zarathustra shook his head and wondered. “What am I to make of this!” he said once more.

“Why, then, did the ghost cry: ‘It is time! It is high time!’

High time — for what?” —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

The Soothsayer

“— and I saw a great sadness come over mankind. The best of them grew weary of their works.

A doctrine went out, and a faith ran alongside it: ‘All is empty, all is the same, all has been!’

And from every hill it echoed back: ‘All is empty, all is the same, all has been!’

We have harvested, it is true: but why did all our fruits turn rotten and brown on us? What fell down from the evil moon last night?

All our work was in vain, our wine has turned to poison, the evil eye has scorched our fields and our hearts yellow.

We have all become dry; and if fire falls on us, we scatter like ash: — yes, we have made fire itself weary.

All our wells have dried up, even the sea has drawn back. All the ground wants to split open, but the depths will not swallow!

‘Ah, where is there still a sea one could drown in’: so goes our lament — out across the shallow swamps.

Truly, we have grown too weary even to die; now we stay awake and live on — in burial vaults!” —

So Zarathustra heard a soothsayer speak; and his prophecy went to Zarathustra’s heart and changed him. He went about sad and weary; and he became like those of whom the soothsayer had spoken.

Truly, he said to his disciples, it will not be long before this long twilight comes. Ah, how am I to save my light and carry it across!

If only it does not suffocate in this sadness! It is meant to be a light to farther worlds, and to the farthest nights!

Troubled like this in his heart, Zarathustra went about; and for three days he took neither food nor drink, had no rest, and lost his speech. At last it happened that he fell into a deep sleep. But his disciples sat around him through long night watches, waiting anxiously to see whether he would wake and speak again and be cured of his gloom.

And this is the discourse that Zarathustra gave when he woke; but his voice came to his disciples as if from far away.

Listen to the dream I dreamed, my friends, and help me guess its meaning!

It is still a riddle to me, this dream; its meaning is hidden inside it and trapped there, and does not yet fly out over it on free wings.

I had renounced all life, so I dreamed. I had become a night watchman and a guardian of graves, up there in the lonely mountain castle of death.

Up there I guarded his coffins: the musty vaults stood full of these trophies of victory. Overcome life gazed at me out of glass coffins.

I breathed the smell of dusty eternities: my soul lay sultry and dusty. And who could have aired out his soul in such a place!

The brightness of midnight was always around me, solitude crouched beside it; and, making three, a death-rattle silence, the worst of my companions.

I carried keys, the rustiest of all keys; and I knew how to open the creakiest of all gates with them.

Like a bitter, evil croaking the sound ran through the long corridors whenever the wings of the gate swung up: this bird screeched spitefully, it did not like being woken.

But more frightening still, and more heart-wringing, was when it fell silent again and all around grew still, and I sat alone in that treacherous silence.

So time passed for me and crept along, if there was still such a thing as time: what do I know of it! But at last the thing happened that woke me.

Three times blows struck the gate, like thunder, and three times the vaults echoed and howled back: then I went to the gate.

Alpa! I cried, who is carrying his ashes up the mountain? Alpa! Alpa! Who is carrying his ashes up the mountain?

And I pressed the key and heaved at the gate and strained. But it was still not open a finger’s breadth:

Then a roaring wind tore its wings apart: whistling, shrilling, and cutting, it hurled a black coffin toward me:

And amid the roaring and whistling and shrilling the coffin burst open and spewed out a thousandfold laughter.

And out of a thousand grimacing faces of children, angels, owls, fools, and butterflies as big as children, something laughed and jeered and roared at me.

I was horribly frightened by it: it threw me to the ground. And I screamed in horror, as I had never screamed before.

But my own scream woke me up: — and I came to myself. —

So Zarathustra told his dream, and then fell silent: for he did not yet know the interpretation of his dream. But the disciple he loved most rose quickly, took Zarathustra’s hand, and said:

“Your life itself interprets this dream for us, oh Zarathustra!

Are you not yourself the wind with its shrill whistling, which tears open the gates of the castles of death?

Are you not yourself the coffin full of life’s many-colored mischief and angel-grimaces?

Truly, like a thousandfold children’s laughter Zarathustra comes into every death chamber, laughing at those night watchmen and guardians of graves, and at whoever else goes rattling gloomy keys.

You will frighten them and knock them over with your laughter; fainting and waking will prove your power over them.

And even when the long twilight comes, and the weariness of death, you will not go under in our sky, you advocate of life!

You have let us see new stars and new splendors of the night; truly, you have spread laughter itself over us like a many-colored tent.

Now children’s laughter will always well up out of coffins; now a strong wind will always come, victorious over all weariness of death: of that you yourself are our guarantee and our soothsayer!

Truly, you dreamed them themselves, your enemies: that was your hardest dream!

But just as you woke from them and came to yourself, so they shall wake from themselves — and come to you!” —

So spoke the disciple; and all the others now crowded around Zarathustra and took him by the hands and tried to persuade him to leave his bed and his sadness and come back to them. But Zarathustra sat upright on his bed, with a strange look in his eyes. Like someone coming home from a long time in foreign lands, he looked at his disciples and searched their faces; and still he did not recognize them. But when they lifted him and set him on his feet, look, all at once his eye changed; he understood everything that had happened, stroked his beard, and said in a strong voice:

“Well then! All this has its time; but see to it, my disciples, that we have a good meal, and soon! That is how I mean to do penance for bad dreams!

But the soothsayer shall eat and drink at my side: and truly, I will yet show him a sea he can drown in!”

Thus spoke Zarathustra. But then he looked for a long time into the face of the disciple who had played the interpreter of dreams, and shook his head as he did so. —

On Redemption

When Zarathustra was crossing the great bridge one day, the cripples and beggars surrounded him, and a hunchback spoke to him like this:

“Look, Zarathustra! The people too are learning from you and coming to believe in your teaching: but for them to believe you completely, one more thing is needed — first you still have to convince us cripples! Here you have a fine selection, and truly, an opportunity with more than one handle to grab it by! You can heal the blind and make the lame walk; and from the one who has too much behind him you could surely take a little off as well: — that, I think, would be the right way to make the cripples believe in Zarathustra!”

But Zarathustra answered the one who had spoken like this: “If you take away the hunchback’s hump, you take away his spirit — so the people teach. And if you give the blind man his eyes, he sees too many bad things on earth: so that he curses the one who healed him. But whoever makes the lame man walk does him the greatest harm: for no sooner can he walk than his vices run away with him — so the people teach about cripples. And why should Zarathustra not learn from the people too, when the people learn from Zarathustra?

But it is the least thing to me, since I have been among people, to see: ‘This one is missing an eye, and that one an ear, and a third one his leg, and there are others who have lost their tongue or their nose or their head.’

I see, and have seen, worse things, and many things so hideous that I would not want to speak of every one of them, and of some I would not even want to keep silent: I mean people who lack everything, except that they have too much of one thing — people who are nothing more than a big eye, or a big mouth, or a big belly, or something else big, — inverse cripples is what I call such people.

And when I came out of my solitude and crossed this bridge for the first time, I did not trust my eyes, and looked, and looked again, and said at last: ‘That is an ear! An ear as big as a man!’ I looked more closely still: and in fact, under the ear something else was moving, something wretchedly small and poor and puny. And truly, the enormous ear sat on a thin little stalk, — but the stalk was a human being! Anyone who held a glass to his eye could even make out a little envious face; and also a bloated little soul dangling from the stalk. But the people told me that the big ear was not only a human being, but a great human being, a genius. But I have never believed the people when they talked about great human beings — and I kept my belief that it was an inverse cripple, who had too little of everything and too much of one thing.”

When Zarathustra had spoken like this to the hunchback, and to those whose mouthpiece and advocate he was, he turned to his disciples in deep displeasure and said:

“Truly, my friends, I walk among people as among the fragments and limbs of human beings!

This is what is terrible to my eye, that I find man shattered and scattered as if over a field of battle and of butchery.

And if my eye flees from the now to the past: it always finds the same thing: fragments and limbs and dreadful accidents — but no human beings!

The now and the past on earth — ah! my friends — that is what I find most unbearable; and I would not know how to live, if I were not also a seer of what must come.

A seer, one who wills, a creator, a future itself and a bridge to the future — and ah, also, as it were, a cripple at this bridge: all of that is Zarathustra.

And you too have often asked yourselves: ‘Who is Zarathustra to us? What shall we call him?’ And like me, you gave yourselves questions for answers.

Is he one who promises? Or one who fulfills? A conqueror? Or an heir? An autumn? Or a plowshare? A doctor? Or one who has recovered?

Is he a poet? Or a truthful man? A liberator? Or a tamer? A good man? Or an evil one?

I walk among people as among the fragments of the future: that future which I see.

And this is all my composing and striving: that I compose into one and gather together what is fragment and riddle and dreadful accident.

And how could I bear to be a man, if man were not also a poet and a guesser of riddles and the redeemer of accident!

To redeem those who are past, and to recreate every ‘It was’ into a ‘So I willed it!’ — that alone would I call redemption!

Will — that is the name of the liberator and bringer of joy: so I taught you, my friends! But now learn this as well: the will itself is still a prisoner.

Willing liberates: but what is the name of the thing that still puts even the liberator in chains?

‘It was’: that is the name of the will’s gnashing of teeth and loneliest gloom. Powerless against what has been done — it is an evil spectator of everything past.

The will cannot will backward; that it cannot break time and time’s greed, — that is the will’s loneliest gloom.

Willing liberates: what does willing itself devise, to get free of its gloom and to mock its prison?

Ah, every prisoner becomes a fool! And the imprisoned will, too, redeems itself foolishly.

That time does not run backward, that is its fury; ‘That which was’ — that is the name of the stone it cannot roll.

And so it rolls stones out of fury and displeasure, and takes revenge on whatever does not feel fury and displeasure as it does.

So the will, the liberator, became a doer of harm: and on everything that can suffer it takes revenge for the fact that it cannot go back.

This, yes, this alone is revenge itself: the will’s ill will against time and its ‘It was.’

Truly, a great folly lives in our will; and it became a curse to everything human that this folly learned to be spirit!

The spirit of revenge: my friends, that has so far been mankind’s best reflection; and wherever there was suffering, there always had to be punishment.

‘Punishment,’ you see, is what revenge calls itself: with a lying word it fakes itself a good conscience.

And because there is suffering in the one who wills, since he cannot will backward, — therefore willing itself and all life were supposed to be — punishment!

And now cloud upon cloud rolled over the spirit: until at last madness preached: ‘Everything passes away, therefore everything deserves to pass away!’

‘And this is justice itself, that law of time, that it must devour its children’: so madness preached.

‘Things are ordered morally, according to justice and punishment. Oh, where is redemption from the flux of things and from the punishment called existence?’ So madness preached.

‘Can there be redemption, if there is an eternal justice? Ah, the stone “It was” cannot be rolled away: all punishments, too, must be eternal!’ So madness preached.

‘No deed can be annihilated: how could it be undone by punishment! This, this is what is eternal in the punishment called “existence,” that existence must also be, eternally, deed and guilt again and again!

Unless the will should at last redeem itself, and willing become not-willing — ’: but you know, my brothers, this fable-song of madness!

I led you away from these fable-songs when I taught you: ‘The will is a creator.’

Every ‘It was’ is a fragment, a riddle, a dreadful accident — until the creating will says to it: ‘But so I willed it!’

Until the creating will says to it: ‘But so I will it! So shall I will it!’

But has it ever spoken like this yet? And when will this happen? Has the will been unharnessed yet from its own folly?

Has the will become its own redeemer and bringer of joy yet? Has it unlearned the spirit of revenge and all gnashing of teeth?

And who has taught it reconciliation with time, and something higher than any reconciliation?

Something higher than any reconciliation the will must will, the will that is will to power —: but how does that happen to it? Who has taught it to will backward, too?”

— But at this point in his discourse it happened that Zarathustra suddenly stopped, looking exactly like someone who has been frightened to the utmost. With frightened eyes he looked at his disciples; his eye pierced their thoughts, and the thoughts behind their thoughts, as if with arrows. But after a little while he was laughing again, and said, soothed:

“It is hard to live with people, because keeping silent is so hard. Especially for a chatterbox.” —

Thus spoke Zarathustra. But the hunchback had listened to the conversation, covering his face as he did; but when he heard Zarathustra laugh, he looked up curiously and said slowly:

“But why does Zarathustra speak differently to us than to his disciples?”

Zarathustra answered: “What is so surprising about that! With hunchbacks one may well speak crookedly!”

“Good,” said the hunchback. “And with pupils one may well tell tales out of school.

But why does Zarathustra speak differently to his pupils — than to himself?” —

On Human Prudence

Not the height: the slope is what is terrible!

The slope, where the gaze plunges down and the hand reaches up. There the heart grows dizzy before its double will.

Ah, friends, can you guess my heart’s double will as well?

This, this is my slope and my danger, that my gaze plunges toward the heights, and that my hand would like to hold on and brace itself — against the depths!

My will clings to man, with chains I bind myself to man, because I am being torn upward toward the overman: for that is where my other will wants to go.

And for this I live blind among people, as though I did not know them: so that my hand may not wholly lose its faith in something solid.

I do not know you people: this darkness and comfort is often spread around me.

I sit at the gateway for every rogue and ask: who wants to deceive me?

This is my first human prudence, that I let myself be deceived, so as not to be on my guard against deceivers.

Ah, if I were on my guard against man: how could man be an anchor for my ball! Too easily I would be torn up and away!

This providence stands over my fate: that I must have no foresight.

And whoever does not want to die of thirst among people must learn to drink from every glass; and whoever wants to stay clean among people must know how to wash even with dirty water.

And so I often spoke to myself for comfort: “Well then! Up, then! Old heart! A misfortune has miscarried for you: enjoy this as your — good fortune!”

But this is my other human prudence: I spare the vain more than the proud.

Is wounded vanity not the mother of all tragedies? But where pride is wounded, something better than pride may well grow.

For life to be good to watch, its play must be played well: but for that it needs good actors.

I have found all the vain to be good actors: they act, and they want people to enjoy watching them, — all their spirit is in this will.

They put on a show, they invent themselves; near them I love to watch life, — it cures me of melancholy.

That is why I spare the vain, because they are the doctors of my melancholy, and keep me attached to man as to a play.

And then: who can measure in the vain man the whole depth of his modesty! I am fond of him, and pity him for his modesty.

He wants to learn his faith in himself from you; he feeds on your glances, he eats praise out of your hands.

He believes even your lies, if you lie well about him: for in its depths his heart sighs: “What am I!”

And if the true virtue is the one that does not know about itself: well, the vain man does not know about his modesty! —

But this is my third human prudence, that I do not let your fearfulness spoil for me the sight of the evil.

I am blissful to see the wonders that the hot sun hatches: tigers and palm trees and rattlesnakes.

Among people, too, there is a fine brood of the hot sun, and much that is wonderful in the evil.

It is true that, just as your wisest did not seem so very wise to me: so too I found people’s wickedness falling short of its reputation.

And often I asked, shaking my head: Why go on rattling, you rattlesnakes?

Truly, there is a future even for evil! And the hottest south has not yet been discovered for man.

How many things are already called the worst wickedness that are only twelve feet wide and three months long! But someday greater dragons will come into the world.

For if the overman is not to lack his dragon, the over-dragon that is worthy of him: much hot sun must still blaze down on damp jungle!

Your wildcats must first have become tigers, and your poisonous toads crocodiles: for the good hunter shall have a good hunt!

And truly, you good and just! There is much in you to laugh at, and above all your fear of what has so far been called “devil”!

You are so foreign in your souls to what is great that the overman would be frightening to you in his goodness!

And you wise men and knowers, you would run from the sunburn of wisdom in which the overman joyfully bathes his nakedness!

You highest people my eye has met! This is my doubt about you and my secret laughter: I guess that you would call my overman — devil!

Ah, I grew tired of these highest and best: out of their “height” I longed to go up, out, away to the overman!

A horror came over me when I saw these best ones naked: then wings grew on me to float away into distant futures.

Into more distant futures, into more southern souths than any artist ever dreamed: there, where gods are ashamed of all clothes!

But I want to see you disguised, you neighbors and fellow men, and well dressed up, and vain, and dignified, as “the good and the just,” —

And I myself want to sit among you disguised, — so that I may mistake you and myself: for that is my last human prudence.

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

The Stillest Hour

“What has happened to me, my friends? You see me distraught, driven away, unwillingly obedient, ready to go — ah, to go away from you!

Yes, Zarathustra must go back into his solitude once more: but this time the bear goes back into his cave without any joy!

What has happened to me? Who commands this? — Ah, my angry mistress wills it so; she spoke to me: have I ever yet told you her name?

Yesterday, toward evening, my stillest hour spoke to me: that is the name of my terrible mistress.

And this is how it happened, — for I must tell you everything, so that your hearts do not harden against the one who is suddenly leaving!

Do you know the terror of someone falling asleep? —

He is terrified down to his toes, because the ground gives way under him and the dream begins.

I tell you this as a parable. Yesterday, at the stillest hour, the ground gave way under me: the dream began.

The hand moved on, the clock of my life drew breath — never had I heard such stillness around me: so that my heart was terrified.

Then it spoke to me without a voice: ‘You know it, Zarathustra?’ —

And I cried out in terror at this whisper, and the blood drained from my face: but I kept silent.

Then it spoke to me again without a voice: ‘You know it, Zarathustra, but you do not speak it!’ —

And at last I answered, like someone defiant: ‘Yes, I know it, but I will not speak it!’

Then it spoke to me again without a voice: ‘You will not, Zarathustra? Is that really true? Do not hide yourself in your defiance!’ —

And I wept and trembled like a child and said: ‘Ah, I would like to, but how can I! Spare me this, at least! It is beyond my strength!’

Then it spoke to me again without a voice: ‘What do you matter, Zarathustra! Speak your word and break!’ —

And I answered: ‘Ah, is it my word? Who am I? I am waiting for someone worthier; I am not worthy even to break on it.’

Then it spoke to me again without a voice: ‘What do you matter? You are not yet humble enough for me. Humility has the toughest hide.’ —

And I answered: ‘What has the hide of my humility not borne already! I live at the foot of my own height: how high are my peaks? No one has told me yet. But I know my valleys well.’

Then it spoke to me again without a voice: ‘Oh Zarathustra, whoever has mountains to move moves valleys and lowlands too.’ —

And I answered: ‘My word has not yet moved any mountains, and what I said did not reach people. I did go to people, but I have not yet arrived among them.’

Then it spoke to me again without a voice: ‘What do you know about that! The dew falls on the grass when the night is at its most silent.’ —

And I answered: ‘They mocked me when I found my own way and walked it; and in truth my feet trembled then.’

And so they said to me: ‘You have unlearned the way, now you are unlearning how to walk as well!’

Then it spoke to me again without a voice: ‘What does their mockery matter! You are someone who has unlearned how to obey: now you shall command!

Do you not know who is most needed by all? The one who commands great things.

To do great things is hard: but the harder thing is to command great things.

This is what is most unforgivable in you: you have the power, and you will not rule.’ —

And I answered: ‘I lack the lion’s voice for any commanding.’

Then it spoke to me again, like a whisper: ‘It is the stillest words that bring the storm. Thoughts that come on doves’ feet guide the world.

Oh Zarathustra, you shall go as a shadow of what must come: in that way you will command, and lead the way by commanding.’ —

And I answered: ‘I am ashamed.’

Then it spoke to me again without a voice: ‘You must still become a child, and without shame.

The pride of youth is still on you, you became young late: but whoever wants to become a child must overcome even his youth.’ —

And I thought it over for a long time, and trembled. But at last I said what I had said at first: ‘I will not.’

Then there was laughter all around me. Woe, how that laughter tore my insides and slit my heart open!

And it spoke to me for the last time: ‘Oh Zarathustra, your fruits are ripe, but you are not ripe for your fruits!

So you must go back into solitude: for you must still grow mellow.’ —

And again it laughed, and fled: then it grew still around me, as if with a twofold stillness. But I lay on the ground, and the sweat poured from my limbs.

— Now you have heard everything, and why I must go back into my solitude. I have kept nothing silent from you, my friends.

But you have heard this from me as well: who is still the most silent of all men — and wants to be!

Ah, my friends! I would still have something to say to you, I would still have something to give you! Why do I not give it? Am I stingy, then?” —

But when Zarathustra had spoken these words, the force of his pain and the nearness of parting from his friends overwhelmed him, so that he wept aloud; and no one knew how to comfort him. But in the night he went away alone and left his friends.

Part Three

Epigraph to Part Three

“You look upward when you long to be lifted up. And I look down, because I am lifted up. Which of you can laugh and be lifted up at the same time? Whoever climbs the highest mountains laughs at all tragic plays and all tragic solemnity.”

Zarathustra, “On Reading and Writing”

The Wanderer

It was midnight when Zarathustra set out over the ridge of the island, so that he would reach the other shore by early morning: for there he meant to board a ship. There was a good harbor there, where foreign ships too liked to drop anchor; and they took along many who wanted to cross the sea from the Blessed Isles. Now as Zarathustra climbed the mountain like this, he thought along the way of all his solitary wandering since his youth, and of how many mountains and ridges and summits he had already climbed.

I am a wanderer and a mountain climber, he said to his heart; I do not love the plains, and it seems I cannot sit still for long.

And whatever may still come to me as fate and experience — there will be wandering in it, and mountain climbing: in the end one experiences only oneself.

The time has run out when accidents were still allowed to befall me; and what could still fall to me now that was not already my own!

It only returns, it finally comes home to me — my own Self, and whatever of it has long been abroad and scattered among all things and accidents.

And I know one thing more: I am standing now before my last summit, and before what has been saved up for me the longest. Ah, I must climb my hardest path! Ah, I have begun my most solitary wandering!

But whoever is of my kind does not escape such an hour: the hour that says to him: “Only now are you going your way of greatness! Summit and abyss — they are now joined into one!

You are going your way of greatness: what until now was called your last danger has now become your last refuge!

You are going your way of greatness: now this must be your best courage, that there is no longer any path behind you!

You are going your way of greatness; here no one shall sneak after you! Your own foot has wiped out the path behind you, and above it is written: Impossibility.

And if from now on you have no ladders left, then you must know how to climb up on your own head: how else would you climb upward?

Up on your own head and away over your own heart! Now the gentlest thing in you must become the hardest.

Whoever has always spared himself much grows sick at last from all his sparing. Praised be what makes hard! I do not praise the land where butter and honey — flow!

Learning to look away from oneself is necessary in order to see much: — every mountain climber needs this hardness.

But whoever, as a seeker of knowledge, is pushy with his eyes — how could he see more of any thing than its foreground!

But you, oh Zarathustra, wanted to see the ground of all things, and their background: so you must climb up over yourself — upward, higher, until even your stars are under you!

Yes! To look down on myself and even on my stars: that alone would I call my summit, that has remained for me as my last summit! —”

Thus spoke Zarathustra to himself as he climbed, comforting his heart with hard little sayings: for he was sore at heart as never before. And when he reached the top of the ridge, look, there the other sea lay spread out before him: and he stood still and was silent for a long time. But the night was cold at this height, and clear, and bright with stars.

I recognize my lot, he said at last, with sadness. Well then! I am ready. Now my last solitude has begun.

Ah, this black, sad sea beneath me! Ah, this pregnant, nighttime sullenness! Ah, fate and sea! To you I must now climb down!

I stand before my highest mountain and before my longest wandering: that is why I must first go down deeper than I ever climbed:

— deeper down into pain than I ever climbed, right into its blackest flood! So my fate wills it: Well then! I am ready.

Where do the highest mountains come from? I once asked. Then I learned that they come out of the sea.

This testimony is written in their rock and in the walls of their summits. It is out of the deepest that the highest must come to its height. —

Thus spoke Zarathustra on the peak of the mountain, where it was cold; but when he came near the sea and at last stood alone among the cliffs, he had grown weary on the way, and more full of longing than ever before.

Everything is still asleep now, he said; even the sea is asleep. Drunk with sleep and strange, its eye gazes at me.

But its breath is warm, I can feel that. And I feel too that it is dreaming. It writhes, dreaming, on hard pillows.

Listen! Listen! How it groans with evil memories! Or with evil expectations?

Ah, I am sad along with you, you dark monster, and angry with myself on your account.

Ah, that my hand does not have strength enough! Gladly, truly, would I redeem you from evil dreams! —

And as Zarathustra spoke like this, he laughed at himself with melancholy and bitterness. “What! Zarathustra!” he said, “do you even want to sing comfort to the sea?

Ah, you loving fool Zarathustra, you who are overblissful with trust! But you were always like this: you always came trustingly up to everything terrible.

Every monster you still wanted to stroke. A breath of warm air, a little soft shagginess on the paw —: and at once you were ready to love it and to coax it.

Love is the danger of the most solitary one, love for everything, if only it is alive! Truly, my folly and my modesty in love are laughable!” —

Thus spoke Zarathustra, and laughed a second time as he did; but then he thought of the friends he had left behind —, and as if he had wronged them with his thoughts, he was angry with himself for his thoughts. And soon it happened that the one who laughed was weeping: — with anger and longing Zarathustra wept bitterly.

On the Vision and the Riddle

I

When word spread among the sailors that Zarathustra was on the ship — for a man who came from the Blessed Isles had come on board with him — there arose a great curiosity and expectation. But Zarathustra kept silent for two days and was cold and deaf with sadness, so that he answered neither looks nor questions. On the evening of the second day, however, he opened his ears again, though he still kept silent: for there was much that was strange and dangerous to listen to on this ship, which came from far away and wanted to go farther still. And Zarathustra was a friend of all those who make long voyages and do not like to live without danger. And look! at last, as he listened, his own tongue was loosened, and the ice of his heart broke: — then he began to speak like this:

To you, the bold seekers and tempters, and whoever has ever set out with cunning sails on terrible seas, —

to you who are drunk on riddles, who delight in twilight, whose souls are lured by flutes into every maze and gulf:

— for you do not want to grope along a thread with a cowardly hand; and where you can guess, you hate to deduce —

to you alone I tell the riddle that I saw, — the vision of the most solitary one. —

Gloomily I walked not long ago through the corpse-colored twilight, — gloomily and hard, with pressed lips. Not only one sun had gone under for me.

A path that climbed defiantly through the scree, a spiteful, solitary path, cheered on by neither herb nor bush any longer: a mountain path crunched under the defiance of my foot.

Striding silently over the mocking clatter of pebbles, crushing the stone that made it slip: so my foot forced its way upward.

Upward: — in spite of the spirit that pulled it downward, pulled it toward the abyss, the spirit of gravity, my devil and archenemy.

Upward: — although he sat on me, half dwarf, half mole; lame; laming; dripping lead into my ear, leaden-drop thoughts into my brain.

“Oh Zarathustra,” he whispered mockingly, syllable by syllable, “you stone of wisdom! You threw yourself high, but every stone that is thrown must — fall!

Oh Zarathustra, you stone of wisdom, you sling-stone, you star-smasher! You threw yourself so high, — but every stone that is thrown — must fall!

Condemned to yourself and to your own stoning: oh Zarathustra, far indeed you threw the stone, — but it will fall back on you!”

Then the dwarf fell silent; and it lasted a long time. But his silence weighed on me; and to be two in this way is, truly, lonelier than to be one!

I climbed, I climbed, I dreamed, I thought, — but everything weighed on me. I was like a sick man worn out by his cruel torment, who is woken again from his falling asleep by a still crueler dream. —

But there is something in me that I call courage: so far it has struck dead every discouragement in me. This courage at last bade me stand still and say: “Dwarf! You! Or I!” —

For courage is the best killer, — courage that attacks: for in every attack there is the sound of drums and trumpets.
