But man is the most courageous animal: with that he has overcome every animal. With the sound of drums and trumpets he has overcome every pain as well; but human pain is the deepest pain.

Courage strikes dead even dizziness at the edge of abysses: and where does man not stand at the edge of abysses! Is seeing itself not — seeing abysses?

Courage is the best killer: courage strikes dead even pity. But pity is the deepest abyss: as deeply as man sees into life, so deeply does he see into suffering.

But courage is the best killer, courage that attacks: it strikes dead even death, for it says: “Was that life? Well then! Once more!”

In such a saying, though, there is much sound of drums and trumpets. Whoever has ears, let him hear. —

II

“Stop! Dwarf!” I said. “I! Or you! But I am the stronger of us two —: you do not know my abysmal thought! That — you could not bear!” —

Then something happened that made me lighter: for the dwarf jumped down from my shoulder, the curious creature! And he squatted on a stone in front of me. But just there, where we had stopped, stood a gateway.

“Look at this gateway, dwarf!” I went on. “It has two faces. Two paths come together here: no one has ever yet walked either of them to its end.

This long lane back here: it lasts an eternity. And that long lane out there — that is another eternity.

They contradict each other, these paths; they run head-on into each other: — and it is here, at this gateway, that they come together. The gateway’s name is written above it: ‘Moment.’

But if someone were to walk on along one of them — and on and on, ever farther: do you believe, dwarf, that these paths contradict each other eternally?” —

“Everything straight lies,” the dwarf muttered contemptuously. “All truth is crooked; time itself is a circle.”

“You spirit of gravity!” I said angrily, “do not take this too lightly! Or I will leave you squatting where you squat, lamefoot, — and I carried you high!

Look,” I went on, “at this moment! From this gateway, Moment, a long eternal lane runs backward: behind us lies an eternity.

Must not whatever of all things can run have already run along this lane once before? Must not whatever of all things can happen have already happened once before, been done, run past?

And if everything has already been here before: what do you think, dwarf, of this moment? Must not this gateway too have — been here before?

And are not all things knotted together so tightly that this moment draws all coming things after it? And so — itself as well?

For whatever of all things can run: in this long lane out there too — it must run once more! —

And this slow spider that creeps in the moonlight, and this moonlight itself, and I and you in the gateway, whispering together, whispering of eternal things — must we not all have been here before?

— and come again and run in that other lane, out there, before us, in this long, eerie lane — must we not come again eternally?” —

So I spoke, more and more softly: for I was afraid of my own thoughts, and of the thoughts behind my thoughts. Then, suddenly, I heard a dog howl nearby.

Had I ever heard a dog howl like that? My thoughts ran back. Yes! When I was a child, in my most distant childhood:

— then I heard a dog howl like that. And I saw it too, its hair bristling, its head raised, trembling, in the stillest midnight, when even dogs believe in ghosts:

— so that I felt sorry for it. For just then the full moon, silent as death, was passing over the house; just then it stood still, a round glow, — still on the flat roof, as if on someone else’s property: —

that was what had terrified the dog then: for dogs believe in thieves and ghosts. And when I heard such howling again, I felt sorry for it once more.

Where now was the dwarf? And the gateway? And the spider? And all the whispering? Was I dreaming, then? Was I waking up? All at once I stood among wild cliffs, alone, desolate, in the most desolate moonlight.

But there lay a man! And there! The dog, leaping, bristling, whining, — now it saw me coming — then it howled again, then it screamed: — had I ever heard a dog scream for help like that?

And truly, what I saw, I had never seen the like of. I saw a young shepherd, writhing, choking, twitching, his face distorted, and a heavy black serpent was hanging out of his mouth.

Had I ever seen so much disgust and pale horror on one face? He must have been asleep? Then the serpent crawled into his throat — and there it bit fast.

My hand pulled at the serpent and pulled: — in vain! it could not pull the serpent out of his throat. Then it cried out of me: “Bite! Bite down!

Its head off! Bite!” — so it cried out of me, my horror, my hatred, my disgust, my mercy, all my good and my bad cried out of me in a single cry. —

You bold ones around me! You seekers and tempters, and whoever of you has set out with cunning sails on unexplored seas! You who delight in riddles!

Then guess me the riddle that I saw back then, interpret for me the vision of the most solitary one!

For it was a vision and a foreseeing: — what did I see back then in a parable? And who is it that must one day still come?

Who is the shepherd into whose throat the serpent crawled like this? Who is the man into whose throat everything heaviest, blackest will crawl like this?

— But the shepherd bit, as my cry had advised him; he bit with a good bite! Far away he spat the serpent’s head —: and sprang up. —

No longer shepherd, no longer man, — one transformed, surrounded with light, who laughed! Never yet on earth has a man laughed as he laughed!

Oh my brothers, I heard a laughter that was no human laughter, — and now a thirst gnaws at me, a longing that is never stilled.

My longing for this laughter gnaws at me: oh, how can I bear to go on living! And how could I bear to die now! —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On Involuntary Bliss

With such riddles and bitternesses in his heart Zarathustra crossed the sea. But when he was four days’ journey from the Blessed Isles and from his friends, he had overcome all his pain —: victorious and with firm feet he stood once more upon his fate. And then Zarathustra spoke like this to his exultant conscience:

“Alone I am again, and I want to be, alone with the pure sky and the open sea; and again it is afternoon around me.

It was in the afternoon that I once found my friends for the first time, and in the afternoon the second time as well: — at the hour when all light grows stiller.

For whatever happiness is still on its way between heaven and earth now seeks a bright soul to lodge in: for happiness all light has now grown stiller.

Oh afternoon of my life! Once my happiness too came down to the valley to seek a lodging for itself: and there it found these open, hospitable souls.

Oh afternoon of my life! What did I not give up to have one thing: this living plantation of my thoughts, and this morning light of my highest hope!

The creator once sought companions, and children of his hope: and look, it turned out that he could not find them, unless he first created them himself.

So I am in the midst of my work, going to my children and coming back from them: for the sake of his children Zarathustra must complete himself.

For at bottom one loves only one’s child and one’s work; and where there is great love of oneself, it is the sign of pregnancy: so I have found it.

My children are still green in their first spring, standing close together and shaken together by the winds, the trees of my garden and of my best soil.

And truly! Where such trees stand together, there are Blessed Isles!

But one day I want to dig them up and set each one apart by himself: so that he may learn solitude and defiance and caution.

Gnarled and bent and with a supple hardness, he shall then stand for me by the sea, a living lighthouse of unconquerable life.

There, where the storms plunge down into the sea, and the mountain’s trunk drinks water, each one shall one day keep his watches by day and by night, for his testing and knowing.

He shall be known and tested, to see whether he is of my kind and descent, — whether he is master of a long will, silent even when he speaks, and yielding in such a way that in giving he takes: —

— so that one day he may become my companion, a fellow creator and fellow celebrator of Zarathustra’s —: one who writes my will on my tablets, for the fuller completion of all things.

And for his sake and for the sake of those like him, I must complete myself: that is why I now turn aside from my happiness and offer myself to every misfortune — for my last testing and knowing.

And truly, it was time for me to go; and the wanderer’s shadow and the longest while and the stillest hour — they all urged me: ‘It is high time!’

The wind blew through my keyhole and said ‘Come!’ The door sprang slyly open for me and said ‘Go!’

But I lay chained to the love of my children: desire laid this snare for me, the desire for love, that I should become my children’s prey and lose myself to them.

Desiring — to me that already means: to have lost myself. I have you, my children! In this having, everything shall be certainty and nothing desire.

But the sun of my love lay brooding on me, Zarathustra was stewing in his own juice, — then shadows and doubts flew over me.

I was already longing for frost and winter: ‘oh, if only frost and winter would make me crack and crunch again!’ I sighed: — then icy mists rose out of me.

My past burst its graves, many a pain that had been buried alive woke up —: it had only been sleeping its fill, hidden in shrouds.

So everything called to me in signs: ‘It is time!’ — But I — did not hear: until at last my abyss stirred and my thought bit me.

Ah, abysmal thought, you who are my thought! When will I find the strength to hear you digging and no longer tremble?

My heart pounds right up into my throat when I hear you digging! Even your silence wants to strangle me, you abysmally silent one!

As yet I have never dared to call you up: it was enough that I — carried you with me! As yet I was not strong enough for the lion’s last exuberance and mischief.

Your heaviness was always terrible enough for me: but one day I shall yet find the strength and the lion’s voice that calls you up!

Once I have overcome myself in that, then I will overcome myself in what is greater still; and a victory shall be the seal of my completion! —

Meanwhile I still drift on uncertain seas; accident flatters me, smooth-tongued accident; I look forward and backward —, and still I see no end.

The hour of my last struggle has not yet come to me, — or is it coming to me just now? Truly, sea and life gaze at me all around with treacherous beauty!

Oh afternoon of my life! Oh happiness before evening! Oh harbor on the high seas! Oh peace in uncertainty! How I distrust you all!

Truly, I am distrustful of your treacherous beauty! I am like the lover who distrusts all-too-velvety smiles.

As he pushes his most beloved away ahead of him, tender even in his hardness, the jealous one —, so I push this blissful hour away ahead of me.

Away with you, you blissful hour! With you there came to me a bliss against my will! Willing, I stand here for my deepest pain: — you came at the wrong time!

Away with you, you blissful hour! Better to take lodging over there — with my children! Hurry! and bless them before evening with my happiness!

There, evening is already drawing near: the sun is sinking. Gone — my happiness! —”

Thus spoke Zarathustra. And he waited for his misfortune all night long: but he waited in vain. The night stayed bright and still, and happiness itself came nearer and nearer to him. But toward morning Zarathustra laughed to his heart and said mockingly: “Happiness runs after me. That comes from my not running after women. But happiness is a woman.”

Before Sunrise

Oh heaven above me, you pure one! You deep one! You abyss of light! Gazing at you, I shudder with godlike desires.

To throw myself up into your height — that is my depth! To shelter myself in your purity — that is my innocence!

The god is veiled by his beauty: so you hide your stars. You do not speak: so you proclaim your wisdom to me.

Mute over the roaring sea you have risen for me today; your love and your shame speak revelation to my roaring soul.

That you came to me beautiful, veiled in your beauty, that you speak to me mutely, revealed in your wisdom:

Oh, how could I not guess all that is bashful in your soul! Before the sun you came to me, the loneliest of all.

We have been friends from the beginning: grief and grimness and ground we have in common; even the sun we have in common.

We do not speak to each other, because we know too much —: we are silent to each other, we smile our knowledge to each other.

Are you not the light to my fire? Do you not have the sister soul to my insight?

Together we learned everything; together we learned to climb above ourselves to ourselves, and to smile without a cloud: —

— to smile down without a cloud, out of bright eyes and from miles away, when under us compulsion and purpose and guilt steam like rain.

And when I wandered alone: for what did my soul hunger in the nights and on wrong paths? And when I climbed mountains, whom did I ever seek on the mountains, if not you?

And all my wandering and mountain climbing: it was only a necessity, and a help for the helpless: — my whole will wants only to fly, to fly up into you!

And whom did I hate more than drifting clouds, and everything that stains you? And I hated even my own hatred, because it stained you!

I bear a grudge against the drifting clouds, those creeping cats of prey: they take from you and me what we have in common — the vast, unbounded Yes-and-Amen-saying.

We bear a grudge against these go-betweens and mixers, the drifting clouds: these half-and-halfers, who have learned neither to bless nor to curse from the ground up.

I would rather sit in a tub under a closed heaven, rather sit in the abyss with no heaven at all, than see you, heaven of light, stained with drifting clouds!

And often I have longed to pin them down with jagged gold wires of lightning, so that, like the thunder, I could beat the kettledrum on their kettle-bellies: —

— an angry kettledrummer, because they rob me of your Yes! and Amen!, you heaven above me, you pure one! You bright one! You abyss of light! — because they rob you of my Yes! and Amen!

For I would rather have noise and thunder and the curses of storms than this cautious, doubting cat-calm; and among men too, what I hate most are all the pussyfooters and half-and-halfers and doubting, hesitating, drifting clouds.

And “whoever cannot bless shall learn to curse!” — this clear teaching fell to me out of a clear sky; this star still stands in my heaven even on black nights.

But I am one who blesses and a Yes-sayer, if only you are around me, you pure one! You bright one! You abyss of light! — then I carry my blessing Yes-saying even into every abyss.

I have become one who blesses and one who says Yes: and for that I wrestled long and was a wrestler, so that one day I might get my hands free for blessing.

But this is my blessing: to stand over each and every thing as its own heaven, as its round roof, its azure bell and eternal security: and blessed is the one who blesses like this!

For all things are baptized at the fountain of eternity and beyond good and evil; but good and evil themselves are only shadows in between, and damp glooms, and drifting clouds.

Truly, it is a blessing and not a blasphemy when I teach: “Over all things stands the heaven Accident, the heaven Innocence, the heaven Chance, the heaven Exuberance.”

“Of Chance” — that is the oldest title of nobility in the world; I gave it back to all things, I redeemed them from their serfdom under purpose.

This freedom and heavenly cheerfulness I set like an azure bell over all things, when I taught that over them and through them no “eternal will” — wills.

This exuberance and this folly I set in the place of that will, when I taught: “In everything one thing is impossible — rationality!”

A little reason, to be sure, a seed of wisdom scattered from star to star — this leaven is mixed into all things: for the sake of folly, wisdom is mixed into all things!

A little wisdom is possible, certainly; but this blessed security I found in all things: that they would rather — dance on the feet of accident.

Oh heaven above me, you pure one! You lofty one! This is what your purity now is to me: that there is no eternal reason-spider and no reason-spiderweb: —

— that you are a dance floor for me for divine accidents, that you are a table of the gods for me, for divine dice and dice players! —

But you are blushing? Did I say something unsayable? Did I blaspheme when I wanted to bless you?

Or is it the shame of being two together that made you blush? — Are you telling me to go and be silent, because now — the day is coming?

The world is deep —: and deeper than the day could ever comprehend. Not everything may have words in front of the day. But the day is coming: so let us part now!

Oh heaven above me, you bashful one! You glowing one! Oh you, my happiness before sunrise! The day is coming: so let us part now! —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Virtue That Makes Small

I

When Zarathustra was back on solid land, he did not go straight to his mountains and his cave, but took many roads and asked many questions and found out this and that, so that he said of himself in jest: “Look, a river that flows back to its source in many windings!” For he wanted to learn what had happened to man in the meantime: whether he had become bigger or smaller. And once he saw a row of new houses; he was astonished and said:

“What do these houses mean? Truly, no great soul set them up as a parable of itself!

Did some silly child take them out of its toy box? If only another child would put them back in its box!

And these rooms and chambers: can men go in and out of them? They seem to me made for silk dolls; or for nibblers, who perhaps also let themselves be nibbled.”

And Zarathustra stood still and thought. At last he said sadly: “Everything has become smaller!

Everywhere I see lower gates: one of my kind can still get through them, but — he has to stoop!

Oh, when will I come home again to where I no longer have to stoop — no longer have to stoop before the small!” — And Zarathustra sighed and gazed into the distance. —

But that same day he gave his discourse on the virtue that makes small.

II

I walk among this people and keep my eyes open: they do not forgive me for not envying their virtues.

They snap at me because I tell them: small people need small virtues — and because I find it hard to swallow that small people are needed!

I am still like the rooster here in a strange farmyard, whom even the hens peck at; but I hold no grudge against these hens for it.

I am polite to them, as to every small annoyance; to be prickly toward small things seems to me a wisdom for hedgehogs.

They all talk about me when they sit around the fire in the evening — they talk about me, but nobody thinks — of me!

This is the new stillness I have learned: their noise around me spreads a cloak over my thoughts.

They make noise among themselves: “What does this dark cloud want with us? Let us make sure it does not bring us a plague!”

And recently a woman snatched back her child, who wanted to come to me: “Take the children away!” she screamed; “eyes like that scorch children’s souls.”

They cough when I speak: they think coughing is an objection to strong winds — they guess nothing of the roaring of my happiness!

“We have no time for Zarathustra yet” — so they object; but what does a time matter that “has no time” for Zarathustra?

And even when they praise me: how could I fall asleep on their glory? Their praise is a belt of thorns to me: it still scratches me when I take it off.

And this too I learned among them: the one who praises acts as though he were giving something back, but in truth he wants to be given more!

Ask my foot whether it likes their lauding and luring tune! Truly, to such a beat and tick-tock it wants neither to dance nor to stand still.

They would like to lure and laud me into small virtue; they would like to coax my foot into the tick-tock of small happiness.

I walk among this people and keep my eyes open: they have become smaller, and are getting smaller and smaller: — and it is their teaching about happiness and virtue that does it.

For they are modest even in virtue — because they want comfort. And only modest virtue gets along with comfort.

They too learn, in their way, to stride and to stride forward: that is what I call their hobbling —. With it they become a stumbling block to anyone in a hurry.

And many of them walk forward and look back while they do it, with a stiffened neck: I like to run into those.

Foot and eye should not lie, nor give each other the lie. But there is much lying among the small people.

Some of them will, but most of them are only willed. Some of them are genuine, but most of them are bad actors.

There are actors among them without knowing it, and actors without wanting it —, the genuine ones are always rare, especially the genuine actors.

There is little of the man here: that is why their women make themselves manly. For only one who is man enough will — redeem the woman in woman.

And this hypocrisy I found the worst among them: that even those who command feign the virtues of those who serve.

“I serve, you serve, we serve” — so prays even the hypocrisy of the rulers here — and woe if the first lord is only the first servant!

Ah, my eye’s curiosity strayed even into their hypocrisies; and I guessed well all their fly-happiness and their buzzing around sunlit windowpanes.

So much kindness, so much weakness I see. So much justice and pity, so much weakness.

Round, fair, and kind they are with one another, as grains of sand are round, fair, and kind with grains of sand.

To embrace a small happiness modestly — that they call “submission”! and meanwhile they are already squinting modestly after a new small happiness.

At bottom, in their simplicity, they want one thing most of all: that nobody hurt them. So they get in first with everyone and do them good.

But this is cowardice: even if it is called “virtue.” —

And when for once they speak roughly, these small people: I hear in it only their hoarseness — for every draft makes them hoarse.

Shrewd they are; their virtues have shrewd fingers. But they lack fists: their fingers do not know how to creep behind fists.

Virtue for them is what makes modest and tame: with it they have made the wolf into a dog, and man himself into man’s best domestic animal.

“We have set our chair in the middle” — so their smirk tells me — “just as far from dying gladiators as from contented sows.”

But this is — mediocrity: even if it is called moderation. —

III

I walk among this people and let many a word fall: but they know neither how to take nor how to keep.

They are surprised that I did not come to rail against lusts and vices; and truly, I did not come to warn against pickpockets either!

They are surprised that I am not ready to smarten and sharpen their cleverness still more: as if they did not already have enough know-it-alls, whose voices squeak at me like slate pencils!

And when I cry: “Curse all the cowardly devils in you that like to whimper and fold their hands and worship”: then they cry: “Zarathustra is godless.”

And their teachers of submission cry it most of all —; but it is precisely into their ears that I love to shout: Yes! I am Zarathustra, the godless!

These teachers of submission! Wherever things are small and sick and scabby, they crawl, like lice; and only my disgust keeps me from cracking them.

Well then! This is my sermon for their ears: I am Zarathustra the godless, who says: “Who is more godless than I, that I may delight in his instruction?”

I am Zarathustra the godless: where do I find my equals? And all those are my equals who give themselves their own will and throw off all submission.

I am Zarathustra the godless: I cook every accident in my own pot. And only when it is cooked through there do I welcome it, as my food.

And truly, many an accident came to me like a master: but my will spoke to it more masterfully still — and at once it lay on its knees, begging —

— begging that it might find shelter and a heart with me, and coaxing me with flattery: “Just look, oh Zarathustra, how only a friend comes to a friend!” —

But why do I speak, where no one has my ears! And so I will shout it out into all the winds:

You are getting smaller and smaller, you small people! You are crumbling away, you comfortable ones! You will perish yet —

— of your many small virtues, of your many small omissions, of your many small submissions!

Too sparing, too yielding: that is your soil! But for a tree to become great, it wants to strike hard roots around hard rocks!

What you leave undone, too, weaves at the web of all mankind’s future; even your nothing is a spiderweb, and a spider that lives on the blood of the future.

And when you take, it is like stealing, you small virtuous ones; but even among rogues honor says: “One should steal only where one cannot rob.”

“It will all work itself out” — that too is a teaching of submission. But I tell you, you comfortable ones: it helps itself, and will help itself to more and more of you!

Ah, if only you would throw off all half willing, and become resolute in sloth as in action!

Ah, if only you understood my word: “Go ahead and do whatever you will — but first be such as are able to will!”

“Go ahead and love your neighbor as yourselves — but first, I tell you, be such as love themselves —

— who love with the great love, who love with the great contempt!” Thus speaks Zarathustra the godless. —

But why do I speak, where no one has my ears! It is still an hour too early for me here.

I am my own forerunner among this people, my own cockcrow through dark alleys.

But their hour is coming! And mine is coming too! Hour by hour they become smaller, poorer, more barren — poor weeds! poor soil!

And soon they shall stand before me like dry grass and steppe, and truly! weary of themselves — and thirsting more for fire than for water!

Oh blessed hour of the lightning! Oh mystery before noon! — One day I will yet make running fires of them, and heralds with tongues of flame: —

— one day they shall yet proclaim with tongues of flame: It is coming, it is near, the great noon!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Mount of Olives

Winter, a bad guest, is sitting at home with me; my hands are blue from the handshake of his friendship.

I honor him, this bad guest, but I like to leave him sitting alone. I like to run away from him; and if one runs well, one gets away from him!

With warm feet and warm thoughts I run to where the wind stands still — to the sunny corner of my Mount of Olives.

There I laugh at my stern guest and am still fond of him, because at home he catches the flies for me and silences much small noise.

For he will not put up with a mosquito wanting to sing, let alone two; he makes even the street so lonely that the moonlight is afraid in it at night.

He is a hard guest — but I honor him, and I do not pray, like the pampered ones, to the potbellied fire-idol.

Better to chatter one’s teeth a little than to worship idols! — that is how my nature wants it. And I bear a special grudge against all fire-idols, the ardent, the steaming, the stuffy.

Whom I love, I love better in winter than in summer; I mock my enemies better and more heartily now that winter sits in my house.

Heartily, truly, even when I crawl into bed —: even then my huddled happiness laughs and makes mischief; even my lying dream laughs.

I — a crawler? Never in my life have I crawled before the mighty; and if I ever lied, I lied out of love. That is why I am glad even in my winter bed.

A humble bed warms me more than a rich one, for I am jealous of my poverty. And in winter it is most faithful to me.

I begin each day with a piece of malice: I mock the winter with a cold bath: and at that my stern housemate grumbles.

I also like to tickle him with a little wax candle: so that at last he lets the sky out of the ash-gray dusk for me.

For I am especially malicious in the morning: at the early hour, when the bucket rattles at the well and the horses whinny warmly through gray streets: —

Impatiently I wait then for the bright sky to rise for me at last, the snow-bearded winter sky, the old man and white-head, —

— the winter sky, the silent one, which often keeps silent even about its sun!

Did I learn the long bright silence from him? Or did he learn it from me? Or did each of us invent it himself?

The origin of all good things is thousandfold — all good, mischievous things leap into existence for joy: how could they ever do that only — once!

A good, mischievous thing is the long silence too, and to look out, like the winter sky, from a bright, round-eyed face: —

— like it, to keep silent about one’s sun and one’s unbending sun-will: truly, this art and this winter mischief I learned well!

It is my favorite malice and art that my silence has learned not to betray itself by being silent.

Rattling words and dice, I outwit the solemn watchers: my will and purpose shall slip away from all these stern spies.

So that no one may see down into my ground and my last will — for that I invented the long bright silence.

Many a clever one I found who veiled his face and muddied his water, so that no one might see through him and down into him.

But it was to him of all people that the cleverer distrusters and nutcrackers came: it was from him of all people that they fished out his most hidden fish!

No, the clear, the brave, the transparent ones — those are to me the cleverest of the silent: their ground is so deep that even the clearest water does not — betray it. —

You snow-bearded, silent winter sky, you round-eyed white-head above me! Oh you heavenly parable of my soul and its mischief!

And must I not hide, like one who has swallowed gold — so that they do not slit my soul open?

Must I not wear stilts, so that they overlook my long legs — all these envy-mongers and misery-mongers around me?

These smoky, stove-warmed, worn-out, mildewed, moping souls — how could their envy bear my happiness!

So I show them only the ice and the winter on my peaks — and not that my mountain still winds all the belts of the sun around itself!

They hear only my winter storms whistling: and not that I also sail over warm seas, like longing, heavy, hot south winds.

They even take mercy on my mishaps and accidents: — but my word is: “Let accident come to me: it is innocent, like a little child!”

How could they bear my happiness, if I did not wrap mishaps and winter hardships and polar-bear caps and cloaks of snowy sky around my happiness!

— if I did not myself take mercy on their pity — the pity of these envy-mongers and misery-mongers!

— if I did not myself sigh and shiver with frost in front of them and patiently let myself be wrapped in their pity!

This is the wise mischief and goodwill of my soul, that it does not hide its winter and its frost storms; nor does it hide its chilblains.

One man’s solitude is the flight of the sick; another’s solitude is the flight from the sick.

Let them hear me chattering and sighing from the winter cold, all these poor squint-eyed scoundrels around me! With such sighing and chattering I still flee from their heated rooms.

Let them pity me and sigh their pity over my chilblains: “He will freeze to death on us yet, on the ice of knowledge!” — so they lament.

Meanwhile I run with warm feet this way and that on my Mount of Olives: in the sunny corner of my Mount of Olives I sing and mock at all pity. —

Thus sang Zarathustra.

On Passing By

So, walking slowly through many peoples and many kinds of cities, Zarathustra went back by roundabout ways to his mountains and his cave. And look, on the way he came unexpectedly to the city gate of the great city: but here a foaming fool sprang at him with outspread hands and blocked his way. This was the same fool whom the people called “Zarathustra’s ape”: for he had picked up from him something of the phrasing and cadence of his speech, and liked, too, to borrow from the treasure of his wisdom. And the fool spoke to Zarathustra like this:

“Oh Zarathustra, here is the great city: here you have nothing to look for and everything to lose.

Why would you wade through this mud? Have pity on your foot! Spit on the city gate instead and — turn back!

Here is hell for hermits’ thoughts: here great thoughts are boiled alive and cooked small.

Here all great feelings rot: here only rattle-thin little feelings may rattle!

Do you not already smell the slaughterhouses and cookshops of the spirit? Is this city not steaming with the fumes of slaughtered spirit?

Do you not see the souls hanging like limp, dirty rags? — And they even make newspapers out of these rags!

Do you not hear how the spirit has turned into wordplay here? It vomits up a disgusting word-swill! — And they even make newspapers out of this word-swill.

They harry one another and do not know where to? They heat one another up and do not know why? They jingle their tin, they clink their gold.

They are cold and seek warmth from burnt spirits; they are overheated and seek coolness from frozen spirits; they are all sick and addicted to public opinions.

All lusts and vices are at home here; but there are virtuous people here too, there is much employable, employed virtue: —

Much employable virtue with writing fingers and hard sitting-and-waiting flesh, blessed with little stars on the breast and padded, rumpless daughters.

There is also much piety here, and much devout spittle-licking and flattery-baking before the God of Hosts.

For “from on high” drips the star and the gracious spittle; toward on high yearns every starless bosom.

The moon has its court, and the court has its mooncalves: but to everything that comes from the court the beggar-people pray, and all employable beggar-virtue.

“I serve, you serve, we serve” — so all employable virtue prays up to the prince: so that the deserved star may at last be pinned on the narrow bosom!

But the moon still revolves around everything earthly: so the prince too still revolves around the most earthly thing of all —: and that is the shopkeepers’ gold.

The God of Hosts is no god of gold bars; the prince proposes, but the shopkeeper — disposes!

By all that is bright and strong and good in you, oh Zarathustra! Spit on this city of shopkeepers and turn back!

Here all blood flows rotten and lukewarm and frothy through all the veins: spit on the great city, which is the great rubbish heap where all the scum comes foaming together!

Spit on the city of dented souls and narrow chests, of sharp eyes, of sticky fingers —

— on the city of the pushy, the shameless, the scribblers and screamers, the overheated overambitious: —

— where everything rotten and rank, leering and lowering, overripe, ulcerous, and plotting festers together: —

— spit on the great city and turn back!” —

But here Zarathustra interrupted the foaming fool and put his hand over his mouth.

“Stop it at last!” cried Zarathustra. “Your speech and your manner have disgusted me for a long time!

Why did you live so long by the swamp that you had to become a frog and a toad yourself?

Does a rotten, frothy swamp-blood not flow now through your own veins, that you have learned to croak and blaspheme like this?

Why did you not go into the forest? Or plow the earth? Is the sea not full of green islands?

I despise your despising; and if you warned me — why did you not warn yourself?

Out of love alone shall my despising and my warning bird fly up for me: but not out of the swamp! —

They call you my ape, you foaming fool: but I call you my grunting pig — with your grunting you are spoiling even my praise of folly.

What was it, then, that first made you grunt? That no one had flattered you enough: — so you sat yourself down beside this filth, so that you would have grounds to grunt a great deal, —

— so that you would have grounds for a great deal of revenge! For revenge, you vain fool, is all your foaming; I have guessed you well!

But your fool’s word does me harm, even where you are right! And even if Zarathustra’s word were right a hundred times over: with my word you would always — do wrong!”

Thus spoke Zarathustra; and he looked at the great city, sighed, and was silent for a long time. At last he spoke like this:

I am disgusted by this great city too, and not only by this fool. Here and there, there is nothing to better, nothing to worsen.

Woe to this great city! — And I wish I could already see the pillar of fire in which it is burned!

For such pillars of fire must go before the great noon. But this has its time and its own fate. —

But this teaching I give you, you fool, in parting: where one can no longer love, there one should — pass by! —

Thus spoke Zarathustra, and he passed by the fool and the great city.

On the Apostates

I

Ah, does everything already lie withered and gray that only lately stood green and bright on this meadow? And how much honey of hope I carried from here into my beehives!

These young hearts have all grown old already — and not even old! only tired, common, comfortable: — they call it “We have become pious again.”

Only lately I saw them running out in the early morning on brave feet: but their feet of knowledge grew tired, and now they even slander their morning bravery!

Truly, many of them once lifted their legs like a dancer; the laughter in my wisdom beckoned to them: — then they thought better of it. Just now I saw one of them bent over — crawling to the cross.

Once they fluttered around light and freedom like gnats and young poets. A little older, a little colder: and already they are dark-mongers and whisper-mongers and stove-squatters.

Did their hearts lose courage, perhaps, because solitude swallowed me like a whale? Did their ears listen for me long and longingly, in vain, for my trumpet calls and herald calls?

— Ah! There are always only a few whose hearts have a long courage and high spirits; and in such as these the spirit, too, stays patient. But the rest are cowardly.

The rest: that is always by far the most of them, the everyday, the superfluous, the many-too-many — all of these are cowardly! —

Whoever is of my kind will also meet the experiences of my kind along the way: so that his first companions must be corpses and jesters.

But his second companions — they will call themselves his believers: a living swarm, much love, much folly, much beardless veneration.

Whoever is of my kind among men should not bind his heart to these believers; whoever knows the fleeting, cowardly human kind should not believe in these springtimes and bright meadows!

If they could be otherwise, they would also will otherwise. Half-and-halfers spoil every whole. That leaves wither — what is there to lament in that!

Let them go and fall, oh Zarathustra, and do not lament! Better still, blow among them with rustling winds, —

— blow among these leaves, oh Zarathustra: so that everything withered runs away from you all the faster! —

II

“We have become pious again” — so these apostates confess; and some of them are still too cowardly to confess even that.

I look them in the eye — I say it to their faces and to the red in their cheeks: you are the kind who pray again!

But it is a disgrace to pray! Not for everyone, but for you and me and whoever has his conscience in his head as well. For you it is a disgrace to pray!

You know it well: the cowardly devil in you, who would like to fold his hands and lay his hands in his lap and have an easier time of it: — this cowardly devil talks you into it: “There is a God!”

With that, though, you belong to the light-shy kind, whom light never leaves in peace; now you must stick your head deeper into night and fog every day!

And truly, you chose your hour well: for the night birds are flying out again just now. The hour has come for all the light-shy people, the hour of evening and of rest, when they do not — “rest.”

I hear it and smell it: their hour has come for the hunt and the procession — not a wild hunt, to be sure, but a tame, lame, sniffing hunt of soft-treaders and soft-prayers, —

— a hunt for soulful, mousy cowerers: all the mousetraps of the heart are set up again! And wherever I lift a curtain, a little night moth comes tumbling out.

Was it perhaps crouching there together with another little night moth? For everywhere I smell small, tucked-away congregations; and wherever there are little prayer closets, there are new prayer-brothers inside them, and the stale air of prayer-brothers.

They sit together for long evenings and say: let us become like little children again and say “dear God”! — their mouths and stomachs ruined by the pious confectioners.

Or they spend long evenings watching a cunning, lurking cross-spider, which preaches shrewdness to the spiders themselves and teaches like this: “Under crosses, the spinning is good!”

Or they sit all day with fishing rods beside swamps and think that makes them deep; but whoever fishes where there are no fish, I would not even call shallow!

Or, pious and pleased, they learn to strum the harp from a songwriter who would like to harp his way into the hearts of young ladies: — for he has grown tired of the old ladies and their praises.

Or they learn to shudder from a learned half-madman who waits in darkened rooms for the spirits to come to him — and the spirit runs off altogether!

Or they listen to an old, restless purr-and-growl piper, who learned the gloom of sounds from gloomy winds; now he pipes as the wind blows and preaches gloom in gloomy tones.

And some of them have even become night watchmen: they now know how to blow horns and go around at night and wake up old things that fell asleep long ago.

Five words about old things I heard last night at the garden wall: they came from such old, gloomy, dried-up night watchmen.

“For a father, he doesn’t care enough about his children: human fathers do it better!” —

“He’s too old! He doesn’t care about his children at all anymore” — so the other night watchman answered.

“Has he got any children? Nobody can prove it, if he doesn’t prove it himself! I’ve wanted him to prove it properly for ages.”

“Prove it? As if he had ever proved anything! Proving comes hard to him; he sets great store by people believing him.”

“Yes! Yes! Faith saves him, faith in him. That’s the way of old people! It’s the same with us!” —

— Thus spoke the two old night watchmen and light-scarecrows to each other, and then tooted mournfully on their horns: so it happened last night at the garden wall.

But my heart writhed with laughter and wanted to break and did not know where to go? and sank into my diaphragm.

Truly, this will yet be my death, that I choke with laughter when I see donkeys drunk and hear night watchmen doubting God like this.

Is the time for all such doubts not long past? Who may still wake up such old, sleepy, light-shy things!

The old gods came to their end long ago, after all: — and truly, they had a good, merry end for gods!

They did not “twilight” themselves to death — that is a lie people tell! On the contrary: they once — laughed themselves to death!

That happened when the most godless word came from a god himself — the word: “There is One God! You shall have no other god besides me!” —

— an old grim-beard of a god, a jealous one, forgot himself like that: —

And all the gods laughed then, and rocked on their chairs, and cried: “Is this not precisely godliness, that there are gods, but no God?”

Whoever has ears, let him hear. —

Zarathustra spoke like this in the town he loved, which is called: the Motley Cow. For from here he had only two more days to go before he would be back in his cave and with his animals; and his soul rejoiced all the while at the nearness of his homecoming. —

The Homecoming

Oh solitude! You, my home, solitude! Too long have I lived wild in wild foreign lands to come home to you without tears!

Now just wag your finger at me, as mothers do; no, smile at me, as mothers smile; now just say: “And who was it that once stormed away from me like a storm wind? —

— who cried as he left: too long have I sat with solitude, and there I unlearned silence! That — I suppose you have learned it now?

Oh Zarathustra, I know everything: and that among the many you were more forsaken, you single one, than you ever were with me!

Forsakenness is one thing, solitude another: that — you have learned now! And that among men you will always be wild and strange:

— wild and strange even when they love you: for above all else they want to be spared!

But here you are at home in your own house; here you can speak everything out and pour out all your reasons; nothing here is ashamed of hidden, hardened feelings.

Here all things come caressing to your speech and flatter you: for they want to ride on your back. On every parable you ride here to every truth.

Upright and forthright you may speak here to all things: and truly, it sounds like praise in their ears, that someone speaks to all things — straight!

But being forsaken is another thing. For do you still remember, oh Zarathustra? That time your bird screamed above you, when you stood in the forest, undecided, where to go? not knowing, near a corpse: —

— when you said: May my animals lead me! I found it more dangerous among men than among animals: — That was forsakenness!

And do you still remember, oh Zarathustra? When you sat on your island, a well of wine among empty buckets, giving and giving away, bestowing and pouring out among the thirsty:

— until at last you sat alone and thirsty among the drunk and lamented at night: ‘Is taking not more blessed than giving? And stealing more blessed still than taking?’ — That was forsakenness!

And do you still remember, oh Zarathustra? When your stillest hour came and drove you away from yourself, when it said with an evil whisper: ‘Speak and break!’ —

— when it made you sick of all your waiting and silence and discouraged your humble courage: That was forsakenness!” —

Oh solitude! You, my home, solitude! How blissfully and tenderly your voice speaks to me!

We do not question each other, we do not complain to each other, we walk openly together through open doors.

For with you everything is open and bright; and even the hours run here on lighter feet. For in the dark, time weighs more heavily on us than in the light.

Here the words and word-shrines of all being spring open for me: here all being wants to become word, here all becoming wants to learn from me how to speak.

But down there — down there all speech is in vain! There, forgetting and passing by are the best wisdom: that — I have learned now!

Whoever wanted to grasp everything about people would have to handle everything. But my hands are too clean for that.

I do not even like to breathe in their breath; ah, that I lived so long among their noise and bad breath!

Oh blissful stillness around me! Oh pure scents around me! Oh, how this stillness draws pure breath from a deep chest! Oh, how it listens, this blissful stillness!

But down there — there everything talks, there everything goes unheard. You may ring in your wisdom with bells: the shopkeepers in the marketplace will out-jingle it with pennies!

Everything among them talks; no one knows how to understand anymore. Everything falls into the water; nothing falls into deep wells anymore.

Everything among them talks; nothing turns out well and comes to an end anymore. Everything cackles, but who still wants to sit quietly on the nest and hatch eggs?

Everything among them talks; everything is talked to pieces. And what yesterday was still too hard for time itself and its tooth: today it hangs, scraped and gnawed, out of the mouths of the people of today.

Everything among them talks; everything is betrayed. And what was once called the secret and the secrecy of deep souls belongs today to the street trumpeters and other butterflies.

Oh humankind, you strange thing! You noise in dark alleys! Now you lie behind me again: — my greatest danger lies behind me!

My greatest danger always lay in sparing and pitying; and all humankind wants to be spared and suffered.

With truths held back, with a fool’s hand and a fool-fond heart, and rich in the little lies of pity: — that is how I always lived among men.

I sat among them in disguise, ready to mistake myself so that I could bear them, and gladly talking myself into it: “You fool, you do not know people!”

One unlearns people when one lives among people: there is too much foreground in all people — what use are far-seeing, far-craving eyes there!

And when they mistook me: I, the fool, spared them for it more than myself: used to being hard on myself, and often even taking revenge on myself for this sparing.

Stung all over by poisonous flies and hollowed out, like a stone, by many drops of malice: that is how I sat among them, and still talked myself into it: “Everything small is innocent of its smallness!”

Especially those who call themselves “the good” I found to be the most poisonous flies: they sting in all innocence, they lie in all innocence; how could they manage — to be just to me!

Whoever lives among the good is taught by pity to lie. Pity makes the air stuffy for all free souls. For the stupidity of the good is unfathomable.

To hide myself and my riches — that is what I learned down there: for I found everyone still poor in spirit. That was the lie of my pity, that with everyone I knew,

— that with everyone I saw and smelled what was enough spirit for him and what was already too much spirit for him!

Their stiff wise men: I called them wise, not stiff — so I learned to swallow words. Their gravediggers: I called them researchers and examiners — so I learned to swap words.

The gravediggers dig themselves into diseases. Foul vapors rest under old rubble. One should not stir up the swamp. One should live on mountains.

With blissful nostrils I breathe mountain freedom again! Redeemed at last is my nose from the smell of all humankind!

Tickled by sharp breezes, as if by sparkling wines, my soul sneezes — sneezes and cheers itself: Gesundheit!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Three Evils

I

In a dream, in my last morning dream, I stood today on a headland — beyond the world; I held a pair of scales and weighed the world.

Oh, that the dawn came to me too early: she glowed me awake, the jealous one! She is always jealous of the glowing of my morning dreams.

Measurable for one who has time, weighable for a good weigher, reachable in flight for strong wings, guessable for divine nutcrackers: that is how my dream found the world: —

My dream, a bold sailor, half ship, half squall, silent as butterflies, impatient as falcons: how did it have the patience and the leisure today for weighing the world!

Did my wisdom perhaps speak to it in secret, my laughing, wide-awake daytime wisdom, which mocks all “infinite worlds”? For it says: “Where there is force, number becomes the mistress too: she has more force.”

How surely my dream looked upon this finite world, not greedy for the new, not greedy for the old, not fearing, not pleading: —

— as if a full apple offered itself to my hand, a ripe golden apple with cool, soft, velvety skin: — that is how the world offered itself to me: —

— as if a tree beckoned to me, a broad-branched, strong-willed tree, bent into a backrest and a footrest for the one weary of the road: that is how the world stood on my headland: —

— as if delicate hands carried a shrine toward me — a shrine open for the delight of bashful, reverent eyes: that is how the world offered itself to me today: —

— not riddle enough to scare human love away, not solution enough to put human wisdom to sleep: — a humanly good thing the world was to me today, the world of which so much evil is said!

How I thank my morning dream that I weighed the world like this, early today! As a humanly good thing it came to me, this dream and comforter of the heart!

And so that I may do as it did by day, and copy and learn its best from it: I will now put the three most evil things on the scales and weigh them well, humanly. —

Whoever taught blessing also taught cursing: which are the three best-cursed things in the world? These I will put on the scales.

Sensual pleasure, lust for power, selfishness: these three have so far been cursed the best and bad-mouthed and lie-mouthed the worst — these three I will weigh well, humanly.

Come on, then! Here is my headland, and there is the sea: it rolls toward me, shaggy, fawning, the faithful old hundred-headed dog-monster that I love.

Come on, then! Here I will hold the scales over the rolling sea: and I choose a witness too, to watch — you, you hermit tree, you strong-scented, broad-vaulted tree that I love! —

On what bridge does the Now cross over to the Someday? By what compulsion does the high compel itself down to the low? And what bids even the highest still — to grow upward? —

Now the scales stand level and still: three heavy questions I threw in, three heavy answers the other pan carries.

II

Sensual pleasure: to all hair-shirted despisers of the body, their thorn and stake, and cursed as “the world” by all believers in worlds behind: for it mocks and fools all muddlers and misleaders.

Sensual pleasure: to the rabble, the slow fire on which it is burned; to all wormy wood, to all stinking rags, the furnace ready to rut and reek.

Sensual pleasure: to free hearts, innocent and free, the garden happiness of the earth, the overflowing thanks of all the future to the Now.

Sensual pleasure: only to the withered a sweetish poison, but to the lion-willed the great cordial for the heart, and the wine of wines, reverently saved.

Sensual pleasure: the great parable-happiness for a higher happiness and the highest hope. For to much is marriage promised, and more than marriage, —

— to much that is more foreign to itself than man and woman: — and who has fully grasped how foreign man and woman are to each other!

Sensual pleasure: — but I want fences around my thoughts and around my words as well: so that the swine and the swooners do not break into my gardens! —

Lust for power: the red-hot scourge of the hardest of the hard-hearted; the gruesome torture that saves itself up for the cruelest himself; the somber flame of living pyres.

Lust for power: the spiteful gadfly set upon the vainest peoples; the mocker of all uncertain virtue; the rider on every horse and every pride.

Lust for power: the earthquake that breaks and breaks open everything rotten and hollow; the rolling, rumbling, punishing breaker of whitewashed tombs; the flashing question mark beside premature answers.

Lust for power: before whose gaze man crawls and cringes and slaves and becomes lower than serpent and swine: — until at last the great contempt cries out of him —,

Lust for power: the terrible teacher of the great contempt, who preaches “away with you!” to the very faces of cities and empires — until it cries out of them themselves, “away with me!”

Lust for power: which climbs alluringly, though, even to the pure and the solitary, and up to self-sufficient heights, glowing like a love that paints purple blisses alluringly on earthly skies.

Lust for power: but who would call it a lust, when what is high longs downward for power! Truly, there is nothing sick and nothing craving in such longing and descending!

That the solitary height may not isolate itself forever and be content with itself; that the mountain may come to the valley and the winds of the heights to the lowlands: —

Oh, who could find the right christening name and virtue name for such a longing! “Gift-giving virtue” — that is what Zarathustra once named the unnameable.

And it was then too that it happened — and truly, it happened for the first time! — that his word called selfishness blessed, the whole, healthy selfishness that wells up from a mighty soul: —

— from a mighty soul, to which belongs the high body, the beautiful, victorious, refreshing body, around which every single thing becomes a mirror:

— the supple, persuasive body, the dancer, whose parable and essence is the soul that delights in itself. The self-delight of such bodies and souls calls itself: “virtue.”

With its words of good and bad, such self-delight shields itself as if with sacred groves; with the names of its happiness it banishes from itself everything contemptible.

It banishes from itself everything cowardly; it says: Bad — that is cowardly! Contemptible in its eyes is the one who always worries, sighs, whimpers, and whoever picks up even the smallest advantages.

It also despises all woe-wallowing wisdom: for truly, there is also wisdom that blooms in the dark, a nightshade wisdom, which always sighs: “All is vanity!”

Shy mistrust counts for little with it, and so does everyone who wants oaths instead of looks and hands: and all all-too-mistrustful wisdom as well — for that is the way of cowardly souls.

Still less does it count the quick-to-please, the doglike one, who lies down on his back at once, the humble one; and there is wisdom too that is humble and doglike and pious and quick-to-please.

Hateful to it, even disgusting, is the one who never wants to defend himself, who swallows down poisonous spit and evil looks, the all-too-patient one, the all-enduring, the all-satisfied: for that is the serf’s kind.

Whether someone is servile before gods and divine kicks, or before men and stupid human opinions: it spits on every serf’s kind, this blessed selfishness!

Bad: that is what it calls everything that is bowed and stingily servile, unfree blinking eyes, pressed-down hearts, and that false, yielding kind that kisses with broad, cowardly lips.

And sham wisdom: that is what it calls everything that serfs and graybeards and the weary make jokes about; and especially all the wicked, witless, over-witty foolery of priests!

But the sham-wise, all the priests, the world-weary, and whoever has a soul of the woman’s and the serf’s kind — oh, what foul play their game has always made with selfishness!

And precisely this was supposed to be virtue and to be called virtue, that one made foul play with selfishness! And “selfless” — that is what all these world-weary cowards and cross-spiders wished to be, and with good reason!

But for all of them the day is coming now, the change, the sword of judgment, the great noon: then many things shall be revealed!

And whoever calls the “I” whole and holy and selfishness blessed, truly, he also speaks what he knows, as one who foreknows: “Look, it is coming, it is near, the great noon!”

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On the Spirit of Gravity

I

My mouth — belongs to the people: I talk too coarsely and too heartily for the silky rabbits. And my word sounds stranger still to all ink-squids and pen-foxes.

My hand — is a fool’s hand: woe to all tables and walls, and whatever else still has room for fools’ doodles, fools’ daubs!

My foot — is a horse’s hoof; with it I trot and tramp over stick and stone, criss-cross across country, and I am devilish with delight at every fast run.

My stomach — is it an eagle’s stomach, perhaps? For it loves lamb best of all. But it is certainly a bird’s stomach.

Fed on innocent things, and on little, ready and impatient to fly, to fly away — that is my nature now: how could there not be something of the bird’s nature in it!

And above all, that I am the enemy of the spirit of gravity, that is the bird’s nature: and truly, deadly enemy, archenemy, primal enemy! Oh, where has my enmity not already flown and flown astray!

I could sing a song about that — and I will sing it: although I am alone in an empty house and must sing it to my own ears.

There are other singers, of course, whose throats are softened, whose hands grow eloquent, whose eyes grow expressive, whose hearts grow wakeful only in a full house: — those I do not resemble. —

II

Whoever one day teaches men to fly will have moved all the boundary stones; all the boundary stones themselves will fly into the air for him; he will baptize the earth anew — as “the Light One.”

The ostrich runs faster than the fastest horse, but even it still sticks its head heavily into the heavy earth: so it is with the man who cannot yet fly.

Earth and life are heavy to him; and that is how the spirit of gravity wants it! But whoever wants to become light, and a bird, must love himself: — that is what I teach.

Not, of course, with the love of the sick and the craving: for with them even self-love stinks!

One must learn to love oneself — that is what I teach — with a whole and healthy love: so that one can bear to be with oneself and does not go roaming about.

Such roaming about christens itself “love of the neighbor”: with this word the best lying and the best hypocrisy have been done so far, and especially by those who were heavy on everyone.

And truly, this is no commandment for today and tomorrow, to learn to love oneself. Rather, of all arts this is the finest, the most cunning, the last, and the most patient.

For to its owner, all that is his own is well hidden; and of all treasure pits, one’s own is dug up last — so the spirit of gravity arranges it.

Almost in the cradle we are handed heavy words and values: “good” and “evil” — that is what this dowry calls itself. For its sake we are forgiven for being alive.

And that is why the little children are allowed to come, so that they can be stopped in good time from loving themselves: so the spirit of gravity arranges it.

And we — we faithfully drag what we are handed, on hard shoulders and over rough mountains! And when we sweat, they tell us: “Yes, life is hard to bear!”

But only man is hard for himself to bear! The reason is that he drags too much that is foreign on his shoulders. Like the camel, he kneels down and lets himself be well loaded.

Especially the strong, load-bearing man in whom reverence dwells: he loads too many foreign heavy words and values onto himself — and now life seems to him a desert!

And truly! Much that is one’s own is hard to bear, too! And much that is inside man is like the oyster: that is, disgusting and slippery and hard to grasp —,

— so that a noble shell with noble ornament must plead for it. But this art too one must learn: to have a shell, and a fair appearance, and a prudent blindness!

Again, many things about man deceive us, because many a shell is poor and sad and too much a shell. Much hidden goodness and strength is never guessed; the most exquisite delicacies find no one to taste them!

Women know this, the most exquisite of them: a little fatter, a little thinner — oh, how much fate lies in so little!

Man is hard to discover, and hardest of all for himself; often the spirit lies about the soul. So the spirit of gravity arranges it.

But he has discovered himself who says: This is my good and evil: with that he has struck dumb the mole and dwarf who says, “Good for all, evil for all.”

Truly, I do not like those either for whom each and every thing is good and this world is even the best. Those I call the all-satisfied.

All-satisfaction, which knows how to taste everything: that is not the best taste! I honor the stubborn, choosy tongues and stomachs that have learned to say “I” and “Yes” and “No.”

But to chew and digest everything — that is the true swine’s way! Always to say Yeah-aw — only the donkey has learned that, and whoever is of his spirit! —

Deep yellow and hot red: that is what my taste wants — it mixes blood into all colors. But whoever whitewashes his house betrays to me a whitewashed soul.

Some are in love with mummies, the others with ghosts; and both alike are enemies of all flesh and blood — oh, how both go against my taste! For I love blood.

And I do not want to live and linger where everyone spits and spews: that is now my taste — I would sooner live among thieves and perjurers. No one carries gold in his mouth.

But still more repugnant to me are all lickspittles; and the most repugnant animal of a man that I found, I baptized parasite: it did not want to love, and yet wanted to live on love.

Wretched I call all those who have only one choice: to become evil beasts or evil beast-tamers: among such I would build myself no huts.

Wretched I call those too who must always wait — they go against my taste: all the tax collectors and shopkeepers and kings and other keepers of countries and counters.

Truly, I learned waiting too, and learned it thoroughly,

— but only waiting for myself. And above all I learned standing and walking and running and jumping and climbing and dancing.

But this is my teaching: whoever wants to learn to fly one day must first learn to stand and walk and run and climb and dance: — one does not fly one’s way into flying!

With rope ladders I learned to climb up to many a window, with nimble legs I clambered up high masts: to sit on high masts of knowledge seemed to me no small bliss, —

— to flicker like little flames on high masts: a small light, to be sure, but still a great comfort to storm-driven sailors and the shipwrecked! —

By many ways and in many manners I came to my truth; not by one ladder did I climb to the height where my eye roams into my distance.

And I only ever asked the way unwillingly — that always went against my taste! I would rather question and try out the ways themselves.

A trying and a questioning was all my going: — and truly, one must also learn to answer such questioning! But that — is my taste:

— not a good one, not a bad one, but my taste, which I no longer either hide or am ashamed of.

“This — is now my way — where is yours?” That is how I answered those who asked me “the way.” For the way — it does not exist!

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

On Old and New Tablets

I

Here I sit and wait, old broken tablets around me, and new tablets too, half written on. When will my hour come?

— the hour of my going down, my going under: for I want to go to mankind one more time.

That is what I am waiting for now: for first the signs must come to me that it is my hour, — namely, the laughing lion with the flock of doves.

In the meantime I talk to myself, as one who has time. No one tells me anything new: so I tell myself about myself. —

II

When I came to mankind, I found them sitting on an old conceit: all of them had long fancied that they knew what was good and evil for man.

All talk of virtue seemed to them an old, tired business; and whoever wanted to sleep well still talked about “good” and “evil” before going to bed.

I disturbed this sleepiness when I taught: what is good and evil, no one knows yet: — unless it is the creator!

— But he is the one who creates man’s goal and gives the earth its meaning and its future: only he creates it, that something is good and evil.

And I told them to overturn their old professors’ chairs, and wherever that old conceit had sat; I told them to laugh at their great masters of virtue and their saints and poets and world-redeemers.

I told them to laugh at their gloomy sages, and at whoever had ever sat on the tree of life like a black scarecrow, giving warnings.

I sat down beside their great avenue of tombs, and even among the carrion and the vultures — and I laughed at all their once-upon-a-time and its crumbling, decaying glory.

Truly, like preachers of repentance and fools, I raised a cry of wrath and outrage over all their great and small, — that their best is so very small! That their most evil is so very small! — so I laughed.

So my wise longing cried and laughed out of me, my longing that was born on mountains, a wild wisdom, truly! — my great longing with its roaring wings.

And often it swept me away, up and off and away, in the middle of my laughter: then I flew, shuddering, an arrow, through sun-drunk rapture:

— out into distant futures that no dream has seen yet, into hotter souths than any sculptor ever dreamed of: where gods, dancing, are ashamed of all clothes: —

— that I speak in parables, that is, and limp and stammer like the poets: and truly, I am ashamed that I must still be a poet! —

Where all becoming seemed to me the dancing of gods and the high spirits of gods, and the world set free and running wild and fleeing back to itself: —

— as an eternal fleeing from each other and seeking each other again of many gods, as the blissful contradicting of each other, hearing each other again, belonging to each other again, of many gods: —

Where all time seemed to me a blissful mockery of moments, where necessity was freedom itself, playing blissfully with the goad of freedom: —

Where I also found my old devil and archenemy again, the spirit of gravity, and everything he created: compulsion, statute, need and consequence and purpose and will and good and evil: —

For must there not be something that is danced over, danced away over? Must there not be — for the sake of the light ones, the lightest — moles and heavy dwarfs? —

III

It was there, too, that I picked up the word “overman” from the road, and that man is something that must be overcome,

— that man is a bridge and not a goal: counting himself blessed for his noon and his evening, as a way to new dawns:

— the Zarathustra-word of the great noon, and whatever else I hung up over man, like a purple second sunset.

Truly, I also let them see new stars, along with new nights; and over clouds and day and night I spread out laughter like a motley tent.

I taught them all my composing and striving: to compose into one and gather together what is fragment in man, and riddle and dreadful accident, —

— as a poet, a guesser of riddles, and the redeemer of accident, I taught them to work at creating the future, and to redeem, by creating, everything that was.

To redeem the past in man and to re-create every “It was,” until the will says: “But so I willed it! So shall I will it —”

— This I called redemption for them, this alone I taught them to call redemption. — —

Now I wait for my redemption, — to go to them for the last time.

For I want to go to mankind one more time: down among them I want to go under, and in dying I want to give them my richest gift!

I learned this from the sun when it goes down, the overrich one: then it pours gold into the sea out of its inexhaustible riches, —

— so that even the poorest fisherman rows with golden oars! For I saw this once, and as I watched I could not weep my fill of tears. —

Like the sun, Zarathustra too wants to go under: now he sits here and waits, old broken tablets around him, and new tablets too, — half written on.

IV

Look, here is a new tablet: but where are my brothers to carry it with me down into the valley and into hearts of flesh? —

So my great love of the farthest demands it: do not spare your neighbor! Man is something that must be overcome.

There are many ways and means of overcoming: see to that yourself! But only a jester thinks: “Man can also be leaped over.”

Overcome yourself even in your neighbor: and a right that you can seize for yourself, you shall not let anyone give you!

What you do, no one can do back to you. Look, there is no retribution.

Whoever cannot command himself shall obey. And many a man can command himself, but there is still much lacking before he also obeys himself!

V

This is what the kind of noble souls wants: they want to have nothing for free, least of all life.

Whoever is of the mob wants to live for free; but we others, to whom life gave itself, — we are always thinking about what we can best give in return!

And truly, it is a noble saying that says: “What life promises us, we want to keep — for life!”

One should not want to enjoy where one gives nothing to enjoy. And — one should not want to enjoy!

For enjoyment and innocence are the most bashful things of all: neither wants to be sought out. One should have them —, but one should rather seek out guilt and pain! —

VI

Oh my brothers, whoever is a firstborn is always sacrificed. And now we are firstborn.

We all bleed on secret altars of sacrifice, we all burn and roast in honor of old idols.

Our best is still young: that tempts old palates. Our flesh is tender, our hide is only a lambskin: — how could we not tempt old idol-priests!

In ourselves he still lives, the old idol-priest, who roasts our best for his feast. Ah, my brothers, how could the firstborn not be sacrifices!

But so our kind wills it; and I love those who do not want to preserve themselves. Those who go under I love with my whole love: for they go over. —

VII

To be true — few can do it! And whoever can still does not want to! But the ones who can do it least of all are the good.

Oh, these good ones! — Good people never speak the truth; for the spirit, to be good in this way is a sickness.

They give in, these good ones, they give themselves up, their heart repeats what it is told, their depths obey: but whoever obeys does not listen to himself!

Everything that the good call evil must come together for one truth to be born: oh my brothers, are you evil enough for this truth too?

The daring venture, the long mistrust, the cruel No, the weariness of it all, the cutting into living flesh — how seldom does that come together! But from such seed truth is begotten!

Alongside the bad conscience all knowledge has grown, until now! Break, break the old tablets, you seekers of knowledge!

VIII

When the water has planks over it, when footbridges and railings leap across the river: truly, then no one is believed who says: “Everything flows.”

Even the blockheads contradict him. “What?” say the blockheads, “everything flows? But the planks and railings are over the flowing water!”

“Over the flow everything is firm, all the values of things, the bridges, the concepts, all ‘good’ and ‘evil’: all of it is firm!” —

But when the hard winter comes, the tamer of the river-beast: then even the wittiest learn mistrust; and truly, then it is not only the blockheads who say: “Should not everything — stand still?”

“At bottom everything stands still” —, that is a proper winter teaching, a good thing for a barren time, a good comfort for hibernators and stove-squatters.

“At bottom everything stands still” —: but the thaw wind preaches against it!

The thaw wind, a bull that is no plowing bull, — a raging bull, a destroyer, who breaks the ice with angry horns! But ice — breaks footbridges!

Oh my brothers, now — is not everything flowing? Have not all the railings and footbridges fallen into the water? Who would still hold on to “good” and “evil”?

“Woe to us! Hail to us! The thaw wind is blowing!” — Preach this for me, oh my brothers, through all the streets!

IX

There is an old delusion, and it is called good and evil. Until now the wheel of this delusion has turned around soothsayers and astrologers.

Once, people believed in soothsayers and astrologers: and that is why they believed, “Everything is fate: you shall, because you must!”

Then again they distrusted all soothsayers and astrologers: and that is why they believed, “Everything is freedom: you can, because you will!”

Oh my brothers, about the stars and the future there has until now only been fancy, not knowledge: and that is why about good and evil there has until now only been fancy, not knowledge!

X

“You shall not rob! You shall not kill!” — such words were once called holy; before them people bowed their knees and their heads and took off their shoes.

But I ask you: where in the world have there ever been better robbers and killers than such holy words?

Is there not in all life itself — robbing and killing? And when such words were called holy, was not truth itself — killed by it?

Or was it a sermon of death, that called holy what contradicted all life and counseled against it? — Oh my brothers, break, break the old tablets!

XI

This is my pity for all that is past: that I see it is abandoned, —

— abandoned to the mercy, the spirit, the madness of every generation that comes and reinterprets everything that was as a bridge to itself!

A great despot could come, a cunning monster, who with his favor and his disfavor would force and squeeze all that is past, until it became a bridge for him, and an omen and a herald and a cockcrow.

But this is the other danger, and my other pity: — whoever is of the mob, his memory goes back as far as his grandfather, — but with his grandfather, time stops.

So all that is past is abandoned: for it could one day happen that the mob became master and drowned all time in shallow waters.

That is why, oh my brothers, a new nobility is needed, which is the adversary of all mob and all despotism and writes the word “noble” anew on new tablets.

For many noble ones are needed, and many kinds of noble ones, for there to be a nobility! Or, as I once said in a parable: “Precisely this is godliness, that there are gods, but no God!”

XII

Oh my brothers, I consecrate you and direct you to a new nobility: you shall become begetters and breeders and sowers of the future, —

— truly, not to a nobility that you could buy like shopkeepers, and with shopkeepers’ gold: for everything that has its price has little value.

From now on let your honor be not where you come from, but where you are going! Your will and your foot that wants to go out beyond yourselves, — let that be your new honor!

Truly, not that you have served a prince — what do princes matter anymore! — or became a bulwark for what stands, so that it would stand more firmly!

Not that your family became courtly at courts, and you learned to stand for long hours in shallow ponds, motley, like a flamingo.

— For being able to stand is a merit among courtiers; and all courtiers believe that the bliss after death includes — being allowed to sit! —

Nor that a spirit they call holy led your forefathers into promised lands, which I do not praise: for where the worst of all trees grew, the cross, — that land has nothing to praise! —

— and truly, wherever this “Holy Spirit” led its knights, on such crusades it was always goats and geese and cross-heads and cranks that ran ahead! —

Oh my brothers, your nobility shall not look backward, but outward! You shall be exiles from all fatherlands and forefatherlands!

You shall love your children’s land: let this love be your new nobility, — the undiscovered land, in the farthest sea! I tell your sails to search and search for it!

In your children you shall make up for being the children of your fathers: so you shall redeem all that is past! This new tablet I place over you!

XIII

“Why live? All is vanity! Living — that is threshing straw; living — that is burning oneself and still not getting warm.” —

Such antique chatter still counts as “wisdom”; but because it is old and smells musty, it is all the more honored. Even mold ennobles. —

Children may talk like that: they shy away from the fire because it burned them! There is much childishness in the old books of wisdom.

And whoever is always “threshing straw,” how should he be allowed to blaspheme against threshing! Such a fool ought to be muzzled!

People like that sit down at the table and bring nothing with them, not even a good hunger: — and then they blaspheme: “All is vanity!”

But eating and drinking well, oh my brothers, is truly no vain art! Break, break the tablets of the never-glad!

XIV
