“To the pure all things are pure” — so say the people. But I tell you: to swine all things turn swinish!

That is why the fanatics and the head-hangers, whose hearts hang down as well, preach: “The world itself is a filthy monster.”

For all of these have unclean spirits; but most of all those who have no rest and no peace unless they see the world from behind, — the believers in worlds behind!

To them I say it to their faces, even though it does not sound pretty: the world is like man in this, that it has a behind, — so much is true!

There is a lot of filth in the world: so much is true! But that does not make the world itself a filthy monster!

There is wisdom in this, that much in the world smells bad: disgust itself creates wings, and powers that can divine where springs are hidden!

Even in the best there is still something disgusting; and the best is still something that must be overcome! —

Oh my brothers, there is much wisdom in this, that there is much filth in the world! —

XV

I heard pious believers in worlds behind say such sayings to their consciences; and truly, without malice or falseness, — although there is nothing in the world more false, nor more malicious.

“Just let the world be the world! Do not lift so much as one finger against it!”

“Let anyone who wants to choke and stab and cut and scrape the people: do not lift so much as one finger against it! That way they will learn to renounce the world.”

“And your own reason — you yourself shall throttle and choke it; for it is a reason of this world, — that way you yourself will learn to renounce the world.” —

— Break, break, oh my brothers, these old tablets of the pious! Unsay the sayings of the world-slanderers!

XVI

“Whoever learns much unlearns all violent desire” — that is what people whisper to each other today in all the dark alleys.

“Wisdom makes you tired, nothing is — worth it; you shall not covet!” — I found this new tablet hanging even in the open marketplaces.

Break, oh my brothers, break this new tablet too! The world-weary hung it up, and the preachers of death, and the jailers too: for look, it is also a sermon for serfdom! —

Because they learned badly, and not the best, and everything too early and everything too fast: because they ate badly, that is where that ruined stomach of theirs came from, —

— for their spirit is a ruined stomach: it advises death! For truly, my brothers, the spirit is a stomach!

Life is a fountain of joy: but for the one out of whom the ruined stomach speaks, the father of gloom, all springs are poisoned.

To know: that is joy for the lion-willed! But whoever has grown weary is himself only “willed,” and all the waves play with him.

And that is always the way of weak people: they lose themselves on their ways. And in the end their weariness even asks: “Why did we ever go on ways at all! All is the same!”

To them it sounds sweet in their ears when someone preaches: “Nothing is worth it! You shall not will!” But that is a sermon for serfdom.

Oh my brothers, Zarathustra comes as a fresh, blustering wind to all who are weary of the way; he will make many noses sneeze yet!

My free breath blows even through walls, and into prisons and imprisoned spirits!

Willing liberates: for willing is creating: so I teach. And you shall learn only in order to create!

And even learning you shall first learn from me, learning well! — Whoever has ears, let him hear!

XVII

There lies the boat, — over there, perhaps, the way leads into the great nothing. — But who wants to step into this “Perhaps”?

Not one of you wants to step into the boat of death! Then how can you claim to be weary of the world!

Weary of the world! And you have not even been lifted away from the earth! I always found you still lusting after the earth, still in love with your own weariness of the earth!

Not for nothing does your lip hang down: — a little earthly wish still sits on it! And in your eye — is there not a little cloud of unforgotten earthly pleasure floating there?

There are many good inventions on earth, some useful, others pleasant: for their sake the earth is to be loved.

And there are some things so well invented that they are like a woman’s breast: useful and pleasant at the same time.

But you who are weary of the world! You who are lazy about the earth! You should be beaten with rods! With strokes of the rod your legs should be made lively again.

For: if you are not sick people and worn-out wretches of whom the earth is weary, then you are sly sloths or sweet-toothed, skulking pleasure-cats. And if you do not want to run merrily again, then you shall — pass away!

One should not want to be a physician to the incurable: so Zarathustra teaches: — so you shall pass away!

But it takes more courage to make an end than to make a new verse: all physicians and poets know that. —

XVIII

Oh my brothers, there are tablets that weariness created, and tablets that laziness created, the laziness that rots: though they speak alike, they want to be heard unalike. —

Look at this man here, wasting away! He is only a hand’s breadth from his goal, but out of weariness he has defiantly lain down here in the dust: this brave one!

Out of weariness he yawns at the way and the earth and the goal and himself: he will not take one step further, — this brave one!

Now the sun glares down on him, and the dogs lick at his sweat: but he lies there in his defiance and would rather waste away: —

— waste away a hand’s breadth from his goal! Truly, you will yet have to drag him by the hair into his heaven, — this hero!

Better still, leave him lying where he has lain down, so that sleep may come to him, the comforter, with cooling, rustling rain:

Let him lie until he wakes up of his own accord, — until of his own accord he takes back all his weariness and what weariness taught through him!

Only, my brothers, chase the dogs away from him, the lazy sneaks, and all that swarming vermin: —

— all that swarming vermin of the “cultured,” which — regales itself on the sweat of every hero! —

XIX

I draw circles around me and holy boundaries; fewer and fewer climb with me up higher and higher mountains, — I am building a mountain range out of holier and holier mountains. —

But wherever you may climb with me, oh my brothers: see to it that no parasite climbs with you!

A parasite: that is a crawling thing, a creeping, cringing thing, that wants to grow fat on your sick and sore corners.

And this is its art: it guesses where climbing souls are weary: in your grief and your gloom, in your tender shame, it builds its disgusting nest.

Where the strong are weak, where the noble are all too mild, — in there it builds its disgusting nest: the parasite lives where the great have small sore corners.

What is the highest kind of all that is, and what is the lowest? The parasite is the lowest kind; but whoever is of the highest kind feeds the most parasites.

For the soul that has the longest ladder and can go down the deepest: how could the most parasites not sit on that one? —

— the most comprehensive soul, which can run and wander and roam the farthest within itself; the most necessary soul, which out of joy flings itself into accident: —

— the soul that is, which dives into becoming; the soul that has, which wants to plunge into willing and desiring: —

— the soul that flees itself, which catches up with itself in the widest circle; the wisest soul, which folly talks to most sweetly: —

— the soul that loves itself most, in which all things have their streaming out and streaming back, their ebb and flood: — oh, how could the highest soul not have the worst parasites?

XX

Oh my brothers, am I cruel, then? But I say: what is falling, one should also push!

Everything of today — it is falling, it is decaying: who would want to hold it up! But I — I want to push it as well!

Do you know the delight that rolls stones down into steep depths? — These people of today: just look at them, how they roll down into my depths!

I am a prelude to better players, oh my brothers! A precedent! Do as I have done before you!

And whoever you do not teach to fly, teach him — to fall faster! —

XXI

I love the brave: but it is not enough to be a swashbuckler, — one must also know: look before you slash, and at whom!

And often there is more bravery in holding back and passing by: so that one saves oneself for a worthier enemy!

You shall have only enemies who are to be hated, but not enemies to be despised: you must be proud of your enemy: so I taught once before.

For the worthier enemy, oh my friends, you shall save yourselves: that is why you must pass many things by, —

— especially much of the rabble, which makes a din in your ears about the people and peoples.

Keep your eye clean of their for and against! There is much right there, and much wrong: whoever looks on grows angry.

To look in, to lash in — there it is all one: so go away into the forests and lay your sword to sleep!

Go your ways! And let the people and peoples go theirs! — dark ways, truly, on which not a single hope flickers anymore, not even like summer lightning!

Let the shopkeeper rule there, where everything that still glitters — is shopkeepers’ gold! It is no longer the time of kings: what calls itself a people today does not deserve kings.

Just look how these peoples themselves now act like shopkeepers: they pick even the smallest advantages out of every heap of rubbish!

They lie in wait for one another, they lie in wait to get something out of one another, — that is what they call “good neighborliness.” Oh, blessed, distant time, when a people said to itself: “I want to be — master over peoples!”

For, my brothers: the best shall rule, and the best also wants to rule! And where the teaching says otherwise, there — the best is lacking.

XXII

If they had — bread for free, woe! What would they cry out for! Their keep — that is what really keeps them busy; and they shall have it hard!

They are beasts of prey: in their “working” — there is still preying, in their “earning” — there is still outwitting! That is why they shall have it hard!

So they shall become better beasts of prey, finer, shrewder, more like man: for man is the best beast of prey.

Man has already robbed all the animals of their virtues: that is because, of all the animals, man has had it the hardest.

Only the birds are still above him. And if man were to learn to fly as well, woe! To what height — would his lust for prey fly!

XXIII

This is how I want man and woman: the one fit for war, the other fit for bearing children, but both fit for dancing with head and legs.

And let every day be lost to us on which we did not dance even once! And let every truth be called false to us that did not bring even one laugh!

XXIV

Your concluding of marriages: see to it that it is not a bad conclusion! You concluded too quickly: so there follows from it — the breaking of marriages!

And better the breaking of marriages than the bending of marriages, the lying of marriages! — A woman once said to me: “Yes, I broke my marriage, but first my marriage broke — me!”

I have always found the badly paired to be the worst of the vengeful ones: they make all the world pay for it that they can no longer run alone.

That is why I want honest people to say honestly to each other: “We love each other: let us see to it that we keep our love! Or shall our vow turn out to be an oversight?”

— “Give us a term and a little marriage, so that we may see whether we are fit for the great marriage! It is a great thing, always to be two!”

This is my advice to all honest people; and what would my love of the overman and of everything that is to come be worth, if I advised and spoke otherwise!

Not only to plant yourselves onward, but upward — for that, oh my brothers, may the garden of marriage help you!

XXV

Whoever has grown wise about old origins, look, he will in the end search for the springs of the future and for new origins. —

Oh my brothers, it will not be long before new peoples spring up and new springs rush down into new depths.

For the earthquake — it buries many wells, it makes many go thirsty: it also lifts inner powers and secret things into the light.

The earthquake reveals new springs. In the earthquake of old peoples, new springs break out.

And whoever calls out: “Look, here is a well for many who thirst, one heart for many who long, one will for many tools”: — around him a people gathers, that is: many who are trying.

Who can command, who must obey — that is what is tried there! Ah, with what long searching and guessing and missing and learning and trying again!

Human society: it is an experiment, so I teach, — a long search: but what it searches for is the one who commands! —

— an experiment, oh my brothers! And not a “contract”! Break, break this word of the soft-hearted and the half-and-halfs!

XXVI

Oh my brothers! With whom does the greatest danger to all mankind’s future lie? Is it not with the good and the just? —

— as with those who say, and feel in their hearts: “We already know what is good and just, and we have it too; woe to those who are still searching here!” —

And whatever harm the evil may do: the harm the good do is the most harmful harm!

And whatever harm the world-slanderers may do: the harm the good do is the most harmful harm.

Oh my brothers, someone once looked into the hearts of the good and the just and said: “They are the Pharisees.” But no one understood him.

The good and the just themselves were not allowed to understand him: their spirit is imprisoned in their good conscience. The stupidity of the good is unfathomably shrewd.

But this is the truth: the good must be Pharisees, — they have no choice!

The good must crucify the one who invents his own virtue! That is the truth!

But the second one to discover their country, the country, heart and soil of the good and the just: that was the one who asked: “Whom do they hate most?”

The creator is the one they hate most: the one who breaks tablets and old values, the breaker, — him they call the lawbreaker.

For the good — they cannot create: they are always the beginning of the end: —

— they crucify the one who writes new values on new tablets, they sacrifice the future to themselves, — they crucify all mankind’s future!

The good — they have always been the beginning of the end. —

XXVII

Oh my brothers, did you understand this word as well? And what I once said about the “last man”? —

With whom does the greatest danger to all mankind’s future lie? Is it not with the good and the just?

Break, break the good and the just! — Oh my brothers, did you understand this word as well?

XXVIII

You are running from me? You are frightened? You tremble at this word?

Oh my brothers, when I told you to break the good and the tablets of the good: only then did I put man out to sea, onto his high sea.

And only now does the great terror come over him, the great looking-around, the great sickness, the great disgust, the great seasickness.

The good taught you false coasts and false securities; you were born in the lies of the good and harbored in them. Everything has been lied crooked and bent out of true, right down to the bottom, by the good.

But whoever discovered the land “man” also discovered the land “mankind’s future.” Now you shall be seafarers for me, brave and patient ones!

Walk upright in good time, oh my brothers, learn to walk upright! The sea is storming: many want to pull themselves upright again by you.

The sea is storming: everything is in the sea. Come on! Up and away! You old sailors’ hearts!

Fatherland, indeed! There our helm wants to go, where our children’s land is! Out there, stormier than the sea, our great longing storms! —

XXIX

“Why so hard?” the kitchen coal once said to the diamond. “Are we not close relatives?” —

Why so soft? Oh my brothers, this is what I ask you: are you not — my brothers?

Why so soft, so yielding and giving way? Why is there so much denial and self-denial in your hearts? So little fate in your look?

And if you do not want to be fates and inexorable ones: how could you one day — conquer with me?

And if your hardness does not want to flash and cut and cut to pieces: how could you one day — create with me?

For the creators are hard. And it must seem bliss to you to press your hand on millennia as if on wax, —

— bliss to write on the will of millennia as if on bronze, — harder than bronze, nobler than bronze. Only the noblest is entirely hard.

This new tablet, oh my brothers, I place over you: Become hard! —

XXX

Oh you my will! You turning of all need, you my necessity! Preserve me from all small victories!

You dispensation of my soul, which I call destiny! You-in-me! Over-me! Preserve me and save me for one great destiny!

And your last greatness, my will, save it for your last, — so that you are inexorable in your victory! Ah, who has not been defeated by his own victory!

Ah, whose eye has not grown dark in this drunken twilight! Ah, whose foot has not staggered and in victory forgotten how — to stand! —

— That I may one day be ready and ripe at the great noon: ready and ripe like glowing bronze, like a cloud pregnant with lightning, like a swelling udder of milk: —

— ready for myself and for my most hidden will: a bow burning for its arrow, an arrow burning for its star: —

— a star, ready and ripe in its noon, glowing, pierced through, blissful before annihilating arrows of the sun: —

— a sun itself, and an inexorable sun-will, ready to annihilate in victory!

Oh will, turning of all need, you my necessity! Save me for one great victory! —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

The Convalescent

I

One morning, not long after his return to the cave, Zarathustra sprang up from his bed like a madman, cried out in a terrible voice, and acted as if someone else were still lying on the bed who would not get up; and Zarathustra’s voice rang out so loudly that his animals came running to him in fright, and out of all the caves and hiding places that neighbored Zarathustra’s cave, every creature scurried away — flying, fluttering, crawling, leaping, each according to the kind of foot or wing it had been given. But Zarathustra spoke these words:

Up, abysmal thought, out of my depths! I am your rooster and your morning twilight, you sleepy worm: up! up! My voice shall crow you awake!

Untie the fetters from your ears: listen! For I want to hear you! Up! Up! There is thunder enough here to teach even graves to listen!

And wipe the sleep and all the dullness and blindness from your eyes! Hear me with your eyes as well: my voice is a medicine even for those born blind.

And once you are awake, you shall stay awake for me forever. It is not my way to wake great-grandmothers from their sleep only to tell them — go back to sleep!

You stir, you stretch, you rattle in your throat? Up! Up! Not rattle — you shall speak to me! Zarathustra calls you, Zarathustra the godless!

I, Zarathustra, the advocate of life, the advocate of suffering, the advocate of the circle — I call you, my most abysmal thought!

Hail to me! You are coming — I hear you! My abyss speaks, I have turned my last depth inside out into the light!

Hail to me! Come here! Give me your hand — ha! let go! Haha! — disgust, disgust, disgust — woe is me!

II

But Zarathustra had hardly spoken these words when he fell down like a dead man and lay for a long time like a dead man. And when he came to himself again, he was pale and trembling, and he stayed lying down, and for a long time he would neither eat nor drink. This went on for seven days; but his animals did not leave him by day or by night, except that the eagle flew out to fetch food. And whatever it fetched and plundered, it laid on Zarathustra’s bed: so that at last Zarathustra lay among yellow and red berries, grapes, rose apples, sweet-smelling herbs, and pine cones. But at his feet lay two lambs, which the eagle had stolen with great effort from their shepherds.

At last, after seven days, Zarathustra sat up on his bed, took a rose apple in his hand, smelled it, and found its smell lovely. Then his animals believed that the time had come to speak with him.

“Oh Zarathustra,” they said, “you have been lying like this for seven days now, with heavy eyes: will you not stand on your feet again at last?

Step out of your cave: the world is waiting for you like a garden. The wind is playing with heavy fragrances that want to come to you; and all the brooks would like to run after you.

All things are longing for you, while you have stayed alone for seven days — step out of your cave! All things want to be your physicians!

Did some new knowledge come to you, perhaps, a sour and heavy one? You lay like leavened dough; your soul rose and swelled over all its rims. —”

— Oh my animals, answered Zarathustra, go on chattering like this and let me listen! It refreshes me so to hear you chatter: where there is chatter, the world already lies before me like a garden.

How lovely it is that there are words and sounds: are not words and sounds rainbows and illusory bridges between things eternally apart?

To every soul belongs another world; for every soul, every other soul is a world behind the world.

It is between the things most alike that illusion lies most beautifully; for the smallest gap is the hardest to bridge.

For me — how could there be anything outside me? There is no outside! But we forget that with every sound; how lovely it is that we forget!

Have not names and sounds been given to things so that man may refresh himself with things? Speaking is a beautiful folly: with it man dances over all things.

How lovely is all talking, and all the lying of sounds! With sounds our love dances on many-colored rainbows. —

— “Oh Zarathustra,” the animals said then, “to those who think as we do, all things dance by themselves: they come and offer each other their hands and laugh and run away — and come back.

Everything goes, everything comes back; the wheel of being rolls eternally. Everything dies, everything blossoms again; the year of being runs eternally.

Everything breaks, everything is joined anew; the same house of being builds itself eternally. Everything parts, everything greets itself again; the ring of being stays eternally true to itself.

In every instant being begins; around every Here rolls the sphere There. The center is everywhere. Crooked is the path of eternity.” —

— Oh you rascally fools and barrel organs! answered Zarathustra, and smiled again. How well you know what had to be fulfilled in seven days: —

— and how that monster crawled down my throat and choked me! But I bit its head off and spat it away from me.

And you — you have already made a barrel-organ song out of it? But now I lie here, still tired from that biting and spitting away, still sick from my own redemption.

And you watched all this? Oh my animals, are you cruel too? Did you want to watch my great pain, the way human beings do? For man is the cruelest animal.

At tragedies, bullfights, and crucifixions he has so far felt most at home on earth; and when he invented hell for himself, look, that was his heaven on earth.

When the great man cries out —: quick, the little man runs up; and his tongue hangs out of his mouth with lust. But he calls it his “pity.”

The little man, especially the poet — how eagerly he accuses life in words! Listen to him, but do not fail to hear the pleasure that is in all accusing!

Such accusers of life: life overcomes them with a blink of the eye. “You love me?” says the shameless one. “Wait a little longer, I have no time for you yet.”

Man is the cruelest animal toward himself; and in everyone who calls himself a “sinner” and a “cross-bearer” and a “penitent,” do not fail to hear the sensual pleasure in this complaining and accusing!

And I myself — do I want to be man’s accuser by saying this? Ah, my animals, this alone have I learned so far: that man needs what is most evil in him for what is best in him —

— that everything most evil is his best power, and the hardest stone for the highest creator; and that man must become better and more evil: —

It was not to this stake of torture that I was nailed — to knowing that man is evil — but I cried out as no one has yet cried out:

“Ah, that his most evil is so very small! Ah, that his best is so very small!”

The great weariness of man — that choked me and had crawled down my throat: and what the soothsayer foretold: “All is the same, nothing is worth it, knowledge chokes.”

A long twilight limped along in front of me, a sadness tired to death and drunk with death, which spoke with a yawning mouth.

“Eternally he comes back, the man you are tired of, the little man” — so yawned my sadness, and dragged its foot and could not fall asleep.

The human earth turned into a cave for me, its breast sank in, everything living became human rot and bones and crumbling past to me.

My sighing sat on all the graves of man and could no longer get up; my sighing and questioning croaked and choked and gnawed and wailed by day and by night:

— “Ah, man comes back eternally! The little man comes back eternally!” —

I had once seen them both naked, the greatest man and the smallest man: all too like each other — all too human, even the greatest!

All too small, the greatest! — that was my weariness of man! And eternal recurrence even of the smallest! — that was my weariness of all existence!

Ah, disgust! Disgust! Disgust! — — Thus spoke Zarathustra, and sighed and shuddered; for he remembered his sickness. But then his animals would not let him go on speaking.

“Speak no further, you convalescent!” — so his animals answered him — “but go out, where the world is waiting for you like a garden.

Go out to the roses and the bees and the flocks of doves! But above all to the songbirds: so that you may learn singing from them!

For singing is for the convalescent; the healthy may talk. And even when the healthy man wants songs, he wants other songs than the convalescent.”

— “Oh you rascally fools and barrel organs, do be quiet!” answered Zarathustra, and smiled at his animals. “How well you know what comfort I invented for myself in seven days!

That I would have to sing again — that comfort I invented for myself, and this convalescence: do you want to make a barrel-organ song out of that right away, too?”

— “Speak no further,” his animals answered him once more; “rather, you convalescent, first make yourself a lyre, a new lyre!

For look, oh Zarathustra! Your new songs need new lyres.

Sing and overflow, oh Zarathustra, heal your soul with new songs: so that you may bear your great destiny, which has never yet been any man’s destiny!

For your animals know well, oh Zarathustra, who you are and must become: look, you are the teacher of the eternal recurrence — that is now your destiny!

That you must be the first to teach this teaching — how could this great destiny not also be your greatest danger and sickness!

Look, we know what you teach: that all things recur eternally, and we ourselves with them, and that we have already been here an eternal number of times, and all things with us.

You teach that there is a great year of becoming, a monster of a great year: like an hourglass, it must turn itself over again and again, so that it may run down and run out anew: —

— so that all these years are the same as themselves, in the greatest and in the smallest as well — so that we ourselves are the same as ourselves in every great year, in the greatest and in the smallest as well.

And if you wanted to die now, oh Zarathustra: look, we know too how you would speak to yourself then: — but your animals beg you not to die yet!

You would speak, and without trembling, but rather breathing a sigh of bliss: for a great weight and a great sultriness would be lifted from you, you most patient one! —

‘Now I die and vanish,’ you would say, ‘and in an instant I am nothing. Souls are as mortal as bodies.

But the knot of causes in which I am entangled comes back — it will create me again! I myself belong to the causes of the eternal recurrence.

I come again, with this sun, with this earth, with this eagle, with this serpent — not to a new life or a better life or a similar life:

— I come again eternally to this same, selfsame life, in the greatest and in the smallest as well, to teach again the eternal recurrence of all things —

— to speak again the word of the great noon of earth and man, to proclaim the overman to mankind again.

I spoke my word, I break on my word: so my eternal lot wills it — as a proclaimer I perish!

The hour has now come for the one who goes under to bless himself. Thus ends Zarathustra’s going under.’” —

When the animals had spoken these words, they fell silent and waited for Zarathustra to say something to them: but Zarathustra did not hear that they were silent. Instead he lay still, with his eyes closed, like someone sleeping, although he was not asleep: for he was just then conversing with his soul. But the serpent and the eagle, when they found him so silent, honored the great stillness around him and carefully slipped away.

On the Great Longing

Oh my soul, I taught you to say “today” as you say “once” and “formerly,” and to dance your round dance over every Here and There and Yonder.

Oh my soul, I redeemed you from every corner; I swept the dust, the spiders, and the twilight off you.

Oh my soul, I washed the petty shame and the corner-virtue off you and persuaded you to stand naked before the eyes of the sun.

With the storm that is called “spirit” I blew across your heaving sea; I blew all the clouds away; I even strangled the strangler that is called “sin.”

Oh my soul, I gave you the right to say No like the storm, and to say Yes as the open sky says Yes: still as light you stand, and now you walk through denying storms.

Oh my soul, I gave you back your freedom over what is created and what is uncreated: and who knows, as you know it, the lust of what is to come?

Oh my soul, I taught you the contempt that does not come like a gnawing worm, the great, the loving contempt that loves most where it most despises.

Oh my soul, I taught you to persuade so well that you persuade the very grounds to come to you: like the sun, which persuades even the sea up to its own height.

Oh my soul, I took from you all obeying, all bending of the knee, and all saying “Lord”; I myself gave you the names “Turning of Need” and “Destiny.”

Oh my soul, I gave you new names and many-colored playthings; I called you “Destiny” and “Circle of Circles” and “Umbilical Cord of Time” and “Azure Bell.”

Oh my soul, I gave your soil all wisdom to drink, all the new wines, and also all the strong wines of wisdom, old beyond memory.

Oh my soul, I poured every sun on you, and every night and every silence and every longing: — and you grew up for me like a vine.

Oh my soul, overrich and heavy you stand there now, a vine with swelling udders and crowded clusters of brown golden grapes: —

— crowded and pressed by your happiness, waiting out of overabundance, and still bashful about your waiting.

Oh my soul, there is now no soul anywhere that is more loving and more embracing and more encompassing! Where would future and past be closer together than in you?

Oh my soul, I have given you everything, and all my hands have grown empty on you: — and now! Now you say to me, smiling and full of melancholy: “Which of us has to give thanks? —

— does the giver not have to give thanks that the receiver received? Is giving not a need? Is receiving not — mercy?” —

Oh my soul, I understand the smile of your melancholy: your overwealth itself is now stretching out longing hands!

Your fullness gazes out over roaring seas and seeks and waits; the longing of overfullness gazes out of the smiling sky of your eyes!

And truly, oh my soul! Who could see your smile and not melt into tears? The angels themselves melt into tears at the overgoodness of your smile.

It is your goodness and overgoodness that will not complain and weep: and yet, oh my soul, your smile longs for tears, and your trembling mouth for sobs.

“Is not all weeping a complaining? And all complaining an accusing?” So you speak to yourself, and that is why, oh my soul, you would rather smile than pour out your sorrow —

— pour out in rushing tears all your sorrow over your fullness, and over all the vine’s urgent craving for the vintner and the vintner’s knife!

But if you will not weep, will not weep out your purple melancholy, then you will have to sing, oh my soul! — Look, I myself am smiling as I foretell this to you:

— sing, with a roaring song, until all the seas grow still to listen to your longing —

— until over still, longing seas the boat floats, the golden wonder, around whose gold all good, bad, and wondrous things leap: —

— many animals too, great and small, and everything that has light, wondrous feet, so that it can run on violet-blue paths —

— toward the golden wonder, the boat that comes of its own will, and toward its master: but that is the vintner, who waits with his diamond knife —

— your great deliverer, oh my soul, the nameless one — — for whom only future songs will find names! And truly, your breath is already fragrant with future songs —

— already you glow and dream, already you drink thirstily at all the deep, ringing wells of comfort, already your melancholy rests in the bliss of future songs! —

Oh my soul, now I have given you everything, even my last, and all my hands have grown empty on you: — that I told you to sing, look, that was my last!

That I told you to sing — speak now, speak: which of us now has to — give thanks? — But better still: sing to me, sing, oh my soul! And let me give thanks! —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

The Other Dance Song

I

“Into your eye I looked not long ago, oh life: I saw gold blinking in your night-eye — my heart stood still with this delight:

— I saw a golden boat blinking on mighty waters, a sinking, drinking, ever-again-winking golden rocking boat!

At my foot, my dance-mad foot, you cast a glance, a laughing, questioning, melting, rocking glance:

Only twice did you stir your rattle with your little hands — and already my foot was rocking in a frenzy to dance. —

My heels reared up, my toes listened to understand you: for the dancer carries his ear — in his toes!

I leaped toward you: you fled back from my leap; and the tongue of your fleeing, flying hair flicked out at me!

Away from you I leaped, and from your serpents: and there you stood already, half turned, your eye full of yearning.

With crooked glances — you teach me crooked ways; on crooked ways my foot learns — tricks!

I fear you near, I love you far; your flight lures me, your seeking stops me short: — I suffer, but what have I not gladly suffered for you!

You whose coldness kindles, whose hatred seduces, whose flight binds, whose mockery — moves:

— who would not hate you, you great binder, entwiner, temptress, seeker, finder! Who would not love you, you innocent, impatient, wind-swift, child-eyed sinner!

Where are you pulling me now, you paragon and runaway? And now you flee me again, you sweet wildcat and ingrate!

I dance after you, I follow you even on the faintest trace. Where are you? Give me your hand! Or just one finger!

Here are caves and thickets: we will go astray! — Stop! Stand still! Do you not see owls and bats whirring away?

You owl! You bat! Do you want to make a monkey of me? Where are we? It was from the dogs you learned this howling and yapping.

You bare your little white teeth at me so sweetly, your evil eyes leap out at me from your curly little mane!

This is a dance over stick and stone: I am the hunter — will you be my hound, or my chamois?

Now beside me! And quickly, you spiteful leaper! Now up! And over! — Woe! I myself have fallen in the leap!

Oh, see me lying here, you insolence, and begging for grace! Gladly would I walk with you — in some lovelier place!

— the paths of love through quiet, many-colored bushes! Or there along the lake: goldfish swim and dance there!

You are tired now? Over there are sheep and evening glows: is it not lovely to sleep while shepherds play their flutes?

You are so terribly tired? I will carry you there, just let your arms sink! And if you are thirsty — I might have something, but your mouth does not want to drink! —

— Oh, this cursed, nimble, supple serpent and slippery witch! Where have you gone? But on my face I feel two spots and red blotches from your hand!

Truly, I am tired of always being your sheepish shepherd! You witch, if till now I have sung to you, now you shall — scream for me!

To the beat of my whip you shall dance and scream for me! I did not forget the whip, did I? — No!” —

II

Then life answered me like this, and held her dainty ears shut as she did:

“Oh Zarathustra! Do not crack your whip so terribly! You know very well: noise murders thoughts — and just now such tender thoughts are coming to me.

We are both a real pair of good-for-nothings and evil-for-nothings. Beyond good and evil we found our island and our green meadow — we two alone! So we really must be good to each other!

And even if we do not love each other from the ground up — must people be cross with each other if they do not love each other from the ground up?

And that I am good to you, and often too good, you know that: and the ground for it is that I am jealous of your wisdom. Ah, that mad old fool of a wisdom!

If your wisdom ever ran away from you, ah! then my love would quickly run away from you too.” —

Then life looked thoughtfully behind her and around her and said softly: “Oh Zarathustra, you are not faithful enough to me!

You do not love me nearly as much as you say; I know you are thinking of leaving me soon.

There is an old, heavy, heavy booming bell: at night its booming carries all the way up to your cave: —

— when you hear this bell strike the hour at midnight, you think about it between one and twelve —

— you think about it, oh Zarathustra, I know it, that you want to leave me soon!” —

“Yes,” I answered, hesitating, “but you know this too —” And I said something into her ear, right in among her tangled, yellow, foolish shaggy locks.

You know that, oh Zarathustra? No one knows that. —

And we looked at each other and gazed at the green meadow, over which the cool evening was just then running, and we wept together. — But then life was dearer to me than all my wisdom had ever been. —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

III

		One!
	Oh man, attend!
		Two!
	What does the deep midnight portend?
		Three!
	“I slept, I slept —,”
		Four!
	“From a deep dream I woke, at its end: —”
		Five!
	“The world is deep,”
		Six!
	“And deeper than the day could comprehend.”
		Seven!
	“Deep is its woe —,”
		Eight!
	“Joy — deeper still than misery:”
		Nine!
	“Woe says: Go!”
		Ten!
	“But all joy wants eternity —,”
		Eleven!
	“— wants deep, deep eternity!”
		Twelve!

The Seven Seals (Or: The Yes-and-Amen Song)

I

If I am a soothsayer, and full of that soothsaying spirit that wanders on a high ridge between two seas —

wanders between the past and the future like a heavy cloud — enemy to sultry lowlands and to everything that is weary and can neither die nor live —

ready for lightning in its dark breast and for the redeeming beam of light, pregnant with lightning bolts that say Yes! and laugh Yes!, ready for soothsaying flashes of lightning: —

— but blessed is the one who is pregnant in this way! And truly, whoever is one day to kindle the light of the future must hang long on the mountain like a heavy storm! —

Oh how could I not burn for eternity, and for the wedding ring of rings — the ring of recurrence!

Never yet have I found the woman by whom I wanted children, except this woman, whom I love: for I love you, oh eternity!

For I love you, oh eternity!

II

If my anger ever burst open graves, moved boundary stones, and rolled old tablets, broken, into steep depths:

If my scorn ever blew moldering words away, and I came like a broom to the cross spiders, and like a sweeping wind to old, stale burial chambers:

If I ever sat rejoicing where old gods lie buried, blessing the world, loving the world, beside the monuments of the old slanderers of the world: —

— for I love even churches and the graves of God, once the sky looks with its pure eye through their broken roofs; I like to sit on broken churches, like grass and red poppies —

Oh how could I not burn for eternity, and for the wedding ring of rings — the ring of recurrence!

Never yet have I found the woman by whom I wanted children, except this woman, whom I love: for I love you, oh eternity!

For I love you, oh eternity!

III

If ever a breath came to me from the creative breath, and from that heavenly necessity which compels even accidents to dance the round dances of the stars:

If I ever laughed with the laughter of the creative lightning, which the long thunder of the deed follows, rumbling but obedient:

If I ever played dice with gods at the gods’ table of the earth, so that the earth quaked and broke open and snorted up rivers of fire: —

— for the earth is a table of the gods, and trembles with creative new words and the throws of gods’ dice: —

Oh how could I not burn for eternity, and for the wedding ring of rings — the ring of recurrence!

Never yet have I found the woman by whom I wanted children, except this woman, whom I love: for I love you, oh eternity!

For I love you, oh eternity!

IV

If I ever drank in full draughts from that foaming bowl of spice and mixing, in which all things are well mixed:

If my hand ever poured the farthest into the nearest, and fire into spirit, and joy into sorrow, and the worst into the kindest:

If I myself am a grain of that redeeming salt which makes all things mix well in the mixing bowl: —

— for there is a salt that binds good to evil; and even the most evil is worthy to be a spice and to make the last foaming over: —

Oh how could I not burn for eternity, and for the wedding ring of rings — the ring of recurrence!

Never yet have I found the woman by whom I wanted children, except this woman, whom I love: for I love you, oh eternity!

For I love you, oh eternity!

V

If I am fond of the sea and of everything that is of the sea’s kind, and fondest of all when it angrily contradicts me:

If that seeking joy is in me which drives the sails toward what is undiscovered, if a seafarer’s joy is in my joy:

If my rejoicing ever cried: “The coast has vanished — now the last chain has fallen from me —

— the boundless roars around me, far out space and time shine for me, come on! up and away! old heart!” —

Oh how could I not burn for eternity, and for the wedding ring of rings — the ring of recurrence!

Never yet have I found the woman by whom I wanted children, except this woman, whom I love: for I love you, oh eternity!

For I love you, oh eternity!

VI

If my virtue is a dancer’s virtue, and I have often leaped with both feet into a golden-emerald rapture:

If my malice is a laughing malice, at home among slopes of roses and hedges of lilies:

— for in laughter all that is evil is gathered together, but pronounced holy and absolved by its own bliss: —

And if this is my alpha and omega, that all that is heavy should become light, every body a dancer, every spirit a bird: and truly, that is my alpha and omega! —

Oh how could I not burn for eternity, and for the wedding ring of rings — the ring of recurrence!

Never yet have I found the woman by whom I wanted children, except this woman, whom I love: for I love you, oh eternity!

For I love you, oh eternity!

VII

If I ever spread still skies above me and flew with my own wings into my own skies:

If I swam playfully in deep distances of light, and the bird-wisdom of my freedom came to me: —

— but this is how bird-wisdom speaks: “Look, there is no above, no below! Throw yourself around, out, back, you light one! Sing! Speak no more!

— are not all words made for the heavy? Do not all words lie to the light one! Sing! Speak no more!” —

Oh how could I not burn for eternity, and for the wedding ring of rings — the ring of recurrence!

Never yet have I found the woman by whom I wanted children, except this woman, whom I love: for I love you, oh eternity!

For I love you, oh eternity!

Part Four

Epigraph to Part Four

Ah, where in the world have greater follies happened than among the pitying? And what in the world has caused more suffering than the follies of the pitying? Woe to all lovers who do not yet have a height that is above their pity! Thus spoke the devil to me once: “Even God has his hell: it is his love of man.” And recently I heard him say this word: “God is dead; God died of his pity for man.”

Zarathustra, “On the Pitying”

The Honey Sacrifice

— And again moons and years passed over Zarathustra’s soul, and he paid no heed to it; but his hair turned white. One day, as he sat on a stone in front of his cave and quietly gazed out — and from there one gazes out on the sea, and away over winding abysses — his animals walked thoughtfully around him and at last came and stood in front of him.

“Oh Zarathustra,” they said, “are you looking out for your happiness?” — “What does happiness matter!” he answered. “I have long since stopped striving for happiness; I strive for my work.” — “Oh Zarathustra,” the animals said again, “you say that as one who has more than enough of good things. Are you not lying in a sky-blue lake of happiness?” — “You jesting rascals,” answered Zarathustra, and smiled, “how well you chose your parable! But you also know that my happiness is heavy, and not like a flowing wave of water: it presses on me and will not leave me, and it behaves like molten pitch.” —

Then the animals walked thoughtfully around him again and then came and stood in front of him once more. “Oh Zarathustra,” they said, “so that is why you yourself keep getting yellower and darker, even though your hair wants to look white and flaxen? Just look, you are stuck sitting in your pitch!” — “What are you saying, my animals,” said Zarathustra, and laughed at it, “truly, I slandered it when I spoke of pitch. What is happening to me happens to all fruits that grow ripe. It is the honey in my veins that makes my blood thicker and my soul quieter as well.” — “So it will be, oh Zarathustra,” answered the animals, and pressed close to him; “but will you not climb a high mountain today? The air is clear, and today one can see more of the world than ever.” — “Yes, my animals,” he answered, “your advice is excellent and after my own heart: today I will climb a high mountain! But see to it that there is honey ready to hand for me up there, yellow, white, good, ice-fresh golden honey in the comb. For know this: up there I want to make the honey sacrifice.” —

But when Zarathustra was up on the summit, he sent home the animals that had come with him, and found that he was now alone: — then he laughed with his whole heart, looked around, and spoke like this:

That I spoke of sacrifices and honey sacrifices was only a trick of speech and, truly, a useful folly! Up here I may speak more freely than in front of hermits’ caves and hermits’ pets.

Why sacrifice! I squander what is given to me, I, a squanderer with a thousand hands: how could I still call that — sacrificing!

And when I asked for honey, I was only asking for bait, and sweet syrup and slime, which even grumbling bears and strange, sullen, wicked birds lick their tongues for:

— for the best bait, such as hunters and fishermen need. For if the world is like a dark forest full of beasts and a pleasure garden for all wild hunters, it seems to me even more, and more to my liking, a rich sea, deep as an abyss,

— a sea full of bright-colored fish and crabs, which might make even gods lust to become fishermen on it, and casters of nets: so rich is the world in strange things, great and small!

Especially the human world, the human sea: — it is into that sea that I now cast my golden fishing rod and say: Open up, you human abyss!

Open up and throw me your fish and your glittering crabs! With my best bait I will bait for myself today the strangest human fish!

— my happiness itself I cast out into all the wide distances, between sunrise, noon, and sunset, to see whether many human fish will not learn to tug and wriggle at my happiness.

Until, biting on my sharp hidden hooks, they have to come up to my height, the most colorful gudgeons of the abyss, up to the most malicious of all fishers of men.

For that is what I am, from the ground up and from the beginning: pulling, pulling in, pulling up, bringing up, a puller, a breeder, and a master of breeding, who once said to himself, and not for nothing: “Become who you are!”

So let men now come up to me: for I am still waiting for the signs that it is time for my going down; I myself do not yet go under, as I must, among men.

That is why I wait here, cunning and mocking, on high mountains, not an impatient one, not a patient one, but rather one who has even unlearned patience, — because he is no longer anyone’s “patient.”

For my destiny gives me time: has it forgotten me, perhaps? Or is it sitting in the shade behind a big stone, catching flies?

And truly, I am fond of it for this, my eternal destiny, that it does not hound me and hurry me, and leaves me time for pranks and malice: so that today I climbed this high mountain to go fishing.

Has any man ever caught fish on high mountains? And even if what I want and do up here is a folly: it is still better than if I grew solemn down below from waiting, and green and yellow —

— a strutting snorter of wrath from waiting, a holy howling storm from the mountains, an impatient one who shouts down into the valleys: “Listen, or I will whip you with the scourge of God!”

Not that I would bear a grudge against such angry men for it: they are good enough for me to laugh at! They have to be impatient, these big noise-drums, who will get their word in today or never!

But I and my destiny — we do not speak to Today, nor do we speak to Never: for speaking we have patience and time and more than time. For one day it must come, and it may not pass by.

What must come one day and may not pass by? Our great Hazar, that is, our great, distant human kingdom, the Zarathustra-kingdom of a thousand years —

How far off may such a “far off” be? What is that to me! But it stands no less firm for me on that account —, with both feet I stand secure on this ground,

— on an eternal ground, on hard primeval rock, on this highest, hardest, primeval mountain range, to which all winds come as to the weather divide, asking Where? and Where from? and Where to?

Laugh here, laugh, my bright and wholesome malice! From high mountains throw down your glittering, mocking laughter! With your glitter, bait for me the most beautiful human fish!

And whatever in all seas belongs to me, my in-and-for-myself in all things — fish that out for me, bring that up to me: that is what I am waiting for, I, the most malicious of all fishermen.

Out, out, my hook! In and down, bait of my happiness! Drip your sweetest dew, honey of my heart! Bite, my hook, into the belly of all black gloom!

Out, out, my eye! Oh, how many seas all around me, what dawning human futures! And above me — what rose-red stillness! What cloudless silence!

The Cry of Distress

The next day Zarathustra was again sitting on his stone in front of the cave, while the animals roamed about out in the world to bring home new food — and new honey too: for Zarathustra had used up and squandered the old honey down to the last grain. But as he sat there in this way, with a stick in his hand, tracing the shadow of his figure on the ground, deep in thought and, truly! not about himself and his shadow — all at once he took fright and started: for beside his shadow he saw another shadow. And as he quickly looked around and stood up, look, there stood the soothsayer beside him, the same one he had once fed and given drink at his table, the proclaimer of the great weariness, who taught: “All is the same, nothing is worth it, the world is without meaning, knowledge chokes.” But his face had changed in the meantime; and when Zarathustra looked into his eyes, his heart took fright once again: so many evil proclamations and ash-gray lightnings passed over that face.

The soothsayer, who had perceived what was going on in Zarathustra’s soul, wiped his hand across his face, as if he wanted to wipe it away; and Zarathustra did the same. And when both of them had silently composed and strengthened themselves in this way, they gave each other their hands, as a sign that they wanted to recognize each other again.

“Welcome,” said Zarathustra, “you soothsayer of the great weariness; it shall not be for nothing that you were once my table companion and my guest. Eat and drink with me today as well, and forgive it if a contented old man sits at the table with you!” — “A contented old man?” answered the soothsayer, shaking his head. “But whoever you are or want to be, oh Zarathustra, you have been up here long enough — before long your boat will no longer sit high and dry!” — “Am I sitting high and dry, then?” asked Zarathustra, laughing. — “The waves around your mountain,” answered the soothsayer, “are rising and rising, the waves of great distress and gloom: soon they will lift your boat too and carry you away.” — At this Zarathustra was silent and wondered. — “Do you still hear nothing?” the soothsayer went on. “Is there not a rushing and roaring coming up from the depths?” — Zarathustra was silent again and listened: then he heard a long, long cry, which the abysses threw to one another and passed on, for none of them wanted to keep it: so evil did it sound.

“You evil proclaimer,” Zarathustra said at last, “that is a cry of distress, and the cry of a man; it may well come out of a black sea. But what is human distress to me! My last sin, which has been saved up for me — do you know what it is called?”

— “Pity!” answered the soothsayer from an overflowing heart, and raised both hands high. “Oh Zarathustra, I have come to tempt you to your last sin!” —

And hardly had these words been spoken when the cry rang out again, longer and more anxious than before, and already much nearer. “Do you hear? Do you hear, oh Zarathustra?” cried the soothsayer. “The cry is meant for you, it is calling you: come, come, come, it is time, it is high time!” —

At this Zarathustra was silent, confused and shaken; at last he asked, like one who hesitates within himself: “And who is it that is calling me there?”

“But you know already,” answered the soothsayer vehemently. “Why do you hide yourself? It is the higher man who is crying out for you!”

“The higher man?” cried Zarathustra, seized with horror. “What does he want? What does he want? The higher man! What does he want here?” — and his skin was covered with sweat.

But the soothsayer did not answer Zarathustra’s fear; he listened and listened toward the depths. But when it stayed quiet down there for a long time, he turned his gaze back and saw Zarathustra standing there trembling.

“Oh Zarathustra,” he began in a sad voice, “you do not stand there like one whose happiness makes his head spin: you will have to dance so that you do not fall over!

But even if you wanted to dance in front of me and leap all your side-leaps: no one shall be allowed to say to me: ‘Look, here dances the last happy man!’

Anyone who came up to this height looking for him would come in vain: caves he would find, no doubt, and caves behind caves, hiding places for those who hide, but no mine shafts of happiness, no treasure chambers, and no new golden veins of happiness.

Happiness — how could anyone find happiness among such buried ones and hermits! Must I still go looking for the last happiness on the Blessed Isles, far away among forgotten seas?

But all is the same, nothing is worth it, no seeking helps, and there are no Blessed Isles anymore either!” —

Thus sighed the soothsayer; but at his last sigh Zarathustra grew bright and sure again, like one who comes out of a deep gorge into the light. “No! No! Three times no!” he cried in a strong voice, and stroked his beard. “That I know better! There are still Blessed Isles! Be quiet about that, you sighing sack of sorrow!

Stop splashing about that, you rain cloud in the morning! Am I not standing here already, wet with your gloom and drenched like a dog?

Now I shake myself and run away from you, to get dry again: you must not wonder at that! Do I seem discourteous to you? But this is my court.

But as for your higher man: very well! I will look for him at once in those forests: that is where his cry came from. Perhaps some evil beast is pressing him hard there.

He is in my domain: here he shall come to no harm! And truly, there are many evil beasts around me.” —

With these words Zarathustra turned to go. Then the soothsayer said: “Oh Zarathustra, you are a rogue!

I know it already: you want to be rid of me! You would rather run into the forests and hunt down evil beasts!

But what good will it do you? In the evening you will have me back anyway; I will sit in your own cave, patient and heavy as a block of wood — and wait for you!”

“So be it!” Zarathustra called back as he went. “And what is mine in my cave belongs to you as well, my guest!

But if you should find any honey still in there, very well! Just lick it up, you grumbling bear, and sweeten your soul! For in the evening we both want to be in good spirits,

— in good spirits and glad that this day has come to an end! And you yourself shall dance to my songs as my dancing bear.

You do not believe it? You shake your head? Well then! Come on! Old bear! But I too — am a soothsayer.”

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

Conversation with the Kings

I

Zarathustra had not yet been an hour on his way in his mountains and forests when all at once he saw a strange procession. Right on the path he meant to go down came two kings walking, adorned with crowns and purple sashes and as colorful as flamingos: they were driving a loaded donkey ahead of them. “What do these kings want in my kingdom?” Zarathustra said to his heart in astonishment, and quickly hid himself behind a bush. But when the kings came up close to him, he said, half aloud, like one who talks only to himself: “Strange! Strange! What rhyme or reason is there in that? Two kings I see — and only one donkey!”

Then the two kings stopped, smiled, looked toward the spot the voice had come from, and afterward looked each other in the face. “People like us think such things too, no doubt,” said the king on the right, “but we do not say them out loud.”

But the king on the left shrugged his shoulders and answered: “That may well be a goatherd. Or a hermit who has lived too long among rocks and trees. For no company at all also corrupts good manners.”

“Good manners?” the other king replied, sullen and bitter. “Then what is it we are running away from? Is it not ‘good manners’? Our ‘good society’?

Better, truly, to live among hermits and goatherds than with our gilded, false, overpainted mob, — even if it calls itself ‘good society,’

— even if it calls itself ‘nobility.’ But there everything is false and rotten, the blood first of all, thanks to old bad diseases and worse artists of healing.

The best and dearest to me today is still a healthy peasant, coarse, cunning, stubborn, enduring: that is the noblest kind today.

The peasant is the best today; and the peasant kind should be master! But it is the kingdom of the mob, — I will not be fooled anymore. And mob means: mishmash.

Mob-mishmash: in it everything is jumbled up with everything else, saint and scoundrel and squire and Jew and every beast out of Noah’s ark.

Good manners! Everything among us is false and rotten. No one knows how to revere anymore: it is precisely that we are running away from. They are sugary, pushy dogs; they gild palm leaves.

This disgust is choking me, that we kings ourselves have become false, draped and disguised in the old yellowed pomp of our grandfathers, show medals for the stupidest and the slyest and for anyone who haggles with power today!

We are not the first — and yet we have to stand for the first: we have finally grown sick and disgusted with this fraud.

We went out of the way of the rabble, of all these screamers and scribbling blowflies, the shopkeepers’ stench, the fidgeting of ambition, the foul breath —: ugh, to live among the rabble,

— ugh, to stand for the first among the rabble! Ah, disgust! Disgust! Disgust! What do we kings matter anymore!” —

“Your old sickness is attacking you,” said the king on the left at this, “disgust is attacking you, my poor brother. But you know that someone is listening to us.”

At once Zarathustra, who had opened his ears and eyes wide to this talk, rose from his hiding place, stepped up to the kings, and began:

“The one who is listening to you, who likes listening to you, you kings, is called Zarathustra.

I am Zarathustra, who once said: ‘What do kings matter anymore!’ Forgive me, I was glad when you said to each other: ‘What do we kings matter!’

But here is my kingdom and my dominion: what might you be looking for in my kingdom? But perhaps on your way you found what I am looking for: namely, the higher man.”

When the kings heard this, they beat their breasts and said with one voice: “We are found out!

With the sword of this word you cut through the thickest darkness of our hearts. You have uncovered our distress, for look! We are on our way to find the higher man —

— the man who is higher than we are, even though we are kings. To him we are bringing this donkey. For the highest man shall also be the highest lord on earth.

There is no harder misfortune in all human destiny than when the mighty of the earth are not also the first men. Then everything becomes false and crooked and monstrous.

And when they are even the last, and more beast than man: then the mob rises and rises in price, and at last even the mob-virtue says: ‘Look, I alone am virtue!’” —

What did I just hear? answered Zarathustra; what wisdom in kings! I am delighted, and, truly, I already feel the urge to make a rhyme on it: —

— even if it turns out to be a rhyme not fit for everyone’s ears. I unlearned long ago any consideration for long ears. Well then! Come on!

(But here it happened that the donkey too got a word in: and what it said, clearly and with ill will, was Yeah-aw.)

	Once — I think it was the Year of Grace One —
	The Sibyl spoke, quite drunk, though wine she had none:
	“Woe, now all goes wrong below!
	Decline! Decline! Never has the world sunk so low!
	Rome sank to whore and to a whorehouse too,
	Rome’s Caesar sank to beast, God himself — became a Jew!”

II

The kings feasted on these rhymes of Zarathustra’s; but the king on the right said: “Oh Zarathustra, how well we did to set out to see you!

For your enemies showed us your image in their mirror: there you looked out with the grimace of a devil, laughing scornfully, so that we were afraid of you.

But what good did it do! Again and again you stung us in ear and heart with your sayings. So at last we said: what does it matter what he looks like!

We must hear him, him who teaches: ‘You should love peace as a means to new wars, and the short peace more than the long!’

No one ever spoke such warlike words: ‘What is good? To be brave is good. It is the good war that hallows every cause.’

Oh Zarathustra, at such words the blood of our fathers stirred in our bodies: it was like spring speaking to old wine casks.

When the swords ran in and out among one another like red-spotted serpents, then our fathers grew fond of life; the sun of every peace seemed to them flat and lukewarm, and the long peace brought them shame.

How they sighed, our fathers, when they saw gleaming, dried-out swords hanging on the wall! Like those swords, they thirsted for war. For a sword wants to drink blood, and sparkles with desire.” —

— When the kings talked and chattered so eagerly about the happiness of their fathers, Zarathustra was overcome by no small desire to mock their eagerness: for plainly they were very peaceable kings that he saw before him, kings with old and refined faces. But he restrained himself. “Well then!” he said. “That way lies the road, there lies Zarathustra’s cave; and this day shall have a long evening! But now a cry of distress calls me hastily away from you.

It honors my cave if kings want to sit and wait in it: but, of course, you will have to wait a long time!

Well, well! What does it matter! Where does one learn to wait better today than at courts? And the whole virtue of kings that is left to them, — is it not called today: being able to wait?”

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

The Leech

And Zarathustra went on, thoughtful, farther and deeper, through forests and past marshy hollows; but as happens to anyone who thinks about heavy things, he stepped on a man without noticing. And look, all at once a cry of pain and two curses and twenty foul insults spattered into his face: so that in his fright he raised his stick and struck the man he had stepped on as well. But the next moment he came to his senses; and his heart laughed at the folly he had just committed.

“Forgive me,” he said to the man he had stepped on, who had got up in a fury and sat down again, “forgive me, and before anything else, hear a parable.

As a wanderer who dreams of faraway things bumps, without noticing, on a lonely road into a sleeping dog, a dog lying in the sun:

— as both of them start up and fly at each other like mortal enemies, these two who are frightened to death: that is how it went with us.

And yet! And yet — how little was missing for them to caress each other, this dog and this solitary one! For they are both — solitary ones!”

— “Whoever you may be,” said the man who had been stepped on, still in a fury, “you are treading on me with your parable too, and not only with your foot!

Look, am I a dog, then?” — and with that the sitting man stood up and pulled his naked arm out of the swamp. For at first he had been lying stretched out on the ground, hidden and unrecognizable, like those who lie in wait for swamp game.

“But what are you doing!” cried Zarathustra in alarm, for he saw that a great deal of blood was flowing down the naked arm, “what has happened to you? Did an evil animal bite you, you unhappy man?”

The bleeding man laughed, still angry. “What business is it of yours!” he said, and was about to walk on. “Here I am at home and in my own domain. Let whoever wants to ask me: but I will hardly answer a clod.”

“You are wrong,” said Zarathustra with pity, and held him fast, “you are wrong: here you are not at home, but in my kingdom, and in it no one shall come to harm.

But call me whatever you like — I am who I must be. I myself call myself Zarathustra.

Come! Up there is the way to Zarathustra’s cave: it is not far — will you not tend your wounds at my place?

Things have gone badly for you in this life, you unhappy man: first the animal bit you, and then — man stepped on you!” —

But when the man who had been stepped on heard Zarathustra’s name, he was transformed. “What is happening to me!” he cried out. “Who else do I care about in this life, except this one man, Zarathustra, and that one animal that lives on blood, the leech?

For the leech’s sake I lay here by this swamp like a fisherman, and my arm, hung out as bait, had already been bitten ten times, when a still finer leech bites after my blood: Zarathustra himself!

Oh happiness! Oh wonder! Praised be this day that lured me into this swamp! Praised be the best, the liveliest cupping glass alive today, praised be the great leech of conscience, Zarathustra!” —

Thus spoke the man who had been stepped on; and Zarathustra was glad of his words and their fine, reverent manner. “Who are you?” he asked, and gave him his hand. “Between us there is much to clear up and to cheer up: but already, it seems to me, a pure, bright day is dawning.”

“I am the conscientious one of the spirit,” answered the one who was asked, “and in matters of the spirit hardly anyone takes things more strictly, more narrowly, and more harshly than I do, except the one I learned it from, Zarathustra himself.

Better to know nothing than to half-know a great deal! Better to be a fool on one’s own account than a sage by other people’s lights! I — go to the ground of things:

— what does it matter whether it is great or small? Whether it is called swamp or sky? A hand’s breadth of ground is enough for me: so long as it is truly ground, solid ground to stand on!

— a hand’s breadth of ground: a man can stand on that. In a true conscience-science there is nothing great and nothing small.”

“Then perhaps you are the expert on the leech?” asked Zarathustra. “And you pursue the leech down to its ultimate grounds, you conscientious one?”

“Oh Zarathustra,” answered the man who had been stepped on, “that would be something enormous; how could I dare to take that on!

But what I am master and expert of is the leech’s brain: — that is my world!

And it is a world, too! But forgive me that my pride speaks up here, for here I have no equal. That is why I said, ‘Here I am at home.’

How long I have been pursuing this one thing, the brain of the leech, so that the slippery truth might no longer slip away from me here! Here is my kingdom!

— for its sake I threw everything else away, for its sake everything else became all the same to me; and right beside my knowledge lies my black ignorance.

My conscience of the spirit wants it so from me: that I know one thing and know nothing of everything else. I am disgusted by all the half-spirited, all the hazy, the hovering, the rapturous.

Where my honesty stops, I am blind, and I want to be blind. But where I want to know, I want to be honest as well — that is, hard, strict, narrow, cruel, inexorable.

That you once said, oh Zarathustra, ‘Spirit is the life that itself cuts into life’ — that led me, and led me astray, to your teaching. And truly, with my own blood I have increased my own knowledge!”

— “As is plain to see,” Zarathustra broke in; for the blood was still flowing down the naked arm of the conscientious one. Ten leeches, you see, had bitten into it.

“Oh you strange fellow, how much this plain sight here teaches me — namely, you yourself! And perhaps I should not pour all of it into your strict ears!

Come, then! Here we part! But I would gladly find you again. Up there is the way to my cave: tonight you shall be my dear guest there!

I would also gladly make it up to your body that Zarathustra trod on you with his feet: I am thinking it over. But now a cry of distress calls me away from you in haste.”

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

The Magician

I

But when Zarathustra came around a rock, he saw, not far below him on the same path, a man who flung his limbs about like a raving madman and at last crashed face down to the ground. “Stop!” Zarathustra then said to his heart. “That one there must surely be the higher man; from him came that terrible cry of distress — I will see whether there is any help to give.” But when he ran up to the spot where the man lay on the ground, he found a trembling old man with staring eyes; and however hard Zarathustra tried to raise him up and set him back on his feet, it was no use. Nor did the unhappy man seem to notice that anyone was near him; rather, he kept looking around with heart-rending gestures, like someone forsaken and isolated by all the world. But at last, after much trembling, twitching, and curling-himself-up, he began to lament like this:

	Who warms me, who loves me still?
	Give me hot hands!
	Give me braziers for the heart!
	Stretched out, shuddering,
	Like someone half dead whose feet are being warmed —
	Shaken, ah! by unknown fevers,
	Trembling before sharp, icy arrows of frost,
	Hunted by you, thought!
	Unnamable! Veiled one! Horrifying one!
	You hunter behind the clouds!
	Struck down by your lightning,
	You mocking eye that looks at me out of the dark:
	— so I lie,
	Bend myself, writhe, tormented
	By all the eternal tortures,
	Hit
	By you, cruelest hunter,
	You unknown — god!
	Strike deeper,
	Strike once more!
	Stab and break this heart!
	Why this torturing
	With blunt-toothed arrows?
	Why do you look again,
	Not weary of human torment,
	With gloating eyes of godly lightning?
	You do not want to kill,
	Only torture, torture?
	Why — torture me,
	You gloating unknown god? —
	Haha! You are creeping closer?
	At such a midnight
	What do you want? Speak!
	You crowd me, press me —
	Ha! already far too close!
	Away! Away!
	You hear me breathing,
	You eavesdrop on my heart,
	You jealous one —
	But jealous of what?
	Away! Away! Why the ladder?
	Do you want in,
	Into my heart,
	To climb in, to climb into my most secret
	Thoughts?
	Shameless one! Unknown — thief!
	What do you want to steal for yourself,
	What do you want to overhear for yourself,
	What do you want to torture out of me,
	You torturer!
	You — hangman-god!
	Or shall I, like a dog,
	Roll over before you?
	Surrendering, beside myself with rapture,
	Wag — my love at you?
	In vain!
	Stab on,
	Cruelest goad! No,
	No dog — I am only your game,
	Cruelest hunter!
	Your proudest captive,
	You robber behind the clouds…
	Speak at last,
	What do you want, you highwayman, from me?
	You lightning-shrouded one! Unknown one! Speak,
	What do you want, unknown god? — —
	What?
	Ransom?
	How much ransom do you want?
	Ask much — so my pride advises!
	And keep it short — so my other pride advises!
	Haha!
	Me — you want? Me?
	Me — all of me?
	Haha!
	And you torture me, fool that you are,
	Torture my pride to pieces?
	Give me love — who warms me still?
	Who loves me still? — give me hot hands,
	Give me braziers for the heart,
	Give me, the most solitary one,
	Whom ice, ah! sevenfold ice
	Teaches to pine for enemies,
	For enemies, even,
	Give, yes, give up,
	Cruelest enemy,
	To me — yourself!…
	Gone!
	He has fled, himself,
	My last and only companion,
	My great enemy,
	My unknown one,
	My hangman-god!…
	— No!
	Come back,
	With all your tortures!
	To the last of all solitary ones
	Oh come back!
	All my streams of tears run
	Their course to you!
	And my heart’s last flame —
	For you it glows anew!
	Oh come back,
	My unknown god! My pain!
	My last happiness!

II

— But here Zarathustra could hold himself back no longer; he took his stick and struck at the lamenting man with all his strength. “Stop it!” he shouted at him, with furious laughter. “Stop it, you actor! You counterfeiter! You liar to the core! I know you well!

I will warm your legs for you, you wicked magician; I know very well how to make it hot — for the likes of you!”

— “Stop,” said the old man, and sprang up from the ground, “do not hit me anymore, oh Zarathustra! I only did it as a game!

Things like this are part of my art; I wanted to put you yourself to the test when I gave you this taste of it! And truly, you saw right through me!

But you too — gave me no small taste of yourself: you are hard, you wise Zarathustra! You strike hard with your ‘truths’; your cudgel forces out of me — this truth!”

— “Do not flatter,” answered Zarathustra, still worked up and scowling, “you actor to the core! You are false: what are you doing talking — about truth!

You peacock of peacocks, you sea of vanity, what were you playing before me, you wicked magician, in whom was I supposed to believe when you lamented like that?”

“The penitent of the spirit,” said the old man, “him — I was playing: you yourself once invented the phrase —

— the poet and magician who at last turns his spirit against himself, the transformed one who freezes to death on his own evil knowledge and conscience.

And just admit it: it took you a long time, oh Zarathustra, to see through my art and my lie! You believed in my distress when you held my head in both your hands —

— I heard you lament, ‘He was loved too little, loved too little!’ That I deceived you so far — my malice rejoiced at that inwardly.”

“You may have deceived subtler people than me,” said Zarathustra harshly. “I am not on guard against deceivers; I must be without caution: so my lot wills it.

But you — must deceive: that much I know of you! You must always mean two, three, four, and five things at once! Even what you confessed just now was not nearly true enough or false enough for me!

You wicked counterfeiter, how could you do anything else! You would put makeup even on your illness if you showed yourself naked to your doctor.

That is just how you made up your lie before me when you said, ‘I only did it as a game!’ There was seriousness in it too; you are something of a penitent of the spirit!

I can guess what you are: you became the enchanter of everyone, but against yourself you have no lie and no cunning left — you are disenchanted with yourself!

You have harvested disgust as your one truth. Not a word of yours is genuine anymore, except your mouth: that is, the disgust that sticks to your mouth.” —

— “Who are you, then!” the old magician cried at this, in a defiant voice. “Who dares speak like this to me, the greatest man alive today?” — and a green flash of lightning shot from his eye at Zarathustra. But the next moment he was transformed and said sadly:

“Oh Zarathustra, I am tired of it, my arts disgust me, I am not great, why do I pretend! But you know it well — I searched for greatness!

I wanted to play a great man, and I persuaded many: but this lie went beyond my strength. I am breaking on it.

Oh Zarathustra, everything about me is a lie; but that I am breaking — this breaking of mine is genuine!” —

“It honors you,” said Zarathustra darkly, looking down to one side, “it honors you that you searched for greatness, but it also gives you away. You are not great.

You wicked old magician, this is the best and most honest thing in you, the thing I honor in you: that you grew tired of yourself and said it out loud: ‘I am not great.’

In this I honor you as a penitent of the spirit: and even if only for a breath and a blink, for this one moment you were — genuine.

But tell me, what are you looking for here in my forests and rocks? And when you laid yourself across my path, what test did you want of me? —

— what were you tempting me with?” —

Thus spoke Zarathustra, and his eyes sparkled. The old magician was silent for a while; then he said: “Did I tempt you? I — am only seeking.

Oh Zarathustra, I am seeking someone genuine, upright, simple, of one meaning only, a man of all honesty, a vessel of wisdom, a saint of knowledge, a great man!

Do you not know it, then, oh Zarathustra? I am seeking Zarathustra.”

— And here a long silence arose between the two; but Zarathustra sank deep into himself, so that he closed his eyes. But then, coming back to the one he was talking with, he took the magician’s hand and spoke, full of courtesy and cunning:

“Well then! Up there the way leads on; there lies Zarathustra’s cave. In it you may seek whomever you would like to find.

And ask my animals for advice, my eagle and my serpent: they shall help you seek. But my cave is large.

I myself, of course — I have never yet seen a great man. For what is great, the eye of even the finest is coarse today. It is the kingdom of the mob.

Many a one have I already found who stretched and puffed himself up, and the people shouted: ‘Look there, a great man!’ But what good are all the bellows! In the end the wind comes out.

In the end a frog bursts that has puffed itself up too long: then the wind comes out. To stab a swollen man in the belly — that is what I call a fine pastime. Listen to that, you boys!
