This today belongs to the mob: who still knows what is great and what is small! Who would have luck searching for greatness! Only a fool: fools have the luck.

You are searching for great men, you strange fool? Who taught you that? Is today the time for it? Oh you wicked seeker, why — are you tempting me?” —

Thus spoke Zarathustra, his heart comforted, and went on his way, laughing.

Retired

But not long after Zarathustra had freed himself from the magician, he again saw someone sitting by the path he was walking, namely a tall man in black with a gaunt, pasty face: he annoyed him enormously. “Woe,” he said to his heart, “there sits gloom in disguise; that looks to me like the priestly kind: what do they want in my kingdom?

What! I have hardly escaped that magician, and must another black artist run across my path again —

— some sorcerer with a laying-on of hands, a dark miracle-worker by the grace of God, an anointed world-slanderer, may the devil take him!

But the devil is never in his place when he would be in place: he always comes too late, this accursed dwarf and clubfoot!” —

Thus Zarathustra cursed impatiently in his heart, and thought how he might slip past the black man with his eyes turned away: but look, it turned out otherwise. For in the same moment the sitting man had already caught sight of him; and not unlike someone to whom an unexpected piece of luck has happened, he jumped up and went straight for Zarathustra.

“Whoever you are, you wanderer,” he said, “help a man who has lost his way, a seeker, an old man who could easily come to harm here!

This world here is strange and far from me, and I heard wild animals howling, too; and the one who could have offered me protection — he himself is no more.

I was looking for the last pious man, a saint and hermit, who alone in his forest had not yet heard anything of what all the world knows today.”

“What does all the world know today?” asked Zarathustra. “This, perhaps: that the old God no longer lives, the God all the world once believed in?”

“You have said it,” answered the old man sorrowfully. “And I served this old God until his last hour.

But now I am retired, without a master, and still not free, and never merry for a single hour anymore, except in memories.

That is why I climbed into these mountains: so that I might at last hold a festival again for myself, as befits an old pope and father of the church — for know this, I am the last pope! — a festival of pious memories and divine services.

But now he himself is dead, the most pious man, that saint in the forest who constantly praised his God with singing and growling.

Him I no longer found when I found his hut — but I did find two wolves in it, howling over his death — for all the animals loved him. Then I ran away.

Had I come into these forests and mountains for nothing, then? Then my heart resolved that I should seek another, the most pious of all those who do not believe in God — that I should seek Zarathustra!”

Thus spoke the old man, and looked with a sharp eye at the one who stood before him; but Zarathustra took the old pope’s hand and gazed at it a long time in admiration.

“Look at that, you venerable man,” he said then, “what a beautiful, long hand! It is the hand of one who has always handed out blessings. But now it holds fast the one you are looking for: me, Zarathustra.

It is I, Zarathustra the godless, who says: who is more godless than I, that I may delight in his instruction?” —

Thus spoke Zarathustra, and with his gaze he pierced the thoughts and the second thoughts of the old pope. At last the pope began:

“Whoever loved and possessed him most has now also lost him most —:

— look, of the two of us, am I not now probably the more godless one? But who could be glad of that!” —

“You served him to the end,” asked Zarathustra thoughtfully, after a deep silence, “do you know how he died? Is it true, what people say, that pity strangled him,

— that he saw how man hung on the cross, and could not bear it that his love of man became his hell and at last his death?” —

But the old pope did not answer; he looked shyly aside, with a pained and gloomy expression.

“Let him go,” said Zarathustra after long reflection, still looking the old man straight in the eye.

“Let him go, he is gone. And though it honors you that you speak only good of this dead one, you know as well as I do who he was; and that he went strange ways.”

“Speaking between the three of our eyes,” said the old pope, cheered (for he was blind in one eye), “in matters of God I am more enlightened than Zarathustra himself — and I may well be.

My love served him for long years, my will followed all his will. But a good servant knows everything, including many things his master hides from himself.

He was a hidden god, full of secrecy. Truly, even to a son he came no other way than by sneaking paths. At the door of his faith stands adultery.

Whoever praises him as a god of love does not think highly enough of love itself. Did this god not want to be judge as well? But the one who loves, loves beyond reward and retribution.

When he was young, this god from the East, he was hard and vengeful, and built himself a hell for the delight of his favorites.

But at last he grew old and soft and crumbly and pitying, more like a grandfather than a father, but most of all like a wobbly old grandmother.

There he sat, withered, in his corner by the stove, fretting over his weak legs, weary of the world, weary of willing, and one day he choked on his all-too-great pity.” —

“You old pope,” Zarathustra broke in here, “did you see that with your own eyes? It could well have happened that way: that way, and some other way too. When gods die, they always die many kinds of death.

But well then! This way or that, this way and that — he is gone! He went against the taste of my ears and eyes; I would not want to say anything worse of him than that.

I love everything that looks clearly and speaks honestly. But he — you know it, you old priest, there was something of your kind about him, of the priestly kind — he had many meanings.

He was unclear, too. How angry he got with us over it, this snorter of wrath, because we understood him badly! But why did he not speak more plainly?

And if it was the fault of our ears, why did he give us ears that heard him badly? If there was mud in our ears, well then! who put it there?

Too much went wrong for him, this potter who had never finished his apprenticeship! But that he took revenge on his pots and his creatures because they turned out badly for him — that was a sin against good taste.

There is good taste in piety too: and it said at last, ‘Away with such a god! Better no god at all, better to make one’s own destiny on one’s own account, better to be a fool, better to be a god oneself!’”

— “What do I hear!” said the old pope here, pricking up his ears. “Oh Zarathustra, you are more pious than you believe, with an unbelief like that! Some god in you converted you to your godlessness.

Is it not your piety itself that no longer lets you believe in a god? And your overgreat honesty will yet carry you off beyond good and evil as well!

Look, then, what has been kept in store for you? You have eyes and hand and mouth, and they have been destined for blessing from all eternity. One does not bless with the hand alone.

Near you, though you want to be the most godless of all, I scent a secret fragrance of incense and well-being from long blessings: it makes me feel both well and woeful.

Let me be your guest, oh Zarathustra, for a single night! Nowhere on earth do I feel better now than with you!” —

“Amen! So be it!” said Zarathustra in great astonishment. “Up there the way leads on; there lies Zarathustra’s cave.

Truly, I would gladly lead you there myself, you venerable man, for I love all pious people. But now a cry of distress calls me away from you in haste.

In my domain no one shall come to harm; my cave is a good harbor. And most of all I would like to set every sad person back on firm land and firm legs.

But who could take your melancholy off your shoulders? For that I am too weak. Truly, we might wait a long time before someone wakes your God again.

For this old God no longer lives: he is thoroughly dead.” —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

The Ugliest Man

— And again Zarathustra’s feet ran through mountains and forests, and his eyes searched and searched, but nowhere was the one to be seen whom they wanted to see, the one in great distress who had cried out in distress. But all along the way he rejoiced in his heart and was grateful. “What good things,” he said, “this day has given me, to make up for its bad beginning! What strange people I have found to talk with!

Now I will chew on their words for a long time, as on good grains; my teeth shall grind and crush them small, until they flow into my soul like milk!” —

But when the path bent around a rock again, all at once the landscape changed, and Zarathustra stepped into a realm of death. Here black and red cliffs jutted upward: no grass, no tree, no bird’s voice. For it was a valley that all animals avoided, even the beasts of prey; only a kind of ugly, thick, green serpent came here to die when they grew old. That is why the shepherds called this valley: Serpents’ Death.

But Zarathustra sank into a black memory, for it seemed to him that he had stood in this valley once before. And much that was heavy weighed on his mind: so that he walked slowly, and more and more slowly, and at last stood still. But then, when he opened his eyes, he saw something sitting by the wayside, shaped like a man and hardly like a man, something unspeakable. And all at once a great shame fell on Zarathustra, that he had looked at such a thing with his eyes: blushing up to the roots of his white hair, he turned his gaze away and lifted his foot to leave this bad place. But then the dead wasteland grew loud: for up from the ground there welled a gurgling and a rattling, the way water gurgles and rattles at night through clogged water pipes; and at last it turned into a human voice and human speech: — and it went like this.

“Zarathustra! Zarathustra! Guess my riddle! Speak, speak! What is revenge on the witness?

I am luring you back, here is slippery ice! Watch out, watch out that your pride does not break its legs here!

You think yourself wise, you proud Zarathustra! Then guess the riddle, you hard nutcracker — the riddle that I am! Then speak — who am I!”

— But when Zarathustra had heard these words — what do you suppose happened then in his soul? Pity fell upon him; and all at once he sank to the ground, like an oak that has long withstood many woodcutters — heavily, suddenly, to the terror even of those who wanted to fell it. But already he was rising from the ground again, and his face grew hard.

“I know you well,” he said, in a voice of bronze: “you are the murderer of God! Let me go.

You could not bear the one who saw you — who saw you always and through and through, you ugliest man! You took revenge on this witness!”

Thus spoke Zarathustra, and he wanted to get away; but the unspeakable one grabbed at a corner of his robe and began again to gurgle and search for words. “Stay!” he said at last —

— “stay! Do not pass by! I guessed what axe struck you to the ground: hail to you, oh Zarathustra, that you are standing again!

You guessed, I know it well, how the one feels who killed him — the murderer of God. Stay! Sit down here beside me; it is not for nothing.

To whom would I go, if not to you? Stay, sit down! But do not look at me! Honor in this way — my ugliness!

They are persecuting me: now you are my last refuge. Not with their hatred, not with their bailiffs: — oh, I would mock such persecution, and be proud and glad!

Has not all success so far been with the well-persecuted? And whoever persecutes well easily learns to follow: — after all, he is already — behind! But it is their pity —

— it is their pity that I am fleeing, fleeing to you for refuge. Oh Zarathustra, protect me, you my last refuge, you the only one who guessed me:

— you guessed how the one feels who killed him. Stay! And if you want to go, you impatient one: do not go the way I came. That way is bad.

Are you angry with me that I have been talk-tangle-mangling too long already? That I am already giving you advice? But know that it is I, the ugliest man,

— who also has the biggest, heaviest feet. Where I have walked, the way is bad. I trample every way to death and to ruin.

But that you passed me by, in silence; that you blushed, I saw it well: by that I knew you for Zarathustra.

Anyone else would have tossed me his alms, his pity, with a glance and a word. But for that — I am not beggar enough, you guessed that —

— for that I am too rich, rich in what is great, in what is terrible, in what is ugliest, in what is most unspeakable! Your shame, oh Zarathustra, honored me!

With difficulty I got out of the crush of the pitying, — to find the only one who teaches today that “pity is pushy” — you, oh Zarathustra!

— whether it is a god’s pity or the pity of men: pity goes against shame. And not-wanting-to-help can be nobler than that virtue which leaps to help.

But that is what all the little people today call virtue itself, pity: — they have no reverence for great misfortune, for great ugliness, for great failure.

I look away over all of these, as a dog looks away over the backs of teeming flocks of sheep. They are little, well-meaning, well-willing gray people.

As a heron looks away in contempt over shallow ponds, with its head thrown back: so I look away over the swarm of gray little waves and wills and souls.

Too long they have been told they are right, these little people: so in the end they were given power too — now they teach: “good is only what little people call good.”

And “truth” today is what the preacher spoke who came from among them himself, that strange saint and advocate of the little people, who testified of himself, “I — am the truth.”

For a long time now this immodest man has been making the little people puff up their crests — he who taught no small error when he taught, “I — am the truth.”

Was an immodest man ever answered more politely? — But you, oh Zarathustra, passed him by and said: “No! No! Three times no!”

You warned against his error, you were the first to warn against pity — not all, not none, but yourself and your kind.

You are ashamed at the shame of the great sufferer; and truly, when you say, “from pity comes a great cloud, beware, you people!”

— when you teach, “all creators are hard, all great love is above its pity”: oh Zarathustra, how well schooled in the signs of the weather you seem to me!

But you yourself — warn yourself too against your pity! For many are on their way to you, many who are suffering, doubting, despairing, drowning, freezing —

I warn you against me too. You guessed my best, my worst riddle, myself and what I did. I know the axe that fells you.

But he — had to die: he saw with eyes that saw everything, — he saw man’s depths and grounds, all his hidden disgrace and ugliness.

His pity knew no shame: he crept into my filthiest corners. This most curious, over-pushy, over-pitying one had to die.

He always saw me: on such a witness I wanted to have revenge — or not live myself.

The God who saw everything, even man: this God had to die! Man cannot bear that such a witness should live.”

Thus spoke the ugliest man. But Zarathustra rose and made ready to go: for he was chilled to his very bowels.

“You unspeakable one,” he said, “you warned me against your way. In thanks for that, I commend my own to you. Look, up there lies Zarathustra’s cave.

My cave is large and deep and has many corners; there the most hidden one finds his hiding place. And close by it are a hundred holes and hideaways for creeping, fluttering, and hopping creatures.

You outcast, who cast yourself out, you do not want to live among men and the pity of men? Well then, do as I do! So you will learn from me too; only the doer learns.

And talk first and foremost with my animals! The proudest animal and the shrewdest animal — they may well be the right counselors for the two of us!” —

Thus spoke Zarathustra, and went his way, more thoughtful and slower even than before: for he asked himself many things and did not easily know the answer.

“How poor man is, after all!” he thought in his heart, “how ugly, how rattling, how full of hidden shame!

I am told that man loves himself: ah, how great this self-love must be! How much contempt it has against it!

This one too loved himself even as he despised himself, — a great lover he is to me, and a great despiser.

I have found no one yet who despised himself more deeply: even that is height. Woe, was he perhaps the higher man whose cry I heard?

I love the great despisers. But man is something that must be overcome.” —

The Voluntary Beggar

When Zarathustra had left the ugliest man, he was cold, and he felt lonely: for much that was cold and lonely went through his senses, so that his limbs too grew colder. But as he climbed on and on, up, down, now past green pastures, but also over wild, stony beds where an impatient brook had probably once gone to bed: — then all at once he felt warmer and more cheerful at heart again.

“What has happened to me?” he asked himself. “Something warm and living refreshes me; it must be near me.

Already I am less alone; unwitting companions and brothers roam around me, their warm breath touches my soul.”

But when he peered about and searched for the comforters of his solitude: look, they were cows, standing together on a hilltop; their nearness and their smell had warmed his heart. These cows, though, seemed to be listening eagerly to someone speaking, and paid no attention to the one who was coming near. But when Zarathustra was quite close to them, he heard clearly that a human voice was speaking from the middle of the cows; and plainly they had all turned their heads toward the speaker.

Then Zarathustra sprang eagerly up the slope and pushed the animals apart, for he feared that some harm had come to someone here, which the pity of cows could hardly help. But in this he was mistaken; for look, there sat a man on the ground, who seemed to be urging the animals not to be afraid of him, a peaceable man and preacher on the mount, out of whose eyes goodness itself was preaching. “What are you looking for here?” cried Zarathustra in surprise.

“What am I looking for here?” he answered. “The same thing you are looking for, you troublemaker! Namely, happiness on earth.

But for that I want to learn from these cows. For, you know, I have been talking to them for half a morning already, and they were just about to give me their answer. Why do you have to disturb them?

Unless we turn and become like cows, we will never enter the kingdom of heaven. For there is one thing we ought to learn from them: chewing the cud.

And truly, if a man gained the whole world and did not learn this one thing, chewing the cud: what good would it do him! He would not be rid of his gloom

— his great gloom: but today it is called disgust. Who today does not have his heart, mouth, and eyes full of disgust? You too! You too! But just look at these cows!” —

Thus spoke the preacher on the mount, and then turned his own gaze on Zarathustra — for until then it had clung lovingly to the cows —: but then he was transformed. “Who is this I am talking to?” he cried in alarm, and sprang up from the ground.

This is the man without disgust, this is Zarathustra himself, the overcomer of the great disgust, this is the eye, this is the mouth, this is the heart of Zarathustra himself.”

And as he spoke like this, he kissed the hands of the one he was speaking to, with overflowing eyes, and behaved exactly like someone on whom a precious gift and jewel has fallen unexpectedly from heaven. But the cows looked on at all of this and wondered.

“Do not speak of me, you strange one! you lovely one!” said Zarathustra, warding off his tenderness, “speak to me first of yourself! Are you not the voluntary beggar who once threw away great riches, —

— who was ashamed of his riches and of the rich, and fled to the poorest, to give them his abundance and his heart? But they did not accept him.”

“But they did not accept me,” said the voluntary beggar, “you know that already. So in the end I went to the animals and to these cows.”

“There you learned,” Zarathustra interrupted the speaker, “how it is harder to give rightly than to take rightly, and that giving well is an art, and the last and most cunning master-art of goodness.”

“Especially nowadays,” answered the voluntary beggar: “today, that is, when everything low has become rebellious and shy and haughty in its own way: in the way of the mob, that is.

For the hour has come, you know it already, for the great, grim, long, slow uprising of the mob and the slaves: it grows and grows!

Now all charity and petty giving-away outrages the lowly; and the overrich had better be on their guard!

Whoever today drips like a big-bellied bottle out of a far too narrow neck: — people today like nothing better than to break the necks of such bottles.

Lustful greed, bilious envy, sullen vengefulness, mob pride: all of it leaped into my face. It is no longer true that the poor are blessed. But the kingdom of heaven is among the cows.”

“And why is it not among the rich?” asked Zarathustra, to tempt him, while he warded off the cows, which were snuffling trustingly at the peaceable one.

“Why do you tempt me?” the other answered. “You know it yourself even better than I do. What drove me to the poorest, oh Zarathustra? Was it not disgust at our richest?

— at the convicts of riches, who pick their profit out of every rubbish heap, with cold eyes and lecherous thoughts, at this rabble that stinks to high heaven,

— at this gilded, falsified mob, whose fathers were pickpockets, or carrion birds, or ragpickers, with wives who were willing, lustful, forgetful: — for none of them has far to go to be a whore —

Mob above, mob below! What do “poor” and “rich” mean anymore today! I unlearned that difference, — and so I fled, farther, ever farther, until I came to these cows.”

Thus spoke the peaceable one, and he himself snorted and sweated as he spoke: so that the cows wondered all over again. But Zarathustra kept looking him in the face with a smile while he talked so harshly, and silently shook his head.

“You are doing yourself violence, you preacher on the mount, when you use such hard words. Your mouth did not grow for such hardness, nor your eye.

Nor, it seems to me, your stomach itself: it turns against all such raging and hating and frothing over. Your stomach wants gentler things: you are no butcher.

Rather, you seem to me a plant-eater and a root-man. Perhaps you grind grain. But surely you are averse to the pleasures of the flesh, and you love honey.”

“You have guessed me well,” answered the voluntary beggar, with a lighter heart. “I love honey, I grind grain too, for I sought what tastes sweet and makes the breath pure:

— and also what takes a long time, a day’s work and a jaw’s work for gentle idlers and day-wasters.

These cows, of course, have gone furthest of all: they invented chewing the cud and lying in the sun for themselves. And they abstain from all heavy thoughts, which bloat the heart.”

“— Well then!” said Zarathustra: “you ought to see my animals too, my eagle and my serpent, — there are none like them on earth today.

Look, that way leads the path to my cave: be its guest tonight. And talk with my animals about the happiness of animals, —

— until I come home myself. For now a cry of distress calls me away from you in haste. You will also find new honey at my place, ice-fresh golden honey in the comb: eat it!

But now take leave of your cows, quickly, you strange one! you lovely one! however hard it may be for you. For they are your warmest friends and teachers!” —

“— All but one, whom I love even more,” answered the voluntary beggar. “You yourself are good, and better even than a cow, oh Zarathustra!”

“Away, away with you! you wicked flatterer!” cried Zarathustra with malice, “why do you spoil me with such praise and flattery-honey?”

“Away, away from me!” he cried once more, and swung his stick at the tender beggar: but the beggar ran nimbly away.

The Shadow

But scarcely had the voluntary beggar run off and Zarathustra was alone with himself again, when he heard a new voice behind him, calling, “Stop! Zarathustra! Wait for me! It’s me, oh Zarathustra, me, your shadow!” But Zarathustra did not wait, for a sudden annoyance came over him at all the crowding and thronging in his mountains. “Where has my solitude gone?” he said.

Truly, it is getting to be too much for me; these mountains are swarming, my kingdom is no longer of this world, I need new mountains.

My shadow is calling me? What does my shadow matter! Let him run after me! I — will run away from him.” —

Thus spoke Zarathustra to his heart, and ran away. But the one who was behind him followed after him: so that soon there were three runners one behind the other, namely the voluntary beggar in front, then Zarathustra, and third and hindmost his shadow. They had not run like this for long before Zarathustra came to his senses about his folly and with one jerk shook off all his annoyance and weariness.

“What!” he said, “have not the most ridiculous things always happened to us old hermits and saints?

Truly, my folly has grown tall in the mountains! Now I hear six old fools’ legs clattering along one behind the other!

But may Zarathustra really be afraid of a shadow? Besides, it seems to me, when all is said and done, that he has longer legs than I do.”

Thus spoke Zarathustra, laughing with his eyes and his bowels; he stopped and turned around quickly — and look, in doing so he almost threw his follower and shadow to the ground: so closely was the shadow already following on his heels, and so weak was he, too. For when Zarathustra looked him over with his eyes, he was startled as if by a sudden ghost: so thin, swarthy, hollow, and worn-out did this follower look.

“Who are you?” asked Zarathustra fiercely. “What are you doing here? And why do you call yourself my shadow? I do not like you.”

“Forgive me,” answered the shadow, “that it is I; and if you do not like me, well then, oh Zarathustra! in that I praise you and your good taste.

A wanderer I am, who has already walked a great deal behind your heels: always on the way, but without a goal, without a home too: so that truly I lack little of being the eternal Wandering Jew, except that I am not eternal, and not a Jew either.

What? Must I always be on the way? Whirled about by every wind, restless, driven on? Oh earth, you have grown too round for me!

I have already sat on every surface; like tired dust I have fallen asleep on mirrors and windowpanes: everything takes from me, nothing gives, I am growing thin, — I almost resemble a shadow.

But it was you, oh Zarathustra, whom I flew and trailed after longest; and though I hid from you, still I was your best shadow: wherever you sat, I sat too.

With you I haunted the farthest, coldest worlds, like a ghost that runs of its own free will over winter roofs and snow.

With you I strove into everything forbidden, everything worst, everything farthest: and if anything in me is virtue, it is that I had no fear of any prohibition.

With you I broke whatever my heart ever revered, I overturned all boundary stones and images, I ran after the most dangerous wishes, — truly, I once ran right over every crime there is.

With you I unlearned my faith in words and values and great names. When the devil sheds his skin, does his name not fall off too? For that too is skin. The devil himself is perhaps — skin.

“Nothing is true, everything is permitted”: so I told myself. I plunged into the coldest waters, head and heart. Ah, how often did I stand there afterward naked, red as a lobster!

Ah, where did all goodness go, and all shame, and all faith in the good! Ah, where is that lying innocence I once had, the innocence of the good and of their noble lies!

Too often, truly, I followed close on the heels of truth: then it kicked me in the head. Sometimes I thought I was lying, and look! only then did I hit upon — the truth.

Too much has become clear to me: now none of it concerns me anymore. Nothing lives anymore that I love, — how should I still love myself?

“To live as I please, or not to live at all”: that is how I want it, that is how the holiest man wants it too. But woe! what is there that still — pleases me?

Do I — still have a goal? A harbor toward which my sail is running?

A good wind? Ah, only the one who knows where he is sailing also knows which wind is good and is his fair wind.

What is left to me now? A heart weary and insolent; a restless will; fluttering wings; a broken backbone.

This hunting for my home: oh Zarathustra, do you know, this home-hunting was my haunting, it is eating me up.

“Where is — my home?” I ask and search and have searched for it, and I have not found it. Oh eternal everywhere, oh eternal nowhere, oh eternal — in vain!”

Thus spoke the shadow, and Zarathustra’s face grew long at his words. “You are my shadow!” he said at last, with sadness.

Your danger is no small one, you free spirit and wanderer! You have had a bad day: see to it that an even worse evening does not come to you!

To restless ones like you, in the end even a prison seems blissful. Have you ever seen how captured criminals sleep? They sleep peacefully, they enjoy their new security.

Beware that in the end a narrow faith does not capture you, a hard, strict delusion! For from now on anything that is narrow and firm seduces and tempts you.

You have lost your goal: woe, how will you laugh off and live down this loss? With it — you have lost your way as well!

You poor roamer and rover, you tired butterfly! Do you want a rest and a home for this evening? Then go up to my cave!

That way leads the path to my cave. And now I want to run away from you again, quickly. Already something lies on me like a shadow.

I want to run alone, so that it grows bright around me again. For that I must stay merrily on my legs a long while yet. But this evening at my place there will be — dancing!” —

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

At Noon

— And Zarathustra ran and ran and found no one anymore, and was alone and found himself again and again, and enjoyed and sipped his solitude and thought of good things, — for hours. But around the hour of noon, when the sun stood directly over Zarathustra’s head, he passed an old, crooked, gnarled tree, which was embraced all around by the rich love of a grapevine and hidden from itself: from it yellow grapes hung down in plenty toward the wanderer. Then he felt the urge to quench a little thirst and break off a bunch of grapes for himself; but when he was already stretching out his arm for it, he felt the urge for something else even more: namely, to lie down beside the tree, at the hour of perfect noon, and sleep.

This Zarathustra did; and as soon as he lay on the ground, in the stillness and secrecy of the many-colored grass, he had already forgotten his little thirst and fell asleep. For, as Zarathustra’s proverb says: One thing is more necessary than another. Only his eyes stayed open: — for they could not get enough of seeing and praising the tree and the love of the vine. But as he fell asleep, Zarathustra spoke to his heart like this:

Still! Still! Did the world not just now become perfect? What is happening to me?

As a delicate wind, unseen, dances on an inlaid sea, light, light as a feather: so — sleep dances on me.

It closes no eye of mine, it leaves my soul awake. It is light, truly! light as a feather.

It persuades me, I do not know how?, it dabs at me inside with a coaxing hand, it compels me. Yes, it compels me, so that my soul stretches out: —

— how long and tired it grows, my strange soul! Did a seventh day’s evening come to it right at noon? Has it wandered too long already, blissful, among good and ripe things?

It stretches out long, long, — longer! It lies still, my strange soul. Too many good things it has tasted already; a golden sadness weighs on it, it twists its mouth.

— Like a ship that has run into its stillest bay: — now it leans against the earth, tired of the long voyages and the uncertain seas. Is the earth not more faithful?

As such a ship lays itself alongside the land and nestles against it: — then it is enough for a spider to spin its thread to it from the land. No stronger ropes are needed there.

Like such a tired ship in the stillest bay: so I too rest now close to the earth, faithful, trusting, waiting, tied to it with the faintest threads.

Oh happiness! Oh happiness! Do you want to sing, oh my soul? You are lying in the grass. But this is the secret, solemn hour when no shepherd plays his flute.

Take care! Hot noon is sleeping on the meadows. Do not sing! Still! The world is perfect.

Do not sing, you songbird of the grass, oh my soul! Do not even whisper! Look — still! old noon is sleeping, it moves its mouth: is it not just now drinking a drop of happiness —

— an old brown drop of golden happiness, golden wine? Something flits across it, its happiness laughs. So — a god laughs. Still! —

— “For happiness, how little is enough for happiness!” So I spoke once, and thought myself clever. But it was a blasphemy: that I have learned now. Clever fools speak better.

Precisely the least, the softest, the lightest, a lizard’s rustling, a breath, a flit, a twinkling of an eye — little makes up the best happiness. Still!

— What happened to me: Listen! Did time fly away? Am I not falling? Did I not fall — listen! into the well of eternity?

— What is happening to me? Still! It stings me — woe — in the heart? In the heart! Oh break, break, heart, after such happiness, after such a sting!

— What? Did the world not just now become perfect? Round and ripe? Oh, the golden round ring, the ripe ring — where is it flying off to? Let me run after it! Quick!

Still — — (and here Zarathustra stretched himself and felt that he was asleep.) —

Up! he said to himself, you sleeper! You noon-sleeper! Come on, get up, you old legs! It is time and overtime, many a good stretch of road is still left for you —

Now you have slept your fill, but for how long? Half an eternity! Come on, get up now, my old heart! How long will it be after such a sleep before you may — wake yourself up?

(But then he was already falling asleep again, and his soul spoke against him and resisted and lay down again) — “Leave me alone! Still! Did the world not just now become perfect? Oh, the golden round ball!” —

“Get up,” said Zarathustra, “you little thief, you stealer of days! What? Still stretching, yawning, sighing, falling down into deep wells?

Who are you, then! Oh my soul!” (and here he took fright, for a sunbeam fell down from the sky onto his face)

“Oh heaven above me,” he said with a sigh, and sat up straight, “are you watching me? Are you listening to my strange soul?

When will you drink this drop of dew that fell down on all earthly things, — when will you drink this strange soul —

— when, well of eternity! you bright, dreadful abyss of noon! when will you drink my soul back into yourself?”

Thus spoke Zarathustra, and rose from his resting place by the tree as if from a strange drunkenness: and look, there the sun still stood directly over his head. From this, though, one might rightly conclude that Zarathustra had not slept long that time.

The Welcome

It was not until late in the afternoon that Zarathustra, after long and useless searching and roaming about, came home to his cave again. But when he stood facing it, no more than twenty paces away, there happened what he now least expected: once again he heard the great cry of distress. And, astonishing! this time it came from his own cave. But it was a long, manifold, strange cry, and Zarathustra could clearly make out that it was made up of many voices: even though, heard from far away, it might sound like the cry from a single mouth.

Then Zarathustra sprang toward his cave, and look! what a sight for the eyes awaited him after that treat for the ears! For there they sat all together, the ones he had passed by during the day: the king on the right and the king on the left, the old magician, the pope, the voluntary beggar, the shadow, the conscientious one of the spirit, the sad soothsayer, and the donkey; but the ugliest man had put on a crown and wound two purple sashes around himself, — for, like all ugly people, he loved to dress up and play the beauty. But in the middle of this gloomy company stood Zarathustra’s eagle, ruffled and restless, for he was supposed to answer too many things for which his pride had no answer; and the shrewd serpent hung around his neck.

All this Zarathustra looked at in great amazement; then he examined each one of his guests with friendly curiosity, read their souls, and wondered again. Meanwhile the assembled company had risen from their seats and were waiting in reverence for Zarathustra to speak. But Zarathustra spoke like this:

“You despairing ones! You strange ones! So it was your cry of distress I heard? And now I also know where to look for the one I looked for in vain today: the higher man —:

— in my own cave he sits, the higher man! But why am I surprised! Did I not lure him to me myself, with honey sacrifices and the cunning calls of my happiness?

But it seems to me that you are poor company for one another, you make each other’s hearts sour, you who cry out in distress, when you sit here together? Someone has to come first,

— someone who will make you laugh again, a good merry clown, a dancer and a wind and a wild thing, some old fool: — what do you think?

But forgive me, you despairing ones, for talking to you in such small words, unworthy, truly!, of such guests! But you do not guess what makes my heart so mischievous: —

— you yourselves do it, and the sight of you, forgive me! For everyone grows brave who looks at a despairing man. To talk courage into a despairing man — everyone thinks himself strong enough for that.

You gave this strength to me myself, — a good gift, my high guests! A proper guest-gift! Well then, do not be angry now if I offer you something of mine too.

This here is my kingdom and my dominion: but what is mine shall be yours for this evening and this night. My animals shall serve you: let my cave be your resting place!

At home with me no one shall despair; in my territory I protect everyone from his wild beasts. And that is the first thing I offer you: safety!

But the second is: my little finger. And once you have that, go ahead and take the whole hand too! and the heart along with it! Welcome here, welcome, my guests and friends!”

Thus spoke Zarathustra, and laughed with love and malice. After this welcome his guests bowed once more and were silent in reverence; but the king on the right answered him in their name.

“By the way you offered us your hand and your greeting, oh Zarathustra, we recognize you as Zarathustra. You humbled yourself before us; you almost hurt our reverence —:

— but who could humble himself as you do, with such pride? That lifts us up ourselves; it is a refreshment to our eyes and hearts.

Just to see this, we would gladly climb higher mountains than this mountain. For we came as sightseers, we wanted to see what makes dim eyes bright.

And look, already all our cries of distress are over. Already our minds and hearts stand open and are enchanted. Little is missing: and our courage will turn to mischief.

Nothing more delightful grows on earth, oh Zarathustra, than a high, strong will: it is the earth’s most beautiful growth. A whole landscape is refreshed by one such tree.

I compare to the pine whoever grows up like you, oh Zarathustra: tall, silent, hard, alone, of the best and most supple wood, magnificent, —

— but in the end reaching out with strong green branches for its dominion, asking strong questions of the winds and the storms and whatever else is at home on the heights,

— answering more strongly, a commander, a victor: oh, who would not climb high mountains to see such growths?

By your tree here, oh Zarathustra, even the gloomy one, the failure, is refreshed; at the sight of you even the restless one grows steady and heals his heart.

And truly, many eyes today are turned toward your mountain and your tree; a great longing has set out, and some have learned to ask: who is Zarathustra?

And all into whose ears you ever dripped your song and your honey: all the hidden ones, those who live alone and those who live alone together, said all at once to their hearts:

‘Is Zarathustra still alive? It is no longer worth living, all is the same, all is in vain: or else — we must live with Zarathustra!’

‘Why does he not come, he who announced himself so long ago? so many ask; did solitude swallow him up? Or should we perhaps come to him?’

Now it is happening that solitude itself grows brittle and breaks open, like a grave that breaks open and can no longer hold its dead. Everywhere one sees the risen.

Now the waves are rising and rising around your mountain, oh Zarathustra. And however high your height may be, many must come up to you; your boat shall not sit on dry land much longer.

And that we despairing ones have now come into your cave and already despair no longer: it is only a token and an omen that better ones are on their way to you, —

— for he himself is on his way to you, the last remnant of God among men, that is: all the men of the great longing, of the great disgust, of the great weariness,

— all who do not want to live unless they learn to hope again — unless they learn from you, oh Zarathustra, the great hope!”

Thus spoke the king on the right, and took Zarathustra’s hand to kiss it; but Zarathustra fended off his veneration and stepped back in alarm, silent and sudden, as if fleeing into far distances. But after a little while he was back with his guests again, looked at them with clear, searching eyes, and said:

My guests, you higher men, I want to speak to you in plain German, and plainly. It was not for you that I was waiting here in these mountains.

(“Plain German, and plainly? God have mercy!” said the king on the left at this point, aside; “you can tell he does not know the dear Germans, this wise man from the East!

But he means ‘German, and gruffly’ — well then! These days that is not yet the worst taste!”)

“You may all be higher men, truly,” Zarathustra went on: “but for me — you are not high and strong enough.

For me, that means: for the inexorable thing in me that is silent, but will not always be silent. And if you do belong to me, it is still not as my right arm.

For whoever stands on sick and delicate legs himself, as you do, wants above all, whether he knows it or hides it from himself: to be spared.

But my arms and my legs I do not spare, I do not spare my warriors: how could you be any use in my war?

With you I would spoil every victory of mine. And some of you would fall over just from hearing the loud roll of my drums.

Nor are you beautiful enough for me, or well-born enough. I need pure, smooth mirrors for my teachings; on your surface even my own image is distorted.

Many a burden presses on your shoulders, many a memory; many a nasty dwarf crouches in your corners. There is a hidden mob in you too.

And even if you are high and of a higher kind: much in you is crooked and misshapen. There is no smith in the world who could hammer you right and straight for me.

You are only bridges: may higher ones stride across over you! You stand for steps: so do not be angry with the one who climbs over you into his height!

From your seed a true son and perfect heir may one day grow for me too: but that is far off. You yourselves are not the ones to whom my inheritance and name belong.

It is not for you that I am waiting here in these mountains, it is not with you that I may go down for the last time. You came to me only as an omen that higher ones are already on their way to me, —

— not the men of the great longing, of the great disgust, of the great weariness, and what you called the remains of God.

— No! No! Three times no! It is for others that I am waiting here in these mountains, and I will not lift my foot from here without them,

— for higher ones, stronger ones, more victorious ones, more cheerful ones, ones who are built square in body and soul: laughing lions must come!

Oh my guests and friends, you strange ones, — have you heard nothing yet of my children? And that they are on their way to me?

Do speak to me of my gardens, of my Blessed Isles, of my new, beautiful kind, — why do you not speak to me of them?

This guest-gift I ask of your love, that you speak to me of my children. For this I am rich, for this I became poor: what have I not given away,

— what would I not give away to have one thing: these children, this living plantation, these trees of life of my will and of my highest hope!”

Thus spoke Zarathustra, and suddenly broke off his speech: for his longing came over him, and he closed his eyes and his mouth against the turmoil of his heart. And all his guests too were silent and stood still and dismayed: except that the old soothsayer made signs with his hands and gestures.

The Last Supper

For at this point the soothsayer interrupted the welcome between Zarathustra and his guests: he pushed his way forward like someone with no time to lose, seized Zarathustra’s hand, and cried: “But Zarathustra!

One thing is more necessary than another, you say so yourself: well then, one thing is more necessary to me now than everything else.

A word at the right time: did you not invite me to a meal? And here are many who have come a long way. Surely you do not mean to fob us off with speeches?

Besides, all of you have already given me too much thought of freezing, drowning, suffocating, and other bodily emergencies: but nobody has given a thought to my emergency, namely starving —”

(Thus spoke the soothsayer; but when Zarathustra’s animals heard these words, they ran away in terror. For they saw that whatever they had brought home during the day would not be enough to stuff this one soothsayer.)

“Not to mention dying of thirst,” the soothsayer went on. “And although I hear water splashing here, like the speeches of wisdom, that is, abundantly and tirelessly: I — want wine!

Not everyone is a born water-drinker like Zarathustra. Nor is water any good for the tired and the withered: we deserve wine, — that alone gives sudden recovery and health on the spot!”

On this occasion, when the soothsayer was demanding wine, it happened that the king on the left, the silent one, got a word in for once too. “As for wine,” he said, “we have taken care of that, I and my brother, the king on the right: we have wine enough, — a whole donkey-load. So nothing is missing but bread.”

“Bread?” Zarathustra replied, and laughed as he said it. “Bread is the one thing hermits do not have. But man does not live by bread alone, but also by the meat of good lambs, of which I have two:

— these we should slaughter quickly and prepare with spice, with sage: that is how I love it. And there is no lack of roots and fruits either, good enough even for gourmets and fussy tasters; nor of nuts and other riddles to crack.

So in a little while we will make a good meal. But whoever wants to eat with us must also lend a hand, kings too. For at Zarathustra’s, even a king may be a cook.”

This proposal was after everyone’s heart: except that the voluntary beggar balked at meat and wine and spices.

“Now just listen to this glutton Zarathustra!” he said jokingly: “is that what people go into caves and high mountains for, to make meals like these?

Now of course I understand what he once taught us: ‘Praised be a little poverty!’ And why he wants to do away with beggars.”

“Be of good cheer,” Zarathustra answered him, “as I am. Keep to your custom, you excellent man, grind your grain, drink your water, praise your cooking: as long as it makes you cheerful!

I am a law only for my own, I am not a law for all. But whoever belongs to me must be strong of bone, and light of foot too, —

— glad for wars and feasts, no gloom-monger, no dreamy dawdler, ready for the hardest thing as for his feast, healthy and whole.

The best belongs to my own and to me; and if we are not given it, we take it: — the best food, the purest sky, the strongest thoughts, the most beautiful women!” —

Thus spoke Zarathustra; but the king on the right replied: “Strange! Did anyone ever hear such clever things from the mouth of a wise man?

And truly, that is the strangest thing about a wise man, when on top of everything he is also clever and no donkey.”

Thus spoke the king on the right, and wondered; but the donkey said Yeah-aw to his speech, with ill will. This, though, was the beginning of that long meal which is called “the Last Supper” in the history books. And at it nothing else was talked about but the higher man.

On the Higher Man

I

When I came to people for the first time, I committed the hermit’s folly, the great folly: I set myself up in the marketplace.

And when I spoke to all, I spoke to none. But in the evening tightrope walkers were my companions, and corpses; and I myself was almost a corpse.

But with the new morning a new truth came to me: then I learned to say, “What do the marketplace and the mob and the mob’s noise and the mob’s long ears matter to me!”

You higher men, learn this from me: in the marketplace no one believes in higher men. And if you want to speak there, very well! But the mob blinks: “We are all equal.”

“You higher men” — so the mob blinks — “there are no higher men, we are all equal, man is man, before God — we are all equal!”

Before God! — But now this God has died. And before the mob we do not want to be equal. You higher men, go away from the marketplace!

II

Before God! — But now this God has died! You higher men, this God was your greatest danger.

Only since he has been lying in the grave have you risen again. Only now does the great noon come, only now does the higher man become — master!

Did you understand this word, oh my brothers? You are frightened: do your hearts grow dizzy? Does the abyss yawn for you here? Does the hellhound yelp at you here?

Well then! Up with you! You higher men! Only now is the mountain of man’s future in labor. God died: now we want — the overman to live.

III

The most careful ask today: “How is man to be preserved?” But Zarathustra asks, as the only one and the first: “How is man to be overcome?”

The overman lies close to my heart, he is my first and only one — and not man: not the neighbor, not the poorest, not the one who suffers most, not the best —

Oh my brothers, what I can love in man is that he is a going over and a going under. And in you too there is much that makes me love and hope.

That you despised, you higher men, that makes me hope. For the great despisers are the great reverers.

That you despaired, there is much to honor in that. For you did not learn how to submit, you did not learn the small prudences.

For today the small people have become master: they all preach submission and modesty and prudence and diligence and consideration and the long and-so-on of the small virtues.

Whatever is of woman’s kind, whatever comes from the serf’s kind, and especially the mob-hodgepodge: that now wants to become master of all human destiny — oh disgust! Disgust! Disgust!

That asks and asks and never grows tired: “How does man preserve himself, best, longest, most pleasantly?” With that — they are the masters of today.

Overcome these masters of today for me, oh my brothers — these small people: they are the overman’s greatest danger!

Overcome for me, you higher men, the small virtues, the small prudences, the grain-of-sand considerations, the anthill’s crawling clutter, the wretched contentment, the “happiness of the greatest number” —!

And despair rather than submit. And, truly, I love you for this, that you do not know how to live today, you higher men! For that is how you live — best!

IV

Do you have courage, oh my brothers? Are you brave-hearted? Not courage before witnesses, but the courage of hermits and eagles, which not even a god watches anymore?

Cold souls, mules, the blind, the drunken I do not call brave-hearted. He has heart who knows fear but masters fear; who sees the abyss, but with pride.

Whoever sees the abyss, but with an eagle’s eyes; whoever grasps the abyss with an eagle’s talons: he has courage. —

V

“Man is evil” — so all the wisest told me, for my comfort. Ah, if only it is still true today! For evil is man’s best strength.

“Man must become better and more evil” — so I teach. The most evil is necessary for the overman’s best.

It may have been good for that preacher of the small people that he suffered and bore the sin of man. But I rejoice in great sin as my great comfort. —

But such things are not said for long ears. Not every word belongs in every snout, either. These are fine, faraway things: sheep’s hooves shall not grab at them!

VI

You higher men, do you think I am here to make good what you made bad?

Or that from now on I wanted to make softer beds for you sufferers? Or to show you restless ones, you who have strayed, you who have climbed astray, new and easier footpaths?

No! No! Three times no! Ever more, ever better ones of your kind shall perish — for you shall have it ever worse and harder. Only so —

— only so does man grow up to that height where the lightning strikes him and breaks him: high enough for the lightning!

My mind and my longing go out toward the few, the long, the far: what would your small, many, short misery matter to me!

You do not yet suffer enough for me! For you suffer from yourselves; you have not yet suffered from man. You would be lying if you said otherwise! None of you suffers from what I suffered. —

VII

It is not enough for me that the lightning no longer does harm. I do not want to conduct it away: it shall learn — to work for me. —

My wisdom has long been gathering like a cloud; it grows stiller and darker. So does every wisdom that is one day to give birth to lightning. —

To these people of today I do not want to be light, nor to be called light. Them — I want to blind: lightning of my wisdom! Put out their eyes!

VIII

Will nothing beyond your ability: there is a wicked falseness in those who will beyond their ability.

Especially when they will great things! For they awaken mistrust of great things, these subtle counterfeiters and actors: —

— until at last they are false even before themselves, squint-eyed, whitewashed worm-rot, cloaked in strong words, in shop-window virtues, in glittering false works.

Be well on your guard there, you higher men! For nothing today is more precious to me, and rarer, than honesty.

Does this today not belong to the mob? But the mob does not know what is great, what is small, what is straight and honest: it is innocently crooked, it always lies.

IX

Have a good mistrust today, you higher men, you brave-hearted ones! You openhearted ones! And keep your reasons secret! For this today belongs to the mob.

What the mob once learned to believe without reasons, who could, with reasons — overturn that for it?

And in the marketplace one persuades with gestures. But reasons make the mob mistrustful.

And if truth ever did win a victory there, then ask yourselves with good mistrust: “What strong error fought for it?”

Beware of the scholars too! They hate you: for they are barren! They have cold, dried-up eyes; before them every bird lies plucked.

Such people boast that they do not lie: but being powerless to lie is still far from being love of truth. Beware!

Freedom from fever is still far from being knowledge! I do not believe in spirits that have gone cold. Whoever cannot lie does not know what truth is.

X

If you want to rise high, use your own legs! Do not let yourselves be carried up; do not sit on other people’s backs and heads!

But you have mounted a horse? You are riding briskly up to your goal now? Well then, my friend! But your lame foot is riding on the horse too!

When you are at your goal, when you leap down from your horse: right on your height, you higher man — you will stumble!

XI

You creators, you higher men! One is pregnant only with one’s own child.

Do not let anyone talk you into anything, or talk anything into you! Who, then, is your neighbor? And even if you act “for the neighbor” — you still do not create for him!

Unlearn this “for” for me, you creators: your very virtue wants you to do nothing with “for” and “in order to” and “because.” You shall seal your ears against these false little words.

“For the neighbor” is the virtue only of small people: there they say “like with like” and “one hand washes the other”: — they have neither the right nor the strength for your self-interest!

In your self-interest, you creators, is the caution and the foresight of the pregnant! What no one has yet seen with eyes, the fruit: that is what your whole love shelters and spares and feeds.

Where your whole love is, with your child, there is your whole virtue too! Your work, your will is your “neighbor”: do not let anyone talk you into false values!

XII

You creators, you higher men! Whoever must give birth is sick; but whoever has given birth is unclean.

Ask the women: one does not give birth because it is a pleasure. Pain makes hens and poets cackle.

You creators, there is much that is unclean in you. That is because you had to be mothers.

A new child: oh, how much new filth came into the world too! Step aside! And whoever has given birth shall wash his soul clean!

XIII

Do not be virtuous beyond your strength! And want nothing of yourselves that goes against probability!

Walk in the footsteps where your fathers’ virtue already walked! How would you climb high if your fathers’ will did not climb with you?

But whoever wants to be a firstling, let him see to it that he does not also become a lastling! And where the vices of your fathers are, there you should not want to pass for saints!

Someone whose fathers kept company with women and strong wines and wild boars: what would it be if he wanted chastity of himself?

It would be a folly! Truly, it seems a great deal to me for such a man if he is the husband of one or two or three women.

And if he founded monasteries and wrote over the door, “The way to holiness,” — I would still say: what for! It is a new folly!

He has founded himself a house of correction and a house of flight: much good may it do him! But I do not believe in it.

In solitude whatever one brings into it grows, the inner brute too. For this reason solitude is inadvisable for many.

Has there been anything filthier on earth so far than the saints of the desert? Around them not only was the devil loose — but the swine too.

XIV

Shy, ashamed, clumsy, like a tiger whose leap has failed: that, you higher men, is how I have often seen you slink aside. A throw had failed you.

But, you dice-players, what does it matter! You did not learn to play and to mock as one must play and mock! Are we not always sitting at a great table of mocking and playing?

And if great things failed you, does that make you yourselves — failures? And if you yourselves are failures, does that make man — a failure? But if man is a failure: well then! Up with you!

XV

The higher a thing’s kind, the more rarely it succeeds. You higher men here, have you not all — failed?

Take heart, what does it matter! How much is still possible! Learn to laugh at yourselves, as one must laugh!

And what wonder that you failed and half succeeded, you half-broken ones! Is it not — man’s future that presses and jostles in you?

Man’s farthest, deepest, star-highest, his tremendous strength: does not all of that foam against itself in your pot?

What wonder that many a pot breaks! Learn to laugh at yourselves, as one must laugh! You higher men, oh, how much is still possible!

And truly, how much has already succeeded! How rich this earth is in small, good, perfect things, in things that turned out well!

Set small, good, perfect things around you, you higher men! Their golden ripeness heals the heart. What is perfect teaches hope.

XVI

What has been the greatest sin here on earth so far? Was it not the word of the one who said, “Woe to those who laugh here!”

Did he find no reasons to laugh on the earth itself? Then he searched badly. Even a child finds reasons here.

He — did not love enough: otherwise he would have loved us too, the laughing ones! But he hated and jeered at us; he promised us weeping and gnashing of teeth.

Must one curse right away where one does not love? That — seems to me bad taste. But that is what he did, this unconditional one. He came from the mob.

And he himself simply did not love enough: otherwise he would have been less angry that people did not love him. All great love does not want love: — it wants more.

Get out of the way of all such unconditional ones! They are a poor, sick kind, a mob-kind: they look on this life with ill will, they have the evil eye for this earth.

Get out of the way of all such unconditional ones! They have heavy feet and sultry hearts: — they do not know how to dance. How could the earth be light to such as these!

XVII

All good things come near their goal crookedly. Like cats they arch their backs, they purr inwardly at their coming happiness — all good things laugh.

A person’s stride betrays whether he is already walking on his own path: so watch me walk! But whoever comes near his goal, dances.

And, truly, I have not become a statue, nor do I stand there yet, stiff, stolid, stony, a pillar; I love running fast.

And even if there are bogs and thick gloom on earth: whoever has light feet runs on over the mud and dances as on swept ice.

Lift up your hearts, my brothers, high! higher! And do not forget your legs either! Lift up your legs too, you good dancers, and better still: stand on your heads as well!

XVIII

This crown of the laughing one, this rose-wreath crown: I myself set this crown on my head, I myself have canonized my laughter. I found no one else strong enough for it today.

Zarathustra the dancer, Zarathustra the light one, who beckons with his wings, one ready for flight, beckoning to all birds, ready and set, a blissfully lighthearted one: —

Zarathustra the soothsayer, Zarathustra the sooth-laugher, no impatient one, no unconditional one, one who loves leaps and side-leaps; I myself set this crown on my head!

XIX

Lift up your hearts, my brothers, high! higher! And do not forget your legs either! Lift up your legs too, you good dancers, and better still: stand on your heads as well!

There are heavy beasts in happiness too; there are clubfoots from the very beginning. Strangely they toil away, like an elephant toiling to stand on its head.

But better to be foolish with happiness than foolish with misfortune, better to dance clumsily than to walk lamely. So learn this wisdom of mine from me: even the worst thing has two good reverse sides, —

— even the worst thing has good legs for dancing: so learn from me, you higher men, how to stand yourselves on your proper legs!

So unlearn your moping and all mob-sadness! Oh, how sad even the mob’s clowns seem to me today! But this today belongs to the mob.

XX

Do as the wind does when it rushes out of its mountain caves: it wants to dance to its own piping, and the seas tremble and leap under its footsteps.

The one who gives donkeys wings, who milks lionesses, praised be this good, unruly spirit, which comes like a storm wind to all of today and to all the mob, —

— which is the enemy of thistle-heads and fiddle-heads and of all withered leaves and weeds: praised be this wild, good, free storm-spirit, which dances on bogs and glooms as if on meadows!

Which hates the mob’s consumptive curs and all the botched and gloomy brood: praised be this spirit of all free spirits, the laughing storm, which blows dust into the eyes of all the black-sighted and the sore-blighted!

You higher men, the worst thing about you is this: none of you has learned to dance as one must dance — to dance beyond yourselves! What does it matter that you failed!

How much is still possible! So learn to laugh beyond yourselves! Lift up your hearts, you good dancers, high! higher! And do not forget good laughter either!

This crown of the laughing one, this rose-wreath crown: to you, my brothers, I throw this crown! I have canonized laughter; you higher men, learn — to laugh!

The Song of Melancholy

I

When Zarathustra was giving these discourses, he stood near the entrance of his cave; but with the last words he slipped away from his guests and fled for a little while into the open air.

“Oh pure smells around me,” he cried, “oh blissful stillness around me! But where are my animals? Come here, come here, my eagle and my serpent!

Tell me, my animals: these higher men, all of them together — do they perhaps not smell good? Oh pure smells around me! Only now do I know and feel how much I love you, my animals.”

— And Zarathustra said once more: “I love you, my animals!” But the eagle and the serpent pressed close to him as he spoke these words, and looked up at him. In this way the three of them were quietly together, and sniffed and sipped the good air with one another. For the air out here was better than among the higher men.

II

But hardly had Zarathustra left his cave when the old magician got up, looked around slyly, and said: “He is out!

And already, you higher men — let me tickle you with this name of praise and flattery, just as he does — already my wicked spirit of deceit and magic is coming over me, my melancholy devil,

— who is an adversary of this Zarathustra from the ground up: forgive him for it! Now he wants to work magic before you, it is just his hour; in vain do I wrestle with this evil spirit.

To all of you, whatever honors you may give yourselves with words, whether you call yourselves ‘the free spirits’ or ‘the truthful’ or ‘the penitents of the spirit’ or ‘the unchained’ or ‘the great longers’ —

— to all of you who suffer from the great disgust as I do, for whom the old God has died and no new god yet lies in cradles and swaddling clothes — to all of you my evil spirit and magic-devil is well disposed.

I know you, you higher men, I know him — I know this fiend too, whom I love against my will, this Zarathustra: he himself often seems to me like a beautiful saint’s mask,

— like a new and wondrous masquerade, in which my evil spirit, the melancholy devil, takes pleasure: — I love Zarathustra, so it often seems to me, for the sake of my evil spirit. —

But already he is coming over me and forcing me, this spirit of melancholy, this devil of the evening twilight: and, truly, you higher men, he has a craving —

— just open your eyes! — he has a craving to come naked, whether male or female I do not know yet: but he is coming, he is forcing me, woe! Open your senses!

The day is dying away, evening now comes to all things, even to the best things; hear now, and see, you higher men, what devil, whether man or woman, this spirit of evening melancholy is!”

Thus spoke the old magician, looked around slyly, and then reached for his harp.

III

	When the air has cleared,
	When already the dew’s comfort
	Wells down onto the earth,
	Unseen, unheard as well:
	— For tender are the shoes
	The comforting dew wears, like all the gently comforting —:
	Do you remember then, do you remember, hot heart,
	How once you thirsted,
	For heavenly tears and the dripping of dew
	Thirsted, scorched and weary,
	While on yellow grassy paths
	Wicked evening glances of the sun
	Ran around you through black trees,
	Blinding, burning glances of the sun, gloating.
	“The suitor of truth? You?” — so they jeered —
	“No! Only a poet!
	An animal, a cunning, preying, creeping one,
	That has to lie,
	That knowingly, willingly has to lie:
	Lusting for prey,
	Masked in motley,
	Its own mask,
	Its own prey —
	That — the suitor of truth? No!
	Only a fool! Only a poet!
	Only speaking motley,
	Shrieking motley out of fool’s masks,
	Clambering about on lying bridges of words,
	On motley rainbows,
	Between false heavens
	And false earths,
	Roaming about, hovering about, —
	Only a fool! Only a poet! ...
	That — the suitor of truth?
	Not still, stiff, smooth, cold,
	Turned into an image,
	Into a pillar of God,
	Not set up before temples,
	A god’s doorkeeper:
	No! An enemy of such statues of truth,
	More at home in any wilderness than before temples,
	Full of a cat’s mischief,
	Leaping through every window,
	Whisk! into every accident,
	Sniffing toward every jungle,
	Sniffing with craving and longing,
	That you might run in jungles
	Among the motley-spotted beasts of prey,
	Sinfully healthy and motley and beautiful,
	With lusting lips,
	Blissfully mocking, blissfully hellish, blissfully bloodthirsty,
	Run preying, creeping, lying: ...
	Or, like the eagle, which long,
	Long stares fixedly into abysses,
	Into its own abysses: ...
	— Oh, how they coil down here,
	Downward, inward,
	Into ever deeper depths! —
	Then,
	Suddenly,
	On a straight track,
	In a blade-drawn attack,
	Swoop down on lambs,
	Headlong down, ravenous,
	Lusting for lambs,
	Grudging all lamb-souls,
	Grimly grudging all that looks
	Sheepish, lamb-eyed, curly-wooled,
	Gray, with lamb-and-sheep goodwill!
	So
	Eagle-like, panther-like
	Are the poet’s longings,
	Are your longings under a thousand masks,
	You fool! You poet!
	You who saw man
	As god as much as sheep —:
	To tear apart the god in man
	As much as the sheep in man,
	And, tearing, to laugh —

That, that is your bliss! A panther’s and an eagle’s bliss! A poet’s and a fool’s bliss!” —

	When the air has cleared,
	When already the moon’s sickle
	Creeps green between purple reds,
	And envious:
	— hostile to the day,
	With every step in secret
	Sickling at the hammocks of roses,
	Until they sink,
	Sink pale, down toward the night:
	So I myself once sank
	Out of my truth-madness,
	Out of my day-longings,
	Weary of day, sick from light,
	— sank downward, evenward, shadowward:
	By one single truth
	Burned and thirsty:
	— do you still remember, do you remember, hot heart,
	How you thirsted then? —
	That I should be banished
	From all truth,
	Only a fool! Only a poet!

On Science

Thus sang the magician; and all who were gathered there went unawares, like birds, into the net of his cunning and melancholy lust. Only the conscientious one of the spirit had not been caught: he quickly snatched the harp away from the magician and cried: “Air! Let good air in! Let Zarathustra in! You are making this cave sultry and poisonous, you wicked old magician!

You seduce us, you false one, you subtle one, toward unknown desires and wildernesses. And woe when people like you start talking and making a fuss about truth!

Woe to all free spirits who are not on their guard against such magicians! Their freedom is finished: you teach and lure them back into prisons, —

— you old melancholy devil, out of your lament sounds a decoy whistle; you are like those who, by praising chastity, secretly invite us to lusts!”

Thus spoke the conscientious one; but the old magician looked around him, enjoyed his victory, and for its sake swallowed the annoyance the conscientious one had caused him. “Be quiet!” he said in a modest voice. “Good songs want to echo well; after good songs one should be silent for a long time.

That is what all these are doing, the higher men. But you have probably understood little of my song? There is little of a magic spirit in you.”

“You praise me,” replied the conscientious one, “by setting me apart from yourself; very well! But you others, what do I see? You are all still sitting there with lusting eyes —:

You free souls, where has your freedom gone! You almost seem to me like people who have long been watching wicked naked girls dancing: your souls are dancing themselves!

In you, you higher men, there must be more of what the magician calls his evil spirit of magic and deceit: — we must be different, I suppose.

And truly, we talked and thought together enough before Zarathustra came home to his cave for me to know: we are different.

We seek different things up here too, you and I. For I seek more security; that is why I came to Zarathustra. For he is still the firmest tower and will —

— today, when everything totters, when all the earth quakes. But you, when I see the eyes you make, it almost seems to me that you seek more insecurity,

— more shudders, more danger, more earthquake. You crave, it almost seems to me, forgive my presumption, you higher men —

— you crave the worst, most dangerous life, the one that frightens me most, the life of wild animals, forests, caves, steep mountains and gorges to lose your way in.

And it is not the ones who lead you out of danger that please you best, but the ones who lead you off all paths, the misleaders. But if such craving in you is real, it still seems to me impossible.

For fear — that is man’s original and fundamental feeling; from fear everything is explained, original sin and original virtue. Out of fear grew my virtue too, and it is called: science.

For the fear of wild animals — that was bred into man longest of all, including the animal he hides and fears inside himself: — Zarathustra calls it ‘the inner brute.’

Such long, old fear, refined at last, made spiritual, made intellectual — today, it seems to me, it is called: science.” —

Thus spoke the conscientious one; but Zarathustra, who was just then coming back into his cave and had heard and guessed the last speech, threw the conscientious one a handful of roses and laughed at his “truths.” “What!” he cried, “what did I just hear? Truly, it seems to me that you are a fool, or else I am one myself: and your ‘truth’ I will stand on its head in a flash.

For fear — is our exception. But courage and adventure and joy in the uncertain, in the unventured — courage seems to me man’s whole prehistory.

He envied the wildest, most courageous animals all their virtues and robbed them of them: only so did he become — man.

This courage, refined at last, made spiritual, made intellectual, this human courage with eagle’s wings and serpent’s shrewdness: that, it seems to me, is called today —”

“Zarathustra!” cried all who sat together there, as if from one mouth, and they broke into a great laughter; but it rose from them like a heavy cloud. The magician laughed too, and said shrewdly: “Well then! He is gone, my evil spirit!

And did I not warn you against him myself, when I said that he was a deceiver, a spirit of lies and deceit?

Especially when he shows himself naked. But what can I do about his tricks! Did I create him and the world?

Well then! Let us be good again, and in good spirits! And even though Zarathustra looks angry — just look at him! he is cross with me —:

— before night comes he will learn again to love me and praise me; he cannot live long without committing such follies.

He — loves his enemies: he understands this art best of anyone I have seen. But he takes revenge for it — on his friends!”

Thus spoke the old magician, and the higher men applauded him: so that Zarathustra went around and, with malice and love, shook hands with his friends — like someone who has something to make good and to apologize for to everyone. But when, as he did so, he came to the door of his cave, look, he was already craving the good air out there again, and his animals — and he wanted to slip outside.

Among Daughters of the Desert

I

“Do not go away!” said the wanderer who called himself Zarathustra’s shadow, “stay with us, or the old, heavy gloom may fall upon us again.

That old magician has already treated us to the worst he has, and look, the good pious pope there has tears in his eyes and has set sail once more on the sea of melancholy.

These kings may still put on a good face in front of us: for they have learned that best of all of us today! But if they had no witnesses, I bet the evil game would start again with them too —

— the evil game of drifting clouds, of damp melancholy, of veiled skies, of stolen suns, of howling autumn winds,

— the evil game of our howling and our cries of distress: stay with us, oh Zarathustra! Here is much hidden misery that wants to speak, much evening, much cloud, much heavy air!

You fed us on strong man’s food and powerful sayings: do not let the soft, womanish spirits fall upon us again for dessert!

You alone make the air around you strong and clear! Did I ever find such good air anywhere on earth as with you in your cave?

And I have seen many lands; my nose has learned to test and appraise many kinds of air: but with you my nostrils taste their greatest delight!

Unless it be — unless it be —, oh, forgive an old memory! Forgive me an old after-dinner song that I once composed among daughters of the desert: —

— for with them there was the same good, bright, Eastern air; there I was farthest from cloudy, damp, melancholy Old Europe!

Back then I loved such Eastern girls and other blue kingdoms of heaven, over which no clouds and no thoughts hang.

You would not believe how prettily they sat there when they were not dancing, deep, but without thoughts, like little secrets, like beribboned riddles, like dessert nuts —

colorful and strange, truly! but without clouds: riddles that let themselves be guessed: for the love of such girls I made up an after-dinner psalm back then.”

Thus spoke the wanderer and shadow; and before anyone could answer him, he had already seized the old magician’s harp, crossed his legs, and looked around him, calm and wise: — but with his nostrils he drew in the air slowly and questioningly, like someone tasting new, foreign air in new lands. Then he began to sing with a kind of roar.

II

The desert grows: woe to him who harbors deserts!

	— Ha! Solemn!
	Solemn indeed!
	A worthy beginning!
	African-solemn!
	Worthy of a lion,
	Or of a moral howler monkey —
	— but nothing for you,
	You loveliest lady friends,
	At whose feet I,
	For the first time,
	A European, under palm trees,
	Am granted leave to sit. Selah.
	Wonderful, truly!
	Here I sit now,
	Near the desert and already
	So far from the desert again,
	And in no way yet deserted:
	That is, swallowed down
	By this smallest of oases —:
	— she had just opened, yawning,
	Her lovely jaws wide,
	The sweetest-smelling of all little mouths:
	And in I fell,
	Down, right through — among you,
	You loveliest lady friends! Selah.
	Hail, hail to that whale,
	If he made things so pleasant
	For his guest! — you understand
	My learned allusion?
	Hail to his belly,
	If it was
	So lovely an oasis-belly
	As this one: which, however, I call into doubt,
	— for I come, after all, from Europe,
	Which is more addicted to doubt than any
	Elderly little wife.
	May God make it better!
	Amen!
	Here I sit now,
	In this smallest of oases,
	Like a date,
	Brown, sweet through and through, oozing gold, lusting
	For a round girl’s mouth,
	But even more for girlish,
	Ice-cold, snow-white, sharp-edged
	Biting teeth: for it is after these
	That the heart of every burning date pants. Selah.
	Like the southern fruits just named,
	Like them, all too like them,
	I lie here, by little
	Winged beetles
	Danced around and played around,
	And likewise by still smaller,
	More foolish, more malicious
	Wishes and whims,
	Besieged by you,
	You mute, you foreboding
	Girl-cats,
