	Dudu and Suleika,
	— ensphinxed, if I may cram into one word
	A great deal of feeling:
	(May God forgive me
	This sin against language!)
	— I sit here, sniffing the finest air,
	The air of paradise, truly,
	Bright, light air, striped with gold,
	As good an air as ever
	Fell down from the moon —
	Whether it was by accident,
	Or did it happen out of exuberance?
	As the old poets tell.
	But I, a doubter, call it
	Into doubt, for, after all, I come
	From Europe,
	Which is more addicted to doubt than any
	Elderly little wife.
	May God make it better!
	Amen!
	Drinking this loveliest air,
	With nostrils swelled like goblets,
	Without future, without memories,
	So I sit here, you
	Loveliest lady friends,
	And watch the palm tree,
	How she, like a dancing girl,
	Bows and bends and sways her hips,
	— one does it too, if one watches long!
	Like a dancing girl who, as it seems to me,
	Too long already, dangerously long,
	Has always, always stood on just one leg?
	— so that she forgot, as it seems to me,
	Her other little leg?
	In vain, at least,
	I searched for the missing twin jewel
	— that is, the other leg —
	In the holy vicinity
	Of her loveliest, daintiest
	Fanning and fluttering and flittering little skirt.
	Yes, if you, you beautiful lady friends,
	Will believe me entirely:
	She has lost it!
	It is gone!
	Gone forever!
	The other leg!
	Oh, what a pity about that lovely other leg!
	Where — might it linger now, and grieve, forsaken?
	The lonely leg?
	In fear, perhaps, of a
	Fierce, yellow, blond-maned
	Lion-monster? Or even, already,
	Gnawed off, nibbled away —
	Wretched, woe! woe! nibbled away! Selah.
	Oh, do not weep,
	Soft hearts!
	Do not weep, you
	Date-hearts! Milk-bosoms!
	You licorice-heart-
	Pouches!
	Weep no more,
	Pale Dudu!
	Be a man, Suleika! Courage! Courage!
	— Or should perhaps
	Something strengthening, heart-strengthening,
	Be in order here?
	An anointed saying?
	A solemn word of comfort? —
	Ha! Rise up, dignity!
	Virtue’s dignity! European dignity!
	Blow, blow again,
	Bellows of virtue!
	Ha!
	Roar once more,
	Roar morally!
	As a moral lion,
	Roar before the daughters of the desert!
	— For virtue’s howling,
	You loveliest of girls,
	Is more than anything
	European fervor, European ravenous hunger!
	And here I stand already,
	As a European,
	I can do no other, God help me!
	Amen!

The desert grows: woe to him who harbors deserts!

The Awakening

I

After the song of the wanderer and shadow, the cave was all at once full of noise and laughter; and since the assembled guests were all talking at the same time, and even the donkey, with such encouragement, no longer kept quiet, a little aversion and mockery toward his visitors came over Zarathustra: even though he rejoiced at their gladness. For it seemed to him a sign of convalescence. So he slipped out into the open and spoke to his animals.

“Where has their distress gone now?” he said, and already he himself breathed more freely after his little weariness, — “with me, it seems to me, they have unlearned their cries of distress!

— though not yet, alas, their crying.” And Zarathustra held his ears shut, for just then the donkey’s Yeah-aw was mingling strangely with the jubilant noise of these higher men.

“They are merry,” he began again, “and who knows? perhaps at their host’s expense; and if they learned to laugh from me, still it is not my laughter that they learned.

But what does it matter! They are old people: they recover in their own way, they laugh in their own way; my ears have put up with worse already and did not grow sullen.

This day is a victory: he is already giving way, he is fleeing, the spirit of gravity, my old archenemy! How well this day will end, which began so badly and so heavily!

And end it will. Already the evening is coming: it rides here over the sea, the good rider! How it sways, the blessed one, the homecoming one, in its purple saddles!

The sky looks on with clear eyes, the world lies deep: oh all you strange ones who came to me, it is already worth it to live with me!”

Thus spoke Zarathustra. And again the shouting and laughter of the higher men came out of the cave: then he began anew.

“They are biting, my bait is working, their enemy is giving way for them too, the spirit of gravity. Already they are learning to laugh at themselves: do I hear right?

My man’s food is working, my sayings full of sap and strength: and truly, I did not feed them on vegetables that bloat! But on warrior’s food, on conqueror’s food: I awakened new desires.

New hopes are in their arms and legs, their hearts are stretching out. They are finding new words, soon their spirit will breathe mischief.

Such food may not be for children, of course, nor for longing little women, old and young. Their bowels are persuaded in other ways; I am not their doctor and teacher.

Disgust is leaving these higher men: well then! that is my victory. In my kingdom they become secure, all stupid shame runs away, they pour themselves out.

They pour out their hearts, good hours come back to them, they celebrate and chew the cud, — they become grateful.

That I take as the best sign: they become grateful. Before long they will be thinking up festivals and setting up memorials to their old joys.

They are convalescents!” Thus spoke Zarathustra joyfully to his heart and looked out; but his animals pressed close to him and honored his happiness and his silence.

II

But suddenly Zarathustra’s ear took fright: for the cave, which until then had been full of noise and laughter, all at once went deathly still; — but his nose smelled a sweet-smelling smoke and incense, as if from burning pine cones.

“What is happening? What are they doing?” he asked himself, and crept up to the entrance, so that he could watch his guests unnoticed. But wonder upon wonder! what did he have to see there with his own eyes!

“They have all become pious again, they are praying, they are mad!” — he said, and was astonished beyond measure. And truly! all these higher men, the two kings, the retired pope, the wicked magician, the voluntary beggar, the wanderer and shadow, the old soothsayer, the conscientious one of the spirit, and the ugliest man: they were all lying on their knees like children and credulous little old women, worshipping the donkey. And just then the ugliest man began to gurgle and snort, as if something unspeakable wanted to come out of him; but when he had really brought it as far as words, look, it was a pious, strange litany in praise of the donkey they worshipped and burned incense to. And this litany went like this:

Amen! And blessing and glory and wisdom and thanksgiving and honor and might be to our God, forever and ever!

— But the donkey brayed in response: Yeah-aw.

He carries our burden, he took on the form of a servant, he is patient of heart and never says No; and whoever loves his God chastises him.

— But the donkey brayed in response: Yeah-aw.

He does not speak: except that he always says Yes to the world he created: so he praises his world. It is his cunning that does not speak: that way he is seldom in the wrong.

— But the donkey brayed in response: Yeah-aw.

Unnoticed he goes through the world. Gray is the coat in which he wraps his virtue. If he has spirit, he hides it; but everyone believes in his long ears.

— But the donkey brayed in response: Yeah-aw.

What hidden wisdom it is, that he wears long ears and says only Yes and never No! Did he not create the world in his own image, that is, as stupid as possible?

— But the donkey brayed in response: Yeah-aw.

You walk straight paths and crooked ones; it troubles you little what seems straight or crooked to us human beings. Beyond good and evil is your kingdom. It is your innocence not to know what innocence is.

— But the donkey brayed in response: Yeah-aw.

Look how you push no one away from you, not the beggars, nor the kings. You let the little children come to you, and when evil boys entice you, you say, in all simplicity, Yeah-aw.

— But the donkey brayed in response: Yeah-aw.

You love she-donkeys and fresh figs, you are no despiser of good food. A thistle tickles your heart when you happen to be hungry. In that lies the wisdom of a god.

— But the donkey brayed in response: Yeah-aw.

The Donkey Festival

I

At this point in the litany, however, Zarathustra could no longer master himself; he himself cried Yeah-aw, louder even than the donkey, and leaped into the midst of his guests, who had gone mad.

“But what are you doing there, you children of men?” he cried, pulling the worshippers up from the ground. “Woe, if anyone but Zarathustra were watching you:

Everyone would judge that with your new faith you were the worst blasphemers or the most foolish of all little old women!

And you yourself, you old pope, how does it square with you yourself, that you worship a donkey here as God in this fashion?” —

“Oh Zarathustra,” answered the pope, “forgive me, but in the things of God I am even more enlightened than you. And that is only fair.

Better to worship God in this form than in no form at all! Think about this saying, my lofty friend: you will quickly guess that there is wisdom in such a saying.

He who said ‘God is a spirit’ — he took the greatest step and leap toward unbelief that has yet been taken on earth: such a word is not easily made good again on earth!

My old heart leaps and skips because there is still something on earth to worship. Forgive that, oh Zarathustra, to an old, pious pope’s heart! —”

— “And you,” said Zarathustra to the wanderer and shadow, “you call yourself a free spirit and fancy that you are one? And here you practice such idol-worship and priest-worship?

Truly, you practice something worse here than with your wicked brown girls, you wicked new believer!”

“Wicked enough,” answered the wanderer and shadow, “you are right: but what can I do about it! The old God lives again, oh Zarathustra, say what you like.

The ugliest man is to blame for everything: he has raised him up again. And if he says that he once killed him: death is always just a prejudice where gods are concerned.”

— “And you,” said Zarathustra, “you wicked old magician, what did you do! Who, in this free age, should believe in you from now on, if you believe in such donkey-god foolishness?

It was a stupidity, what you did; how could you, you shrewd one, do such a stupid thing!”

“Oh Zarathustra,” answered the shrewd magician, “you are right, it was a stupidity, — and it was hard enough for me, too.”

— “And you of all people,” said Zarathustra to the conscientious one of the spirit, “consider, and put your finger to your nose! Does nothing here go against your conscience? Is your spirit not too clean for this praying and the fumes of these praying brothers?”

“There is something to it,” answered the conscientious one, and put his finger to his nose, “there is something in this spectacle that even does my conscience good.

Perhaps I am not allowed to believe in God: but it is certain that God seems to me most believable in this form.

God is supposed to be eternal, according to the testimony of the most pious: whoever has so much time takes his time. As slowly and as stupidly as possible: with that someone of that kind can still go very far.

And whoever has too much spirit might well fall in love with stupidity and folly themselves. Think about yourself, oh Zarathustra!

You yourself — truly! even you might well become a donkey out of overflow and wisdom.

Does not a perfect sage like to walk the crookedest paths? Appearances teach it, oh Zarathustra, — your appearance!”

— “And you yourself, last of all,” said Zarathustra, and turned to the ugliest man, who was still lying on the ground, raising his arm up to the donkey (for he was giving it wine to drink). “Speak, you unspeakable one, what have you done there!

You seem to me transformed, your eye glows, the mantle of the sublime lies around your ugliness: what did you do?

Is it true, then, what those others say, that you raised him up again? And what for? Was he not killed off and done away with for good reason?

You yourself seem to me awakened: what did you do? what did you turn around? What made you turn and convert? Speak, you unspeakable one?”

“Oh Zarathustra,” answered the ugliest man, “you are a rogue!

Whether He is still alive, or alive again, or thoroughly dead, — which of us two knows that best? I am asking you.

But one thing I know, — I learned it from you yourself once, oh Zarathustra: whoever wants to kill most thoroughly laughs.

‘One kills not with anger but with laughter’ — so you once said. Oh Zarathustra, you hidden one, you destroyer without anger, you dangerous saint, — you are a rogue!”

II

Then it happened that Zarathustra, astonished at so many roguish answers, sprang back to the door of his cave and, turning to all his guests, cried out in a strong voice:

“Oh you rogues and fools, every one of you, you jesters! Why do you disguise and hide yourselves from me!

How each of your hearts was wriggling with delight and malice, because at last you had once again become like little children, that is, pious, —

— because at last you did again as children do, that is, prayed, folded your hands, and said ‘dear God’!

But now leave me this nursery, my own cave, where today all childishness is at home. Out here, cool off your hot childish exuberance and the noise of your hearts!

Of course: unless you become like little children, you will not enter that kingdom of heaven. (And Zarathustra pointed upward with his hands.)

But we do not want to enter the kingdom of heaven at all: we have become men, — so we want the kingdom of earth.”

III

And once more Zarathustra began to speak. “Oh my new friends,” he said, — “you strange ones, you higher men, how well I like you now, —

— since you became joyful again! Truly, you have all blossomed: it seems to me that such flowers as you need new festivals,

— a little brave nonsense, some divine service and donkey festival, some old joyful Zarathustra-fool, a rushing wind that blows your souls bright.

Do not forget this night and this donkey festival, you higher men! That you invented here with me, that I take as a good omen, — such things only convalescents invent!

And if you celebrate it again, this donkey festival, do it for love of yourselves, do it also for love of me! And in remembrance of me!”

Thus spoke Zarathustra.

The Sleepwalker’s Song

I

Meanwhile, however, one after another had stepped outside, into the open air and the cool, thoughtful night; but Zarathustra himself led the ugliest man by the hand, to show him his night-world and the great round moon and the silver waterfalls near his cave. There at last they stood still beside one another, nothing but old people, but with comforted, brave hearts, and wondering to themselves that they felt so well on earth; and the secrecy of the night came closer and closer to their hearts. And once again Zarathustra thought to himself: “Oh, how well I like them now, these higher men!” — but he did not say it aloud, for he honored their happiness and their silence. —

But then there happened the thing that was the most astonishing of that long, astonishing day: the ugliest man began once more, and for the last time, to gurgle and to snort, and when he had brought it as far as words, look, a question sprang round and clean from his mouth, a good, deep, clear question, which stirred the heart in the body of everyone who heard him.

“My friends, all of you,” said the ugliest man, “what do you think? For the sake of this day — I am content for the first time to have lived my whole life.

And that I bear witness to this much is still not enough for me. It is worth it to live on the earth: one day, one festival with Zarathustra has taught me to love the earth.

‘Was that life?’ I will say to death. ‘Well then! Once more!’

My friends, what do you think? Will you not say to death, as I do: Was that life? For Zarathustra’s sake, well then! Once more!” —

Thus spoke the ugliest man; but it was not long before midnight. And what do you suppose happened then? As soon as the higher men heard his question, they became aware all at once of their transformation and their recovery, and of who had given it to them: then they sprang toward Zarathustra, thanking him, revering him, caressing him, kissing his hands, each in the way that was his own: so that some laughed and some wept. But the old soothsayer danced with pleasure; and even if, as some storytellers think, he was full of sweet wine at the time, he was certainly fuller still of sweet life, and had renounced all weariness. There are even some who tell that the donkey danced then: for it was not for nothing that the ugliest man had given it wine to drink beforehand. This may have been so, or it may have been otherwise; and if in truth the donkey did not dance that evening, still greater and stranger wonders happened then than the dancing of a donkey would be. In short, as Zarathustra’s saying goes: “What does it matter!”

II

But Zarathustra, while this was happening with the ugliest man, stood there like a drunken man: his gaze went dim, his tongue stammered, his feet swayed. And who could guess what thoughts were running over Zarathustra’s soul just then? But plainly his spirit drew back and fled ahead and was in far distances and, as it were, “on a high ridge, as it is written, between two seas,

— wandering between the past and the future like a heavy cloud.” Gradually, however, while the higher men held him in their arms, he came back to himself a little, and with his hands he fended off the throng of those who revered him and worried about him; but he did not speak. All at once, however, he turned his head quickly, for he seemed to hear something: then he laid his finger to his lips and said: “Come!”

And at once all around it became still and secret; but from the depths the sound of a bell came slowly up. Zarathustra listened for it, as did the higher men; but then he laid his finger to his lips a second time and said again: “Come! Come! It is getting close to midnight!” — and his voice had changed. But still he did not stir from the spot: then it became stiller and more secret still, and everything listened, even the donkey, and Zarathustra’s animals of honor, the eagle and the serpent, and likewise Zarathustra’s cave and the great cool moon and the night itself. But Zarathustra laid his hand to his lips a third time and said:

Come! Come! Come! Let us wander now! It is the hour: let us wander into the night!

III

You higher men, it is getting close to midnight: so I want to say something into your ears, as that old bell says it into my ear, —

— as secretly, as terribly, as heartily as that midnight bell speaks it to me, which has lived through more than any one man:

— which already counted out the heartache-heartbeats of your fathers — ah! ah! how she sighs! how she laughs in her dream! the old, deep, deep midnight!

Hush! Hush! Then many things are heard that may not grow loud by day; but now, in the cool air, when all the noise of your hearts has grown still as well, —

— now it speaks, now it is heard, now it creeps into nocturnal, overwakeful souls: ah! ah! how she sighs! how she laughs in her dream!

— do you not hear how she speaks secretly, terribly, heartily to you, the old, deep, deep midnight? Oh man, attend!

IV

Woe is me! Where has the time gone? Did I not sink into deep wells? The world is asleep —

Ah! Ah! The dog howls, the moon shines. I would sooner die, die, than tell you what my midnight heart is thinking just now.

Now I have died already. It is over. Spider, what are you spinning around me? Do you want blood? Ah! Ah! The dew is falling, the hour is coming —

— the hour when I shiver and freeze, the hour that asks and asks and asks: “Who has heart enough for it?

— who shall be lord of the earth? Who will say: this is how you shall run, you great rivers and small!”

— the hour draws near: oh man, you higher man, attend! this speech is for fine ears, for your ears — what does the deep midnight portend?

V

It carries me away, my soul is dancing. Day’s work! Day’s work! Who shall be lord of the earth?

The moon is cool, the wind is silent. Ah! Ah! Have you flown high enough yet? You danced: but a leg is still no wing.

You good dancers, now all joy is over, wine has turned to dregs, every cup has grown brittle, the graves are stammering.

You did not fly high enough: now the graves stammer, “Redeem the dead, then! Why is the night so long? Does the moon not make us drunk?”

You higher men, redeem the graves, then, wake the corpses! Ah, what is the worm still digging for? It is near, the hour is drawing near, —

— the bell is booming, the heart is still rattling, the woodworm is still digging, the heart-worm. Ah! Ah! The world is deep!

VI

Sweet lyre! Sweet lyre! I love your tone, your drunken toad-call tone! — from how long ago, from how far away your tone comes to me, from far away, from the ponds of love!

You old bell, you sweet lyre! Every pain has torn into your heart, father-pain, fathers’-pain, forefathers’-pain; your speech has grown ripe, —

— ripe like golden autumn and afternoon, like my hermit’s heart — now you speak: the world itself has grown ripe, the grape is turning brown,

— now it wants to die, to die of happiness. You higher men, do you not smell it? A scent is secretly welling up,

— a fragrance and scent of eternity, a rose-blissful, brown, gold-wine scent of old happiness,

of drunken midnight-dying-happiness, which sings: the world is deep, and deeper than the day could comprehend!

VII

Leave me! Leave me! I am too pure for you. Do not touch me! Has my world not just now become perfect?

My skin is too pure for your hands. Leave me, you stupid, oafish, stuffy day! Is the midnight not brighter?

The purest shall be lords of the earth, the most unknown, the strongest, the midnight souls, who are brighter and deeper than any day.

Oh day, you grope for me? You fumble for my happiness? To you I am rich, solitary, a treasure pit, a chamber of gold?

Oh world, you want me? Am I worldly to you? Am I spiritual to you? Am I godlike to you? But day and world, you are too clumsy, —

— have shrewder hands, reach for deeper happiness, for deeper misfortune, reach for some god or other, do not reach for me:

— my misfortune, my happiness is deep, you strange day, but still I am no god, no god’s hell: deep is its woe.

VIII

God’s woe is deeper, you strange world! Reach for God’s woe, not for me! What am I! A drunken sweet lyre, —

a midnight lyre, a bell-toad that no one understands, but that must speak, before the deaf, you higher men! For you do not understand me!

Gone! Gone! Oh youth! Oh noon! Oh afternoon! Now evening has come, and night and midnight, — the dog howls, the wind:

— is the wind not a dog? It whimpers, it yaps, it howls. Ah! Ah! how she sighs! how she laughs, how she rattles and gasps, the midnight!

How soberly she speaks just now, this drunken poetess! has she drunk herself past her drunkenness? has she grown overwakeful? is she chewing the cud?

— her woe she chews over again, in her dream, the old, deep midnight, and her joy still more. For joy, even if woe is deep: joy is deeper still than misery.

IX

You vine! Why do you praise me? Did I not cut you! I am cruel, you are bleeding —: what does your praise of my drunken cruelty want?

“What has become perfect, everything ripe — wants to die!” so you speak. Blessed, blessed be the vintner’s knife! But everything unripe wants to live: woe!

Woe says: “Go! Away with you, woe!” But everything that suffers wants to live, so that it may become ripe and joyful and full of longing,

— longing for what is farther, higher, brighter. “I want heirs,” so speaks everything that suffers, “I want children, I do not want myself,” —

But joy does not want heirs, nor children, — joy wants itself, wants eternity, wants recurrence, wants everything-eternally-the-same.

Woe says: “Break, bleed, heart! Walk, leg! Wing, fly! Onward! Upward! Pain!” Well then! Up, then! Oh my old heart: woe says: “Go!”

X

You higher men, what do you think? Am I a soothsayer? A dreamer? A drunkard? An interpreter of dreams? A midnight bell?

A drop of dew? A haze and fragrance of eternity? Do you not hear it? Do you not smell it? Just now my world became perfect, midnight is also noon, —

pain is also a joy, a curse is also a blessing, night is also a sun, — go away, or you will learn: a wise man is also a fool.

Did you ever say Yes to a single joy? Oh, my friends, then you said Yes to all woe as well. All things are linked, laced, in love, —

— if you ever wanted one time twice, if you ever said: “You please me, happiness! Quick! Moment, remain!” then you wanted everything back again!

— everything anew, everything eternally, everything linked, laced, in love, oh, that is how you loved the world, —

— you eternal ones, love it eternally and for all time: and to woe, too, you say: go, but come back! For all joy wants — eternity!

XI

All joy wants the eternity of all things, wants honey, wants dregs, wants drunken midnight, wants graves, wants the comfort of tears at the grave, wants gilded sunset —

— what does joy not want! it is thirstier, heartier, hungrier, more terrible, more secret than all woe, it wants itself, it bites into itself, the ring’s will wrestles in it, —

— it wants love, it wants hatred, it is overrich, it gives, throws away, begs someone to take it, thanks the one who takes, it would gladly be hated, —

— so rich is joy that it thirsts for woe, for hell, for hatred, for disgrace, for the cripple, for world, — for this world, oh, you know it well enough!

You higher men, it is for you that it longs, joy, the unruly, the blissful, — for your woe, you who have turned out badly! All eternal joy longs for what has turned out badly.

For all joy wants itself, and so it wants misery too! Oh happiness, oh pain! Oh break, heart! You higher men, learn it, then: joy wants eternity,

— joy wants the eternity of all things, wants deep, deep eternity!

XII

Have you learned my song now? Have you guessed what it wants? Well then! Up, then! You higher men, now sing me my round!

Sing me the song yourselves now, the song whose name is “Once More,” whose meaning is “to all eternity!” — sing, you higher men, Zarathustra’s round!

	Oh man, attend!
	What does the deep midnight portend?
	“I slept, I slept —,
	From a deep dream I woke, at its end: —
	The world is deep,
	And deeper than the day could comprehend.
	Deep is its woe —,
	Joy — deeper still than misery:
	Woe says: Go!
	But all joy wants eternity
	wants deep, deep eternity!”

The Sign

But on the morning after this night Zarathustra sprang up from his bed, girded his loins, and came out of his cave, glowing and strong, like a morning sun that comes out of dark mountains.

“You great star,” he said, as he had once spoken before, “you deep eye of happiness, what would all your happiness be, if you did not have those you shine for!

And if they stayed in their rooms while you are already awake and come and give and hand out: how your proud shame would be angered at that!

Well then! they are still asleep, these higher men, while I am awake: these are not my true companions! It is not for them that I am waiting here in my mountains.

I want to go to my work, to my day: but they do not understand what the signs of my morning are; my step — is no wake-up call for them.

They are still asleep in my cave, their dream is still chewing on my midnights. The ear that hears me, — the heeding ear is missing from their limbs.”

— Zarathustra had spoken this to his heart as the sun rose: then he looked up questioningly, for above him he heard the sharp cry of his eagle. “Well then!” he called up, “so it pleases me and so it befits me. My animals are awake, for I am awake.

My eagle is awake and, like me, honors the sun. With eagle’s talons he reaches for the new light. You are my true animals; I love you.

But I still lack my true men!” —

Thus spoke Zarathustra; but then it happened that he suddenly heard himself swarmed and fluttered around as if by countless birds, — but the whirring of so many wings and the crowding around his head was so great that he shut his eyes. And truly, it fell upon him like a cloud, like a cloud of arrows that pours itself out over a new enemy. But look, here it was a cloud of love, and over a new friend.

“What is happening to me?” thought Zarathustra in his astonished heart, and he slowly sat down on the great stone that lay beside the mouth of his cave. But as he reached with his hands around him and above him and below him, fending off the tender birds, look, something stranger still happened to him: for in doing so he reached, without noticing, into a thick, warm, shaggy mane; and at the same moment a roar rang out in front of him, — a soft, long lion’s roar.

“The sign is coming,” said Zarathustra, and his heart was transformed. And in truth, when it grew light before him, there lay at his feet a mighty yellow beast, and it pressed its head against his knees and would not leave him, out of love, and acted like a dog that finds its old master again. But the doves were no less eager with their love than the lion; and every time a dove flitted across the lion’s nose, the lion shook its head and wondered and laughed at it.

To all this Zarathustra said only one word: “My children are near, my children” —, then he fell completely silent. But his heart was loosened, and tears dropped from his eyes and fell on his hands. And he paid no more heed to anything, and sat there motionless, no longer fending off the animals. Then the doves flew to and fro and perched on his shoulder and caressed his white hair and did not tire of their tenderness and rejoicing. But the strong lion kept licking the tears that fell on Zarathustra’s hands, and roared and growled shyly as he did. That is how these animals carried on. —

All this lasted a long time, or a short time: for, properly speaking, there is no time on earth for such things —. Meanwhile, however, the higher men in Zarathustra’s cave had woken up and were forming themselves into a procession, to go out to meet Zarathustra and offer him their morning greeting: for when they woke they had found that he was no longer among them. But when they reached the door of the cave, and the sound of their steps ran ahead of them, the lion started violently, turned all at once away from Zarathustra, and leaped, roaring wildly, at the cave; and the higher men, when they heard it roar, all cried out as with one mouth, and fled back, and in an instant they were gone.

But Zarathustra himself, dazed and estranged, rose from his seat, looked around him, stood there in amazement, questioned his heart, came to himself, and was alone. “What was it I heard?” he said at last, slowly. “What just happened to me?”

And already the memory came back to him, and he grasped in a single glance everything that had taken place between yesterday and today. “Here is the stone,” he said, and stroked his beard, “on this one I sat yesterday morning; and here the soothsayer came up to me, and here I first heard the cry that I heard just now, the great cry of distress.

Oh you higher men, it was your distress that the old soothsayer foretold to me yesterday morning, —

— to your distress he wanted to seduce and tempt me: oh Zarathustra, he said to me, I come to seduce you to your last sin.

To my last sin? cried Zarathustra, and laughed angrily at his own word: what was it that was saved up for me as my last sin?”

— And once more Zarathustra sank into himself, and sat down again on the great stone, and pondered. Suddenly he sprang up, —

“Pity! Pity for the higher man!” he cried out, and his face turned to bronze. “Well then! That — has had its time!

My suffering and my pity — what does it matter! Am I striving for happiness? I am striving for my work!

Well then! The lion has come, my children are near, Zarathustra has grown ripe, my hour has come: —

This is my morning, my day is beginning: rise up now, rise up, you great noon!” —

Thus spoke Zarathustra, and he left his cave, glowing and strong, like a morning sun that comes out of dark mountains.
